{p. III}

# Foreword.

The expeditions and journeys that provided the material for the present work were not yet on the itinerary of my second departure for East Asia, which took place in early August of the year 1900. The decision to cross the southern part of Manchuria owes its origin to a spontaneous impulse that only occurred to me in Beijing when, after the conclusion of the preliminary peace at the beginning of the year 1901, I saw myself at the end of my activity as representative of the *Vossische Zeitung* [Voss's Newspaper] in the Chinese theatre of war, while my appetite for enterprise was not yet satisfied. Only a few days separated the decision and the start of its execution. Under these circumstances, there could naturally be no question of scholarly preparation beyond the knowledge of the regions to be traveled that I had gathered during my stays in East Asia, and for a proper assessment of the subsequent chapters, I consider it necessary to confess that the task I envisioned upon my departure from Beijing initially appeared to me to be a purely journalistic one. If I express the hope that the reader will find in this work somewhat more than a travel narrative suited to the taste of the educated newspaper reader, the reason lies primarily in the—at least initially—rather planless design of these journeys, in the course of which the objectives expanded along with the growing tasks. I hardly need emphasize that, as soon as circumstances permitted, I also engaged very thoroughly with the questions treated here on a theoretical level, and that the diary format corresponds to the journals kept on the journey only in its form, its factual skeleton, and its dates, whereas the actual elaboration was the work of the past year.

If I have retained the diary format, stylistic reasons were primarily decisive for this. Furthermore, the questions treated here are so far from being common knowledge among readers that one is forced to assume only a fairly slight familiarity with them. A systematic presentation, however, easily tempts one into a doctrinaire tone in such cases, whereas the diary format permits a more artistic treatment of the material, which in this version is brought far more vividly before the reader's eyes and therefore sticks with them more easily. Whether the execution corresponds to the intention, I cannot judge for myself; nevertheless, the aforementioned reason was primarily decisive for me in justifying a departure from the otherwise customary form of presentation—which, of course, is by no means to say that I allowed myself any poetic license regarding the subject matter treated that might give rise to doubts about the seriousness of the scholarly treatment of the material.

{p. IV}

Then, too, the diary dates protect me against the reproach that individual judgments and observations which I report as such have meanwhile been overtaken by events. History has certainly marched on since those days, and the reader will in certain cases, particularly in those sections dealing with Russia's position and intentions in southern Manchuria, be aware that more recent events have occurred that partly supplement and partly confirm my own observations. In the work itself, the reader's attention has been explicitly drawn to this at individual points by means of notes. I therefore kindly ask that this circumstance be remembered when reading in such cases.—

For an understanding of certain questions concerning connections with China and the recent Chinese campaign, the remark may perhaps be useful that the present work is in many respects a continuation of my work *Deutschland in China* [Germany in China], published by the same house. This applies particularly to the political and economic sections, for a closer understanding of which a recourse to the first, more theoretical book of the aforementioned work should prove expedient, while the descriptive chapters of *Deutschland in China* find their direct continuation in *Durch die Mandschurei und Sibirien* [Through Manchuria and Siberia].

For a long time I hesitated over whether I should include a map with the book. As far as Siberia and the Trans-Siberian Railway are concerned, I refrained from doing so because these regions are by no means unknown with respect to the points that could have been marked on a small map, and the reader of the work surely possesses a desk atlas that is entirely sufficient for this purpose. The question proved more difficult when it came to the southern Manchurian regions. My own map sketches cover only a small area, and as a result I could only have provided my itinerary, which, however, would rarely have served the intended purpose for the reader. Consequently, I set out in search of an existing map, though without finding the one I was looking for. Moreover, it would have presented difficulties to reconcile the spelling of the names on the maps with the phonetic transcription of place names that I chose for my book. I am well aware that, with my transcription of proper names, I place myself in opposition to prevailing usage. However, I proceed from the viewpoint that a name on a map should primarily serve to convey to the traveller, as accurately as possible, the sound of the place name that is familiar on the spot to the native from whom the traveller inquires. My method was always this: I had a native on the spot pronounce a name for me until I was myself in a position to repeat it accurately, and indeed in such a way that the native had nothing left to correct in my pronunciation. Then I wrote the name down in our alphabet exactly as I heard it. I consider this method to be the correct one, and I can approve of the efforts—which, particularly with regard to China, aim at implementing as uniform a transcription of place names as possible, with postal considerations playing a major role—only if the chosen transcription corresponds to how the name is pronounced on the spot. In such a case, the disadvantages of schematisation are greater than the advantages brought by simplifying specifically European communication. Admittedly, I must concede that I am myself inconsistent with regard to this theory. A single individual simply cannot fight against entrenched errors, and the post office would probably {p. V} not know where to send a letter destined for “Peking” or, worse yet, in French “Pékin”, if on the address I were to record the local pronunciation as “Baitsing”. It is very difficult to develop from this dilemma a compromise that does justice both to scholarship and to practical needs. Nevertheless, where special maps are concerned, I maintain first of all the standpoint that the pronunciation of the place name on the spot must be decisive for its transcription. For a special map serves primarily to orient a traveller, and his knowledge of route and region drawn from the map is at an end if, guided by it, he is unable, with the help of natives, to identify the local name and the name found on the map as the same geographical entity. Perhaps a compromise could be reached whereby maps would fundamentally adopt phonetic transcription according to local pronunciation and add in parentheses the erroneous, but unfortunately established, transcription. With regard to China in particular, the chapter of transcribing place names is especially disagreeable, and I have personal reason to raise the issue here. For in my search for a special map of the southern Manchurian regions through which I travelled, I also obtained the recently published “Mukden” sheet of the *Karte von Ost-China* [Map of East China], which is currently in course of publication by the Cartographical Department of the Royal Prussian Land Survey. I was very pleased when I learned of this new publication, because I hoped that order would at last be brought to the appalling chaos of our cartography of China, or at the very least that a uniform method would be attempted. My own judgment in this matter is admittedly based on a relatively small body of empirical experience—given the vast territorial expanse of China. Nevertheless, on the basis of my own travels and surveys, I am able in individual cases and along certain stretches to conduct precise spot checks. Insofar as these have been carried out for the sheets published thus far, “Mukden” and “Tsingtao”, I regret having to say that the disappointment I felt after examining these two sheets was complete. I will speak here only of the “Mukden” sheet, and indeed only of the area to which my personal and direct observations relate: with a few exceptions, I have been unable to reconcile any of the place names I recorded along the Jinzhou–Yingkou stretch with those marked on the map. Well, that is the general defect and grievance in the case of those Chinese maps that do not cover the linguistic area of the Mandarin dialect, or at least of the Peking dialect, which is very similar to it. For mostly—one might almost say regularly—Chinese cartographic material forms the basis of a European map of China. The cartographer usually follows the method—which I myself often employ for my private use—of having a Chinese *lettré* pronounce the Chinese characters appearing on the map, or, if he can read Chinese himself, pronouncing them to himself and then transcribing them phonetically or according to certain other principles. But if a European has mastered Chinese to such an extent that he can read a Chinese map, the rule can be established almost without exception that he has, at best, mastered only the two dialects mentioned above, which can almost be treated as a single dialect. The Chinese *lettré*, of course, likewise speaks the language of the educated, the Mandarin dialect, and thus it happens that on existing Chinese maps one finds almost without exception the {p. VI} transcription of the Chinese characters in the Mandarin dialect, regardless of whether it is understood in the regions in question at all or not. Now, to be sure, the Mandarin dialect is the language of scholars, and since only such a person can become an official, of officials as well; and there are of course people in all provinces who are capable of understanding a place name pronounced to them in the Mandarin dialect—although there are not precisely many of them. Yet however often I have made this experiment, both in northern Shandong and in the most diverse dialect regions of southern China, even the so-called educated Chinese person has only ever understood me once I showed him the character in question on the Chinese map. And the telling thing in this was always the observation that, when reading the character, the man initially never read it in the Mandarin dialect, but in the local dialect—proof that the pronunciation of the place name in the Mandarin dialect was not familiar to him at all—not even to him, the educated Chinese! Yet a map is surely nonsensical if, in order to understand it in the practical pursuit of its primary purpose—that is, on a journey—one must always keep in reserve, as a key, a Chinese scholar or official who converts the transcription in question (presuming it to be correct) into the original Chinese character, and only then pronounces this character once more in the local dialect, so that after this metamorphosis one finally knows where one is, or where one wants to go.*)

So much for this question! It deals with a fundamental ill of cartography relating to China, and I therefore regret doubly that the German map series of East Asia currently in course of publication apparently disregards completely the points of view set forth here. In the case of the "Mukden" sheet, there may also have been some tacking between Chinese, Russian, and Japanese maps, the latter two of which are likewise of an extremely doubtful nature as regards transcription, especially the Russian ones. Nevertheless, much remains incomprehensible to me—to single out just one example, why the German cartographer writes "kin tschou fu" and explains in the "Explanation of Chinese Names" that "k before i and ü equals ts." Even though the first word of the city name just mentioned is pronounced quite distinctly "Chin" on the spot, spelling it with "ts" would still come substantially closer to reality. Why, though, write "k" where one is supposed to read "ts"? That is the practitioner's point of view.

But the "Mukden" sheet also puts gross errors into the world. They relate mainly to the railway. First of all, the publisher explains on the index sheet to the map that, of the planned railways, only the German projects have been included, because sufficient material was available only for these. Nevertheless, on the "Mukden" sheet one finds railways and railway projects concerning non-German lines. Compliance with one's own rules therefore does not seem to be taken very strictly. If, however, one may assume that the publisher or editor of this map sheet was subsequently able to obtain sufficient map material {p. VII} to justify deviating from his announced principles, he should at least have ensured that this map material was indeed sufficient. That, however, is unlikely to have been the case. For while the so-called "Imperial Chinese Railway" continues from Jinzhoufu for a considerable distance in the direction of Mukden, crosses the Daling River in this direction, and only turns almost at right angles to the SE at the station of Goubangzi—which cannot be found on the map at all—the cartographer of the "Mukden" sheet has the railway turn ESE immediately after Jinzhoufu, crosses the Daling River with it near its mouth, leads it through the saltwater marshes situated north of the Gaizhou Bank, has it cross the Liao River a little below the town of Tianzhuangtai, runs it past the city of Niukou (Newchwang) on the left bank of the Liao River, leaving the city several kilometers to the south, and finally has it join the Russian "Chinese Eastern Railway" at the station of "Dashiqiao." I do not wish to presuppose of the author of the map sheet that he does not know that the "Imperial Chinese Railway" ends at Newchwang, and that the "Chinese Eastern Railway" begins at the same city, so that in Newchwang itself English interests (appearing in Chinese guise) and Russian interests meet in the form of railways. Nor do I wish to presuppose in him knowledge of the fact that the Jinzhoufu–Mukden section, which he still marks as "under construction," is no longer being built beyond Goubangzi station, because the Russians refuse to allow the English railway company to cross the "Chinese Eastern Railway," which runs west of Mukden. These are primarily political questions, which concern the geographer only secondarily, although he really ought to know them as well. But as such, the cartographer should *absolutely* have known that a railway does not run through salt marshes that are, moreover, actually drawn on the map; furthermore, that a railway bridge across the Liao River has never existed; and that the "Imperial Chinese Railway" terminates on the *right* bank of the Liao River, quite close to its mouth, whereas the Russian "Chinese Eastern Railway" begins on the *left* bank of the Liao River, several kilometers upriver from the foreign settlement. These are errors that ought all the less to have occurred, since these are finished railways.

A map is a documentary record—at any rate, the geographer regards it as such—and when assumptions are included in a map that can be proven neither on the basis of verified material nor by personal experience, it is a duty to bring this to the attention of the unsuspecting map reader. I do not wish to generalize and pass a verdict on the entire "East Asia" map for these reasons. Yet one would not expect such glaring errors in an undertaking published by the Cartographic Department of the Royal Prussian Land Survey. *China, the vast, immense China, is in fact still so little explored in its details—geographically as well—and Chinese cartographic material has been so little verified and has so often proved misleading, that the time is likely not yet ripe to bring about even now the publication of a fundamental map of China whose scale is large enough to warrant the demands that one has a right to make of specialized maps.* —

::: notes
*) An example of dialect differences: In South China, a river appears on the map as “Pe-kiang.” That is Northern Chinese and means “North River.” On the spot, no one understands if you ask for the “Pe-kiang,” because the same written character is pronounced “Pak-koyk” there. This example also shows that the Mandarin dialect can by no means be described as the Chinese written language taking the place, as it were, of High German, since the characters are simply pronounced quite differently depending on the region. This goes so far that Chinese and Japanese can even communicate with each other by means of Chinese characters, without being able to understand one another if each were to read the characters aloud in their own language.
:::

The illustrations in the present work are for the most part reproductions of my own photographs. From a number of photographs that were unsuitable for direct reproduction because of their lack of sharpness {p. VIII} or their inartistic composition, drawings in pen and ink were produced by the painter Mr. C. Arriens in Berlin. Mr. Arriens has been a loyal collaborator, whom I thank above all for his sensitive appreciation of those elements in the illustrations that are characteristic, or ethnologically or geographically significant.

At this point, I warmly thank all those who have kindly lent their assistance to the present work.

Wilmersdorf near Berlin, October 1902.

Rudolf Zabel.

{p. IX}

{p. [20]}

# Book 1. Through Manchuria.

{p. 3}

## Chapter I. From Peking to the Eastern End of the Great Wall.

### 1. From Peking to Tanggu.

Peking, January 27, 1901 (the Kaiser’s birthday).

Peking, future international garrison city! The occupation of the Legation Quarter is already underway, and individual plots are already being surveyed and plans drawn up for barracks buildings. In the entire quarter between the Hatamen and Qianmen on the one side and Sir Robert Hart Street on the other, there is no longer a single house that does not bear the marking: “Occupied”—by this or that legation. The German legation is particularly conspicuous. Walking down Legation Street, one sees that almost the entire quarter lying between the German mission building and the Hatamen bears the inscription: “Occupied by the German Legation.” Recently, notices have also been posted with the remark: “Building site of the German protection force.” Only here and there has a foreign power smuggled itself in, its seal to be found on some small Chinese property. To make up for this, however, the German minister has in turn requisitioned plots in foreign sectors. Later on, matters can easily be sorted out; there will be swapping back and forth until everything is neatly rounded off. It would not be bad at all if this policy were applied on a grander scale. Even now, after all, England leaves its leased territory at Weihaiwei virtually lying fallow. Some people who hear the grass grow whisper that {p. [22]} England intends, should all of Shandong ever become German, to offer Weihaiwei as a present to the German Emperor or use it as an object of exchange à la Heligoland. That, of course, is nonsense. To be sure, it would not be unwelcome if we, too, held in other spheres of interest some small, relatively worthless scrap of land that we could, when the opportunity arose, dispose of profitably and at good value. In any case, the eventual arrangement among the powers within China would not be nearly so perilous for us in such an eventuality if we took precautions for it in good time. Whoever holds the most at the moment of partition will, by all human reckoning, also keep the most.

Since the domestic concerns of the future international fortress right next to the imperial palace in the sacred city of Peking hold little interest for me, however, and I have in particular a strong aversion to taking part in the garrison life developing in Peking over the coming months with all the unpleasantness of military routine, I decide on a side trip to Manchuria, from where rumors of war and alarms of battle still penetrate even as far as Peking. There I hope still to find a rewarding field of work. So today I pack up the bare essentials for the journey, inform the most faithful of my Chinese boys, Wang Faxian, that tomorrow morning at 7:30 we must arrive at the Peking railway station opposite the Temple of Heaven together with our baggage, and instruct him to prepare for an extended journey. As usual, he has no objection.

January 28.

Anyone who has closely followed Russia’s policy in East Asia over recent years could hardly be in any doubt that Russia would exploit the turmoil of 1900 to secure some advantage for its own private account. Year after year one sees Russian advances in fields where they would never have been expected; the financial community in particular found the extraordinary prosperity of the Russo-Chinese Bank rather surprising. Yet with us in Germany, the constant refrain regarding the question of “Russia in East Asia” is: “quieta non movere!” When the masses are in motion, however, letting them continue to flow wherever they please simply will not do. In my view, it is impossible for us to remain content with adopting an attitude of “laisser faire, laisser aller” toward Russia’s East Asian policy. On the other hand, among the commercial powers that share interests in East Asia, the saying goes: “Russia is the one and only enemy we all have in East Asia.” It is absolutely essential to take a clear stance on this question for once. Russia has withdrawn its entire military force to Manchuria. No one knows what it is doing there, what its political intentions are, or what its commercial-political desires and accomplishments may be. The British press claims that Russia has annexed Manchuria. No one knows whether it is true. Manchuria is sealed tight against news. I shall attempt to penetrate the secret. This task will not be easy.

{p. [23]}

Ever since its complete restoration, the railway from Peking to Tianjin and beyond has been in German hands. Four companies of the Railway Regiment were assigned the task of bringing the line into operation. It is well known that all the nations took part in repairing the railway line. Count Waldersee regarded restoring this link between Peking and the sea as one of the first and most important tasks remaining to be accomplished upon his arrival, and so the work was pushed forward zealously and finally completed in mid-December 1900. The work was delayed most by the reconstruction of the destroyed bridges, especially the large Peiho Bridge near Yangcun, the building of which had fallen to the German railway battalion under Major Bauer. The line is secured by numerous staging posts of all nations, which stretch along both the railway track and the highway, permitting a completely safe and hazard-free journey from Peking. The first posts along the railway, counting from Peking, belong to the Japanese. Those that follow are mostly German or French.

The railway line now runs closer to Peking than was previously the case. Given the great aversion to the iron horse that the Chinese have hitherto displayed, they refused to permit the tracks to approach close to the city, even though the railway—built, to be sure, mostly with British capital—enjoys Chinese state protection and is, in name at least, a Chinese state railway. It bears the proud name "Imperial Chinese Railway." Now the line has been extended and runs directly into the city of Peking itself—not, indeed, into the Tartar City, but only near the central southern gate of the Chinese City. Yet the station, or rather the site where the station is one day to stand, lies precisely between two of China's exalted sanctuaries, between the Temple of Heaven and the Temple of Agriculture, to the side of the road that leads in a straight line toward the Imperial Palace. To bring the railway into the city, a breach was made in the city wall. That in itself is an insult of the worst kind to the good city of Peking. By this, but above all by the fact that the locomotive now takes its path right between the holiest sanctuaries of China, in which only the Emperor is permitted to offer sacrifice, the *feng shui*—the auspicious harmony of the place, to which the Chinese attach such great importance—must be thoroughly ruined. If the Chinese were able to do as they liked, they would undoubtedly treat this railway line after the restoration of peace in exactly the same way they once treated the first railway, Shanghai–Wusong, which they bought up, tore up, and shipped to Formosa to let rust away there.

I cannot help thinking of this as I ride through the breach in the wall in a well-heated carriage, with the temperature outside at about 10 degrees Réaumur below zero, and as we now head out into the desolate country between Peking and the sea. This road has become historic; on it the greatest and most important operations of several campaigns have taken place, and of all the roads along which I have travelled in China {p. 6}, the land to the right and left displays most of all the picture of destruction, of devastation. Yet the forces of reconstruction have already come into their own again. Thanks to the industrious and order-loving spirit of the Germans and Japanese, who resemble each other in this respect, neat station buildings have already been erected everywhere upon the ruins of the old ones; in the vicinity of the stations one finds clean, tidy little cottages providing quarters for the soldiers; and gradually a brisk bustle is reasserting itself among the Chinese who have returned, are rebuilding their houses, and are beginning once more to till their fields. Striking too is the petty trade that has revived along the railway line. Whole crowds of Chinese come flocking up, offering pastries, or eggs, or fruit, or the perfumed American cigarettes that enjoy great popularity among the soldiers in the absence of any others. The Chinese is very quick to spot an opportunity to do business, and above all he likes to trade with Europeans, because he gets better prices from them. The *coolie*, who has little to lose, has probably rarely been better off and has never worked for such high wages or percentages as during the period of the occupation of Zhili Province.

We pass all the places where skirmishes took place; we arrive at Langfang, well known from the Seymour Expedition. The landscape is the same to the right and left, fields and yet more fields, the entire countryside steeped in monochrome gray, with only a few withered branches stretching toward the sky here and there, usually marking the burial grounds near villages. Finally, toward midday, we reach a large bridge: the reconstructed railway bridge at Yangcun. The Peiho is frozen, several junks are trapped fast in the ice, and above it, assembled makeshift from salvaged iron parts and wooden beams, spans the Peiho Bridge. We have arrived at Yangcun Station. A pleasant, fairly large station building. It is decked with flags and wreathed in garlands; a large "W. II." is painted on a paper sign. It is still the decoration from yesterday's celebration of the Kaiser's birthday. At all the stations along the line, flags have been flown and greenery put up, as far as any was to be had. The foreign staging posts have decorated themselves as well.

Opposite the station building, a German flag flutters over a row of barracks, beside it the flag of the Red Cross. A field hospital! At the station, bustle and activity reign, as everywhere. Mingling with the soldiers of the railway regiment who operate the train are the infantrymen stationed here on staging duty. The staging commander is personally at the tracks, as he likely is every day and for every train. The railway is to him what the newspaper or the pub is to us back home. At the station he hears the news from Peking or Tientsin; there he greets old friends and comrades and makes new acquaintances. He recounts what news he himself has gathered, and travelers passing through unpack what they know. Fact and fiction—all jumbled together. And there is more gossiping on such occasions than at any ladies' coffee circle!

I open the carriage window and look out. An honest musketeer comes along carrying a large tub containing something steaming, and calls through the open window: {p. 7} “Want some tea?” “How much is it then?” comes the answer. “It doesn’t cost a thing!” “Well then, come right into the parlour!” The good-natured Holsteiner steps into the carriage with his tub, the mess-tin lids are taken off, and the ladling begins. Meanwhile, news is exchanged and politics debated to everyone’s heart’s content. Here along the railway line, the “ninth companies” are stationed on communications duty. The lads were the last to come out, and already they talk of nothing else than “going back home.”

[Illustration: Interior of one of the Beitang forts (p. 12).]

The signal for departure is given; the tea-man jumps with his tub from the train, which is already in motion again. The landscape becomes just as bleak as it was before, and weary of looking, I lean back into my corner. It is already late afternoon when we pull into the station at Tianjin. A great deal has changed there since my last stay. The station building has been completed again; back then the Russians held the railway, but now it is under German administration. And above all else: the coolies have become incredibly insolent. They jostle and shove around the traveler, they pounce on my luggage, which they want to haul over to the train for Tanggu, and one thrashes the other to snatch the earnings away from him. In all, I have six pieces of luggage, of {p. [26]} which two weigh about a hundredweight each, while the others weigh only a few pounds. In the blink of an eye I have ten coolies around me; four each want to carry the large trunks, and of the other pieces each coolie takes one. The lightest weighs about a pound, but the fellow heaves at it as though it were a hundredweight load. Finally my boy, a burly, strong Shandong man, succeeds—with the help of liberally distributed slaps and raps on the head—in reducing the importunate crowd to four, who tie the luggage together and carry it off on their bamboo poles without great exertion.

If I do not wish to miss the connection to Shanhaiguan, I must travel on to Tanggu that very same day. A great difference is indeed to be noted between transport then and now. The Russians, who were running the railway at the time the East Asian Expeditionary Corps arrived, regularly took nearly three hours to cover the distance between Tianjin and Tanggu; now it takes barely an hour. In general, only the highest praise can be spoken of the German operation of the line. Our railway troops had tasks to accomplish here that were not only interesting, but also made great demands on the efficiency of the force. Unlike under the Russians, who took no money, the line is operated for fares. I shall carefully preserve the little handwritten and partly poorly printed tickets as interesting mementos.

In Tanggu I find more or less everything as it was. I put up at the so-called “Foreign Hotel,” which had already given me a hospitable welcome once before, after the storming of the Beitang forts. Now the “hotelier”—a new host, by the way, no longer the Armenian, but a Galician Jew—has fitted out a real guest room. This I move into. In the room there is also a bed, a real, proper bed! Since I first set foot off the *Rhein* at Taku, this is once again the first real bed I have slept in. For so long, a few woolen blankets, a Chinese sheepskin, and a well-heated *kang* were my only sleeping accoutrements. And I felt more comfortable with these than later in a real, soft bed.

During the campaign Tanggu achieved a certain fame, or rather notoriety, namely as the place where goods and property ceased to be safe. As is well known, all goods and provisions destined for the interior of Zhili province passed through Tanggu. As long as the Peiho or at least the Taku roadstead was free of ice, Tanggu was the only loading point. Since the roadstead froze over, it has at least been the transshipment point for goods arriving by rail from Shanhaiguan. The same applies to consignments from the interior destined for Europe. As a result, {p. 9} one finds quite considerable stocks of goods accumulated there from time to time. If one now compares the quantity of lost—that is to say, stolen—goods with the sum total of imported goods, a very considerable percentage of loss results. There are probably few military units that could not report stolen shipments, particularly of drinks. The main “hub” for these thefts, however, was Tanggu. Here the business was conducted on a truly grand scale, and let it be noted at once that it was not the Chinese who were the thieves

[Illustration: A victim of Chinese destructive fury (p. 12).]

were, but European soldiers. Nor could the thefts ever have taken place on such a scale had the rogues not been aided by the businessmen in Tongku themselves. With commendable candour, the proprietor of the Foreign Hotel told me various details. Thus it happened that Cossacks—Russians are most often referred to as the thieving rats—turned up offering an ox, on another occasion 500 sacks of flour at 50 cents a sack, then again a crate of beer, a crate of champagne; indeed, whole junk-loads were sold. But the thieves did not spare their buyers either; they broke into their premises {p. 10} themselves again by night and robbed and plundered whatever they could find. The next day they would go to the competitor and dispose of the goods there. Sometimes it happened that goods were stolen from one man, which the other bought, only to have them stolen from him in turn and found the next day with the first owner. At that time, a kind of communism seems to have prevailed among the shopkeepers in Tongku, or at least among the vagabond adventurers—and there were quite a number of them. In the possession of one of these “merchants,” a substantial lot of preserved fruit was found that had been destined for the German field hospitals and stolen. He met with his well-deserved punishment: two years’ imprisonment and a fine of 3,000 dollars.

By way of illustration, another delightful story that the talkative innkeeper tells me in an indescribably comical mixture of Austrian and Jewish jargon: “So I bought an ox from a Cossack—for twenty dollars. Max comes to me, I show him the ox, says he, that’s my ox, they stole it from me in the night. Say I to Max: ‘Max,’ say I, ‘the ox cost me twenty dollars what I gave the Cossack. We’re both businessmen; good, I’ll give you back the ox if you give me back my twenty dollars.’ ‘Good,’ says Max, says he, ‘I’ll give you twenty dollars.’ He gives me twenty dollars, takes the ox, and walks off. I go get the Cossack, say to him: ‘You dog, you stole the ox what you sold me for twenty dollars from Max! I had to give back the ox to Max, you give me back the twenty dollars.’ Max had his ox back, I myself had back my twenty dollars from Max, plus twenty dollars from the Cossack. I made twenty dollars pure profit.”

That is how business is done in Tongku.

### 2. A Much-Disputed Railway Line.

29 January.

After all the hardships that expedition life entailed, traveling by train seems like pure pleasure to me. In general, one only learns to appreciate the achievements of our European civilization once one has had to do without them. Back home, it is easy for us to complain about the railway, the post, and the telegraph whenever they do not function quite as we would like. Learning to appreciate these institutions properly is something one only does abroad.

To be sure, even in China there is no unclouded joy, as I am soon to discover once again. Having looked forward to continuing my journey in a nice, heated carriage, I am sorely disappointed when, at Tanggu, I board a “first”-class compartment whose windows are partly smashed and whose walls still show the holes torn by enemy bullets. The wooden blinds have been pulled up after a fashion, but that does little good against the icy air that now whistles through the bullet holes and other leaks in the carriage. There is, to be sure, a little stove in this “saloon carriage,” but it has too much draft, so that the railway soldier—who, on account of the stove refusing to burn, gets treated every five minutes by the non-commissioned officer in charge of the train to the choicest flowers of barrack-square invective—uses half {p. 11} a can of kerosene to get the fire going. Even so, it refuses to burn. At last, the united efforts of the soldier, the non-commissioned officer, a fellow-traveling Cossack staff surgeon, and yours truly succeed in coaxing the fire to life. The two of us last-mentioned then volunteer to tend the stove—not, of course, out of charity, but from sheer self-interest. Yet our iron comrade is of little help. The pipe serving as a chimney passes out into the open through a large square hole in the carriage roof, and all the heat takes the very same route.

[Illustration: Emergency platform at Hangtou (p. 12).]

It was in this condition that the Germans only recently took over railway operations from the Russians. The stove is already a new acquisition. Before handing the line over to the Germans, the Russians removed all the heaters. Yet not only did they remove these, leave the carriages unrepaired, and allow the locomotives to get filthy, but they also transferred by far the greater part of the rolling stock, especially engines, to the section beyond Shanhaiguan, which they intend to keep permanently.

{p. 12}

Around 10 o’clock in the morning, the train leaves Tanggu station. We pass through the terrain on which the Beitang forts lie. They are occupied by small guard detachments. Above them fly the German and Russian flags. — Beitang Station! The train stops; a small detachment of German artillerymen from the heavy howitzer battery stationed in Tanggu gets out and marches over toward the fort to supply their comrades stationed there with fresh provisions, above all with water. The region really deserves to be passed over in silence. It is desolate. Between the causeways that connect the forts with one another—where last autumn, when we took the works at a run, everything was still full of water—all is now dry, frozen, and the uneven ground is covered with a white layer that is neither ice nor snow, but salt deposited from the brackish, evaporated water. The train now crosses the small river separating Forts I and II from the others. Counting the large rampart-enclosed military camps, I make out seven fortifications in all. Beside the railway embankment lies a derailed locomotive.

After about an hour and a half’s ride, the call comes: “All out!” We have arrived at the Hang He, specifically at Hangkou Station. The bridge over the river has been destroyed. One must transfer to another train waiting on the opposite bank of the river. Scarcely has our train halted when a hundred coolie arms stretch out toward our luggage; likewise, all the goods destined for onward transit, mailbags, etc., are unloaded and carried by the coolies across a fairly wide and solid temporary wooden bridge. The Russians, who had repaired the railway line as far as Shanhaiguan, chose not to subject themselves to the arduous labor of building this bridge, and I believe the Germans too will leave its reconstruction to the owners of the railway, particularly since the repairs would take so much time that in the meantime the roadstead of Taku will be open again, thereby substantially diminishing the importance of the railway for military purposes.

In Hangkou, a shortage of passenger carriages is already making itself felt. We therefore board a “second”-class carriage, which differs from the “first”-class saloon carriage in that the wooden benches have straight rather than curved backs and seats, much like the better fourth-class carriages back home. In return, however, this carriage possesses the great advantage of being excellently heated, and this circumstance more than makes up for all its shortcomings.

Besides the Cossack staff surgeon, who is bundled up in his thick fur coat and does not take it off despite the glowing stove, there are three German officers in the compartment: Major Borkenhagen, commander of the heavy field howitzer detachment in Tanggu; Captain von Koxzing of the field artillery regiment in Tientsin; and Ordnance Lieutenant Deutsch. These three gentlemen form a commission whose task is to pass judgment on the artillery pieces captured by Germany in China and to select those that are to go to the Zeughaus. The gentlemen have already been to the arsenals in Peking and Tientsin, as well as to the Taku {p. 13} and Peitang Forts, and have issued their orders there. Now they are travelling to Shanhaiguan to inspect the guns found in the forts there. At the railway station in Tanggu, one could already see an entire train loaded exclusively with Chinese cannons of all kinds, including a number of splendidly crafted old bronze guns that will be on display to be admired in the Zeughaus.

I have often heard it said that we had really done China considerable harm through the seizure and destruction of so many guns and weapons. In response, I can only point out again and again that Zhili is only *one* province of China, and that our troops—that is, the international troops—have by no means combed through even the whole of Zhili. To be sure, the arsenals in Tientsin, Peking, and several other cities have been destroyed, and the Peitang and Taku Forts will be razed, perhaps also the Lutai and Shanhaiguan Forts. But there are still many forts in China, and we have found arms and ammunition supplies in almost every larger city—or rather, failed to find them. So let us not be too boastful of our successes in China. We have barely scratched China's skin, and it has far too much resilience to be taken so lightly in the future as has been done in the past.

Early in the afternoon we approach the town of Lutai. It is probably best known for the army that withdrew from there upon the arrival of the allied forces and was never seen again. A series of military operations was directed against it, without anyone being able to lay hands on it. This army was stationed in no fewer than seventeen forts that encircle the town proper like a belt. Yet these are not forts in our sense, but fortified military camps mounted with artillery. Since the departure of the Chinese, the Russians have maintained a guard in the forts, but since the Russians handed the railway over to the Germans, the largest of them has been garrisoned by—six men.

The station buildings along this line are not as distinguished and elegant as those on the Peking–Tanggu line; for the most part, they were built only recently by German soldiers using timber and corrugated iron underpinned with masonry. In Lutai, I greet the officer of the railway regiment stationed there, who has created a very cosy home for himself out of these limited means—small, but charming.

The next station is Kaiping, known for its coal mines. They belong to the Chinese Engineering and Mining Co. and supply an excellent coal with which not only Tientsin but also a large portion of the steamers operating in North Chinese waters are provided. It is a good, rich coal, far more valuable than Japanese coal, which burns away too quickly.

{p. [32]}

The countryside now begins to become somewhat more pleasant. Here and there, ridges of hills approach quite close to the railway; the fields are well cultivated and show good care; at the stations, the Chinese offer fruit, chestnuts, nuts, cakes, and the like for sale. But beggars and the sick, among them unfortunate lepers, also crowd around the carriages and plead for alms. Thus the journey continues without variation until, already in the dark, at around seven in the evening, we arrive in Tangshan, which lies roughly halfway between Tanggu and Shanhaiguan. Today the train goes only as far as this station, so we disembark and are warmly received by the German staging post stationed there.

The Tangshan railway station is relatively elegant and large. It has a well-concreted platform and a proper, large station building. The town is quite significant, especially because of the nearby mines and the large factory buildings that have been established here along the railway. Above all, there are railway workshops, iron foundries, and engineering works of considerable size. At present, to be sure, they are still at a standstill—less because there would be nothing to do or because this area had suffered particularly from the Boxers or troops, but because the Chinese workers, who mostly came from Hong Kong or Shanghai, fled to their homes at the beginning of the Boxer unrest and have not yet returned. Several large mission houses also stand in Tangshan; the missionaries, to be sure, likewise preferred to reach safety in good time. The German staging post has quartered itself in one of these buildings, where a number of guest rooms have been fitted out. Everything is, of course, wartime simple, but neat and clean—like in a hotel—and is also run as such, because every evening guests are expected from the trains who cannot be accommodated anywhere else. Under these circumstances, it is self-evident that one does not stay for free. But the prices are at cost, and no charge is even made for the room. In any case, the officer responsible for household management has made all the arrangements with positively touching care and great circumspection—proof that the German officer, when required, can play the hotelier himself.

So in the evening we celebrate a cheerful reunion in the staging post hotel: the major, the captain, the ordnance sergeant, the Russian Cossack staff surgeon—who, incidentally, is treated as a guest of the German Empire and does not have to pay anything—and I. In the pleasant company of the staging officers, the dinner tastes splendid to us after having fed "out of the crate" for the two preceding days, and it is late before we seek out our beds.

During the night, the station is fired upon several times from a cemetery situated higher up. The night before, a sentry shot and killed a Chinese man who attacked him, and the Chinese are evidently out for revenge.

Moreover, the joy with the fine staging house is soon to come to an end. The rightful owners have already reported back and are demanding their house return. Negotiations for a compromise are indeed underway, but the missionaries insist {p. 15} on their bond. Thus the German staging post will likely soon have to move out of the beautiful property that it took so much trouble to fit out.

30 January.

This morning at 10 o'clock the train continues in the direction of Shanhaiguan. We enter a mountainous region, the same formation as throughout North China: bare, treeless hills and behind them blue, partly rugged peaks; the individual ridges are separated by fairly flat valleys through which the railway wends its way. It certainly strikes me that the region here is more prosperous than in the south. One can see beautifully built houses, well-tended fields, and above all very many more trees. The villages are consistently the most beautiful feature of the landscape.

A few kilometres from Qinhuangdao, the rival port to Shanhaiguan, which the British in particular have expanded and put in order, we cross a fairly wide river on a bridge nearly five hundred metres long, rising about ten metres above the water level. When we arrive at Qinhuangdao station, where a fairly strong detachment of German troops is also stationed, it is already dark. The port proper lies about an hour away from the station. The British have built a connecting line there, which, however, has no direct link to the tracks of the main line. Why this connection is not being built, I do not know. In general, the rule is that Shanhaiguan and Qinhuangdao complement each other. That is to say, if the wind comes from the south-east, Shanhaiguan is usually iced over; if the wind comes from the north-east, one cannot land at Qinhuangdao. The British probably attach such importance to this port at present because at Shanhaiguan they have the uncomfortable neighbourhood of the Russians. On the other hand, Russia has refused to contribute to the port expansion works undertaken in Qinhuangdao by the allied powers, simply because it is banking on Shanhaiguan and therefore does not want to help improve a landing place that serves essentially foreign interests.

### 3. The Gates Are Closed.

Another three-quarters of an hour’s train ride, and we have arrived in Shanhaiguan. I take leave of my traveling companions, of whom the German officers head for the German staging base, while the Russian hurries to his regiment, which is quartered near the station. As I have done so often, I would ask for the hospitality of the German staging base, but it lies nearly an hour away from the station, and since I am unfamiliar with local conditions and would face too many difficulties with my luggage, especially in the dark, I prefer to spend the night at the station. It has turned bitterly cold. I find it to be 19 degrees Réaumur below zero. In addition, a biting wind is blowing. In the station building, they are already prepared for guests; for lodging purposes, they have {p. 16} an old, fairly broad sofa, which is put at my disposal, and so I camp out in the office of Shanhaiguan station, where Herr Ries from Hamburg, by trade a sergeant in the 2nd Railway Company, discharges his duties as stationmaster.

To refresh myself a little after the long train journey, I seek out a restaurant near the station kept by a Japanese. It is furnished about as comfortably as a beer tent on a fairground back home; one sits on crude wooden benches and eats from wooden tables whose legs are simply driven into the ground. In the middle of the house, which is fitted with paper walls, stands a small iron stove doing its level best to make our stay somewhat more tolerable. At a neighboring table Russian soldiers are drinking vodka, and at another Japanese coolies are talking in shrill voices. Apart from these accompaniments, the “dinner” is quite good. Once I have this main attraction of the said establishment behind me, I turn up the collar of my Siberian fur coat, ordered from Vladivostok, pull my otter-fur cap down over my ears, and—move on to another drinking establishment. This one is somewhat more inviting. It is kept by two Frenchwomen, one of whom must once have sung beautifully, while the other, with a little imagination, might still pass today—by Shanhaiguan standards—as passable. Many cavalrymen are sitting with these hostesses. They are Russian officers enjoying themselves splendidly over champagne, vodka, and, last but not least, a great deal of confectionery. In this lively company I am evidently no unwelcome guest. I am immediately recognized as a German and celebrated as such. The Russian loves to fraternize with Germans. In his tipsy good nature he is simply priceless. The amiability and the pressing of drinks, the toasts and handshakes, the kissing among one another—all of this makes for a truly delightful spectacle for the sober onlooker. And so for an hour I feel quite comfortable in this merry circle of drinkers; there is singing, the older Frenchwoman with the voice that must once have been beautiful sings too, we all sing, and could well have sung until early morning had I not wanted to ride out to the roadstead at the crack of dawn the next day to look for a steamer to take me to Port Arthur. I intend to make this place the starting point of my Manchurian journey. I therefore took my leave early and stretched out on the broad chaise longue in the office of Shanhaiguan station.

January 31.
The next morning it is still just as bitterly cold as the evening before. Nevertheless, I pack my things onto two Chinese carts and ride to the roadstead, which lies a good hour away from the station. The route leads along a heavily traveled road through the Shanhaiguan forts, which, seventeen in number, surround the town and at the time fell into the hands of the Allies without a blow being struck, just like the Lutai forts. Running alongside the road is a small narrow-gauge field railway on which goods landed at Shanhaiguan are transported to the station. The flags of the Powers fly over the forts.

{p. 17}

After an hour’s ride on the jolting cart, I finally reach the sea. With the mountains behind me, the view opens before me onto the wide expanse of the sea, the Gulf of Liaodong. “Shanhaiguan,” translated into German, means: “Mountains by the Great Sea.” Here is the spot where the Great Wall of China meets the Pacific Ocean.

We are now only a few hundred meters from the “water’s edge”; to the right of the road stand two large temples, with a German sentry in front. That is the German staging post. Already one can see the steamers lying far out in the roadstead. Now we have arrived at the coastal dune as well—but alas, the harbor is completely frozen over. Any communication with the ships outside is impossible. From an English shack-keeper who ekes out a miserable existence in a wretched mud hut on the beach, I learn that all the passenger ships left the harbor this morning on account of the ice and steamed further out to sea. That the roadstead of Qinhuangdao is frozen over I already know, otherwise I would have disembarked there. So there is nothing left for me to do but have my carts turned around and return to Shanhaiguan.

Perhaps I can wait there for some time. Any day now the "Apenrade," a German steamer, is supposed to arrive, which can take me to Chefoo, from where Port Arthur can be reached in ten hours by another steamer. With that I console myself. But it is as though everything has conspired against me; at the station I learn that the "Apenrade" has been shipwrecked and cannot make it up here at all. For me, however, that means: either turn back or travel directly overland from Shanhaiguan to Port Arthur! Since I am no friend of turning back, despite the obstacles standing in the way of the undertaking, I quickly decide to choose the overland route through Manchuria, and I immediately make my preparations for this expedition on my own initiative.

When I made the decision to embark on the journey through Manchuria, I was well aware of the difficulties and great dangers associated with such a venture in these warlike times. Nor do I by any means disregard the warnings conveyed to me, but my mind is made up and I stick to my resolution.

At all events, the Russians are also cautious enough not to let anyone easily look into their cards. After all, they have succeeded so far in preventing unwelcome news about their actions in Manchuria from reaching the public. If I therefore undertake to lift somewhat the veil that lies over these events, this likewise requires great caution.

In retrospect, I am glad that I took my route overland. For had I carried out my original plan—that is, had I traveled to Port Arthur in order to obtain permission from the competent authorities there to travel in Manchuria—I would never have reached my goal. Such permission would have been absolutely necessary, firstly, in order to be able to travel on the railway, which served exclusively military purposes {p. 18}, secondly, to secure myself against being regarded as a spy anywhere and arrested, but thirdly also because, for the sake of personal safety, I could by no means dispense entirely with the protection of the Russian troops. As an example of Russia's attitude toward the Manchurian question, the following fact may serve: The German Major Borkenhagen was entertaining a plan to travel a bit further into Manchuria on the railway, as far as that was possible. He spoke about this with the Russian station officer on duty in Shanhaiguan, but soon abandoned his plan of his own accord. Immediately after this conversation, the Russian declared to the German station officer that it would have been quite impossible for him to let the major travel. Yet he had not wanted to tell him directly that travel was not permitted for foreign officers. The brief debate that followed did nothing to change the fact that Russia exercises its sovereign rights of occupation over Manchuria and the railway beyond Shanhaiguan—which, moreover, still runs a considerable distance through the province of Zhili—in such a manner that it will not permit any German officer to travel on this railway*). In Port Arthur, the chief of police had a mutual acquaintance suggest to an English officer who spent his free time taking walks whether he might not prefer to depart from Port Arthur. Well, in Port Arthur the Russian does, after all, have a right to get rid of individuals inconvenient to him. But the case just mentioned proves that he is already claiming this right for the whole of Manchuria and part of Zhili as well, even toward German officers, and while the joint operations of the powers in China are still ongoing. Light is also shed on Russia's stance on the Manchurian question by the fact that Russia has withdrawn foreign-service and wartime pay from its troops ever since they have been operating only in Manchuria. Russia thus no longer considers Manchuria to be foreign territory.

Toward me as a civilian, the Russian station officer has fewer objections of principle. This may perhaps be due to the fact that I have already dined and clinked glasses with him. Such things sometimes help overcome such scruples. But in return he is the most zealous warner and alarmist, whereas the German station officer, Lieutenant Heinrich, while drawing my attention to the menacing bands of brigands and other perils, does so less to dissuade me from my plan than to ensure I do not underestimate the dangers. In the end, I succeed in obtaining the Russian's consent for my onward journey. He even gives me a letter of recommendation to another officer who manages the station at Tchentcheusu, probably thinking all the while that he will see me again in a few days.

::: notes
*) In fact, permission was also subsequently refused to German officers of the expeditionary corps who wished to choose the route through Manchuria in order to connect with the Trans-Siberian Railway, including, among others, the previously mentioned Major Bauer of the railway regiment.
:::

{p. 19}

[Illustration: Lutai Railway Station (p. 13).]

## Chapter II. In the Land of the Honghuzi.

### 1. Beyond the Great Wall.

I feel like a smuggler as I find myself on the Russian train on 31 January, heading north. The train departs Shanhaiguan around 5 o'clock in the evening. I am assigned a compartment that has been reserved for me by order of the stationmaster; inside, the temperature is at least 5 degrees below zero. The carriages are unheated, except for a single one that contains a small stove. The Russian railway soldiers traveling on the train huddle around the stove, but it is impossible for an ordinary mortal to stay in that carriage because the stove smokes terribly. I therefore return to my compartment, wrap myself from head to toe in my furs and woolen blankets, and try to picture the night ahead of me in a somewhat rosy light and imagine that I am not freezing. That helps a little.

Just past the station, we pass through an opening. A breach had to be made in the Great Wall, which begins here near Shanhaiguan, in order to allow the railway line to pass through. This place is, in a sense, the gateway to Manchuria, although the political border of the province of Zhili extends even further to the north. Shanhaiguan {p. 20} forms a highly important point altogether. Here, the mountains that stretch from the interior in a north-easterly direction press close to the sea and form the only place where one can reach China proper from the north without having to cross the mountain range. The pass is quite narrow, only a few kilometers wide, and is blocked by the Great Wall as well as the aforementioned forts. In addition, here lie the only two ports, Shanhaiguan and Qinhuangdao, which in wintertime permit traffic between Zhili together with its hinterland, Manchuria, and Mongolia on the one hand, and the sea on the other. A military position, therefore, that can occasionally attain great significance!

We may have been traveling for a good half hour or so—in the meantime it has grown dark—when the train comes to a halt on the open track. The engine has broken down and must be repaired. This takes a full four hours. The day before, a train had derailed on the line.

At the stations we have stops, often an hour long. Strikingly large is the number of Russian-speaking Chinese. The Chinese understands very easily how to adapt to a new master and turn him to his advantage. In the carriage, I converse for some time with an intelligent Cantonese who understands English. He speaks very ill of the Russians; they rob and steal wherever they can find anything. He runs a wholesale business in Jinzhoufu, but since the Russians have been there, he no longer feels comfortable and wants to return to the south.

Regular railway service is maintained only as far as the station of Jinzhoufu; a train runs there daily. The line is not long, yet it took until around 5 o'clock in the morning before we arrived at the said station. With the help of my trunks, I have made myself a makeshift bed in the crowded carriage and remain on the train even after arrival until daybreak.

1 February.

We are now inside Manchuria. One can see already from the construction of the houses that one is dealing with a different tribe here than further south. The pointed, sweeping roof that characterizes houses in China proper has here given way to a flat, barrel-vault-like one. From a distance, the Manchurian houses look like railway cars. Given the great scarcity of wood prevailing here, they are built almost entirely of stone, and indeed not bricks, but hewn fieldstones, which gives Manchurian domestic architecture something very solid. Only the roof is supported by timberwork and consists of a thick layer of reeds, upon which is applied an equally thick layer of greasy loess soil, bound by chopped straw and a little lime. Inside, the house resembles the Chinese type.

{p. 21}

When I awake from a brief, restless slumber around 6 o'clock in the morning, I find myself completely alone with my Chinese servant in the cold, dirty railway carriage. I pack up my things and have a couple of coolies summoned, who are to be found everywhere in large numbers in China and are always ready for work. They have to take my luggage out of the carriage and set it down in front of the station building. What to do now? I know that railway service ends here. To be sure, the track is passable for a few more kilometers, but no regular trains run, only work trains as needed. I try to speak to the station officer, but he is nowhere

[Illustration: Across the ice of the Dalinghe (p. 21).]

to be found. While I make vain efforts to make myself understood to the Russian soldiers, I see several engines being coupled together, to which a saloon car is attached. Fortunately, I meet a non-commissioned officer who speaks broken German; I ask him about the station officer, to whom I wish to present my recommendation, and the non-commissioned officer points to the saloon car. Just then, an officer with a thick, black, shaggy fur cap on his head steps out of the car; the non-commissioned officer tells me: “Speak English to him,” and I walk up to the officer. I address him in English and hand him the card from the station commandant in Shanhaiguan. The officer takes the card from me, reads it, and says: “Why don't you speak German to me? If you address anyone here in English, you won't get an answer. Besides, we {p. 22} know each other, from Tientsin—don't you remember our dinner on the railway bridge anymore?” Had I been blind? Why, it is a good acquaintance from Tientsin, Staff Captain Losev of the Railway Regiment, who in his time restored the Tanggu–Tientsin line and later repaired the Tanggu–Shanhaiguan railway. We shook hands. “Well, where are you headed, then?” “Through Manchuria.” “Do you have a passport, then?” “No, I'll manage to get through.” “The railway ends a few kilometers further on; how do you intend to make your way then?” “Then I'll take a Chinese cart.” “Well, suits me. Quick, where is your luggage? Put it right in the car here; the train is leaving immediately. Come in here, please.” He opens the door to the saloon car, which serves as his quarters, as there are otherwise no living accommodations available. It is a long car, quite comfortably furnished and divided into two fairly large compartments, one of which is occupied by the staff captain, the other by the mechanical engineer Konievsky. In each of the two rooms stands a handsome wooden bedstead with a proper bed. Other adjoining rooms are also present, a stove that heats well warms both compartments, and soon the servant brings in the steaming samovar; a glass of good tea—“chai,” as the Russian says—is meant to compensate me for the privations and discomfort of the past night. Outside it is cold, with a biting wind blowing, and the air lies leaden-gray over the landscape.

We have a bare half hour's ride. I am heartily glad to have found the staff captain, for I was still afraid that I would be sent straight back from Jinzhoufu to Shanhaiguan. The further I penetrate into the country, the more I can hope that I will be tolerated there. In any case, luck has been on my side. For this morning the staff captain intends to undertake the transport of the first four engines across the frozen Daling River. The river is about 500 meters wide; the bridge across it was completely destroyed by Chinese troops. Indeed, the railway from Jinzhoufu to Newchwang and Mukden, respectively, is for the most part destroyed. Losev's task is to restore it.

Meanwhile, the weather outside has changed remarkably. The leaden atmosphere has drawn closer and closer, and soon the storm is lashing against the windows of the car, sweeping thick dust, indeed outright sand and small pebbles along with it. When the train stops and the call comes: “All out!” we try to protect ourselves against the fine dust as best we can. It is weather such as I have never experienced before and would not care to experience again. The storm, still increasing in force, lashes the skin dreadfully; it is as if one's cheeks were being rubbed with sandpaper in twenty degrees of frost. Without pressing a handkerchief tightly against one's mouth and nose, it is impossible to breathe at all; at times the storm becomes so fierce that it is impossible to keep upright against the pressure. I therefore prefer to lie flat on my {p. 23} face behind the railway embankment for a time and wait until the storm blows itself out. Even the weather-hardened Russians must at times yield to the fury, and for all the discomfort of the situation, it is rather comical to see a whole row of fur-clad figures offering their backsides to the foul weather in a stooped posture. Little by little it clears somewhat; high dunes have formed behind the railway embankment, and the sand has to be scraped out of the tracks to clear the rails again.

[Illustration: The destroyed railway line (p. 26).]

The bridge over the Daling River itself is a small masterpiece. For beyond this river they have no engines; consequently it is necessary to get some across before one can think of an efficient repair of the railway line. The river is wide and has a considerable gradient, so that it usually does not freeze over completely. For this reason the water had to be checked at the rapids with brushwood and beams, so that it could freeze more easily. Once it was completely frozen over, long beams were laid across it as railway {p. 21} ties, the rails were spiked onto them, and our four engines are the first to be transported across the river one by one in this manner. The crossing proceeds smoothly. Only at the riverbanks must a helping hand be lent and the bed of ties be levered sideways to achieve the necessary curves. It is indeed a pleasure to see these weather-hardened and stalwart figures at work. This much is certain: the Russian is a good field soldier.

Once all the locomotives have been brought across the river, we travel about four kilometers further. The saloon car is left behind, and we board a freight car that has been makeshiftly fitted out as a workshop for the engine drivers and mechanics. Then the train comes to a halt, right on the open track. It is already afternoon, and any thought of moving on is out of the question. So I have to spend the night in the workshop car. Since it is dreadfully filthy, I have a couple of coolies give it a rough cleaning. Inside stands a small stove; coal is fetched from one of the engines, and soon a cheerful little fire is flickering, making the stay bearable. Captain Losev and the engineer travel back to Jinzhou Fu on one of the engines. Before doing so, however, having likewise tried in vain to dissuade me from my plan, the captain yielded to my urging and placed a draisine at my disposal, on which I will continue my journey tomorrow. It is a peculiar sort of draisine, for it lacks the pumping mechanism, so that it can only be pushed or pulled. Nevertheless, I am grateful, even though I can use the draisine for only a single day’s journey, because some thirty kilometers further on the railway line is completely destroyed.

I make myself as comfortable as possible in the workshop car. From the railway soldiers I borrow a mess kit, have a coolie fetch fresh water for me from the locomotive boiler, and start boiling water. Then I buy a loaf of Russian army bread from the soldiers—a thick, black, heavy bread, but one that tastes excellent. I sent the other coolie to the surrounding villages to procure eggs, salt, tea, Chinese pearl barley, and a chicken for me. After an hour he returns, and soon the water for tea is bubbling over the fire, in which, given the preciousness of fuel, the eggs are first soft-boiled. Meanwhile, my boy plucks the chicken that the coolie procured; it is, to be sure, already quite an old chicken, but two hours of boiling will at least yield a good soup. One by one, the engine drivers appear. They have put their boilers in order and extinguished the fires, and now they too want to make themselves comfortable in the car. It serves as their night quarters, and they do not mind in the least that I am to share it with them. One of them speaks German. And so we converse with one another. We share our provisions, as we later will our sleeping quarters. The men are cheerful, good-natured fellows who behave with utmost civility and do full credit to the reputation claimed by the Russian railway troops as an elite corps.

{p. 25}

[Illustration: On the draisine (p. 26).]

{p. 26}

### 2. On the Draisine.

2 February.
This morning my draisine journey begins. I have my luggage loaded onto this strangest of all vehicles in which I have ever travelled, then wrap myself up thoroughly in furs and woollen blankets, have two ropes tied to the front of the draisine, harness four coolies to them who have to pull, and promptly at 8 o’clock in the morning the journey gets under way on the lonely line. I am completely alone, accompanied by no one except my Chinese servant. The two of us are perched high atop the trunks, the revolver sits loosely in my belt, and my rifle lies beside me. Now let the robbers come.

What trouble it is with the coolies! The fellows exert themselves like the strongmen of Hamburg and yet make no headway. Finally I give up the tiresome shouting of “kuai-kuai” (“forward, forward!”) and resign myself to my fate that, after all, we shall proceed at nothing faster than a walking pace. Fortunately, the fierce wind of recent days has died down. From my high perch I let my eyes wander across the country, but what can be seen is no more than a rather dreary salt steppe to the right and left, now and then a few villages, a couple of ploughed fields, a frozen puddle. Before me the dull, yawn-inducing railway line that stretches out of sight, and otherwise nothing, not a bird, not a sound in nature except the squeaking of my draisine's ungreased wheels.

The railway line itself is also passable only in sections. In several places the track has been torn up, and we then have to drag the draisine over the bare ground to the next spot where we can put it back on the rails. For that matter, the railway is poorly built. Often there are gaps of several decimetres between the sleepers and the roadbed; the ground has subsided that much in certain places. Of the bridges and overpasses along the entire line, not a single one has been completed; instead, the tracks have for the time being been routed around the spots in question. Only those bridges that were indispensably necessary have been finished.

After about two hours, my four coolies go on strike. They have had nothing to eat yet, they say, and refuse to go any further. Nothing remains for me but to pay the men off and fetch new coolies from a nearby village.

After a while a draisine comes towards me. It is a Russian patrol from Goubangzi station, which I hope to reach today. The soldiers halt; one of us had to lift his draisine off the track to let the other pass. In order to initiate communication, I show the soldiers an identification document that Losev wrote into my diary, but not one of the men can read, even though it is in Russian. But since they evidently possess the requisite respect for the written word as such, they lift their draisine off the track and let mine pass. After an almost endless journey, during which, however, no untoward incidents arise, I finally arrive in the early afternoon, around 3 o’clock, at Goubangzi, the junction station where the line forks in the direction of Mukden and Newchwang. With joy I see the white-blue-red flag gleaming already in the distance. There the second battalion of the Second {p. 27} East Siberian Rifle Regiment is quartered. The station lies in the open field; no hill rises nearby, not a tree is to be found; only to the west in the distance can the Guangliangshan ridge be seen.

The first thing I do is report to the Russian supreme commander. It is Colonel Belatsky. Astonished questions as to how I came to this region, who

[Illustration: Goubangzi Station at the junction of the railways from Peking to Mukden and Newchwang (p. 26).]

I was, what I wanted here, amazement that I had gotten through unmolested; only yesterday on that same stretch between Dalinghe and Goubangzi a Russian wagon transport was attacked by more than a hundred well-armed bandits, the entire region is constantly being made unsafe by Chinese gangs and regular troops, it is completely out of the question for me to travel further. I ask that they might then provide me with an escort or allow me to travel from stage to stage with the patrols. That cannot be done without orders from above. Had I not called upon Colonel Modl, then? That I had prudently not done; he certainly would not have let me travel. In short, the result is: I am welcome as a guest at the station, but for a continuation {p. 28} of the journey the colonel cannot take responsibility, unless under military escort, with a “convoi”. Otherwise I would simply have to turn back. For that matter, the colonel himself does not speak a word of German, but the regimental doctor, Dr. Petersen, acts as intermediary. He is a most charming gentleman, and with his help I finally succeed in persuading the colonel to telegraph to Colonel Medl at Chenchenzi that a German étranger had arrived unexpectedly at the station, intended to travel overland to Port Arthur since the sea route was closed, and requested after the fact the grant of a convoi, as further travel without a convoi was impossible for security reasons. — I myself do not even know what a dangerous journey I made today, and in the course of all these negotiations I acquire a positive respect for myself, yet I think to myself: Come what may, at all events I am right in the middle of Manchuria, have seen many of the things I wanted to see, and if they want to see me out again by one route or another, they cannot very well blindfold me after all. And even if they do, well, that will do me no harm now either. So I resign myself to my fate and am not at all displeased that I am to take a day of rest tomorrow at the warm station, seeing that things do not move so quickly with the Russian telegraph.

#### 3 February.

I use today’s day of rest to look around the station a bit, although there is really not much to see. I take a few photographs, watch an officer lungeing his horse, and get my bearings regarding the branch line to Mukden. The building in which the officers live is quite clean and well furnished inside. It evidently served previously as a residence for the European station official, was destroyed, and later rebuilt by the Russians. The fireplaces built directly into the walls, in which a cheerful fire flickers, are very cozy. It is incredible what heat the Russians, who are fairly immune to the cold, can endure. For the most part, the rooms are overheated. Every officer has his comfortable camp bed with him, which folds up into a small box and is very handy. The field bakery and the water-filtration apparatus are not lacking at any station. Everywhere there is excellent black bread. The samovar stands on the table all day long, and people indulge in it diligently. The Russian food we receive is very hearty, but not particularly tasty; usually the dishes are too greasy. The best part of the meal is undoubtedly the so-called “zakuska,” the “appetizer,” which is eaten before the hot courses. They consist entirely of good little delicacies: caviar, piquant canned fish, ham, salad, and so forth. Usually one is already so sated by the zakuska, to which belong—at least—three vodkas, that one can hardly enjoy any of the hot dishes.

By chance, the officers are invited to lunch with the Manchurian village headman, and I too receive a direct invitation when they learn of my presence. Toward midday, a guard of eight men stands ready to accompany {p. 29} us to the headman. It is remarkable what respect the Russians have, after all, for the men with queues. They never go anywhere alone, always accompanied by an escort with fixed bayonets. I am also urgently advised not to go into the village alone;—in Zhili we were not so fearful.

Outwardly, the village headman differs in no way from his subordinates. Nor does he seem to care much about his appearance, for his coat is torn and positively glistens with grease, especially at the back where the queue hangs, and in front where drops are wont to fall while he eats. Nonetheless, the Russian officers fraternize with him in the most friendly manner, shake his hands, occasionally put their arms around him as well, and through it all the Manchu knows how to maintain his dignity, which the officers do not always manage to do. Later they plan to make the headman drunk on vodka, but he, accustomed to his potent samshu, in the end proves able to hold his liquor better than the Russians.
