Meanwhile, the soldiers stand in the courtyard and keep watch so that no harm comes to their masters, perhaps also to escort them safely home should alcohol, if not the Chinese, play a trick on them. On the return from the Chinese meal, which proceeds like all the others but produced quite good food, one makes one's way back home by stages {p. 30}; on the way, stops are made here and there, one looks at this and that, and lingers for some time first in front of, and later inside the house of a Catholic Christian family whose members are said to have suffered greatly at the hands of the Honghuzi *). Here too, the Russians fraternize most vigorously with the Chinese; people embrace, and it falls little short of kissing. And yet we are always surprised when Russia emerges from negotiations with the Chinese with greater success than we do. The Russian simply stands closer to the Asiatic race of the Chinese than the other Europeans do, and while we work with European matter-of-factness and reprisals, he embraces the Chinese and wrings the finest concessions from him in the guise of the most intimate friendship, while also letting reprisals peek out of his pocket—reprisals which the Chinese, for his part, fears more from him than from anyone else. For he knows full well that in such matters the Russian brooks no nonsense and actually strikes once he has raised the stick. Velvet paws and an occasional bloody scratching—that is how the Russian makes the Chinese compliant.

When we have returned to our quarters, we find Colonel Modl’s telegraphic permission to continue the journey, along with the order to provide me with an escort of ten Cossacks as far as Yingkou (Newchwang). Thus this important question is settled, and I accordingly make my arrangements for continuing the journey. Foremost among these is procuring a cart and draft animals. It is too cold for riding, for it is no pleasure to sit on a horse in at least 15 degrees of frost. Moreover, I want to record as accurate an itinerary as possible, and therefore prefer to sit, well wrapped up, in a Chinese cart. I send my servant to the same village headman with whom we had dined, and after long negotiations and efforts, and after the staging post commandant has also spoken a stern word, we finally succeed in hiring an open farm cart for the price of ten dollars to Yingkou, to which three animals are to be harnessed. The cart is to be ready at eight o’clock the next morning and equipped to travel at a continuous trot.

In these negotiations, both between the Chinese and the Russians and between the Russians and myself, two interpreters play the principal role: a Chinese who speaks Russian, and a Russian who speaks German. In general, the Russian soldier speaks no second language, and it should come as no surprise that there are many who cannot even speak proper Russian, since their mother tongue is Kyrgyz, Turkestani, Little Russian, Turkmen, Kurdish, or some other idiom. The Jews, on the other hand, generally speak German, and for the most part, {p. 31} at the subsequent Russian staging posts where I address the commander in German, he first calls for a Levisohn, a Jesaias, or a Judassohn in order to be able to communicate with me with his help. So it is in Goubangzi, too: a Polish non-commissioned officer of the Mosaic faith, and even more of Mosaic speech, interprets what the colonel has to say to me in a thoroughly comical manner of speaking. Almost always, his speech begins with the words: “The high and mighty, highborn noble gentleman, he has sayed: . . . .” Then follows what he “has sayed.” Incidentally, the Jews in the Russian army are said to be good soldiers.

::: notes
*) Honghuzi—literally "Red Beards"—is what the Manchu calls the robbers who ply their trade in Manchuria more than in any other province of China. The name is to be explained by the fact that on their raids the robbers frequently seek to make themselves unrecognizable by means of beards. And it is with red hair and a red beard that the Chinese imagines the devil. The honghuzi gave the Japanese a great deal of trouble during the Sino-Japanese War, and even now the Russians are not finding it easy to deal with them.
:::

They are said to conduct themselves well during their arduous five-year term of service, and I saw a good number in non-commissioned officer and sergeant positions. They are considered the most educated among the soldiers because, unlike most of their comrades, they can write.

### 3. A Skirmish with Honghuzi.

1 February.

At 8 o'clock in the morning we stand ready for departure, my trunks are ready, and my ten Cossacks are already mounted on their small Siberian horses; naturally, my carter {p. 32} with his cart is missing. It takes repeated summonses to induce him to finally appear after half an hour, and when he happily trots up, he has two miserable mules and an old nag hitched before the cart. My Chinese boy, who is already familiar with my particular standards in this regard, therefore turns the team right back around immediately and eventually returns after another half hour. He has now picked out the best stock the peasant has in his stable, and the cart is at last in proper order and harnessed with two neat ponies and a strong mule. The trunks are lashed down onto the wagon, then a place is prepared for me, likewise one for my Chinese boy, and the train indeed sets into motion around 9 o'clock: ahead three Cossacks, then the wagon, and finally the rest of the riders. But scarcely are we outside the gates and have taken our leave of the Russian officers when the driver and, in addition, three other Chinese leap onto the cart with us and block my view. I ask what on earth these fellows want. It is then explained to me that these are the *mafus* (grooms) and the people who are to look after the cart. Such is the Chinese: a fellow for every beast, and yet another for the cart. Despite their protests, I chase the troublesome company down from the cart, tolerating aside from the driver only one other man, who speaks Russian and can at least be used as an interpreter. The region through which we travel is designated by the Chinese as Guangliang, and the mountain range stretching away to our left they call Guangliangshan.

The route leads us at first in a predominantly easterly direction across the railway line, whose alignment it essentially follows; indeed, we cross the tracks for the first time before their junction. The northern branch runs on in the direction of Mukden, though it is not fully completed, and the southern towards Yingkou. Aside from the railway telegraph, a second telegraph line also runs through this area: the telegraph of the Imperial Chinese Telegraph Administration. The railway line is no longer passable; the rails have been lifted from the sleepers, and these have been hauled off or lie thrown together in heaps beside the embankment. All of the road overpasses are destroyed. The region makes a rather desolate impression; the few villages through which one passes are in an unusually pitiful condition, even by Chinese standards, the houses built for the most part only of gaoliang straw and smeared with mud, the enclosures surrounding each homestead likewise consisting only of mud, and the roads are so dusty that our little cavalcade is virtually continuously enveloped in a cloud of dust. Not a tree, not a shrub, relatively little tilled land, but in its place frozen marshes and salt steppe across which the railway embankment runs. The country is so flat that when travelling to the left of the embankment, one sees nothing of what lies to the right of it, and vice versa. If to this picture one adds the insecurity prevailing all around us, one can form a fair idea that living—or rather vegetating—here permanently cannot count among life's greatest pleasures, and that such a desolate country provides the ideal refuge for all manner of bandits and riffraff, whether in or out of a uniform jacket.

{p. 33}

The latter leave their calling card with me as well. We are not far from the village of Liangjiatu, a rather large settlement, when the Chinese on my cart suddenly begin shifting restlessly in their seats and looking continually in one direction. I inquire what is the matter and learn that suspicious riders have been spotted back there, probably Honghuzi. I draw my Cossacks' attention to them, and indeed, we see about thirty horsemen halted among burial mounds at a distance of about 1,000 meters. Unconcerned by this, we continue on our way; but once we have passed the village and emerge again on its eastern side, we come under fire and

[Illustration: Banquet at the village headman's in Gouhanzi (p. 29).]

see the horsemen reappear. The fellows hit nothing; evidently they shoot without aiming, as the Chinese usually do, because they themselves are more afraid of their gun than the person they are shooting at. Unconcerned by the banging of shots, during which our Chinese virtually curl up into themselves, we continue on our way; but when the supposed Honghuzi make ready to accompany us—at a respectful distance, to be sure—and keep shooting, the Cossacks leap from their horses, take cover behind them, lay their rifles across the saddles, and return fire. Then, in a flash, six of them are back on their horses and gallop straight at the horsemen. The latter, however, are no less nimble than the Russians, and at a dead gallop they scatter, all in different {p. 31} directions. One of the Cossacks shoots one man's horse dead beneath him. When they reach the spot, the poor beast is lying on the ground; the fellow has vanished into the nearby village.*) As we cannot delay for long, the Cossacks soon rejoin me, and I continue on my way, forewarned by this incident and richer by an experience. In any case, the incident proves to me that it would have been a foolhardy gamble had I tried to continue my journey from Gouhanzi alone.

Soon after this adventure, we cross the railway once again, leaving it to our left, and now take an east-southeasterly direction. We are located directly north of the northernmost inlet of the Gulf of Liaodong. Shortly after midday, we arrive at the Russian staging post of Tujatai, where First Lieutenant Kozmin is the post commander and my Cossacks are relieved. Through the mediation of Mr. Levisohn, I tell the captain where I have come from and where I am bound, and report my skirmish with the Manchus. He is not surprised by this, but explains that such things happen almost every day. The patrols that patrolled the railway line had to deal with bandits or regulars almost daily.—Good prospects for the onward journey!

### 4. By Chinese Cart to Yingkou (Niuzhuang).

After a half-hour rest over tea and black bread, I continue my journey. Since I always keep the cart at a trot and the roads are for the most part remarkably good, I cover a fairly considerable distance. I pass through an area that seems somewhat more welcoming; the expanse of cultivated land increases, and mostly bean stubble is to be seen. Since no detailed map of this region exists, I record as precise an itinerary as possible, in the course of which it strikes me that the villages across an entire large district bear one and the same name: they are all, without exception, called Taipingzhuang. I travel for nearly an hour and a half, and whenever I ask the name of a village, I hear this same single name time and again. Incidentally, similar things happened to me several times during the remainder of my journey. I have no explanation for it, but wish at least to record the fact.**)

Near the village of Chonenpu we cross the coastal river Liuli He, and then turn to the southeast, a direction we maintain for some time from then on. Near the village of Gaojiatai, around three o'clock in the afternoon, I reach a fairly wide trade road that leads north from Yingkou. Here, for the first time, I encounter long wagon trains loaded with kaoliang and beans, as well as bean cake. In winter, an extraordinarily brisk cart traffic prevails in Manchuria, the scale of which one {p. 35} can hardly imagine. The carts are heavily laden and usually drawn by five to seven animals.

::: notes
*) Incidentally, as it later turned out, the horsemen were not bandits, but a Chinese cavalry patrol. It belonged to a regular army of several thousand men that hard pressed the Russian positions at Goubangzi and Chenlichenxiu a few days later. It was under the command of the Manchu General Su, the military commander of Mukden prior to its occupation by the Russians.

**) They are presumably collective names for entire districts, like "Fuhne", "Mark", etc., with us.
:::

In the late afternoon, after a journey of about 80 li,* I reach Shuangtaizi, on the 300-metre-wide Shuangtaizi River, across which runs a railway bridge that lies, however, completely in ruins. In the town there is a large Daoist monastery in which the Russian staging post has quartered itself. Here I shall spend the night.

[Illustration: My Cossacks (p. 32).]

To my surprise, at this remote staging post I also find a lady. It is the wife of the staging post commander, Staff Captain Grodinsky. When, as a newlywed, he had to depart from his garrison in Nikolsk to march to Manchuria and Beijing, his young wife followed him and has now sought to make herself at home with him at this remote station. In general, Russian officers' wives are very courageous; it is by no means rare for them to follow their husbands and take the field with them. I made the acquaintance of one lady who took part in the entire campaign by her husband's side, and who had been to Beijing and Shanhaiguan, and later to Mukden and elsewhere. Most of the officers' wives, to be sure, live in Port Arthur and see their {p. 36} husbands only occasionally. In any case, the presence of a pleasant young lady brings a welcome change to my nomadic life, and although we can converse only by means of an interpreter, the time passes as swift as an arrow. I am received with all the hospitality of Siberia, and the woman's managing hand ensures that I am served a dinner such as I could not wish better—and that in the interior of Manchuria on a day when I have been fired upon by *honghuzi* or regulars. For once, the interpreter this time is the son of honest German parents who had emigrated to Łódź. He bore the name of Böttcher and is almost childishly delighted to be able to speak German once again.

::: notes
*) A li is slightly over half a kilometre.
:::

5 February.

My escort has been reinforced by three riflemen, who are to do some shopping in Yingkou for the captain and his wife respectively. First I must cross the Shuangtaizi He, and it takes some effort to get the animals across the ice. The actual settlement itself lies only on the right bank of the river, opposite the Daoist monastery where I had been quartered, and in its streets a fairly brisk grain market is underway. Then we continue southwards, and on this day I cover more than 100 li. The road ran between the railway line to the left and the Imperial Chinese Telegraph to the right; we pass alternately through cultivated land and wasteland, but encounter fewer villages than yesterday. In places the countryside becomes quite desolate, and one is delighted when, after hours of travel, one once again sees the railway-carriage-like houses of the Manchus silhouetted against the horizon in the distance. The flat landscape, which offers few resting points for the eye, often tempts one into the most fantastic mirages. One takes a bean stalk standing close by for an ox, and villages often appear at least ten kilometres away when one is already within about a kilometre of them.

Halfway there, we encounter a Cossack patrol scouting the railway line. No incidents of an unpleasant nature occur.

After about 70 li, we pass under the railway embankment; the overpass is destroyed, but the rails are still in place. From here on, the region becomes livelier again. We meet several caravans of carts; to our left in the distance, we can already see the blue mountains that lie before the Liaodong Peninsula and continue into it. The Chinese call this mountain range Liangjiashan. From this point on, it was said to be only 100 li to Yingkou.

After about 30 further li, I reach the great Liao River, specifically at the town of Tianzhuangtai, the first town I have seen since Chenzhousu. Here, a company of the Fourth Rifle Regiment is stationed on railway guard duty. The staging post commandant with whom I lodge is still a young man, who was stationed for a long time in Petersburg and was then transferred to Siberia. He raves to me about Paris and about {p. 37} European life; he has travelled through a large part of Europe and now sits here at a lonely staging post, living on his fond memories, growing bitter and languishing. He longs to return to European life and to his books. He is quite well-versed in the literature of his country as well as in German and French literature, and feels his present seclusion from the world to be a cruel fate.

[Illustration: Draw-well at a Manchurian fanke (p. 31).]

6 February.
I set out early in order to arrive in Yingkou as soon as possible. At first we travel along the ice of the Liao River (the Chinese call the river the Dalan River), then we cross over to the other bank. The railway had already turned slightly westward some 20 li before Tianzhuangtai. It avoids crossing the wide river, remains on its right bank, and finally ends right by the river near its mouth, opposite the Chinese city of Yingkou. Following the bend of the river, my route leads slightly eastwards. The lines of wagons grow more frequent; often there are some 40 or more Chinese carts rolling past us heavily laden. The drivers have an immense respect for the Cossacks. As soon as they see them approaching, they steer clear in a wide arc, and on such occasions it happens several times that two or three carts drive over one of the numerous earthen embankments and overturn, much to the Cossacks’ delight. The closer we come to Yingkou, the more numerous the wagon trains become; right up to the houses of the European settlement one can see the high clouds of dust they throw up. Around noon, I arrive at the extensive {p. 38} buildings of the Russian railway, the “Chinese Eastern Railway,” which are now barely an hour away from the city of Yingkou. At one o’clock, I make my entrance into Yingkou with my cavalcade, stared at with curiosity by everyone I meet. I am directed to the German hotel, owned by a former German ship captain named Decker; I dismiss my Cossacks with a tip and a few bottles of vodka after reporting them to the town commandant, and rejoice at the journey now behind me and the rest that I shall seek here. Only at the hotel do I learn that I am the first European since the beginning of the Boxer unrest to have arrived overland in Yingkou—or, as Europeans are accustomed erroneously to call it, in Newchwang.*) My journey seems to cause quite a sensation among the citizens of Yingkou.

::: notes
*) The Chinese city of Newchwang lies several miles upstream on the Liao River.
:::

{p. 39}

[Illustration: Barricades on the “bund” of Newchwang at the time of the Boxer fighting (p. 38).]

## Chapter III. Russia in Manchuria.

### 1. How Manchuria Became Russian.

7–14 February.

To assess Russian actions in East Asia, it is of interest to examine, in a concrete case, how the Tsarist Empire conducts frontier policy.

Several months elapsed between the outbreak of the Boxer disturbances in the province of Zhili and in Manchuria. This fact has given rise to a variety of conjectures and suspicions regarding Russia. One must grant our kindred neighbours across the Channel that they know exceedingly well how to expose the vulnerabilities revealed by the policies of other powers. From such operations to more or less substantiated suspicions is then but a single step. On the English side, it was first asserted that the Russians had done their part to cause the unrest to break out in Manchuria as well, and that they deliberately refrained from pacifying the country in order to be able to claim that it remained persistently unsafe and that the presence of troops was therefore absolutely necessary. This was then to be continued until people had grown accustomed to Russia being entrenched in Manchuria. And given the short memory with which foreign policy operates nowadays, that is by no means difficult to achieve. Complete clarity will probably never be shed on the Russian manoeuvres.

The first attack by the Boxers on the foreign settlement in Newchwang took place on 4 August 1900; about 150 Boxers appeared on the small “bund” of the foreign settlement {p. [58]} at a barricade manned by the Volunteer Corps and attacked the small garrison. The affair must have been comparatively harmless, for the Chinese, who at first merely shouted and performed their dances, were allowed to come right up to the barricade. As before the single combats of Homeric heroes, a battle of words ensued at first; the Chinese leader boasted and jeered that they should go ahead and shoot, as he was invulnerable anyway, etc. He even climbed onto the barricade with theatrical words and brandished his knife, until a volunteer seized him by the arm and pulled him over to the other side, where he was bound. With that, however, a thirst for action seems to have come over Newchwang's volunteer militia as well, for now they began to open fire. Hardly had the shooting begun, however, when the band of Boxers already wheeled about and took to their heels. Ten dead remained on the field. After some time, a detachment of Russian marines appeared. They had nothing left to do, except that they opened rapid fire on one fellow who had jumped into the river out of terror—fire that took effect. After this cheap victory, they took the captured Boxer leader, marched in triumph with him to the large square where the government yamen stands, tied him firmly to a stone, and a “squad” of Cossacks and volunteers, about fifty men, fired a volley at the prisoner from a distance of about 100 (!) metres. When that had been done, a mandarin came walking along, positioned himself beside the executed man, and wanted to say something. They silenced him by forcing him to take the corpse of the Boxer leader onto his back and throw it into the river a few hundred metres away.

Towards 9 o'clock the Boxers had regrouped and joined forces with Chinese soldiers. They prowled around in the reeds and sedge grass behind the foreign settlement. Four hundred Russian marines, five hundred Cossacks, and several companies of infantry engaged them and drove the Chinese back into the city. Throughout the afternoon, two Russian gunboats then shelled the Chinese city, a bombardment answered by the Chinese with machine-gun and infantry fire. But this entire bombardment was not much more than a farce. No damage was done to the city; rather, they shot over it on principle—ostensibly to hit the fleeing enemy, but in fact to spare the Chinese city, because the Russo-Chinese Bank, which evidently has strong interests in the city, declared that a regular bombardment would cause it the greatest damage.

Peculiar was the behaviour of the Daotai*) in this matter. During the Boxer attack in the morning, he sent word to the Russian commander requesting protection for his own person, specifically against Chinese soldiers and Boxers. When a detachment of Russian soldiers marched up to provide him with the requested guard for his yamen, he had, however, long since vanished from Yingkou with all his valuables and departed for Tianzhuangtai.

As a result of the mediation of several European merchants residing in Newchwang, the bombardment was halted in the late afternoon. Several intrepid men, under the leadership {p. [59]} of the head of the firm Bush Bros., Mr H. A. Bush, went into the Chinese city and sought to get hold of the leaders of the Chinese guilds, as well as a Chinese government official. They succeeded in this, and so that very evening an agreement was concluded between the Russians and the Chinese according to which the bombardment was to cease, whereas the guild leaders, on the other hand, undertook to ensure that the weapons located in the Chinese city were handed over by 10 o'clock the following morning.

::: notes
*) Chinese district governor.
:::

After sunset on the same day, by order of Vice-Admiral Alexeyev, the Chinese flag flying from the flagpole of the Chinese Maritime Customs was hauled down and the Russian flag hoisted, which flies there to this day and presumably for all eternity will continue to

[Illustration: Russian staging post at an inn in Tudiatai (p. 31).]

fly. From that moment on, Newchwang has in fact been under Russian administration. The Daotai had fled, allegedly with the consent of the Russians, who indeed also permitted him to travel by train with bag and baggage to Tianzhuangtai, which they could easily have prevented. The suspicious charade of the Chinese official, which I mentioned earlier, is cast in a somewhat different light by this. Even if annexation had not yet been formally declared, official documents were not long in coming. The first read, in literal translation, as follows:

"Yingkou, August 6, 1900.

To the Consuls of the Treaty Powers, Yingkou.

Your Excellencies! The hostile conduct of the Chinese authorities, who first stirred up the rebellion and then declared they could not maintain order, {p. 42} ended in an open attack on our forces, which we had stationed in the city in accordance with the wishes of the consular corps. During the said attacks, the Chinese authorities fled, leaving the city to its fate. In order to prevent lawlessness and pillaging on the part of the Chinese mob and to protect the trade of the port and foreign property, the Russian military authorities consider it necessary to place the city under the guard of Imperial Russian troops. It must be added that the perfidious destruction of the Chinese Eastern Railway by insurgents and Chinese troops likewise makes the maintenance of a military force in Yingkou necessary to protect the railway line. The Imperial Government nevertheless recognises as a fundamental principle that Yingkou is a treaty port; that is, the provisional administration shall be established in the interest of the Russians as well as of the foreigners and the Chinese, and does not intend to impair the rights and privileges which they formerly enjoyed in Yingkou. The foreign community is requested to note that the sole task of the provisional Russian administration is to maintain peace and order and to restore trade, and in these efforts I hope to be able to count on the sincere support of the foreign representatives. I have the honour to be Your Excellencies' sincere servant, E. Alexeyev, Vice-Admiral."

The tenor of this document is thoroughly loyal, and one might indeed be inclined to place full confidence in the assurances it contains, had one not already been duped all too often by fine Russian assurances. Alexeyev in particular is one of the craftiest men in the Far East, an exquisite master at operating with fine words and the most soothing assurances, of whom it could never be said that he failed to keep one of his promises, yet who, through it all, gives only such assurances as can be interpreted at will. "Be gentle as doves and wise as serpents," as the Bible says. The application of this scriptural saying can be seen illustrated, among other things, in the consummate manner in which the Muscovite pushed the Japanese to the wall in this entire affair. I recall that in the Sino-Japanese War, Japan had occupied Manchuria, and that Russia, France, and Germany forced the Mikado to withdraw from and abandon Manchuria once more in the Treaty of Shimonoseki. It is not surprising that Japan recalled its old interests in these regions at the very moment when the Russian was about to snatch the tasty morsel away right under its nose. German merchants know only too well what Germany forfeited in Japan by its stance during the conclusion of the Peace of Shimonoseki, without receiving the slightest compensation for it on the other side. They see no outcome of the operation other than that the further advance of Russian power toward the south of East Asia was quite pointlessly aided and abetted. The Tsarist Empire alone derived benefit from the intervention at the Sino-Japanese peace settlement; Germany in particular alienated Japan at that time—a nation that we out here in the East may yet need more than Russia—and Russia's gratitude, unfortunately, in no way corresponds to the sacrifices we made for its policy.

{p. [61]}

In short, this time too Japan had kept its eyes open with regard to Manchuria and sent two warships to Newchwang to protect the settlement. Since the operations in China were regarded as a joint undertaking, and Japan was perhaps the most loyal of all the powers when it came to subordinating itself to a unified supreme command, the Japanese saw nothing amiss in their ships at Newchwang being placed under the Russian commander Alekseyev, who was their senior in both rank and age. The day of the engagement came; fighting went on for an entire day, Yingkou was even bombarded, and during all these engagements, as well as those that followed, it did not occur to the Russian commander even once to call upon the Japanese subordinate to him—who were merely awaiting his orders—to take part. That is typically Russian! In the face of these developments, the Japanese, who throughout the joint operations had never introduced the slightest discord into the concert of the powers and had conscientiously avoided all friction, confined themselves to withdrawing their ships with the onset of the cold season and lodging a diplomatic protest against the nature of Russian conduct in Manchuria. Thus the Russians had in fact succeeded once again in squeezing the Japanese out here as well, and they could now trumpet to the world that they alone had saved Newchwang and Manchuria, although the Japanese would gladly have helped, and perhaps the English as well. The next step that Russia took after this “rescue”, however, was to claim permanent possession of slant-eyed Manchuria from China, and it ultimately met with compliance, if only in the sense that a highwayman meets with compliance from the princely father of his abducted bride.

{p. 41}

On 9 August, a total of seven Russian, two Japanese, and one British warship lay in the harbour. The commander of the Russian gunboat “Ovotschne”, de Collongue, had been appointed temporary civil governor of Newchwang, but a change was already made in the administration on 10 August. The former Russian consul in Newchwang, Ostroverkhov, was appointed civil administrator, while the aforementioned de Collongue became town commandant.

On 18 August, the consuls received the following letter from Civil Governor Ostroverkhov: “In accordance with the instructions I have received from the Commander-in-Chief of the province of Kwantung and of the forces stationed therein, I have the honour to transmit to you herewith a copy of the translation of the ‘Regulations for the Imperial Russian Provisional Civil Administration of the Port of Newchwang’, which was confirmed on 27 July (9 August by our calendar, that is, four days after the capture of the city) by His Excellency Vice-Admiral Alekseyev.”

I am in a position to publish the text of this official document, which may be of historical interest; it reads:

“Following the occupation of the port of Newchwang by Imperial Russian troops, the administration has been established as follows:

1. The highest administrative official is the Chief Administrator, who is appointed by the Commander-in-Chief of the province of Kwantung and the troops stationed therein, and confirmed by imperial edict.

2. An administrative council with an advisory vote is attached to the Administrator. This council shall be convened, if necessary, and composed as follows: the town commandant, a representative of the consular corps, a representative of the foreign merchant community, a representative of the Chinese guilds, the Commissioner of Customs, and the district physician. This administrative council shall be presided over by the Civil Governor or, in the event of his absence, by the town commandant, who is also to deputise for the Civil Administrator in cases of illness. Furthermore, a municipal council consisting of representatives of the Chinese merchant community is attached to the Civil Administrator, to be convened in matters concerning native merchants and residents. Two secretaries are assigned to the Civil Administrator, as are the following officials, who are heads of the various administrative departments: 1. the chief of police, 2. the tax inspector and treasurer, 3. the president of the municipal court, and 4. the district physician, in addition to the necessary staff of interpreters. The town commandant has been appointed to defend the port against any contingencies and to secure the proper transit of goods by land and river. All troops stationed in the locality are subordinate to him, with the exception of soldiers employed in police service. The rights and duties of the commandant shall be regulated by special orders of the Commander-in-Chief.

3. The Civil Administrator has the right to issue martial laws, to levy poll taxes on the natives, and to dispose of the movable and immovable property of the Chinese government. He decides on the appropriation of funds that are allocated or collected to defray administrative costs and municipal expenses, and maintains relations with foreign representatives.

4. For the maintenance of order in the city, the Chief of Police (an officer) has been appointed, to whom an assistant and an adequate number of police officers are assigned. In order to maintain order on the river and among the junks, a guard ship is established under the command of an officer and an adequate number of harbor police. In addition, in order to establish thorough order in the city, the police are tasked with determining the number of inhabitants, houses, and public institutions. The harbor police are to prevent the arrival of junks carrying weapons, Chinese soldiers, and munitions of war.

5. The tax inspector and revenue officer, together with his staff, collects from the Chinese population the customary taxes and levies, as well as the taxes and levies imposed {p. 45} by the Civil Administrator. The tax inspector collects and disburses all funds, both government funds and taxes and levies, and is responsible for them. The accounts must be rendered every three months to the Commander-in-Chief of Kwantung Province.

6. The Chinese Maritime Customs Office shall come under the supreme supervision of the Imperial Russian Government, but will retain its present organization. The rights of the officials with respect to their service and salary remain unchanged. On the other hand, the Russian Government is at the same time responsible for the revenues of the customs office and will be represented in the customs service by a commissioner whose rights

[Illustration: Russian staging post in the Laoye Temple of Shanhaiguan (p. 35).]

are to be determined in a special official document. In the Chinese Maritime Customs Service there is a separate department for the collection of customs duties from Chinese junks and from the Chinese coastal crews; its revenues shall not be included in the maritime customs revenues, but shall be at the disposal of the Civil Governor. The head of this department will receive a salary supplement.

7. In order to decide legal cases between natives, a court shall be established in accordance with the principles of the mixed courts in China. Under its jurisdiction fall both police court matters and cases between Chinese and Russians or those foreigners who have no consular representation of their own on the spot. Disputes between foreigners who are sued by Chinese belong before the respective consular court; cases in which a Chinese is sued by a foreigner belong before {p. 16} the municipal court. Capital crimes committed by Chinese, as well as highway robbery, treason, or the smuggling of contraband of war, shall be tried before a court-martial composed of officers.

8. To exercise supreme supervision over the sanitary conditions of the city and to provide sanitary assistance to the inhabitants, a physician with the necessary auxiliary personnel has been appointed.

9. The heads of the various departments, namely the Chief of Police, the tax inspector and revenue officer, the municipal judge, and the sanitary commissioner, as well as the commissioner of the customs office, shall be appointed or dismissed by decree of the Commander-in-Chief of Kwantung Province and of the troops stationed therein, upon the proposal of the Civil Administrator.

10. The Civil Administrator possesses the right, in agreement with the individual department heads, to engage foreigners and Chinese for service in the various administrative departments and to determine their salaries.

11. The regulations for each individual administrative department are to be drafted by the Civil Administrator in conjunction with the heads of the various departments, and submitted to the Commander-in-Chief for approval.

12. For meeting the expenditures of the provisional civil administration and covering its costs, the Russian Government grants advances, which are to be covered by the taxes and revenues collected from the Chinese, as well as by the revenues of the Chinese department of the Maritime Customs Office.”

Thus far the official document, which is signed by the “Commander-in-Chief of Kwantung Province as well as its troops and of the naval forces in the Pacific,” Vice Admiral Alekseyev.

In addition, I would add that the revenues of the Maritime Customs Office, in so far as they derive from foreign vessels, are for the time being collected by the Russian Government and paid into the Russo-Chinese Bank, or as they say, “deposited.” Moreover, let it also be noted at once that the Russians, as representatives of the Chinese Government, also collect from Europeans the customary three percent ad val. fee on land sales, which was formerly paid to the Chinese Government as a levy for registering the purchase in the land registry. This regulation was published on December 19, and all land sales that have taken place since the time of the Russian occupation are deemed not legally valid unless the three percent has been paid in arrears.

The foregoing decrees came into effect on the day of their publication. Since then, everything provided for in them has been put in place. A vast army of officials is stationed in Newchwang, but up to the time I arrived there, the Russians had not yet been able to restore safety and order. It has remained a matter of fine words. Brigandage continues to flourish in the city, and Russian soldiers occasionally join the Chinese bandits. Friction arose between the civil governor and the garrison commander; the two departments therefore do not work sufficiently hand in hand; on the official side the word "provisional" is constantly on everyone's lips, and yet the very first decree already speaks of quarterly reckonings by the tax inspector, and the officials of the civil government are engaged for years to come. Russia is thus contemplating quite a lengthy provisional arrangement. Meanwhile, negotiations are already under way with China. Two versions of a Manchurian agreement have become known. Officially, Russia denies the existence of such an agreement. Yet in fact the annexation of Manchuria has already been carried out; no one {p. [65]} can deny it, I have seen it with my own eyes*). But the result of the annexation is not a particularly gratifying one. How, indeed, can anything good come of an administration in which it is bound to happen that the chief of police is dismissed after a short time as incompetent, that the entire police force is driven off as untrustworthy, that the police assistant is banished to Sakhalin because, with the help of his Chinese interpreters, he extorted from a Chinese

[Illustration: Donkey caravan crossing the Liao River on the ice near the town of Tianzhuangtai (p. 37).]

wholesale merchant nearly 30,000 taels over time? How can an administration succeed when its entire regime is based on the exclusive mediation of roguish Chinese interpreters, former mafoos and coolies from Vladivostok, and when finally, {p. 48} to cap it all, the civil administrator's own salary is reduced by way of punishment because his revenues are so large that he knows no other way to invest them than in gambling?

::: notes
*) The reader is reminded that these reflections on the situation in Manchuria were written down at the beginning of the year 1901. In the meantime, in view of the Anglo-Japanese Alliance, Russia has, as is well known, declared itself ready to evacuate Manchuria after the expiry of a certain period. That it is serious about this intention, no one will believe. Whether and how it will be realised depends on the strength and determination of the "East Asian Dual Alliance".
:::

15 February.

Today I am sending a long dispatch home, in which I describe conditions as I found them. It is not possible for me to transmit this dispatch directly over the wire here in Newchwang, because instances of postal censorship give reason to fear that the dispatch will never reach Europe in its true wording. The telegraph line to Port Arthur, the so-called Imperial Chinese Telegraph, is in Russian hands. I am therefore forced to send my dispatch in a sealed envelope under a cover address to Chefoo, in order to have it sent from there by wire.

### 2. Newchwang, the Natural Gateway to Manchuria.

February 16–20.

To assess the whole Manchurian question, it is necessary to examine the significance of Newchwang more closely. As is well known, a number of international treaty ports exist along the Chinese coast where Europeans are permitted to trade. In each of these ports, which are subject to specific regulations and rules established in the commercial treaties between China and the individual powers, there is a Chinese customs office that clears the various steamers in and out and levies the tariff-prescribed duties from exporters and importers. These customs offices constitute the only administration of maritime customs in China established on European models, which was created by and is subordinate to the world-renowned Sir Robert Hart.

The "treaty ports" existing along the Chinese coast and also in the interior along the larger rivers are, in a sense, the joint dominion of the international trading powers. In them, complete equality of rights prevails among the various nations; they are the consular seats of the foreign commercial states. The fact that in some of these ports individual nations have established settlements for themselves on specific territories—so-called settlements, such as we find in Shanghai, Tientsin, Hankow, etc.—does not alter the fact that everywhere and without exception the principle of the "open door" is maintained, and by no means merely with respect to trade, but also with respect to the administration of the settlements. For example, German merchants sit on the municipal councils of both the British and French settlements in Shanghai as voting members with full rights, and it would occur to no one to describe or recognize either of these settlements as British or French colonies. It is inherent in the historical development of these open commercial ports that they are entitled to maintenance and protection on the part of *all* nations whose unimpeded competition in the Chinese market is guaranteed by the commercial treaties with China and among one another.

{p. [67]}

If this or that port has special concerns or demands to present to the Chinese government, it proceeds independently, usually, of course, in agreement with and supported by or through the mediation of the local consular corps.

Now, as an international treaty port, Newchwang fundamentally stands in no different position than, for example, Shanghai. And yet it was quietly tolerated that since August 6, 1900, Russia has established the described government in Newchwang, to which it indeed appends the adjective "provisional," without, however, indicating what kind of government it intends to establish definitively in Newchwang once it abolishes the provisional one.

[Illustration: A bean transport (p. 51).]

In circles that approve of Russia's actions in Manchuria, the example of Tientsin has been cited. It was said that it was wartime, and no one could find anything wrong if the state that assumed the protection of a threatened city also administered it during a turbulent period. But this overlooks the fact that the situation in Tientsin is, after all, fundamentally different. To be sure, a government was established in Tientsin, namely a provisional government, which will be followed again by a Chinese one after the conclusion of peace; but firstly, this administration extends only to the Chinese city, not to the foreign settlement of Tientsin, and secondly, it is composed of representatives of the powers involved. In Newchwang, however, only the Russian rules and governs, and indeed his authority extends not only to the Chinese city of Yingkou, but also to the foreign settlement of {p. 50} Newchwang. The protest of the predominantly British mercantile community of this place has remained unsuccessful.

Yet Newchwang is even more than an open port; it is the actual key to Manchuria. If we take up the map, we see how Manchuria, with the Liaodong Peninsula, thrusts its southernmost tip into the Gulf of Pechili. Now, it is a phenomenon known not only in East Asia that wholesale trade tends to follow the course of the great rivers—in China perhaps with the sole exception of the Yellow River, which, on account of its low water level, places too great obstacles in the way of navigation. In Manchuria we find the following waterways: to the east of the Liaodong Peninsula, the basin of the Yalu River, and to the west, that of the Liao River. The river basin of the Yalu is particularly noteworthy in that it forms the border between Korea and China; it is thus an area that is also of great political interest. In terms of commercial policy, however, the important circumstance is that this border region is virgin agricultural land. Until the second half of the past century, the land north of the Yalu was uncultivated. For political reasons, it lay fallow. Under the name of "No Man's Land" it may have been mentioned here and there, but rarely in connection with trade and commerce. In this so-called "no man's land," which was brought under cultivation only in the last fifty years, predominantly by immigrants from Shandong, there are still large forests with fine stands of timber; the settlements situated at the mouth of the Yalu are therefore important entrepôts and markets for timber, and while the forest wealth of northern Korea has already suffered considerably, No Man's Land still supplies significant quantities of timber. To be sure, with the predatory exploitation being carried on, there is an imminent danger that before long the mountain heights here will be just as bare as in the rest of China.

Given the well-known tensions prevailing between Russia and Japan over Korea, it is perhaps not surprising that there has hitherto been a lack of people who have turned their eyes to these tracts of China. One therefore has all the more reason to draw attention to the Yalu; especially for us Germans, this region is gaining in importance the more we gain a foothold in Shandong, for it is, in a sense, a hinterland of Shandong. Not only did it draw and still draws its population from there, but as a producing region it is also otherwise in direct touch with Shandong, for example in terms of silk production. The trade of the Yalu region has hitherto been exclusively junk trade, but I am convinced that it would be well worth opening one of the existing timber markets at the mouth of the Yalu as an international commercial port. In any case, we Germans ought to keep our eyes open regarding these regions and not let ourselves be deterred by unfounded fears of getting caught between the millstones of Japan and Russia.

As long as we do not yet possess an open port on this river, we must also exclude its basin when speaking of the trade routes currently under consideration for Manchuria. The actual trade route through Manchuria passes via Yingkou. I have already mentioned that at Yingkou, the Chinese town where the international settlement of the same name—called "Newchwang" by the English after the city situated a few miles upstream {p. 51}—is located, the summer and winter trade routes from the north converge. "Newchwang" is one of the many ports in North China that are closed to navigation by ice throughout the winter. On the other hand, a characteristic feature of Manchuria is the enormous cart traffic in winter. I have never seen anything in any province of China that could even remotely compare with this traffic. In Tianzhuangtai, for instance, I came to an inn where the stabled mules and ponies numbered several thousand (!), and there were several such inns in that town. The trade of Newchwang is of a different nature in summer than

[Illustration: Grain (kaoliang) warehouse in Newchwang (p. 51).]

in winter; in winter, the Newchwang merchant looks more toward the interior of the country, whereas in the fine season he looks out to sea. In winter, the Manchurian farmer brings his produce—beans, bean cake and oil, kaoliang, etc.—on carts from the interior many hundreds of li to Yingkou. When navigation reopens, the foreign shipping companies have their hands full transporting all these staple goods to the south, but mostly to Japan. The realm of the Mikado is the chief customer for the beans and the products derived from them. In addition, there is a brisk trade in castor oil, sesame, silk, and skins or furs, as well as in maize and millet, the rice of the North Chinese.

Yet for imports as well, Newchwang is in a sense the sole mouth of Manchuria, although the trade in European goods is largely in Chinese {p. 52} hands, and for the most part in those of intelligent Cantonese. The import of so-called "piece goods"—that is, manufactured and fancy goods of all kinds—is very considerable, and Germany has a substantial share in it.

To illustrate this, here are a few more figures:

In the fiscal year 1899 (the year 1900 cannot be taken into account as a normal year owing to the unrest), the total value of Newchwang's trade amounted to the respectable sum of 48,357,623 Haikwan taels (approx. 150 million marks). Compared with the previous year, which stood at 32,141,000 taels, this represents an increase of 49 percent. If this latter figure is compared in turn with that of 1888, one finds that the trade of Newchwang grew by 236 percent in ten years—a proportion that stands unique in Chinese customs statistics. In particular, the value of goods imported from foreign countries increased three hundred and fifty-fold during these ten years. From this one can see that Newchwang is a port whose importance relative to the old and well-known commercial ports of China, especially Shanghai and Tientsin, is undergoing a very substantial relative increase. In 1899, the number of ships calling at Newchwang reached 1,161, and the tonnage of these vessels exceeded one million register tons for the first time. Regrettably, it must be stated that the number of German vessels has declined compared to earlier years, whereas Japanese shipping in particular has risen steadily and rapidly thanks to the efforts and endeavors of its government. It is of great interest for the commercial history of East Asia to point out this circumstance, and the brief span of three trade years provides an instructive illustration of the growth of the Japanese Empire's commercial relations. Allow me to compile the figures for these years.

In the year 1897, the following ships called at the port of Newchwang: 165 British, 71 German, 62 Chinese, 52 Japanese, 31 Swedish-Norwegian, 3 Dutch, and 1 (!) Russian. In the year 1898, there anchored in Newchwang 312 British, 211 Japanese, 144 German, and no Russian ships. In the last financial year, 1899, the figures had shifted as follows: 384 (!) Japanese ships, 316 British, 220 Chinese, 94 German, 46 Swedish-Norwegian, 7 Russian, and 2 Danish and 2 American ships each. The enormous increase in the Japanese share at the expense of that of the other countries is admittedly attributable primarily to the fact that during these years a truly outstanding market for beans opened up in Japan, and the Japanese naturally send their own ships to import this product into their country. Of the beans to the value of 8,691,236 taels that were shipped in 1899, approximately 93 percent went to Japan alone. On the other hand, the flourishing of Japanese trade in Newchwang must also be attributed to the energetic activity of the Japanese government. Apart from Britain, Japan is the only country that maintains a career consul in Newchwang, and Japanese consuls are known for working for their country's commerce, and working vigorously—something that often cannot be said of those of other nations, who regard their position more as a representative one. But not only that. Japanese students, disguised as Chinese, {p. 53} traversed the country and thus became acquainted with trade at its source. As a result, the Japanese colony in Newchwang is quite substantial, and given the business acumen and energy of this island nation, it would not surprise me if before long Newchwang becomes a port in which the Japanese element predominates. That German trade in particular could be so severely displaced in Newchwang I attribute mainly to the fact that the German Empire is inadequately represented there consularly in every respect. The administrator of the vice-consulate is an old man, an English merchant who also administers the

[Illustration: Cart traffic in Yingkou (p. 51).]

American, Norwegian-Swedish, and Dutch consulates. Even so, in a quarter of a century he has not been able to make a success of himself. That such a representative is not fit to set German trade in Newchwang back on its feet requires no proof. Newchwang is by no means a lost cause, and Germany would certainly have paid more attention to the place had its attention been properly drawn to it. But the acting German vice-consul did nothing to conduct himself in a manner appropriate to his position during this important and critical time. Throughout the entire period of the Russian occupation, when the other consuls, especially the Japanese and British, were sending home dispatch after dispatch, Mr. Baudinel was absent from almost all conferences of the consular corps; and when, on the occasion of a visit by Vice Admiral Alekseyev to Newchwang, it was a matter of safeguarding German interests (Cossacks had raided a German hotel), he did nothing other than write a subservient letter to the all-powerful Russian, in which he begged his forgiveness for not being able to pay {p. 51} his respects to him. I myself and other residents of Newchwang, during an audience we had with Alekseyev, saw this letter lying in the wastepaper basket (!). An Englishman drew our attention to it. Truly—shameful!

Unfortunately, Germany adheres to the principle of deciding to install consuls—especially career consuls—only after German interests have crystallized in a given place. The Japanese do it differently: as soon as a port is newly opened, they first place a consul there, and indeed a career consul, who studies the locality and local conditions and then draws commerce in his wake. The German Empire can learn a great deal from young Japan out here. In any case, we must endeavor to free ourselves from the principle that we have done enough when, in any place where the appointment of a consular representative has become an absolute and urgent necessity (for we simply will not make up our minds to take this step any earlier), we hand the consulate over to a merchant, who can never match even a mediocre career consul. What we need out here are career consuls, commercial experts as it were, and above all in vulnerable places such as Newchwang—men who know how to protect their own standing and that of the German Empire and German trade.

Above all, however, it is ill-advised to entrust our consular representation to an English merchant who also administers three other consulates on the side. Today more than ever, Newchwang needs an able German consul who has a firm hand and knows how to assert himself. Once we have achieved this, we will hopefully also go a step further and finally reconcile ourselves to the idea of posting to China only consuls who have specialised in the country’s conditions. Our Chinese-trained interpreters are usually far better acquainted with conditions than the frequently transferred consuls, and these latter are often enough forced to be advised by their interpreters down to the smallest detail. Why have jurists who came from who-knows-where and will go who-knows-where as soon as they have served their time in China—which they themselves, unfortunately, often regard merely as a transitional period—when the interpreters can do their work better? Let the consular career in China be opened up to interpreters to the widest possible extent, and we will fare well by it. The same applies to the legation post in Peking. Sooner or later, we will have to come around to viewing the great Chinese Empire as a world unto itself, one that demands to be specially studied and treated as such. And then, I fear, events in the near future will follow in such rapid succession that we must take good care to train a solid staff of younger consular candidates specifically schooled in East Asian affairs—men who are not merely able to affix a few stamps, collect a few dollars for clearing a German steamer inward and outward, or receive higher- or lower-ranking travellers passing through, but who also learn the language of the country, do not change their posts too frequently, and understand how to study the country’s trade and provide pointers on where and how the German merchant is able to do business in China. At least, that is how the Japanese consuls do it. And in doing so, Japan is surpassing its masters.

{p. 55}

### 3. In Audienz beim russischen Höchstkommandierenden.

21 February.

This morning, the Commander-in-Chief of the Russian army and navy in the Far East, Vice Admiral Alekseyev, arrived in Newchwang. He had announced an inspection tour to Newchwang and Mukden some time ago, and I had extended my stay in Newchwang beyond what was originally planned partly because I wanted to await his arrival. I was also granted the privilege today of being received in audience by His

[Illustration: The Chinese government yamen in Newchwang (p. 10).]

Excellency. For eastern Russian Asia, Alekseyev is almost what the Tsar is for the western Russian Empire. He commands independently not only Manchuria, the Russian expeditionary forces, and the Russian navy stationed in the Pacific, but his voice is virtually an order for the whole of eastern Siberia as well. Among the Russians, the navy indeed holds a higher rank than the land army; the naval officers are also, in general, far better educated than those of the army, and Alekseyev in particular is considered one of the most capable senior Russian officers. In any case, he is more master over his subordinates than any other foreign general, thanks to the despotic form of government of the Tsarist empire.

{p. 56}

When one seeks an audience with such a potentate, one must also have something to say. And since I make it a principle to avoid interviewing any influential personality merely to satisfy curiosity, given that the gentleman in question very quickly sees through the intention and one may then easily end up more foolish after the interview than before, I sought out a matter of concern and was also fortunate enough to be able to present a request whose granting or refusal was bound to be more instructive for me than any harmless answers to tricky questions.

The audience takes place in the Russian consulate, although the admiral, owing to the very mediocre housing conditions in Newchwang, has preferred to live in his saloon carriage at the Russian railway station. Shortly after three o'clock, his standard is hoisted on the flagpole of the Russian consulate—the signal to those gathered in the waiting room that he has just driven up. In the waiting room, just about everyone of any standing or name in Newchwang has assembled. First of all, the only two career consuls of foreign powers, the British and the Japanese, in full dress. Then a number of merchants who have this or that to present, the directors of the Russo-Chinese Bank, and so on. The honours are done by the Russian consul and the civil administrator Ostroverkhov, while the vice admiral’s political and diplomatic adviser, Mr Korostovets, assists him during the individual receptions.

The opportunity to present requests and complaints must be seized, and there is much to complain about. A few days ago, marauding Cossacks attempted to break into the English consulate. The Chinese watchman barred their entry and closed the gate. At that, one of the Cossacks raised his rifle and shot the watchman in the abdomen through the door. The Cossacks escaped. For as long as I have been in Newchwang, no one has been able to track the fellows down. Rumours were indeed circulating that the murderer had been found and executed by firing squad under martial law, but in reality this is not the case; the investigation that was launched did lead to a trail, but to no punishment.

Another complaint is brought forward by the head of an English firm based in Newchwang. He wished to bring up a substantial sum of silver to Newchwang for the branch of the Yokohama Specie Bank, but the Russians in Port Arthur are preventing him from using the railway, on which everyone else travels free of charge.

The German consul, too, should have used the opportunity to lodge a complaint. The German hotel in which I am staying was attacked one night. The intruders were tampering with the gate and threatening the Chinese watchman. Awakened by the commotion, the hotelkeeper and I ultimately had to rush downstairs, revolver and Winchester in hand, to investigate the cause of the racket, only to find that it was not the Chinese bandits—who carry on their mischief every night in the Chinese city—who had paid us a visit, but our protectors, the Cossacks. The next day the hotelkeeper went to the German consul to request vigorous protection against a recurrence of such assaults; the consul, however, took no steps to comply with this request.

{p. 37}

And so the audience proceeds: first come the official representatives of the foreign states, then the directors of the Russo-Chinese Bank, and finally the rest *secundum ordinem*. I personally am received together with the head of the firm Bush Brothers, the leading English house in town, and the director of the Yokohama Specie Bank. His Excellency receives us standing, graciously offers his hand, and is of a truly excellent amiability. Alekseyev is of stocky build and possesses a stately *embonpoint*; above the round curve of his cheekbones flash a pair of friendly yet cunning eyes; his lightly tanned, intelligent face, with its slightly curved, prominent nose, is framed by a full black beard parted in the middle. Such is roughly the appearance of the future Russian Minister of the Navy.

First, after inviting us all to be seated, he greets me, specifically asking whether I speak French. Perhaps he does not know quite what I want of him, and he probably suspects, quite rightly, that the others will not understand French. The conversation is therefore conducted in French. I begin by reminding him that I had already been presented to him at Beitang on the day of the assault, speak briefly of my journey from Peking to Newchwang, and finally explain to him that I intend to travel through Manchuria via Mukden, Jilin, Harbin, etc., to Vladivostok, but that given the present insecurity in the country I cannot risk making the journey without assuring myself of his support and protection. He asks me this and that about the execution of my plan and ultimately declares to me {p. 58} with positively charming amiability that he would grant me every facility within his power for this journey.

Thereupon, the other two gentlemen's further concerns are discussed. Of interest in this regard is the Vice Admiral's solemn declaration that Russia will never compete with Newchwang through Port Arthur—an assurance received not entirely without skepticism, especially as the Admiral asserts on the other hand that the railway connecting Newchwang and Port Arthur would not be opened to public traffic before a year and a half has passed. Until that time, Russia itself requires the line exclusively for military purposes and for bringing up the necessary materials to complete the track. It is a rather comforting assurance for Newchwang that it is to do without the overland link to Port Arthur for another year and a half. Mr. Bush objects, however, that he cannot then understand why the railway is nevertheless already carrying general freight for private individuals once again. The Vice Admiral disputes this fact; Mr. Bush maintains it and substantiates it with examples. Personally, I have also seen carloads of bean cake being transported on the Mukden–Port Arthur line, and I know that other goods have come up from Port Arthur for which freight was paid, and at an extraordinarily high rate at that. Two possibilities therefore remain: either the railway officials are officially forbidden to transport private goods, but do so anyway and pocket the money, or Russia does not take the prohibition against using the line to transport merchandise belonging to private individuals all that seriously and maintains it only officially in order to enforce it in specific cases when it suits them. The latter assumption is probably correct, and the former cannot be ruled out. What can be clearly and distinctly gathered from Vice Admiral Alekseyev's remarks, however, is that Russia intends to maintain the current status in Manchuria for at least another year and a half, whether it decides to scale back its demands in response to the protests of the Powers or not. Its policy in this matter will be this: as long as the protest persists, it steps back slightly and contents itself with whatever small advantages are conceded. But once the energy of the protesters wanes, it will find its way back all the more surely, and then no one will pay attention. That is what Russia is counting on. We are always poking fun at England's jealousy in watching to ensure that Russia does not expand too quickly; we make fun of it and have no idea what debt of gratitude we actually owe England for this policy of acting as the world's gendarme, and specifically of standing guard against the Tsarist Empire. Perhaps later on we will finally bring ourselves to recognize that this was one of the many magnificent services England has rendered to the world at large, even if in the belief and with the intention of serving exclusively its own interests.

{p. 59}

[Illustration: Mountain formations on the Liaodong Peninsula (p. 62).]

## Chapter IV. Through the Liaodong Peninsula.

### 1. By the Manchurian Railway to Port Arthur.

23 February.

Every morning at 9 o'clock a train departs from Newchwang in the direction of Dashiqiao, the junction station for the Mukden–Port Arthur line. I do not wish to linger in Newchwang any longer, but intend to reach Chefoo as quickly as possible so that I can send several reports home from there, as I do not trust the Russian post. The day before yesterday, therefore, immediately after my audience with Alekseyev, I sat down and repeated in writing my request for a proper passport for travel through northern Manchuria, asking that the passport be sent to me in Chefoo.*) This morning I depart. After much running back and forth at the Russian railway station an hour away from Newchwang, I finally manage to have a ticket issued. It cost nothing. Although I arrived a full hour before the train's departure, issuing the ticket, signing and countersigning, and whatever else had to be done took up so much time that I almost missed the train. At last I am seated in {p. 60} a third-class compartment (others are not available, as all the first- and second-class carriages are occupied by officers and civil officials). As long as the trip costs nothing and one must be glad to be taken along at all on a railway serving exclusively military purposes, there can be no objection. Besides, quite a number of officers who do not have a private compartment are traveling in the same carriage. We pass through a treeless but well-cultivated plain; the train stops at several small stations until at last in Dashiqiao it is: "All change!" as the carriage in question continues on to Mukden. In German, Dashiqiao means "big stone bridge." And such a bridge does indeed exist.*)

::: notes
*) As was to be assumed from the outset, I have not yet (1902) come into possession of the passport and can hardly hope to do so any longer. At the same time, this confirms my assumption that I would never have received permission to travel through Manchuria if I had begun my journey from Port Arthur, as I had originally intended.
:::

Here I have a layover of about an hour, as we must wait for the train coming down from Mukden. I leave my luggage in the care of my boy, who sits down on it, while a few hundred coolies loiter about and try to snatch the pieces of luggage out of his hands unless he beats them off. The unemployed coolies are an outright plague in Manchuria wherever they have to deal with Russians. An incredibly disrespectful rabble! And the Russians are to blame for it. In Dashiqiao, for example, four or five soldiers have been equipped with knouts and tasked with keeping the bothersome coolies away from the trains. They sweep among them like madmen, but the coolies are usually nimbler than they are and skillfully dodge the blows, thoroughly enjoying the spectacle of these raging Rolands. The Cossacks clearly find amusement in it as well, and the whole thing makes the impression of a comical game of tag to the delight of the soldiers and the Chinese; if a blow does occasionally land, the Chinese takes no offense, but it certainly inspires no respect for Russian authority.

It happens to be the time of the Chinese New Year. I am reminded of this when I see a long New Year's procession with all manner of masquerade coming along. A number of people in whimsical theatrical costumes—their faces painted, some disguised as women—approach. They are all walking on stilts strapped to their legs. The procession proceeds to the Russian station and the officers' quarters to perform its dances there. The Chinese regards New Year greetings as a welcome opportunity to beg, and the Russian is generally not stingy on such occasions. My boy, the Shandong man, explains to me that these are people from Zhili, as the New Year stilt procession performed here is customary among them. Manchus and Shandong people would never do such a thing.

::: notes
*) It is, in general, the pleasant thing about many Chinese place names that one can immediately deduce from them a geographically significant feature of the place in question. A few examples: Peking, northern capital; Nanking, southern capital; Newchwang, cow market; Yingkou, fly-sack (the place is notorious for the incredible swarms of flies found there in summer); Dagu, great opening (mouth of the Peiho), etc.
:::

{p. 61}

The train from Mukden pulls in, my luggage flies into the compartment, and I immediately occupy an entire bench arranged to fold down. On it I spread out my blankets and furs, and my bed for the night is ready.

The train is quite full. It swarms with Russian officials wearing uniforms similar to military dress, but distinguished from the active military—who wear oval cockades—by their round cockades. They are all figures in whom one can immediately recognize the weather-beaten toughness that simply goes with this harsh climate. One often sees wild, disheveled beards, but mostly above them a pair of good-natured little eyes gazing shrewdly out into the world.

The distance to Port Arthur is approximately 360 kilometers. Nevertheless, our train is not scheduled to arrive there before 9 o'clock tomorrow morning; it thus takes exactly 21 hours to travel this short distance. Of the dawdling pace of Russian railway operations, one can {p. 62} hardly form an idea with our home notions of punctuality. Stops are made at every station, often for up to an hour. A hundred times the carriage door is slammed open and shut; the journey is far from pleasant.

The landscape, by the way, is by no means devoid of charm. At first, one travels along the foot of the mountain range that cuts through the Liaodong Peninsula and ends only at Port Arthur. The heights, though treeless and bare, are nevertheless interesting simply because they are heights and mountains—one learns to be modest in China! Every now and then, steep knolls rise up directly from the plain before them, often crowned with a temple, lending the Manchurian mountain landscape something characteristic. The villages here no longer bear a specifically Manchurian character; one sees pointed roofs again, as in Zhili and Shandong, a circumstance indicating that we find ourselves in a land where Chinese colonists strongly assert their influence. After a while, the railway cuts through the mountains in rather sharply cut passes, and that makes the journey even somewhat romantic. It is 6 o'clock in the evening. Night begins to veil the landscape. I stretch out on my wooden bench and fall into a restless sleep, disturbed less by the rattling of the carriage than by the endless slamming of doors and the long, noisy stops at the stations.

February 21.

At daybreak I am up again. We are still four hours away from Port Arthur. Now, for the first time, I notice properly constructed overpasses and bridges again. Hitherto the track had always been routed around the site where an overpass was eventually to stand—proof that this railway too must have been built in considerable haste, and that they are only gradually beginning to put the finishing touches to the work.

Incidentally, right here in the south of the Liaodong Peninsula numerous terrain difficulties had to be overcome. The region is very mountainous, especially at the narrowest point of the peninsula, where it narrows like a spit of land and the mountains approach close to the sea on both sides. The southern tip itself, on which Port Arthur lies, widens again.

It seems to me as though the train travels at greater speed the closer we get to Port Arthur. There is a fair amount of life at the stations; officials, and in some cases officials' wives, board the train, traveling into the city to run their errands and do their shopping; the compartment fills with all kinds of chattering and—curiously enough—book-"browsing" folk. Once more, the high walls of a 23-meter-deep cutting (this figure was given to me by a Russian railway official traveling with us) tower up on both sides, shutting out light and view; then we slowly descend into the valley of a small river, and already we see the first houses of Port Arthur appearing on the heights before us—barracks buildings, of course. Following the course of the river, we turn around the last outlying hill, upon which stands a tall lighthouse, and then pull up before a wooden ramp in the midst of an immense swarm of screeching, clamoring, and brawling coolies, armed for the most part with bamboo poles, who immediately storm the compartments and set {p. 63} about snatching the travelers' belongings from their hands without any invitation, until here too the Cossacks appear with their knouts and—this time to the blessing of traveling humanity—whip the coolies down from the carriages and the platform. This is Port Arthur, railway station!

At last I am ready to depart with four coolies carrying my belongings. We still have about a quarter of an hour to the center of town; I yearn for the hotel and

[Illustration: The port of Niuzhuang during the construction of the Chinese Eastern Railway (p. 59).]

above all for the necessary cleansing of my “body”. The coolies are made to understand that they are to take me to the largest hotel; they nod and set my luggage down at a dreadful dive. A grubby, dyed-in-the-wool Russian who does not speak a single syllable of any civilized language receives me, and finally I manage to find out that not a room is left. Assuming that the business about the “first-rate” hotel was a misunderstanding, I have myself directed to another. Alas, that dive {p. 61} is much, much worse, and once again no room is to be had—thank God! I still think in my youthful recklessness. I shall therefore try my luck with a third. My standards, however, have already dropped quite extraordinarily. They set me down at a third hotel. There a room is available. The Russian waiter boy first leads me through a foul-smelling courtyard, then up a narrow flight of stairs, a so-called chicken ladder, to a low upper floor. He stops in front of a crude wooden door opening onto a flimsy gallery facing the courtyard, opens it, and—I recoil from the dreadful odor that wafts toward me from the “room” intended for me. A tiny little chamber with a slanting roof, inside a narrow iron bed with dirty covers, a bedside mat, and a chair upon which stands the washbasin, the windows pasted shut with paper strips in the Russian fashion and impossible to open. It is simply appalling, and the thought of having to spend even a single night in it fills me with dread. In despair, I initially leave my trunks downstairs in the hallway under the care of my boy, consume some of the zakuska on the bar counter, and drink a vodka, thoroughly Russian. That is my morning coffee. Then, however, I immediately seek out a gentleman who is an official of the Russo-Chinese Bank and to whom I have letters of recommendation. Fortune favors me: in that very same institution I also run into a good acquaintance, a fellow traveler from the *Hamburg*. “Tell me, the hotel situation here seems to be in a pretty bad state.” “Yes, no decent person can stay in a hotel here. But if you are willing to make do with a sofa at my place—you know, one of those old-fashioned ones with broad armrests——.” “Oh, warmest thanks, with the greatest of pleasure!”

### 2. Port Arthur.

February 25.  
Port Arthur is the Russian Kiaochow. Only, after the occupation the Germans had to start completely from scratch and, so to speak, conjure a new city out of the ground with Tsingtao, whereas in Port Arthur the Russians found an already built, European-style modern naval port. Accordingly, they have more or less left everything as they found it. This goes so far that they have not permitted a single private individual to build thus far.
