If certain firms did not wait for a license, they must reckon with the risk that one fine day the authorities will simply have the building torn down again or confiscate it for themselves. Indeed, up to now not even sales of government land have taken place; it is said to be doubtful whether it will ever come to that at all, since there is in fact an intention to reserve Port Arthur exclusively for naval and military purposes. The buildings that have been erected so far are military or government structures; private individuals almost all live in restored Chinese houses that have been makeshift-adapted for their residential and business purposes. Nowhere in the East have I seen such wretched offices as in the Russian Tsingtao.

Port Arthur possesses a truly magnificent harbor. It opens out toward the sea through a fairly narrow exit, bounded to the right and left by mountains {p. 65} rising steeply from the water. The harbor basin proper forms, so to speak, a small inland lake, enclosed on all sides by sheltering heights and connected to the sea only by a narrow, short channel. At all events, from the town of Port Arthur one always sees only the small inland lake, and can catch a view of the open sea from only a few spots. The location is thus extraordinarily favored by nature. All around on the heights stand forts, and nowhere else in the world, in all likelihood, is the ban on photography enforced as rigorously as here. If, despite this, I manage to snap a picture here and there, I am constantly aware that this is actually a crime that could easily make me acquainted with Russian fortress walls.

Nature has been lent a helping hand—and indeed, it was the Chinese who did so. For under the direction of French and German instructors, they had a large, deep basin dug below the mountain that borders the harbor entrance on the east, completely sheltered from any inclemency of the weather. The Russians now use it as a naval harbor. Only warships and vessels of the so-called Volunteer Fleet are permitted to anchor in this basin. Along its shore stand warehouses and cranes, and a dock capable of accommodating the largest vessels is built directly alongside it; in short, a model nautical facility has been created here of which China could be proud, and which it surely yielded only with great anguish to its lease-eager neighbor in the north.

Port Arthur is a naval port and a military station in the truest sense of the word. In the harbour, warship lies alongside warship, and the streets teem with soldiers of every branch of service. There are said to be 10,000 men stationed in Port Arthur in land troops alone. In any case, one sees massive complexes built up with barracks and quarters, along with long rows of magazines and reserve storehouses, well supplied with all kinds of war materiel—quite apart from the troops and depots located in the ten or so forts surrounding Port Arthur.

{p. 66}

The deep water of the harbour is bounded by a long spit of land extending from the hills situated west of the harbour entrance. Seen from the opposite heights, these resemble a sleeping tiger, and with a little imagination the spit in question can be claimed as the tail of this tiger. Consequently, among the non-Russians of Port Arthur it is also generally called the “tiger’s tail”. It runs essentially in a south-west to north-east direction. The water behind it—that is, north of it—is rather shallow, and there is an intention to fill in this part of the harbour in order to reclaim building land.

Port Arthur itself, the town, clusters around the aforementioned hill on which the lighthouse stands. The main complex lies to the south, though houses and streets in the valley already stretch right around the hill. The plan is to move the small residential and commercial quarter—which is to be retained merely to meet local needs—entirely away from the harbour area, specifically to the northern slope of the hill. In general, the intention is to separate government, military, and residential or commercial quarters strictly from one another. Commercial firms will presumably be tolerated only in such numbers as are necessary to meet the needs of the colony itself. In any event, Alekseyev spoke the absolute truth when he assured that Russia did not intend to create competition for Niuzhuang in Port Arthur. In Port Arthur, certainly not. But elsewhere instead. More on this later, however.

Viewed as a fortress, Port Arthur ought to appear impregnable. That the Japanese nevertheless succeeded in occupying Port Arthur during the Sino-Japanese War was due to corrupt Chinese administration and betrayal. In any case, the Japanese did not succeed in capturing the town from the seaward side either; rather, they had to take the overland route and come from the north. Because of the surrounding hills crowned with forts, warships wishing to bombard Port Arthur would have to fire with heavy howitzers if they wanted to hit the town. It is a military position of the first rank, prepared by nature and developed by engineering. Good roads have also been provided everywhere; above all, excellent connecting roads lead across the heights from fort to fort. In short, everything has been done to make Port Arthur impregnable. At any rate, one cannot escape this impression when standing on Lighthouse Hill and looking down upon the harbour, the throng of houses, the forts, and the barracks. Russia possesses a position here that commands respect, but in the end it has done little itself to create it. Almost everything was already there; the improvements Russia has carried out here do not bear such a proportion to what it found that one could sing Russia any particular hymn of praise with respect to Port Arthur. The railway alone is an exception.

Let us descend again from Lighthouse Hill to the town! As noted, everything is essentially still as it was in Chinese times. Only the Chinese signboards have vanished, and in their place we see Russian inscriptions everywhere. In the street, the heavily fur-clad *izvozchik*, the cab driver, adjusts his cap, and the Polish booth-keeper, in distinctly Jewish German, advertises his felt boots or his cigarettes.

Along the north side of the harbour runs the railway; on the east side is a “bund” some 100 metres long, the only street in Port Arthur laid out {p. 67} and constructed—that is, paved—as such. Along this “bund” are the “offices” of several major European firms. The American Trading Company maintains a branch office there, as does an English import firm. In addition, the street contains a large Russian shop and a restaurant, the finest in town. The rest is occupied by the complex of the Russo-Chinese Bank. In fact, the entire street frontage belongs to the latter institution, and it would long ago have built itself a palace there commensurate with its monopoly and the amount

[Illustration: Characters from the masquerade procession (p. 60).]

it earns in Port Arthur, had it obtained permission to do so and not perhaps had to reckon with being evicted from there altogether, and with the possibility that in a year and a day, once Russia’s lofty plans for the future should be realized, it might be entirely pointless to maintain more than a small branch in Port Arthur. The Manchurian headquarters will then perhaps be relocated to Dalian Bay, where Russian commercial interests are slated to concentrate at some later date.

{p. 68}

I have already spoken on several occasions of the Russo-Chinese Bank and explained that it is not merely a powerful commercial institution in China, but also an extraordinarily important political tool in the hands of the Russian Finance Minister, Witte. Of interest, therefore, is the following detail, which is probably not very well known even in financial circles, but which nonetheless sheds a peculiar sidelight on Russia's political relationship with China. At the time, people were surprised that shortly after the founding of the Russo-Chinese Bank, the Chinese Empire withdrew its deposits from the Imperial Chinese Bank and transferred them to the newly established bank. Now, it is a fact that the Russo-Chinese Bank was established with capital raised half by the Russian side, but the other half by the—Chinese government. The bank thus actually operates with Chinese money. What statesmanship must have preceded this before China decided to make common financial cause with Russia! Anyone who knows the tenacity of the Chinese in money matters can imagine it. And yet the administration of the Russo-Chinese Bank is exclusively in Russian hands. China, despite being a half-shareholder, has no say whatsoever in the administration of this institution, which operates in a thoroughly Russian spirit. Now, that is all well and good as long as China remains exclusively in the role of a coupon-clipper. It has gladly made use of this activity, usually drawing advances against the handsome dividends. The great shrewdness of Russian financial policy toward China, however, lies in the fact that, as a result of these connections, Russia can constantly exert political pressure on China through the bank. If the Russian government has any claim against the Chinese, or if anything happens that must be represented by the Russian minister in Peking, Russia is in the fortunate position of being able to dictate compensation to China by simply threatening to deduct the demanded sum from China's capital share in the Russo-Chinese Bank. This is a simple expedient that rarely fails in its effect. As a consequence, Russia is really always in advance of China; the latter's assets, insofar as they are tied up in the Russo-Chinese Bank, are therefore better suited to making China compliant with Russian wishes than any political pressure or feat of arms. And Russia does not fail to tug vigorously at these leading strings by which it holds China.

Apart from the Russo-Chinese, there is no other bank in Port Arthur. It holds a monopoly and, out here, more or less plays the part of the Russian State Bank in general. Here, too, were turned over the enormous quantities of silver that the Russians brought back from their China campaign, just as Port Arthur was the central eastern market for the Russians upon their return from China. In various places in Port Arthur one can still see the photographs in which Russian officers had their portraits taken amidst mountains of silver shoes and the bank's Chinese shroffs*) who weighed the silver.

::: notes
*) Cashiers.
:::

{p. 69}

That retail trade flourishes in view of Port Arthur's large population will come as no surprise. Indeed, in certain streets one finds shop after shop, and inside mostly practical goods—above all, fine warm clothing and footwear. But everything is exorbitantly expensive! Port Arthur is one of the most expensive places in the entire East; perhaps only Vladivostok is more expensive still. The main reason for this is said to be the abnormally high rents for dwellings and shops. Having to pay 100 dollars or more a month for a tiny, dark, and poorly furnished room is by no means uncommon.

Gratifyingly, the largest business in Port Arthur is in the hands of two German firms that enjoy a special reputation among the Russians: namely, the Vladivostok firm of Kunst & Albers and the Chefoo firm of Sietas, Block & Co., both of which have branches here.

Export trade from Port Arthur hardly exists. The hinterland, the Liaodong Peninsula, is too poor, produces too little, and trade from the interior of Manchuria passes through Newchwang. In return, however, Port Arthur supplies the inhabitants along the railway line up to Mukden

{p. 70}

with provisions, above all with canned goods. To be sure, as far as the army is concerned—and really it is only the army that comes into question—the supplies are drawn from the government depots in Port Arthur, from which the merchant community gains nothing.

For the sake of completeness, moreover, it must be mentioned that in probably no other place in the East is womanhood accorded so free a scope as right here. It is particularly the demimonde that cavorts here, against which only a meager contingent of respectable women can be set. Men of a Don Juan disposition would find here a choice among all nationalities, from the supple Japanese girl to the vulgar Austrian woman and on to the American beauty, and a Leporello could certainly bring the catalogue of beauties—perhaps less desirable than desired—up to two thousand and eleven with little effort. These conditions are the undoing of the Russian officers.

All the fine "loot" from the Summer Palace near Beijing has gone the way of so much on earth in this manner, and in Port Arthur that way leads through the brothel to the Russo-Chinese Bank, every month, often in thousands of dollars. While I am in Port Arthur, a highly sensational incident happened to take place: a Russian officer shot at one of these ladies because she would not marry him. Fortunately, the shot struck only a less noble part, but despite this proof of love, the beauty did not marry her hot-headed suitor after all. She has no need to, she says. Shocking! says the Englishman. But that is simply part of Port Arthur's character, and people there speak of such matters as casually as we would say "Cheers!"

The Russian is exceptionally sociable, and the number of Russian taverns and restaurants in Port Arthur corresponds accordingly. One generally eats well there, while matters are rather more moderate when it comes to drinking. Everything, however, is unreasonably expensive. Russian cuisine has something delightful in the so-called *zakuska*, the "side dish," all kinds of piquant, fine delicacies enjoyed with bread and butter. In every restaurant one finds a buffet richly stocked with them. Regarding forks, the Russian is not so fussy. Everyone helps themselves with the same fork. It is clean again once it has been thoroughly licked off. Then it passes into other hands and mouths.

One can also find a theatre in Port Arthur, a real, permanent theatre. We visited it yesterday evening. Here one meets all the world, but especially the demimonde. During the intermissions, vodka is drunk and *zakuska* eaten at an especially well-stocked bar; it is swarming with officers, and in the upper galleries with soldiers, who, as back home, pay half price. The actors—and even more so the actresses—who find favor receive lavish presents: silversmith work, gold watches, jewelry, etc., precious and splendid, just as in Petersburg. The acting is excellent, in Little Russian dialect, accompanied by pleasant singing and superb national dancing. One almost forgets that one is in the Far East, at the southern tip of Manchuria, and is reminded of the harsh reality only when one is outside again, when the *izvoshchik* offers his droshky and one {p. 71} [Illustration: Daoist temple on the northern Liaodong Peninsula (p. 62).]

{p. 72}

declines with thanks, saying: "No, my little dove!" In Port Arthur, one may even say "my little dove" to a policeman, and he does not even take offense.

26 February.

"Do you know Dalny yet?" asks my host from the Russo-Chinese Bank in Port Arthur. "I'm sorry, I have not yet had the pleasure. Who is he?" The host laughs and says: "It is not a 'he' at all, it is an 'it,' but a very interesting 'it' that you must see under all circumstances." The door opens to the large bank office where the customers are attended to, and a tall gentleman enters the private office, his still-youthful face framed by a full beard. The green-trimmed uniform fits his body comfortably; on the large-visored officer's cap I see the round civil service cockade, and on the collar a green facing with a crossed hammer and anchor. "Oh, look how well that works out! Mr. Timm—Mr. Zabel! Mr. Zabel does not know Dalny yet, just imagine!—would you take him along? You are traveling back this evening, aren't you? Oh, you could find no better guide for Dalny than Mr. Timm." "Then it's agreed: this evening at 5 o'clock at the station. I will wait for you in front of my carriage, the second to last on the train. You have to walk around the back of the train; the carriage has only one door."

Well, good heavens, how on earth was I supposed to know Dalny? What strange ideas people have about an inquiry-minded stranger, as if he ought to know everything intimately right away, just because he has seen a little more of the world than an ordinary settlement colonist. When I was in Kobe last year and had been there for only two days, I met a gentleman on the street. I asked my companion: "Who is that?" "What, you don't know him yet? Why, that is so-and-so." After two days I am supposed to know all of Kobe!

### 3. Dalny, the East Asian San Francisco.

“Dalny” is a Russian word and means “the distance” in German. The Russian loves to give his city foundations flowery names, e.g. Vladivostok, the “stronghold of the East.” So too Dalny, “the distance.”

Once I have figured that out, I also know what it is all about. Already in Newchwang I had heard that Russia was in the process of building a great trading center in the south of the Liaodong Peninsula, “a completely modern city on the French model,” I was told, and people spoke of it as of something utterly mysterious, almost fairytale-like, that one could hardly quite believe. People spoke of it as of a utopia and delighted in vague notions. No one had seen the place yet, and even later in Chefoo, hardly anyone there knew anything about it either, despite its proximity. Only this or that captain had once, on the southern coast of the Gulf of Talienwan, cast his {p. 73} [Illustration: Dalny on the Bay of Talienwan, the East Asian San Francisco of the future (p. 78).]

{p. 71}

anchors to unload rails or cement barrels or machinery. And I am certain that at home, too, it will come as a surprise that here we have to do with nothing other than the new Vancouver or San Francisco on the other shore of the Pacific Ocean, with the terminus of the Trans-Siberian Railway, with the great future Russian transit port on whose quay the great central station will rise, where after completion of the railway travelers at the ticket window will ask: “One second-class direct to Berlin, please! ... Please, London, first class! via Vlissingen! ...”

Naturally, I intended to pay a visit to this fairytale new city if that were at all possible; only the name “Dalny” was still new to me, and like so many other people in the East, I had always assumed that it was the port of Talienwan itself. I therefore gladly seize the opportunity offered to me, arrive at the station at the appointed hour by Mr. Timm’s parlor car, and steam off in a northerly direction in the cozily warmed carriage over a glass of tea together with the engineer, the chief physician of the military hospital in Dalny, and a third government official. Again the train rushes through the mountains of southern Liaodong, again it moves forward through high cuttings and over bridges and little bridges, for four hours, to the station of Nansanshilibao, where the last carriages of the train are uncoupled. While the main body of the train continues on toward Mukden and Newchwang respectively, another locomotive is coupled to our four cars, and after a ride of about thirty minutes in an easterly direction, brings us to the Gulf of Talienwan; the train stops at an open spot, in the midst of a half-finished underpass. But before us lies a veritable sea of lights toward which we are now heading. It is Dalny.

One has often read in novels of how in America cities grew out of the ground, one might almost say, overnight. The North American West has much to tell of such foundations. And for anyone accustomed to the slow and steady development of our cities at home, the tales and accounts of such instant cities certainly have something strange and unfathomable about them. While a new city there, to be sure, owes its origin to the entrepreneurial spirit of one or more speculators, or else to some new opening up of treasures that the earth had hitherto kept hidden, here on the Bay of Talienwan it has occurred, probably for the first time in the history of European nations, that a state has carried out such a costly speculation at its own expense and its own risk without these prerequisites. Indeed, it may well be the first time that a state has openly appeared as a city speculator, building a city entirely from the ground up, and then inviting the nations of the earth to come there, to view the homesteads that the bold speculator has built, and to acquire them—the more expensive, the better. Quite apart from everything else, this is at the very least an engineering task of the highest interest and appeal, and the whole undertaking is arguably without precedent in colonial history.

The only place that might possibly be drawn upon for comparison is Tsingtao. Here, too, they started with next to nothing; they even had to remove what little was there—a few wretched Chinese huts—first, in order to create a completely level base for {p. 75} the new city that was to rise upon this ground. But in the end, apart from the few and most indispensable government buildings, the development of the commercial section of the city was left to private enterprise. In Dalny they do things differently. Here, a few architects, following their own discretion with almost unlimited funds—at any rate, funds abundantly allocated for the task—are building a completely new city, to which a certain destiny is imparted and a certain opportunity offered from the outset by the railway,

[Illustration: The official development plan of Dalny (p. 76).]

to expand and develop profitably in a specific direction. Certainly a task worthy of the sweat of the noblest, on which many an architect and many a local politician would enthusiastically collaborate as a rarely granted opportunity to test the power of their theories in reality.

The father of this bold speculation is the Minister of Finance, Witte. Out here he is regarded as the most capable Russian minister, and especially for East Asia as the "man who has arrived." At any rate, {p. 76} Russia owes a large part of its East Asian influence to him; Pokotilov, the director of the Russo-Chinese Bank in Beijing, is his political agent, and it is an open secret that he works and accomplishes more for Russia than the envoy himself. As for Dalny, it is, so to speak, the clasp on the long chain of Russian successes in East Asia, to which is attached the anchor with which Russia secures its commercial interests in the ice-free ocean. The great Asian landmass bounded by the colors of the Russian Empire is surrounded by the ice of the ocean for half the year. Consequently, it is a vital question for Russia to create for itself, in one way or another, a permanent connection to the open sea, if the Trans-Siberian Railway is not to lie fallow for transit traffic for half a year. And that is why Russia does not run its Trans-Siberian Railway to Vladivostok, as had originally been planned, but instead seeks a place further south, on the permanently open sea. Originally, Talienwan was chosen for this. But it proved too exposed to winds and swells. Consequently, they moved to the opposite shore and established a city in the open field, which they named Dalny. Once the city is finished, the merchants and non-military personnel from Port Arthur will be settled in Dalny. In Russia such things are done *par ordre de mufti*. Instead of Port Arthur, Dalny will then be pushed all the more vigorously as a commercial center. And when one knows this and considers the efforts Russia is making with Dalny, one must admit that Alekseyev spoke the absolute truth when he assured the merchants of Newchwang that Russia would never offer Newchwang competition with Port Arthur. But he concealed from them that this would happen with Dalny. Such is what one calls diplomacy. Work on Dalny's construction has been underway for only two years. On the site where the city already partially rises now, and partially will rise in the future, there are two Chinese settlements, small fishing and farming villages. These were bought up, the houses torn down, and only the trees that characterize almost every village in China were left standing. They are to form the basis for two public parks provided for in the development plan. This development plan itself is quite interesting. It proceeds from the idea that Dalny is to become a maritime city. Consequently, the so-called center of the city lies on the water. The city itself is divided into four sharply separated quarters: the part nearest the water and the harbor is to become the so-called commercial town, where the offices of the firms are planned, the warehouses—in short, everything necessary for the life and bustle of a great commercial city. The railway is also to terminate in this commercial town, running, coming from the west, for a stretch along the edge of the harbor. The station itself fronts onto a large semicircular square that opens toward the sea.

On the side facing away from the harbor lies the so-called residential town. It is to form, as it were, the European "East" of the city—that is to say, the quarter where those people are to move who are employed in the commercial town, in the harbor, and so on; in short, the city of employees and the common folk. For the heads of the firms and for all those who can afford it, a city district in the west has been provided, which will be separated from the residential town by one of the future parks and has been christened the "villa town." {p. 77} Here the distinguished private residences are to be erected. It is thus assumed, after all, that the people living in Dalny will do such good business that they will soon be able to build villas. At the diametrically opposite end, the Chinese town, the "City," is to follow, separated from the European town by the other future park.

The commercial town will be separated from the residential town by a wide avenue. This street leads from the station in a straight line through the center of the city toward the

[Illustration: The future residential town in Dalny (p. 76).]

Chinese City. Approximately halfway along, a large circular plaza is planned, into which the main thoroughfares of the commercial town and the residential town feed. Around this circular plaza ten monumental buildings are to be grouped: namely, the Russo-Chinese Bank, the post and telegraph office, a theater, the police department and fire watch, the town hall, a clubhouse, the courthouse, and three additional private banks. One is evidently banking on tremendous commercial traffic, given that four large banks are provided for. In addition, further monumental buildings are planned in other squares: namely, an Orthodox cathedral, a Catholic church, an English church, a Protestant church, a museum, a government house, an administrative building, a large residential building for officials, a Central Hotel, a boys' and girls' school, a civic hall

{p. 78}

and an exchange, a market hall, and various other houses of worship and schools. To begin with, all of these buildings are to be erected at government expense. Added to these are the model designs for private residences and commercial buildings, the main thoroughfares—in short, the entire skeleton of the city, onto which private speculation is then to fatten the flesh. In addition, however, the construction plan provides for a relatively large government quarter, which is already nearing completion. This quarter is already a little town in its own right, where the approximately 2,000 Europeans working in Dalny reside.

At first glance, Dalny’s development plan has something captivating about it. It is the work of the brilliant director of the works, Government Architect Sakharov, who performs the functions of a chief engineer and thus at the same time occupies the position and rank of an absolute ruler in his district. At his side stands Mr. Timm, who is specifically in charge of the harbor construction. He is a Russian German by birth. The municipal organization work, especially the civil engineering, is under Engineer Gaedicke, whereas the architectural work is under a Pole named Skolimovsky, who has a technically accomplished and artistic assistant in the architect Junghändel, a Saxon.

When we arrive in Dalny, we still have about twenty minutes to walk before reaching the so-called government quarter, where Mr. Timm’s house is located. It is already around ten o'clock in the evening when we arrive there. Mr. Timm invites me into his house and goes immediately to the chief engineer to obtain permission for everything to be shown to me. In Russia, as is well known, everything that is not specifically permitted is forbidden. Permission is graciously and generously granted, and the chief engineer himself sends word that it would be his pleasure to guide me around personally tomorrow afternoon. Mr. Timm intends to devote the morning to me.

*     *     *

February 27.

And so it happens. Early in the morning, Mr. Timm and I ride across the terrain. We leave the government quarter with its numerous finished and half-finished houses and head toward the mountains that rise about two kilometers from the sea. When we have reached a vantage point high enough, we turn and now have the entire Gulf of Talienwan lying before us. Across the bay we see the houses of the town of the same name, behind which mountains rise once more, their peaks vanishing into the blue haze. Directly at our feet, however, lies the plain on which Dalny is to arise. There is indeed something sublime in the thought that in ten years a great city is to lie here, a metropolis in the true sense of the word, from which one will be able to cross Asia in eleven days to set foot once more on home soil in Berlin or elsewhere in Europe. Perhaps San Francisco once looked like this too, perhaps New York as well. Up there one can freely let one’s imagination roam and populate the vast expanse below with people of all nations, the harbor with ships, the quays with workers, and the planned streets with houses, {p. 79} above which the spires and domes of the churches and the roofs and battlements of the monumental buildings will tower. In reality, one now sees lying before oneself only the small, modern government town built in the Swiss villa style, alongside which the actual metropolis of Dalny has yet to rise. One sees the dredgers at work in the harbor and the large cranes turning and swiveling with enormous blocks of concrete that are sunk into the sea to form the side walls of the quays. We ride back down and inspect the

[Illustration: Houses for officials under construction in Dalny (p. 80).]

harbor works, which are under Mr. Timm’s particular direction. Long rows of these huge concrete blocks that are to be sunk into the depths are stacked by the water. Elsewhere, workers are busy building an embankment out into the sea. Later on, once it has become a mole, passenger and cargo steamers will dock along this embankment. Out there in the sea lie large dredgers scooping out the seabed to make the harbor navigable for the world’s greatest colossal ships. Further out in the water, rubble is piled upon rubble to construct a breakwater to protect the entrance to the harbor against the onslaught of the waves.

{p. 80}

Further along we come to a spot where sand is being poured into the sea and a basin is being built for smaller cargo steamers and for boats. A little further still, we see the preliminary work for a small dock. Where a peninsula juts out into the sea, thereby forming a natural small basin, work is underway staking out a large dock—a dock in which the largest ships in the world can be docked, even if they were to grow larger still. On the spit of land itself, we already find completed a good number of workshops, foundries, turning shops, steam hammers—in short, everything needed for machine building and shipbuilding. Mr. Timm's saloon carriage, in which we traveled together to Dalny, was built in these workshops. All along the harbor basin, however, run tracks on which the massive cranes and working machinery move, and behind them stand sheds upon sheds filled with building materials, tools, etc. Just how immense the dimensions are on which work is conducted here can be seen from the following charming episode, which possesses the virtue of truth. When work began on carrying out the plans envisioned for Dalny, cement emerged as one of the largest expenditure factors. It was an enormous quantity that was needed. A competitive tender was then opened for the supply. One factory, in cooperation with others, was in a position to undertake the deliveries. But the price demanded per ton seemed too high; the factory, however, declared that it could not supply it any cheaper. Thereupon, the following course of action was taken. The rumor was spread that excellent limestone had been discovered near Dalny and that they now intended to set up their own cement factory. But the factory remained unyielding. The game thus had to be played out to the end. In fact, limestone was present, but it was of little use for this purpose and even less sufficient in quantity. Nevertheless, the Russians ordered the machinery necessary for setting up a cement factory, and since the large cement factory naturally had its spies as well, they were forced actually to erect the machines and begin producing cement. All of this together required an expenditure of several hundred thousand rubles. Only when the large cement syndicate saw that work had actually commenced did it yield and quote a lower price. Thereupon, their own cement manufacturing was shut down again, and a contract was concluded with the large factory. If, then, several hundred thousand rubles need not be spared merely to force a factory into a small price reduction, and if, despite the loss of those several hundred thousand rubles, a substantial gain still results for the client on this contract, what colossal quantities must be needed!

At midday I am invited to lunch at the home of Chief Engineer Sakharov, who has built himself a house that is less imposing on the outside, but appointed with all the greater luxury within. The entire conversation revolves around nothing other than Dalny and its future. High-flying plans, presented with enthusiasm, are what I get to hear, and my doubts must strike their sails before the triumphant confidence conjured up by the optimism with which the artist believes in the realization of his plans.

Then the chief engineer leads me through the completed part of Dalny; we inspect the new hospital, then he shows me houses for lower and higher officials, workers' houses, {p. 81} which are intended to meet the demands of modern hygiene and social policy; each family occupies a little house of three rooms, a kitchen, and a cellar; each group of houses situated around a large courtyard has an ice cellar; every dwelling has its own small yard and garden; in the corner apartments of the side wings of the horseshoe-shaped housing complexes reside subordinate officials charged with supervising the entire complex.

In the government quarter there is also a small hotel, furnished by the state right down to the napkins and leased out at a moderate price; furthermore, there is a small clubhouse for the officials, a large public garden with a bowling alley, then a barrack-like building for unmarried officials, which on the inside, however, is furnished quite comfortably, and a large number of Chinese houses built with more than superfluous luxury. The Swiss chalet style predominates.

Dalny is to become a free port, Sakharov tells me, and the railway freight rates are to be set so cheaply that they can defy any competition from maritime shipping. Both in land sales and in the leasing or selling {p. 82} of buildings, the most extensive accommodations and support will be granted to all interested parties without distinction of nationality, and everything possible will be done to interest the world, and especially the East, in Dalny. Fast steamship lines, connecting with the through trains, are to link traffic with the cities of China and Japan; in short, they want to make Dalny, as it were, the center of the Far East. And that is why Russia needs a secure railway link through Manchuria to Siberia. Hence Russia's efforts in Manchuria, hence the struggle for an open port, and hence already now the sealing off of Vladivostok through the new import tariff! Dalny is merely the crowning of a pet plan long cherished by Russia. Around Dalny there will perhaps entwine, within the next ten years, a wreath of fears and hopes of all those whose pocketbooks are invested in East Asian affairs. And one can harbor no doubt that, given the energy with which Russia pursues its East Asian plans, it will also succeed in making Dalny the focal point of *its* East Asian interests, perhaps even the focal point of East Asian interests in general. But because matters may turn out this way, it is necessary that we draw little, unknown Dalny somewhat out of the obscurity of its provisional existence.

Now, in conclusion, one more word that must also receive its due. Even if we are alarmed by Russia's advances in Manchuria, even if we are privately compelled reproachfully to remind Russia of the dubious role it has played out here over the past six months and must hold up a mirror to it, even if we may eventually have to be armed to call a halt to Russia where it goes too far, we must nevertheless not fail to recognize on the other hand that the center of gravity of general East Asian interests will be shifted to the north should Russia's future Manchurian plans regarding Dalny materialize. For us Germans, however, this holds three advantages: first, that Shandong—*our* Shandong—is brought closer to this center. Second, experience has shown that, of all nations, Germans have hitherto found it easiest to gain a firm foothold and do business in those places where Russia has permitted competition among nationals of different countries at all; and finally, third, perhaps an even more important question than the partition of China is the opening up of Siberia—fertile, virgin Siberia, which unfortunately possesses the regrettable trait of being almost less known than China. Through the opening of the Trans-Siberian Railway, Siberia will in a certain sense become the hinterland of Dalny. This, to be sure, is music of the future. But music of the future that will perhaps not take very long to become popular. This, too, we must bear in mind if we wish to form a judgment on the Manchurian question and do not want to expose ourselves to the reproach that our judgment is one-sided.

{p. 83}

28 February (on the high seas).

For the present, my Manchurian travel program must be regarded as concluded. The campaign in Zhili is not yet over, and I must be prepared for the possibility that major new events might once again summon me to the theater of war. At present, however, things do not yet look that way. Nevertheless, I must expedite my departure for the sole reason that I must lose no time in dispatching some important news home from Chefoo.

[Illustration: Residential houses intended for Chinese in Dalny (p. 81).]

The passenger train to Port Arthur departs Dalny at four o'clock in the morning. As it is usually very crowded, on the advice of newly made friends in Dalny I head to the railway station late in the evening, board a compartment of the waiting train, and lie down there to sleep. To be sure, my rest lasts only until the arrival of the numerous other passengers, who soon leave me with nothing more than a corner in which I may try to let my tired eyes close from time to time. Yet the company prattles and chatters in such a chaos of sounds that sleeping is quite impossible. I have the dubious pleasure of having, directly opposite me, an elderly Russian {p. 84} lady with her roughly seventeen-year-old son take their seats. The two seem to take the liveliest interest in humor and poetry; at any rate, the young gentleman continually reads aloud poems from a Russian book, and the old lady bursts into fits of laughter from time to time. I feel a certain envy toward my Russian colleague and momentarily regret keenly that I am not a Russian author. Meanwhile, this regret soon gives way to annoyance at the perpetual disturbance, and in the end I inwardly curse the Russian poet who continually jolts me from my sleep through the effect of his works. After a journey that seems longer to me than the entire trip from Newchwang to Port Arthur, I finally arrive back in Port Arthur in the morning, unwashed, covered in dust, and with aching eyes. I hail an izvozchik, who takes me to the residence of my host, where I first attempt to revive my slumbering nerves with a cold-water rubdown; then I head to the Bund to purchase tickets for myself and my good Chinese servant for the crossing to Chefoo, and make several farewell visits in haste. The steamer is scheduled to put to sea in the evening. The connection with Chefoo is fairly regular; a ship departs almost every evening. One goes on board at nightfall, and when one wakes up the next morning and looks out the cabin window, the pleasant harbor scene of Chefoo already comes into view. One more zakuska, one more merry farewell toast—which people in Port Arthur also know how to appreciate—and then I bid farewell to my friends in Manchuria and to Manchuria itself, with the silent hope in my heart of seeing them again in the course of this campaign, only to head north, with Alekseyev's recommendations in my pocket, and thus reach the Trans-Siberian Railway.

The steamer on which I travel belongs to an English company and is anything but elegant: an old paddle steamer that seems originally to have been built for river navigation! At first I have a quite acceptable cabin berth, but later I have to give it up because a lady comes on board who relieves me of the possession of my cabin. The captain, however, has a camp bed set up for me in the dining saloon, and on this I gently fall asleep, only to awake on the morning of 1 March in the roadstead of Chefoo. Manchuria lies behind me; connection with the world has been regained. In Chefoo there are newspapers once again—yes, newspapers! I believe the last one I read was an issue of the *Ostasiatischer Lloyd* [*East Asian Lloyd*] that still bore the date of the year 1900.

{p. 85}

[Illustration: Dredgers in the Dalny roadstead (p. 79).]

## Chapter V. Russia and Manchuria.

Chefoo, March 1–8.

I have positively studied the newspapers in order to reconnect myself with events on the world stage. In Zhili all is quiet for the present, nor have any military events occurred during my Manchurian expedition—at least none of any consequence. I have therefore missed nothing, and in all likelihood will miss nothing if I sit quietly here for a while longer. The passport promised by Vice-Admiral Alexeyev is also taking its time. So I have leisure. From the dispatches that have arrived from home, I see that not too much of novelty has happened there either. The Chinese Question seems to have disappeared from the center of interest; people are only still arguing back and forth over whether a Russian-Chinese agreement regarding Manchuria exists or not. From Russian semi-official quarters the existence of such a treaty is flatly denied. Dr. Morrison, on the other hand, stands firm on it. And that means something. For his political feelers in Peking reach into the European legations just as much as into the Chinese yamens; he can afford to keep a whole staff of Chinese political researchers, and is therefore usually very reliable and credible in Chinese affairs.

During my stay in Newchwang, in-the-know Russian officials spoke quite openly to me about the agreement, which was treated as a done deal. At that time, people still viewed {p. 86} the matter as fairly harmless; they did not yet know that the opposition of the other powers with interests in China would after all become serious. In the meantime, the new German Reich Chancellor, evidently to throw a morsel into the squawking beaks of the fanatical Russia-worshipers back home, has declared that the Anglo-German Agreement does *not* apply to Manchuria. At least, that is the dispatch received by the *Ostasiatischer Lloyd* [East Asian Lloyd]—a paper strongly pro-Russian and, in recent times, ever since it began receiving a substantial imperial subsidy for direct German telegrams from home, turned thoroughly consul-general-esque. In curious contradiction to this, however, stands the report that the British Foreign Secretary is said to have declared in the House of Commons that the Anglo-German Agreement does apply to Manchuria after all, which is also what one would assume from its wording. The situation is thus more blurred today than at the time when Morrison first published the text of the agreement.

It is actually incorrect to speak of an agreement that relates *only* to Manchuria. Article 8 of the treaty, according to Morrison's version, is said to read: “Without the consent of Russia, China may not grant to any other power, or to the subject of such, any mining, railway, or other rights in the Russian-Chinese border provinces, namely in Manchuria, Mongolia, and Turkestan (Kashgar, Yarkand, and Khotan). Nor may China itself build any railways in these provinces without the consent of Russia. Finally, outside Newchwang, no land in these provinces may be leased to a subject of any other nation.”

In the long run it becomes tedious to repeat that our knowledge and conceptions of China are the most inadequate imaginable. Nevertheless, at this point I must once again get to the bottom of an error that I have encountered both in the newspapers back home and out here in the discussion of these questions. Namely, the notion that these borderlands are vassal states of China, and as such are supposedly not part of the fabric of the Chinese Empire at all. They certainly do belong to it, in part even extraordinarily firmly! Manchuria in particular, as the ancestral homeland of the ruling dynasty, is linked with China in the closest manner. According to ancient custom, it is from Manchuria that the Chinese Banner troops are recruited, as well as their Banner generals—some of the most influential figures in the great Chinese Empire! Who on earth coined the foolish notion that Manchuria is a “vassal state”? On the contrary, it is the ancestral land, no less than Brandenburg is the ancestral land of the Kingdom of Prussia. To be sure—today we have a Kingdom of Prussia and no Kingdom of Brandenburg. But does that make the former political Brandenburg suddenly a vassal state of Prussia? Likewise, we have an empire that is no longer called “Manchuria,” but “China.” It happens very often in world history that a conqueror ascends the throne of a culturally superior empire and assumes its title, believing that the ancient name will lend him greater prestige. But did the German lands become vassal states of Rome when Charlemagne and his successors had themselves crowned Roman emperors? If the people who drafted the German-English Agreement had wanted to exclude Manchuria, well, then they could have {p. 87} said so. How untenable the excuse is that Manchuria does not really belong to China is shown by the published draft of the treaty itself, which codifies the *actual* rule of Russia not only in Manchuria, but also in the remaining Russian-Chinese borderlands.

The conditions in Turkestan are, of course, different for us than in Mongolia and Manchuria. Nevertheless, it is characteristic that Russia is permitted to sever from the torso of China a territory approximately the size of non-Russian Europe, and that the German press claims this does no harm, that they are merely "vassal states." We shall see where our policy of "laisser faire, laisser aller" toward Russia in East Asia will lead us. The answer to the question of whether the Russo-Chinese treaty has already received its ratification or not, or ever will receive it, is fundamentally quite immaterial. For it is no longer a question of whether this formality has been or will be fulfilled, but rather of the fact that Russia *is already de facto in the exercise of the rights demanded in the draft contract—has entered into these rights!*

Even the first article of the agreement contains a sheer sham provision: "Since the Emperor of Russia wishes to express his friendly feelings toward China, he agrees that Manchuria shall be completely restored to China, that the recent warlike events in that province shall be paid {p. 88} no further heed, and that the Chinese administration shall in every respect re-enter the status quo ante." What is that supposed to mean: "Manchuria is completely restored to China," when Russia is allowed to keep military forces permanently along the railway line; when China is not permitted to raise an army in Manchuria again until the railway is completed, and then only with the approval of Russia, which determines its strength; when even the Chinese police forces to be maintained in the province are subject to Russia's supreme control; when China is permitted to employ in the northern provinces no nationals of other states, but only Russians, as military instructors; when the local authorities on the Liaodong Peninsula are placed under Russia's strict control; when China is not permitted, without Russia's consent, to grant economic concessions of any kind to nationals of other states in the northern provinces, indeed not even the acquisition or leasing of land outside Newchwang; and when, though it is not in the treaty, but is how it is to be handled, Russia places the administration of the Chinese government officials under the supervision and direct control of Russians? It is obvious that under these circumstances the severance of Manchuria from China, even if not a formal one, is nonetheless a de facto one, and that it is a farce under such conditions to emphasize that Manchuria is being restored fully and entirely to China. This first article is evidently not calculated for China, but is intended to influence the foreign powers, vis-à-vis whom they wish to deny that Russia has annexed Manchuria. It would likewise be a farce if Russia were to withdraw its troops from Manchuria. In that case it would simply reinforce its railway guards accordingly, and that these are to be withdrawn has never been spoken of and never will be spoken of.

The system for acquiring Manchuria is, of course, an extraordinarily convenient and cheap one. China provides the experienced bureaucratic apparatus, pays all administrative costs out of its own pocket, and is liable with all its possessions for any damage inflicted on the Russians in any way in Manchuria, yet the country is governed exclusively by Russians and exclusively for Russians. With this, however, the provinces of Manchuria, Mongolia, and Chinese Turkestan are removed from the so-called Open Door policy, and in addition China is still liable for damage inflicted on Russia by Chinese within a part of Russia! What more could one ask for? A politically well-thought-out and profitable plan! Surely, however, in the face of these facts, one will no longer be able to close one's eyes to the insight that through such a separate treaty the economic strength of China is weakened in the most sensitive manner to the detriment of the country itself and of the other powers who deal with China purely on a commercial-policy basis. But that was precisely what the Anglo-German Agreement was intended to prevent!

If, however, Germany even today still maintains the just standpoint it originally adopted when it committed itself to the principle of preserving the economic and political status quo in China, then it is its duty and its right to lead the way with a resolute veto on this question as well, now that it has, after all, been appointed by the Powers as the executive agent out here. It is perhaps not widely known that before the {p. 89} proposal was made to entrust the supreme command to a German, Russia likewise already had a popular general in reserve in Kiev in the event that the Powers should agree upon a Russian. Yet the German Emperor’s proposal to appoint Count Waldersee commander-in-chief in China was by no means unwelcome to Russia. Now it no longer needed to concern itself with so-called general international interests and could pursue its own special interests. Yet now that Germany has indeed assumed political hegemony out here, it is its duty to assert it in the general interest. From the German standpoint, too, this is logical and necessary if one is serious about the catchphrase of “*Weltpolitik*.” The only state pursuing *Weltpolitik* out here has hitherto been Russia alone. All the other nations have done little serious work for themselves in Zhili, but Russia has made good use of its time. And even if it has had incomparably harder work in Manchuria than the others elsewhere, even if it is still not in a position today to pacify the province permanently (the Powers have not managed that in Zhili either), it is nevertheless working toward its goal—the very real goal of a great Russian-Asiatic world empire. East Asia for Russia! In a tiny span of time, the Tsarist Empire has covered a tremendous distance toward this goal; in this past year, it has pushed its giant bulk a good stretch further south. Where is this to lead? And will the European Powers not perhaps miss the right moment to call out an effective “Thus far and no further!” to this further advance? We are, it seems to me, well on our way to “missing” it.

Anyone, however, who might believe that Russia now has land enough and would of its own accord halt its steps on the path southward at natural obstacles and political boundaries would fall into a serious error. The importance of railways for the commercial opening of China is well known, but Russia has amply demonstrated to us that it translates the word “railway” as “possession of the land through which the line runs,” and that to it a railway is at the very least a political border that it can close whenever it chooses. In § 12 of the draft treaty, Russia demands from China a concession to build a trunk or branch line of the Manchurian railway to Peking or the Great Wall. As I have learned from a good source, the plan is this: from Shanhaiguan, where the Mukden or Newchwang–Shanhaiguan line—annexed by Russia and not yet returned—terminates, the railway is to run in a directly southwesterly direction toward Peking. Thereby, not only is the British Shanhaiguan–Tianjin–Peking line to be killed off, but competition is also to be created for the Kaiping coal mines situated along this route, namely by opening up coal deposits said to exist in the north of Zhili Province, northeast of Peking. A second line would then seek to connect with the Trans-Siberian Railway along the great tea route Kalgan–Kyakhta, etc., so that Russian railways would lead out from Peking in two directions. Russia goes still further, however, and specifically in a southerly direction. There exists an important railway concession from Peking to Hankou that was acquired by Belgians. It is said to be a fact that the Belgians obtained this concession through the aid of the French government, {p. 96} which in turn is said to have been put forward by Russia. This is talked about out here in informed circles as something quite well known. The result will be that in a few years Russia will have its locomotives whistling in Hankou, in which it takes an extraordinary interest on account of the tea trade. Just think: on the Yangtze, a sphere of interest claimed exclusively by Britain for many years, and recently half-conquered by Germany! Sincere admiration for Russia’s East Asian policy, and above all for its *spiritus rector*, Finance Minister Witte!

Yet in political dealings with Russia, one should not forget the lessons it has taught us in this eventful year. To anyone who has occupied himself somewhat more thoroughly with these matters, it must gradually have become clear that no power on earth possesses anything approaching the political influence and political prospects in China that Russia does. Russia knew this before the political turmoil began; it did for itself what it deemed necessary to do, and did so with its characteristic thoroughness and brutality. On the other hand, it knew just as well how to free itself at the right moment from the international cant about humanity and other empty platitudes, and thus no longer let itself be deceived about its true interests—just at the right moment to prevent the breach with China from becoming unbridgeable. Despite the catastrophes of Blagoveshchensk and Tanggu, despite the plundering by its soldiers, despite the looting of the holiest Chinese sanctuaries, the Russian has not antagonized China so deeply that he cannot turn back; on the contrary, he knows his adversary and knows that he commands immense prestige among the Chinese. Russia acted shrewdly from *its* standpoint when it abandoned the allies at a favorable hour and looked only to buttering its own bread. The perfidy of which it has thereby made itself guilty remains, of course, and cannot be undone by anything, but no more can its successes for Russian policy.

Thus I have unreservedly given expression to the view that forced itself upon me during my stay in Manchuria. Whether the message will be very welcome everywhere, I do not know, but I hope at least that it will not be dismissed out of hand without examination, even if one does not agree with it.

{p. [109]}

# Book 2. Through Siberia.

{p. 93}

[Illustration: Askold Island at the entrance to the harbor of Vladivostok.]

## Chapter I. By Ship to Vladivostok.

### 1. A Side Trip to South China.

(From a letter.)

Hong Kong, June 21, 1901.

. . . But now hear above all what has happened in the meantime. For since my last letter from Chefoo at the beginning of March, you have remained without news. Meanwhile, a great deal has occurred. So attend devoutly to my report, which runs as follows.

As you know, after my expedition through Manchuria I took an extended rest in Chefoo. I wanted to wait and see whether any world-shaking events were brewing on the theater of war. But since these were too long in coming, I used the time to join a new friend, the engineer Schauer, who was just about to set out on a minor expedition into the gold districts of northern Shandong—the first since the outbreak of the unrest. Schauer is an engineer of the German Society for Mining and Industry Abroad, and specifically "deals exclusively in gold." You will receive the detailed report\*) on this expedition by the next mail from Shanghai {p. 91}. I had returned to Chefoo only a few days before when, like a bolt out of the blue, a wire reached me reporting the death of my friend Ho Amei in Canton. You know that Ho Amei was a respected South Chinese merchant. He had prospected the provinces of Guangdong and Guangxi in all directions for precious ores and minerals, and himself held several mining concessions. Through his good offices, dating back to my visit in December 1899, I already owed one of the best South Chinese concessions, which we intended to exploit jointly. His son Ho Ke Yong, who was educated in my parents' house some fifteen years ago, informed me of his father's death with deep sorrow, and this news was important enough for me to arrange my travel plans accordingly at once. I chose the next steamer bound for the south and traveled on it, without touching Shanghai, directly via Amoy and Swatow to Hong Kong, arrived there at the end of March, and then proceeded to Canton to express my condolences to Ho Ke Yong and at the same time to look after my own interests. From then on, I was now in Hong Kong, now in Canton. In Hong Kong I met quite a number of dear old friends from Shanghai again, and in the small, but all the more convivial European colony in Canton I spent delightful hours. The memory of it brings before me above all the images of the amiable German consul Kallen; the interpreter of the German consulate, Lange, one of the foremost connoisseurs of China, who is soon to come to Chefoo as vice-consul; as well as the head of the firm Melchers & Co., Mr. Südhans. Above all, I like to think back on the happy hours in the home of Mr. Lange, whose wife is also to be thanked for the genuinely German spirit and cheerfulness that brought such an invigorating element into the thoroughly international character of Canton's social life.

::: notes
\*) This report has meanwhile appeared in the *Finanz-Chronik* [*Financial Chronicle*] published in London, specifically in Vol. VI, Nos. 37 (September 11, 1901) to 43 (September 25, 1901).
:::

But I could not bear sitting still in one place for long. Nor did I resign myself to doing so any longer than was strictly necessary to come to an arrangement with Ho Amei's legal successors and conclude some new contracts with Chinese parties. If you had ever been in the position of having to negotiate with Chinese or conclude contracts with them, and moreover with people who are generally not accustomed to doing business with Europeans, you might perhaps be able to appreciate what an achievement it represents that I was able, within the very first fortnight, to—bring a contract to completion. The deliberations always took place in a large conference, in which all members of the family down to the third and fourth degree in descending and ascending lines participated; and then, in the end, the contract still had to be submitted for appraisal to ever so many old friends of the family and ever so many wise men and old greybeards, each one of whom brought forward a new objection and yet another piece of foolishness that had to be refuted with a considerable display of glibness. Fortunately, Ho Ke Yong still speaks German reasonably well, so that communication posed few difficulties. The worst part was when the oral agreements were now to be codified, and that in both the Chinese and German languages. This was done by the Chinese first drafting a text in Chinese on their side in long private conferences, and I a text in German on mine. Both texts were then {p. 95} translated and compared with one another, merged, and ultimately a joint contract emerged, which received its final official seal of attestation at the German consulate. The superintendent of the Berlin Mission kindly assisted me with the final editing of my agreements with the Chinese.

Once my business affairs had thus been placed on a legal footing, Canton could hold me no longer, and I decided to spend some time looking around the interior of the country again. I therefore engaged Ho Amei’s former Chinese expert, a well-travelled man, as a guide, hired a Chinese junk, which I equipped as best I could with European necessities and above all provisioned well, and then set out on an extended expedition into the interior. Since long-distance travel in South China can be undertaken only by water and piracy is still quite in vogue, the consulate deemed it appropriate to notify the Viceroy of my travel plans. And indeed, on my travels I did not have to endure any harassment whatsoever. Wherever my junk appeared, flying from its mast the German flag given to me by the commander of the German gunboat *Jaguar*, which was anchored in the river off Canton, I was received with respect. Preparations had been made everywhere for my coming. The Viceroy had informed all authorities and instructed them to assist me in every way and to protect me. As soon as the flag came into view, emissaries duly appeared from station to station from the commanders of the numerous Chinese war junks that perform patrol duty on the rivers, as well as from the mandarins of the numerous towns I passed, to present me with their superiors' visiting cards and offer me a military escort, which, however, I declined in most cases. Very frequently the mandarins also appeared in person and repeatedly invited me to visit them in their name or lodge with them. I invariably declined this, however, and confined myself to entertaining the distinguished gentlemen with coffee or cocoa, which they sipped with relish, or at least to sending my visiting card to their residence via a boy in return for their visit. Since the mining districts of particular interest to me lie in the basin of the North River (the Beijiang), it was only natural that I made my way to these parts, and I chose them all the more gladly as the objective of this journey of mine because it was an area that, for the researcher—and particularly for one whose attention is directed more towards economic and transport-policy questions than geographical ones—was for the most part still virgin territory. I travelled up the North River as far as the mouth of its largest western tributary, the Lianjiang, then turned into it and followed its course as far as the district town of Lianshan, the transit port for overland traffic between the basin of the North River—that is to say, Guangdong Province—and the extraordinarily productive province of Hunan lying behind it. The journey proceeded rather slowly. Since the prevailing wind was generally against us and Chinese river junks cannot tack for lack of a keel, which is unusable given the often very low water level of South Chinese rivers, the crew had to haul the boat from shore along almost the entire route using a long rope fastened to the {p. 96} very top of the mast. I cannot describe to you here in detail the particulars of this interesting journey, and wish only to tell you very briefly that I reached the border of the Miao territories. These begin at the corner formed by the three provinces of Guangdong, Guangxi, and Hunan. By Miao are meant well-known remnants of the original Chinese population, who have preserved themselves in considerable purity in their customs, dress, hairstyle, etc., and in particular have not yet been subjugated politically by the Manchu dynasty. The people, for example, do not wear queues, but rather hair tied up high, which is indeed the original Chinese style, since the queue was only imposed upon the Chinese by the Manchus. There was no purpose in my penetrating further into the Miao territories, as my Chinese companion's linguistic knowledge failed him and I was also not adequately equipped for such an expedition. However, I have resolved to investigate these regions especially at a later date. That the yield will be worthwhile, I have no doubt. The scientific yield of even this journey was not insignificant; in particular regarding the economic and political aspect, I succeeded in making some interesting observations. From a purely geographical standpoint, too, the trip was very interesting. There one finds extensive hill country—one might almost say mountainous country—with extraordinarily characteristic landforms, lush meadows, forest, water, and romantic valleys of erosion reminiscent of parts of Saxon Switzerland. Geologically, too, there is much to be gained here, just as in South China generally numerous geographical problems still await their solution. As you know, Richthofen's *China* still requires completion with regard to South China*). Among other things, on the border of Guangdong and Guangxi I discovered a cave approximately 15 kilometres long, through which an underground river runs that is navigable for nearly half its course. I studied the Yuanshan district somewhat more thoroughly. It is very rich in ores of all kinds; in general, on the voyage one frequently comes across places where coal is found, some of it of surprisingly good quality. The region, as I have said, was still untouched by Europeans. In time, however, one will inevitably have to turn to studying the hinterland of Canton more closely if one does not wish to rely for one's knowledge exclusively on the statements of Chinese middlemen, who naturally have an interest in keeping trade with Europeans in their own hands.

Since 15 June I have been safely back, have settled my affairs in Canton and Hong Kong, and tomorrow I shall embark on board the English steamer *Sunda* to travel first to Shanghai and from there via Japan to Vladivostok.

Originally I had intended to return to Port Arthur and from there traverse Manchuria northward in order to reach the connection with the Trans-Siberian Railway by this route. Unfortunately, I still do not have in my hands the passport {p. 97} promised to me by Vice-Admiral Alexeyev. I am also convinced that he will not send it, and therefore I do not wish to make my further travel plans dependent on it. In order, however, to somewhat complete my knowledge of Manchuria nonetheless, I shall take another route: namely, from Vladivostok through the formerly Manchurian parts of Siberia, and then along the Amur on the border of Manchuria. There, too, I hope to be able to carry out some interesting studies.

::: notes
*) A work whose first part has begun to appear in the meantime (Tiessen, *China, das Reich der achtzehn Provinzen* [China, the Realm of the Eighteen Provinces]), claims to be considered a supplement and modernisation of Richthofen's book.
:::

### 2. Von Hongkong nach Nagasaki.

Shanghai, 29 June 1901.

I am ready to travel. The numerous farewell visits to friends and acquaintances have been paid. Today at noon I ate for the last time at the German Club. My heavy luggage has already been shipped from Hong Kong to Germany on a Norddeutscher Lloyd vessel. The few suitcases I have with me contain only the most essential travel necessities. One of them is filled to the brim with books about Siberia, which I bought partly in Max Nößler's German bookstore and partly in Kelly & Walsh's English bookstore. There were not very many English books about Siberia, and far fewer German ones. For the most part, the available works are purely scholarly in nature and deal with Russia's advance in Asia. Travelogues in the German language did not exist at all, and those available in English did not cover my route.

In the roadstead of Woosung, the port of Shanghai, lies anchored the Norddeutscher Lloyd steamer *Bayern*, which is to take me to Nagasaki. The Chinese "agent" of my hotel, the Astor House, is therefore instructed to have my luggage taken to the tender *Bremen*. At 6 o'clock in the afternoon I embark there myself, and an hour later I find myself in the port of Woosung and on board the Lloyd steamer *Bayern*. Alongside lies the steamer *Rhein*, on which I had previously travelled to Taku. This time she is on her homeward voyage and fully occupied with relieved sailors, who greet us with loud hurrahs. At around 10 o'clock at night the *Bayern* puts to sea. The large ship turns slowly out of the mouth of the Huangpu, from which navigational obstacles are soon to be removed, and is soon carried by the sluggish waves of the Yangtze. Here and there, ship lights and light buoys marking the fairway blink at us from right and left; then comes the lightship, where the Yangtze pilots are usually dropped off, and shortly before midnight we steer out into the open sea. China—farewell!

30 June.

We are sailing on the high seas in fairly pleasant weather. Two years ago this German postal line between China and Japan did not yet exist; it was only made possible in the autumn of 1899 through the merger of the two largest German steamer lines, the Norddeutscher Lloyd and the Hamburg-America Line.

{p. 98}

The progress of German shipping in East Asia was quite considerable during the past year as well. It is largely connected with the journey that the director-general of the Hamburg-America Line, Herr Ballin, undertook to East Asia. Even if negotiations concerning the purchase of the ships and facilities of a large English and an even larger Chinese coastal shipping company have not yet concluded, the fact remains that, as an initial result of Herr Ballin's visit, the so-called "Jebsen Line"—which handles traffic from Shanghai to Tsingtao, Chefoo, and Tientsin, and maintains a regular, subsidised postal steamer service between Tsingtao and Shanghai—has, insofar as it is a postal line, passed into the possession of the Hamburg-America Line. A second, one might well say truly magnificent success consists in the Hamburg-America Line establishing a regular passenger service beyond Japan to San Francisco, so that the globe is encircled by a distinguished German steamer line. The success is all the more significant as no steamers currently operate on this route that could even remotely compare with the steamers *Hamburg* and *Kiautschou*, which are scheduled to run here regularly. It is of great importance that this line is being established just now. For in East Asian circles it is reportedly known that the "Empress Line" intends to establish an express steamer service between San Francisco and Dalny concurrently with the opening of the Trans-Siberian Railway. Should the Siberian railway really generate such a rush of passengers as people seem to assume, it would be a small matter for the German postal line to include Dalny and Nagasaki in its passenger service beyond Tsingtao. Then the Shanghai–Dalny–Nagasaki triangle would be closed simply by inserting the Tsingtao–Dalny–Nagasaki route.

How seriously the two large German shipping companies intend to approach the cultivation of the East Asian field is also demonstrated by the fact that the two lines have jointly brought under their influence the *Ostasiatischer Lloyd* [East Asian Lloyd], which appears weekly in Shanghai and is the only German organ in the Far East—for the *Deutsch-Asiatische Warte* [German-Asian Watch] published in Tsingtao is now merely a subsidiary of the *Ostasiatischer Lloyd*.

Nagasaki, July 1.

The sailing time between Shanghai and Nagasaki is 36 hours; the *Bayern* adheres to it almost to the minute. We departed from Wusong around 10 o'clock on Saturday evening, and today, Monday, a few minutes before 10 o'clock in the morning, the steamer casts anchor in Nagasaki harbor. Punctuality is the greatest virtue of our German mail steamers. When I think of my voyage from Hong Kong to Shanghai on the English mail steamer *Sunda* (P. and O.), I appreciate this virtue all the more. The departure time in Hong Kong was initially set for 4 o'clock in the afternoon; all passengers are on board, awaiting the signal of the steam whistle. Then, a quarter of an hour before 4 o'clock, the chief steward arrives and announces that the steamer will not sail until 4 o'clock in the morning. After three days we arrived at the mouth of the Yangtze. That was at 6 o'clock in the afternoon. Had we not lost twelve hours in {p. 99} Hong Kong, we would have been able to proceed into Wusong and Shanghai that very day. As it was, however, we could not head for Wusong until the following morning. And so we did sail a short way at daybreak, but then the first-class English mail steamer managed to idle about on the Yangtze until 5 o'clock in the afternoon because its agency had not procured a pilot in good time. And this despite the fact that the agency had known for nearly twelve hours, by telegram from the Gützlaff lighthouse, that the steamer had arrived. That was indeed a bit too much even for the Englishmen on board, and they declared that they intended to publish the fact in one of the "Shanghai Newspapers." Later, however, I looked in vain through all the "Shanghai Newspapers" for any notice of the incident. The Englishman is simply too well schooled in patriotism to trumpet abroad things so detrimental to his fatherland.

Nagasaki is new to me. On a trip to Japan that I took a year and a half ago, I did not touch at this important place, the Japanese Hamburg. The harbor itself is wonderfully sheltered; it is a basin neither too large nor too small which, enclosed on all sides by hills, affords secure protection against wind and weather. The harbor is a naval port, and the hills enclosing it are heavily and skillfully fortified.

Nagasaki is, as mentioned, the Japanese Hamburg—at least, so it presents itself to the observer privileged to stay there for only a short time. It is, as it were, the junction of the shipping lines connecting Asia and Europe with Japan, and across the Pacific Ocean with America. To be sure, Russia carries by far the largest transport of goods and passengers from "Russia in Europe" to "Russia in East Asia" by means of the steamers of the Russian so-called Volunteer Fleet. These are indeed private steamers, but they are all constructed in such a way that they can be used as auxiliary cruisers, and they maintain close ties with the government in other respects as well. This is evident if only from the fact that the company is obliged to employ a certain number of Russian naval pensioners as officials. But this traffic hardly enters into consideration for communication between southern and northern East Asia, since the steamers of the Volunteer Fleet, on their way from Odessa to Vladivostok, call almost without exception at no East Asian ports other than Singapore, and then proceed immediately to Port Arthur. The steamers require an average of two months for the entire voyage, and are therefore virtually unused except by Russians.

A large share of the communication within East Asia, and especially with the north, is handled by the great Japanese mail shipping line, the Nippon Yūsen Kaisha—parenthetically, the most heavily state-subsidized line in the world in relative terms. It maintains the principal traffic between Japan on the one hand and Shanghai, Chefoo, Port Arthur, Newchwang, the Korean ports, and Vladivostok on the other. For all these lines, Nagasaki is the true terminal port. Nor must Manila be forgotten in this enumeration, communicating no less with Nagasaki than with Hong Kong.

{p. 100}

Lying in the harbor are three large French battleships, one American, and several Russian warships.

The foreign settlement in Nagasaki is small, and Nagasaki's overseas trade corresponds to it. The relatively largest business probably consists in coal. This is found nearby and brought right up to the harbor basin by rail. As a result, the price can be set fairly low. To be sure, Japanese coal cannot be compared in quality to Cardiff coal; it is said to be about 30 percent lower in value than the latter, which means that for the same work performance, approximately 30 percent more Japanese coal is consumed. In spite of all this, it still turns out to be cheaper than English coal, and it therefore dominates the East Asian market. In any case, it is qualitatively to this day still the best East Asian coal. Hankow coal was indeed praised to me from various quarters as being better, but for the time being it is not yet a factor in the East Asian market, since the mines near Hankow, which are in Chinese hands, have not hitherto been in a position to produce enough. Then there is a large French coal mine in Hongay (Tonkin), belonging to the "Société des charbonnages du Tonkin." However, the coal extracted here first requires special processing before it is usable for general shipping needs. It is now pressed into briquettes. And even then, it is said to require special grates and a special manner of handling during combustion. In spite of substantial support from the French government, the company has operated at a deficit until now, and has only turned the corner somewhat through the energetic efforts of a new director, a Swiss.
