As to the value of our Shandong coal, absolute certainty has not yet been reached. Rumor has it that the coal is not exactly the best in quality, but compensates for this disadvantage through its quantity.

As soon as our foot has touched Japanese soil, a friendly man steps up to us, his legs clad in long, dark-blue, tight-fitting, jersey-like trousers, to which the sock with the separated big toe is directly joined, his chest covered by a loose blue jacket. He politely takes off his cap, which turned out a bit tall for his small head and is modeled in cut after our soldiers' caps, makes his bow, and politely invites us to take a seat in the jinrikisha he pulls. There one sees at once: order and culture prevail here! In China, ten fellows with their vehicles would immediately have formed a veritable wagon fort around us, and with laughing faces would have invited us to take a seat by uttering a terribly coarse-sounding, but as inarticulate as possible "Hey!", until we would have had to bring our little walking stick into play, which in such cases very often renders good service as a little interpreter. But here everything proceeds orderly and politely. The jinrikishas are drawn up in rows, like the cabs back home; no more rickshaws pull up than are needed; the coolie moves deliberately, not too fast and not too slow, and then he trots through the streets of Nagasaki. The first impression is an extremely civilized one—that is, if one comes from China. This impression is moderated just a little when one rides through the Japanese streets and encounters half a dozen strapping young Japanese women, one of whom, with great nonchalance, straightens her disarranged {p. 101} dress. This mixture of a superficially adopted culture with Malayo-Mongolian essence, called Japanese, meets us at every turn. Eminent experts on Japan have expressed themselves quite extensively on this question and therefore spare me doing the same here. A traveler passing through should accept conditions with less criticism than one who intends to concern himself especially with a country. But writing a book about Japan is not my intention. Hence, only a few notes here!

If one has no guidebook, Baedeker, or similar books, then one does best to entrust oneself to one's rickshaw coolie. He has his particular route along which he takes travelers. So "go on!" First we ride through the foreign settlement. One half of it consists of the offices of commercial houses and banks, the other half of sailors' pubs, which often, by their very appearance, constitute the grossest violation of good taste. First of all, there is usually a beauty standing in front of the door, whose greatest beauty is mostly her well-fed figure. She belongs to the general category of international rabble, speaks a bit of just about every language, and possesses such knowledge of human nature that she will never address a German other than in German, a Frenchman always in French, an Englishman or American always in English. Where her cradle stood is mostly obscure, usually no doubt in Galicia or Romania, to judge at least by the German she speaks, and Patriarch Abraham certainly did his part in his day to make possible the existence of this painted creature in a dressing gown, who calls out to you: "Please, gentlemen, come on in!" One of my fellow travelers from the steamer seems to subscribe to the maxim: "if evil boys entice thee, follow them not, but go before them." At any rate, he is the first to follow this enticement, even if the boy here is a maiden—an imperishable one, to be sure, at least forty-five years old already! In for a penny, in for a pound! So we have to go inside with him into this noble hotel. I do not know whether I shall still recall all the names written outside on the signs: "Deutsches Gasthaus," "Oriental Hotel," "American Bar," "Hotel de France," "Taverna italiana," "Rossija Gostinniza"—I believe those are all of them. So we, too, order a whisky and soda. Just as we have taken the first sip, the beauty appears with the sweetest face in the world and asks whether we would not like to drink wine... Pay the bill—post-haste! and faster than we came in, we are already outside again, not without having left behind an outrageous sum for our whisky and soda.

Such taverns offer, one might say, the reverse side of European Nagasaki. A good and distinguished hotel, by the way, can be recommended: the Nagasaki Hotel, where I found food exceptionally good by East Asian standards.

Now it is the turn of Japanese pleasures. Our gentlemen rickshaw coolies drive us through the city, always between the dainty Japanese shops where one can buy the most charming Japanese wares, so pretty and so cheap. Naturally, they drive us to a temple. This craze must have been taught to them by the Americans. The Americans run to every temple they see or that is listed in their “Guide Book”. And they are {p. 102} all in there! But once you have seen a few Buddhist temples, you know exactly what the others look like. To be sure, the temple we visited is very beautifully situated high above the city, and one has a lovely view of the harbour and the roofs of the town, especially the latter.

We climb back down the hill that we had had to climb up to the temple. From this physical exertion one of our party has grown thirsty, and so we follow once more the enticements of smiling sirens, Japanese ones this time. Right where the long flight of stairs leads up to the temple hill, a Japanese teahouse sign beckons above, and a Japanese tea girl below. We step inside through the doorway, which is lightly closed off by long hanging Japanese beaded curtains made of little pieces of bamboo, into the room. Here everything is furnished in the Japanese style. We are led up the stairs; up here stand a European table and European chairs. On these we sit down. Very little is said; we look at each other and await the things to come. The milieu of the “geisha”! First, several maids appear with fans. Each of them fans everyone, and since we cannot answer their Japanese questions, we fan the little Japanese women right back in reply. Then we order. We ask for “Tansan”, a Japanese mineral water. That is understood, but it is also the only Japanese word that we understand. Yet no, one more: “Geisha”! This word has now been uttered as well; eyes light up—we are going to see a real geisha! My companions do not yet know the sight of such an enchanting creature and are full of suspense. Evidently they are expecting a beauty who will sing “The Goldfish Pond” to them in Japanese in melting tones, or “I am the little geisha of Japan”, and ask them for lessons in kissing. So they want to hear a geisha. She is sent for; every respectable teahouse has its house geisha, as it were, who is always available. And appear she does, but faces grow longer and longer. This geisha has already been past thirty for several years! To be sure, she must once have been beautiful, at least so one of my companions consoles himself—the one who would rather go ahead of the wicked boys. Then she begins to play on a mandolin-like instrument. That already sounds a little nicer. Then, however, she begins to sing, and scores a resounding success—of the most negative kind, to be sure. Her forte is screeching, her piano squeaking; only her falsetto is nature. For she sings only in falsetto. At this the guests turn away in horror; only that one companion of mine, who simply lives deeply in illusions, remarks that while she had not sung beautifully, she was nevertheless interesting in herself, and presses a yen into her hand as a token of his appreciation—something he will not do again later! When, however, he is then asked to fork out another yen for the single bottle of Tansan, which is otherwise to be had everywhere for twenty cents at most, even he begins to grumble. In the end he remarks that he had imagined a real geisha rather differently after all.

Now, however, the rickshaw coolies have caught on. Seeing our predilection for such establishments, whose chief characteristic is that one is fleeced beyond all measure, they know quite a number more of them. For they get a percentage there. And so it is not long before they stop again before an establishment said to house geishas. We {p. 108} wave them off. After a little while they stop again and turn round with the meaningful word: “Geisha”! Again we wave them off; they trot on, but after a little while they stop once more and whisper with a wink: “Geisha”! It truly seemed as though in all Nagasaki there were no sights to be seen other than geishas. In the face of such persistence others too would eventually have relented, and so the companion who always likes to lead the way declares that the geisha we had seen had been an old screw, and perhaps the young geishas would be nicer. I have no further inclination to chase after these geisha adventures—which are, after all, interesting enough for newcomers—and take my leave, as I also have some more serious business to attend to. In the evening I meet the jolly company again; they are sorely disappointed by the first impression Japan has made on them. Indeed, much foolish stuff has been produced in the way of infatuation with Japan, and it is no wonder that this takes its revenge. I, too, must confess that the first impression I gained a year earlier during my first stay in Japan likewise fell, in every respect, far short of what I had imagined of Japan and its inhabitants, and it was not exactly softened on subsequent visits. One expects to find a civilized people and finds a typical Asian people, who so far have copied little more of what we call civilization than throat-clearing and spitting. This applies, after all, to all aspects of life. For the thoroughbred European, however, it stands out as downright disagreeable in what the Englishman refers to simply as “Japanese life”. One must have reached a high degree of resignation to find pleasure in it in the long run.

I have the choice of whether to travel on further to Yokohama on the *Bayern*, or directly to Vladivostok on a coastal steamer. My ticket is made out for Yokohama. My inquiries reveal, however, that a steamer leaves for Vladivostok as early as the following day, and that the next steamer, belonging to the Nippon Yusen Kaisha, will not depart until the second half of July. To be sure, if I chose this steamer, I would get to know several Korean ports, but then the stay in Japan, which would be of little benefit for my purposes, would cost me more than two weeks, which I would rather not spend on it, because I want to be back home by the beginning of September at the latest.

Thus, at the Lloyd agency—which here, to be sure, goes under the name of the German-Japan firm Ahrend & Co., but is managed by the staff of the branch of the great Vladivostok house of Kunst & Albers—I purchase a ticket to Vladivostok for the German steamer *Daphne*. This belongs to the “Ostasiatische Handelsgesellschaft” [East Asian Trading Company], but has already been chartered for years by the firm of Kunst & Albers. The ticket costs forty yen. The yen is the Japanese circulating coin; it generally stands at par with the Mexican dollar. As is well known, Japan has a gold standard, and the yen is therefore properly speaking a token coin. However, the gold coin dates only from recent times, and since Japan previously had a silver currency, it comes about {p. 101} that the silver yen also represents roughly the true monetary value. Japan has had a peculiar experience with its types of money. When the gold standard was introduced, the gold coins were made with too high a fineness. In the period after the introduction of the gold coin, one received gold everywhere, but soon the banks systematically held back the gold coins and sold them to Europe at a good profit because of their high fineness. Thus it comes about that at present one can get hold of gold coins in Japan just as little as before the introduction of the gold standard. On the other hand, it is quite interesting how Japan manipulates the paper yen in Korea, and in fact an interesting economic struggle is taking place here between the paper yen and the paper ruble. As is well known, Korea is disputed territory between Japan and Russia. Through the skillful exploitation of Korea’s domestic political divisions, both powers have managed, with varying success, to secure their influence in the island empire.

Initially, Japanese influence was predominant, until the day of the assassination of the Korean empress—which to this day has not been fully cleared up—in consequence of which the emperor fled to the Russian legation. It is from that time that an agreement dates between Russia and Japan, according to which both states wish to assert their influence equally *). Naturally, this is only a truce, and it is probably beyond all doubt that the Korean question can be resolved in no other way than by force of arms. At least among the Russians, this view is universal. In the meantime, however, each is preparing the ground as well as possible. In this, money plays a very special role, specifically paper money. It is well known that the paper money issued by a state is nothing other than a promissory note for a specific sum, which under certain conditions must be paid in cash from the state treasury in gold, and in silver-standard countries in silver. The financial standing of a country naturally depends not on the quantity of paper money issued, but on the sum of gold or silver that guarantees the value of the paper. Indeed, the wealth of a country always consists solely in its reserve of monetary metal. A very simple example from India will suffice to make clear how a battle can now also be fought out with paper currency. India, as is well known, consists of a considerable number of independent principalities whose sovereigns administer their country autonomously, albeit under the control of an English resident minister. The danger that India, or at least one or another of these in part quite powerful princes, will one day revolt against English rule has by no means been banished. India is by no means a secure possession; a skillfully led uprising could overthrow the entire rule of England, whose chief weakness consists in the fact that the greater part of its military forces in India is recruited from Indian native tribes. Now, the Indian princes are for the most part very rich, and specifically in cash gold. England’s policy was therefore, and still is today, aimed at persuading the Indian princes either to deposit their money in English banks or at least to exchange it for English treasury notes. The result in both {p. 105} cases is the same. In the event of an uprising, England has the princes in its grip insofar as England is then in possession of what is truly the only valuable money, whereas the princes, with their bundles of English treasury notes, have little more in hand than a packet of paper. The more foreign paper money a country buys, therefore, the more dependent it becomes on the state whose paper it takes. In private commerce, this dependence then manifests itself, in the worst case, in the buyers losing their money—compare Portugal and Greece. In Korea, this principle is now being exploited politically just as in India; Russia as well as Japan takes care to withdraw as much cash money from the country as possible and to place its treasury notes there instead. If war should ever break out between Japan and Russia, the question of which of these two powers has the most paper money circulating in the country will also be of great importance for Korea’s stance in this conflict. Russia is pursuing precisely the same principle in Manchuria through the Russo-Chinese Bank, and its successes in this sphere are enormous. When I traveled through Manchuria, the Chinese—despite the uprising that was then still very much en vogue—were already taking Russian paper money with great preference, from Shanhaiguan on.

::: notes
*) At present, Japan seems to have the upper hand once again.
:::

What interests me in particular about the yen right now is the circumstance that mine are running out. I must therefore betake myself once again to a bank and change money. This activity always fills me with dread, because I know in advance that I will get annoyed, and that is something one generally avoids in the East. The annoyance, however, stems from the gruesome way one gets one’s throat cut by the banks when changing money. For this is how it is done.

First, the method of exchange customary with us! Suppose I come from France, have superfluous francs, and need English money for a journey to England to be undertaken shortly. Then I consult the exchange list, look up how many francs go to a pound sterling, take my francs, carry them to the banking house, and ask whether the firm will change my francs into pounds at the day’s rate, which it will gladly do. The banker deducts perhaps a quarter or at most, at the very most half a percent in exchange fees, and the matter is settled.

Now the Asian method. I have Mexican dollars that I want changed into roubles. The Mexican dollar is the currency in common circulation in China. Any sensible person would now think that the bank would fix an exchange rate between the Mexican dollar and the rouble. But by no means—the bank would not earn enough that way! So it slips an intermediate currency in between and first calculates the difference between the Mexican dollar and this intermediate link. (The exchange rate, of course, already contains a handsome profit for the bank.) Only then does it calculate the rate between the sum already converted into the intermediate rate and the roubles due on that sum. In that is contained the second high exchange profit. In Shanghai this intermediate link is called the “tael”; in Japan it is the yen. The bank is thus not content with a single exchange fee, but takes a double one, so that the bankers may become rich men all the faster.

{p. 106}

And what sort of percentages the banks operate with can be seen from the following. I go to a bank in Nagasaki and ask for the exchange rate of the Mexican dollar to the rouble. They explain to me quite simply: the difference between the Mexican dollar and the yen is five percent, the difference between the yen and the rouble is also five percent, so for 100 dollars you would get 90 roubles. So this band of robbers operates at ten percent! In Shanghai I was able to get roubles for dollars with a difference of at least only two percent. Only yesterday at the Russo-Chinese Bank I changed 100 dollars and received 96 roubles for them after all. This morning I return there, bringing along a whole bag full of silver dollars. There is no particularly great value in it, but 500 dollars in silver is quite a bagful. I had not wanted to drag it around with me the day before. I want to have my dollars changed into roubles, each and every one. They calculate the sum for me; it is much lower than I had calculated it to be. Yes, they explain to me, the silver dollar has fallen by four percent since yesterday. Today it stands at 92 to 100 roubles. The rouble has made a big “jump.” To that I tell the bank clerk he may kindly let his roubles “jump” as much as they want to jump, take my 500 dollars under my arm again, pack them into my rickshaw, and change them in Vladivostok at Kunst & Albers, who charge somewhat more civilised percentages. What makes the Asian banks a public menace to the harmless traveller is the undeniable fact that, in addition to their interpolated intermediate rate, these institutions also have two different rates that are applied depending on whether the banks are buying or selling. If, for instance, one goes into the bank and wants pounds for one’s dollars, one has to pay a sum for the pounds that is considerably higher than what one would receive if one wanted to change the same sum of pounds into dollars, quite apart from the loss caused by the double conversion, which would be the same in both cases. Therefore, traveller, should you ever set your feet in these parts, beware of the banks! They have corrupted the entire monetary circulation in East Asia.

What varieties of currency one has to deal with in East Asia in the small area between Hong Kong, Shanghai, Chefoo, Tianjin, Port Arthur, and Nagasaki, I should like to mention briefly: first, Hong Kong dollars; second, Chinese dollars; third, Mexican dollars; fourth, cash; fifth, Haikwan taels; sixth, Kaifang taels; seventh, roubles; eighth, yen. That the banks make their cut with such a multiplicity of currencies within what is, viewed from the standpoint of travel and trade, only a very small circle, one cannot hold against them; but *how* they make it is nothing other than simply . . . . shameless!

Should anyone intending to make this trip ever get hold of these pages, I might perhaps earn their gratitude by disclosing the only method I know to avoid this exploitation, at least in Nagasaki. There exists a considerable number of Japanese money-changers there. Their exchange rates naturally follow those of the banks as far as *whole* dollars, roubles, yen, etc. are concerned. When it comes to fractional coins, however, they call a *halt*. It is therefore best to have a coolie bring along a large {p. 107} bag into which one tosses the exchanged one-, three-, five-, and ten-kopeck pieces into which one converts one's dollars. For the smaller the coin, the greater the profit! For instance, in 20- and 15-kopeck pieces I received as much as 98 kopecks to the dollar, and in copper coins even 101 kopecks to the dollar. Compared to the exchange rate of 92 roubles for 100 dollars, this latter rate already represented a gain of twelve roubles on 100 dollars, which one can readily obtain in return for the small trouble of counting out the copper coins. In Vladivostok, every shopkeeper will gladly change the small coin. To be sure, this experiment remains effective only so long as it is not practiced too often. For if many people get wind of the secret and scour the tills of the small Japanese money-changers in Nagasaki for Russian copper coins, the law that goods become more expensive with rising demand will come into effect here too. Therefore, let whoever reads the recipe here not betray it to anyone else!

### 3. From Japan to Siberia.

2 July.

I slept on board the *Daphne* for the first time last night, and abominably at that. Fifteen years ago, the *Daphne* was still a first-class passenger steamer. One can really see from her what progress travel comforts have made in that span of time, or indeed how passengers' expectations have grown. If one winds one's demands back to the standpoint of fifteen years ago, then one can after all endure it on the *Daphne*. Old Captain Nissen, incidentally an experienced mariner well known along the entire coast of China, positively loves his *Daphne*, maintaining that she is a lucky ship. When one raises an objection, for instance that this is down to him because he is a lucky fellow, he contradicts with an ophimetic tilt of the head and asserts, “No...o, dat’s down to the ‘schip.’” Well, if I find myself feeling somewhat less uncomfortable on board his lucky ship, that is due less to the “schip” than to him. For he is truly an amiable captain, a real greybeard full of chit-chat. This voyage will be his last; then he will return in honour to Europe as a pensioner.

At 12 o’clock noon sharp, the *Daphne* weighs anchor. The last bales are stowed in the ship’s hold; they bear the inscription: “Export goods, from Hamburg to Vladivostok.” The official from Kunst & Albers, who has put a white Russian reserve officer’s cap on his head, waves a final greeting to the steamer from his agency boat; the manoeuvre of turning the steamer in the narrow fairway takes some time yet, then we steam past the French, Russian, and Japanese warships and out of the harbour. Forward flies the flag of Hamburg, on the signal mast the house flag of Kunst & Albers, on the other mast the Russian flag, and at the stern the German. Thus German-Russian is now the banner.

Outside, the weather is bad. The sun disappears, a brisk breeze springs up, and it rains. The captain, however, fears only fog, and prophesies a great future and boundless riches for whoever invents the binoculars through which one can {p. 108} see through the fog. The *Daphne* has her cabin accommodations aft; above them is the deck for the twenty passengers the steamer can accommodate—quite comfortable, of course, if one takes the standards of fifteen years ago as a baseline. The captain has even partially converted the deck into a palm grove, and the Chinese steward has hung his supply of bananas, brought from Hong Kong, from the highest pole supporting the canvas awning. Strolling, if not exactly under, then at least beside palms, and with alluring fruits of the south above us, we thus steer into Siberian waters. To be sure, it seems one is not meant to walk beside palms with impunity either, for the joy of walking does not last long. The wind becomes too violent, the rain drips through the light awning, and in the end it has to be taken down because of the wind. So, off to the cabin! Outside, however, it is storming, the rain pours down, the *Daphne* rocks and dances, the sea rages and demands its victim ... and gets it, too, sometimes right through the cabin window.

I have a cabin to myself. That is a great advantage. To be sure, I cannot imagine how anyone besides myself could have found room in it. On the other hand, it is also the rearmost in the ship. Directly beneath me, the propeller turns and the steering gear rumbles. On the side where the cabin adjoins the ship's side, it slopes down—a veritable upside-down garret room. The berth is very narrow. It is a so-called transverse bunk. When the ship rolls, I bang alternately headfirst against the ship's side and feet-first against the inner wall. But the cabin also possesses another, very wicked quality. Since it forms the extreme end of the vessel, whenever the ship pitches (and it pitches constantly), the cabin rises about a metre and a half into the air each time, and then sinks a metre and a half below the normal waterline, thus moving in oscillations of about three metres up and down, roughly fifteen oscillations a minute. A voyage of just over three days and three hours makes 75 hours. Forty-five metres a minute makes 2,700 metres in an hour, and in 75 hours 202,500 metres—that is, 202.5 kilometres added to the distance between Nagasaki and Vladivostok, which I travel free of charge! Really, then, forty yen is too little for the ticket!

I have absolutely no inclination toward seasickness—that is to say, I am by no means inclined to get it. Even so, I get it. I have not yet calculated how many thousands of kilometres the sum of my sea voyages amounts to; it is a goodly amount. By now I ought gradually to have become seaworthy. The captain, to be sure, says there are even old sea captains who have spent their whole lives at sea and yet still get seasick in foul weather. That comforts me. Incidentally, at dinner I was the only one who held out until the last course. That I owe, {p. 109} to be sure, more to my willpower than to the good food. For the food already seems calculated to ensure that most of the passengers become seasick. After dinner, I therefore seek a little recreation on deck. The cabin is so stuffy. So I put on my waterproof raincoat and climb up to the upper deck, despite the rain, wind, and tossing. The wind now strikes me as quite pleasantly refreshing. I station myself at the railing, facing in such a way that the wind drives the rain straight into my eyes. Ah, how restorative! I gaze over the railing; it seems to me as though a shark is eyeing me from below, looking so lurking, so expectant. But no, surely that is only a phantom of the sea, perhaps a sea serpent, which are said to occur here as well—July—it is just the right season for it. So I continue gazing over the railing, and from sea phantoms my mind turns to figments of the brain; the association of ideas carries me along, one thought chasing the next. I recall a conversation with a ship’s doctor friend of mine who maintained that seasickness was an obsessive idea and originated in the brain. The thought makes me shudder; no, on the contrary, I have the distinctly unpleasant sensation that seasickness comes from the stomach. I consider how this theory might be supported with evidence... Damned shark, don’t gawk so stupidly... “Hullo, what are you doing back there then?” suddenly rings out behind me. The captain really must have eyes everywhere! “Captain, I was feeding the shark a bit.”

On the Sea of Japan, 3 July.
We are approaching the Korean coast. The captain points to the left with his hand and declares: “There it is, can’t you see it?” No, I cannot see it. Here we are not far from Masanp'o, which the Russians have occupied. According to other reports, they have not occupied it. Where does the truth lie? A state treaty between Russia and Korea, according to which Korea cedes this place to Russia as a naval station and outpost between Vladivostok and Port Arthur, has not been made known. It seems to come down to the fact that Russian private individuals, while in agreement with the government and probably at its instigation, did acquire land there to establish a coaling station, but purely as private individuals. The Japanese naturally took their countermeasures at once, and on the very same basis: namely, they have now for their part acquired almost all the other land that the Russians did not buy. If Russia now comes along and wishes to take the island politically as well, Japan has an evident right to deny it this. In terms of cunning, the Japanese is quite a match for the Muscovite. Masanp'o may after all one day prove a very pretty pretext for a Russo-Japanese war over Korea. When it will come is questionable, but I have received the impression that it will come soon, unless a modus for an amicable settlement is quickly found—an outcome to which the Japanese are inclined despite the English alliance.

{p. 110}

The company of passengers on the *Daphne* is a rather motley assortment. A Courland count with his young wife, an amiable and pleasant couple; then a Russian captain with his wife, who speaks German, English, and French just as fluently as Russian. The ship’s *chronique scandaleuse* whispers that she had eloped with a young naval officer and has now been retrieved in Japan by her husband. Such things happen even in the best of families in Russia and cause no particular stir. Russian society ladies, for that matter, usually command several languages. Then there is a young German-Russian from Libau, who relates with immense pride that he is making a journey “round the world” and flirts with his English, but who otherwise belongs to that class of sporting travellers who set off on their journey, if possible, with merely a single suit, one shirt, and a kopeck in their pocket, and upon their return pay the usual visits to newspaper editorial offices, where the junior local reporter is given the momentous assignment of writing five lines about the man for the local section. Five lines, and by no means any more! Another variety of megalomania brought on by compounded vanity. Furthermore, we have an American miss on board, who comes from Port Arthur and is bound for Vladivostok, very plainly dressed, as an American miss always is in such circumstances. Then an English itinerant preacher, who does not speak his English but quacks it, and whom one usually sees on deck as the faithful cavalier of the American Lady of the Camellias. Finally, travelling in the cabin (there is, *nota bene*, only one class on the *Daphne*) are another Russian couple, who are constantly reading verse (evidently newlyweds), then the maidservant of one of the couples, and an old Russian babushka who has a remarkable eye for the best piece lying on the serving platter and often skewers it from far away with deadly accuracy. When it comes to helping themselves, the Russians are not at all timid, and, at least when the soup tureen or the roast platter is set on the table, they subscribe to the principle, deeply rooted in human nature: “Modesty is an ornament, but you get further without it.” Ordinarily, I have always made it a rule, when dining at a table with several others, to offer the dish to the others first. As is well known, politeness generally obliges politeness in return, and so it was to be hoped that the others, out of courtesy and inborn modesty, would always take the smaller portion and leave me the large one. With the Russians, this is the first time that this speculation upon the nobler impulses in man, which has otherwise always served me quite well, has failed.

The weather today is dreadful once again. Thank goodness I am the fortunate owner of a long cane chair in which one can stretch out quite comfortably—a blessing on the high seas! Lying flat, as everyone knows, is the best remedy for seasickness. The fresh air on deck blows around my nose, another small aid against seasickness, and finally, a fairly heavy canvas has been stretched over the cargo hatches today. So, I shall lie down in my "longchair"! What do I see—it is occupied, a lady is lying full length in it!

{p. 111}

A lady . . hm . . one must be polite. So I leave her lying there. Lo and behold, she gets up. I immediately dart for my chair and place my coat upon it. Then I hurry to my cabin and fetch a book. I return—someone is lying in my chair again, my coat beside it! This time it is a man; I do believe it is the globetrotter himself. No room for consideration here. Out! . . . the chair is mine, and to me it belongs! After a short while the lady returns; she sees the chair occupied and me in it. She casts languishing looks at me. Terribly sorry, in this sea state I am entirely impervious! Since that hour, I mercilessly throw anyone out of my chair who has made themselves comfortable in it during my absence, whether man or woman. This is by no means rudeness, but simply self-defense.

4 July.

Is the weather any better today? I think not. But perhaps one gets used to it. Possible! At any rate, I have the feeling today that when the steamer rises, I already make myself light, and when it plunges, heavy. Thus I imagine that I am the one producing the rocking motion—mere fancy, to be sure, but it helps. Not thinking about it helps too. Distraction, then! Good, I shall work, and in the breaks reflect on how best to annoy my fellow passengers. I think that works best if, while working, I deprive them of any hope of sitting in my chair. Since I type on a typewriter, it would be far more convenient for me to take a camp stool and sit on that. But I wouldn't dream of it! I pull my long chair up before a large crate on which I place my typewriter, and seat myself in my chair. Thus I let my fellow passengers stroll around me with envious glances, which amuses me so much that I have now been working the whole day through.

5 July.

What is this? Today there is no rocking at all! The sea is as smooth as glass, reflecting every little cloud in the sky almost without distortion. Eight hours left to Vladivostok! So I suppose I must deduct 21.6 kilometers of rocking motion from the 202.5 kilometers. The rarest people, who had otherwise crawled away into their cabins, can be seen on deck today. The awning is once again stretched over the quarterdeck, and on the left hand, Manchurian-Siberian hills can already be seen beckoning. The captain thinks it will be very hot ashore today. Siberia and hot—for the educated Central European, two utterly irreconcilable concepts!

The eight hours pass more quickly than expected. At 3 o'clock sharp in the afternoon, we drop anchor in the harbor of Vladivostok. The police boat appears. "Passports!" . . . echoes into the cabin. The passports are simply handed over; the captain stacks them in a pile, folded just as they are. "How many passengers?" asks the police chief. "So and so many," answered the captain. The police chief counts the passports, the number tallies, then {p. 112} with a forceful blow he strikes his stamp onto the back of the passports without examining them any further. From the elegance with which he performs this stamping operation, one may allow oneself to infer the extraordinarily great practice he must have in this activity. Uninspected, our passports are returned to us.

The "Daphne" comes from Hong Kong. The plague is there. The steamer must therefore go into quarantine if it has not been at least ten days underway from Hong Kong. Fortunately, this is the tenth day, and it expires at 1 o'clock in the afternoon. Strictly speaking, we should have to remain in quarantine for another hour. But the captain negotiates the hour away with the good-natured port officer. After all, it is the good old "Daphne" of Kunst & Albers, well known in Vladivostok, so that works out fine!

{p. 113}

[Illustration: Vladivostok: Landing stage before the Naval Palace (p. 114).]

## Chapter II. Zwing-Ost.

### 1. Life and Bustle on the Golden Horn.

In Vladivostok I have an esteemed and good acquaintance who occupies a "senior position" at the firm of Kunst & Albers, and of whom I think back only with feelings of the warmest gratitude, as it was his helpful care that not only saw me through the pitfalls of a first stay in a country whose language I do not speak, but whose helping hand I also experienced later on, all the way to Sretensk and beyond, with ever-renewed thanks. We became acquainted last year during our shared voyage on the *Hamburg*, and so my first visit in Vladivostok is to him. I find him in his office {p. 111} just as he is about to go for tea. The first piece of news I learn over the steaming samovar, to be sure, is not particularly encouraging. It ran, short and succinct: "The Amur is currently at its lowest water level, and things look even more bleak on the Shilka. Prepare yourself for the possibility that you may be stuck here or elsewhere for weeks, unable to move on, or else travel home via America." The latter is completely out of the question; I am firmly resolved to make the journey through Siberia under all circumstances, and so nothing remains for me but to fetch my things from aboard the *Daphne*, drive to a hotel, and quietly wait until the rain is kind enough to fall in the headwaters of the Shilka. As soon as that news arrives, I can then travel toward the water. Thus I must try—something I do not usually like to do—to kill time decently in Vladivostok.

Since January 1 of this year, Vladivostok has had an import tariff. To be sure, alcoholic beverages, foreign tobacco, etc., had already been subjected to a fairly substantial duty earlier, but the tariff was generalized according to the principles prevailing in European Russia only from the date mentioned. This innovation represents a considerable disadvantage for the provinces that must obtain their foreign goods via Vladivostok. Presumably, this tariff was introduced in order to favor from the outset the free port of Dalny, soon to be established on the Bay of Talienwan at the southern tip of Manchuria and near Port Arthur, where the actual terminus of the Trans-Siberian Railway will be. That Vladivostok is demoted by these measures to the simple capital of the "Primorskaya" (the province by the sea), by having the transit traffic of the Siberian Railway withdrawn from it, is obvious. This, of course, was not part of the plan from the beginning, and is by no means particularly agreeable to the large import houses in Vladivostok. But the Russian government does not concern itself with that, its measures here being determined exclusively by foreign-policy and military considerations.

Yet customs inspection seems to be handled just as laxly as passport inspection, at least when it comes to travelers' luggage. I have loaded my things onto a sampan manned by a greasy Chefoo Chinese as tall as a tree, and have myself rowed ashore. A building that looks like a custom house is nowhere to be seen upon landing; perhaps that large shed made of corrugated iron over there is the custom house. To be sure, the shed gives more the impression of a warehouse. My Chinese drops me off with my luggage at a meager landing stage, and I now await what is to come. I wait for the customs officials. True, I see several officials who might have belonged to customs loitering about; a few men in white blouses and official caps also approach me, but they do not say a word, merely looking at me. I look back at them. And when we have looked at each other for a while, they turn around and walk away. After a while, other officials arrive who again look like customs officials. To them {p. 115} too I must not be uninteresting, for they, too, look at the waiting traveler for a while. This time, however, I turned my back on them. At that, they go away as well. Finally the business becomes tedious to me, and so I hire a Chinese cart, which loads up my luggage and drives off. I take a seat in a droshky, a vehicle none too clean, but still tolerable. My *izvozchik*—that important Russian word, which I still remember from Port Arthur—wears a long, pleated coat of black velvet, a red sleeved waistcoat over it, and on his head a top hat of the well-known shape of Quaker hats. He speaks fluent Russian, nothing else, and on the instructions of my amiable acquaintance takes me to a hotel that bears the name *Sibirskoye Podvorye*, in German "Siberian Inn." My Chinese carter is directed to the same address as well, and then off we go up the hill. My little carriage has only two horses; it is an incomplete troika; to be a real troika, it must have three horses. The middle horse goes under a high wooden arch, trotting along, while the left horse gallops beside it; the third is missing. And so it goes, uphill, downhill, over potholes and ruts, always at the same wild pace. After a short {p. 116} time I land at the hotel, the *gostinitsa*, and ask the hotelier, a Jew who receives me in the jargon of Łódź ghetto German, for a room—the Russian says a "number." I get my number.

My *gostinitsa* is the exact opposite of the Nagasaki Hotel, or the Astor House in Shanghai, or the Hôtel de l’Europe in Singapore, or the Galle Face Hotel in Ceylon. There, after all, one has one’s bathroom, one’s washing facilities, one’s bedding, one’s towel, one’s pillow, one’s light. Here one has none of that. Except for the washbasin, one must bring all of this along oneself if one wants to have it and not pay dearly for it. Fresh water for washing is indeed provided, but so little that one may well assume with good reason that fresh water is an expensive commodity here. There is no bath in the hotel, but there is a bathhouse nearby. A bath there costs one rouble and 80 kopecks, almost four marks—fresh proof of my theory about the dearness of water in Vladivostok. I want a pillow. I get it after a long wait, and find ten kopecks a day charged for it on the bill. I want bed linen. I get it, that is to say a pillowcase and a single bedsheet. On the bill it stands at 20 kopecks a day. Thank God I remember that I have a few towels lying deep in my trunk, otherwise another ten kopecks. For each light I am charged 20 kopecks a day. And yet the room costs two roubles a day. These unaccustomed miseries run, with few exceptions, through the whole of Siberia, and there are only a few hotels that cater directly to foreign traffic and are fitted out accordingly. The Russian, however, always travels with his own bedding, his towels, his soap, his pillows, and his blankets. For he finds nothing more than a bedstead and a crude mattress in his “number”—a fine number, that!

Vladivostok also has a larger hotel, but its prices are correspondingly high. Now, I have no desire to pay 10–15 roubles a day for a room, meals excluded of course, and there is yet another reason why I do not move into the “Pacific Hotel”. Namely, an operetta troupe plays there from 9 to 12, and from midnight to 2 a.m. a tingeltangel. From 2 a.m. until dawn the *chambres séparées* resound with bacchanalia, and there is therefore every likelihood that one will not close an eye all night. On the first evening I spend in Vladivostok, my acquaintance takes me to the theatre in the Pacific Hotel. They are performing *Mam’zelle Nitouche*, the well-known French operetta. In part it is acted very nicely, and sung too; of course, a large part of the enjoyment is lost on me because I do not understand the Russian text. The theatre itself is small, but intimately built, though quite plain. On the left is the governor’s box, on the right the admiral’s; they rarely remain empty. One sees a great deal of interesting women’s faces and elegant gowns, but even more uniforms. In Russia every official wears a uniform corresponding to his rank. Just as there are military ranks, so there are also civil ranks that rank equally with the military ones. The {p. 117} [Illustration: Vladivostok: View over the City (p. 118).]

{p. 118}

gymnasium director, for example, has the rank of major general, the doctor the rank of second lieutenant, etc. In the end, everyone seems to have some bit of rank. During the intermissions, the smoking and drinking male contingent gathers in the hotel’s large dining hall, which serves as a foyer; others let themselves be caressed by the ladies in the hallways; still others stand dully against a wall somewhere with a cigarette butt, sentimentally staring holes into the air. During the performance itself, however, the audience is attentive and, whenever anything pleasant happens on stage, enthused to a degree that must be a real pleasure for the actors. Then the whole fiery Slavic nature of the Russian breaks forth; people shout “Bravo!”, stamp their feet on the ground, clap their hands, and shout “Bis!” (where we say “Da capo!”), and once the passage is repeated again, the very same jubilation starts up all over again. In Port Arthur I attended a farewell performance in which the departing theatre director was fairly showered with silver and gold dedicatory gifts, and credible witnesses assured me that he had not tossed these over the footlights to himself, but that they were truly gifts from theatre enthusiasts. The admission prices, by the way, are not inconsiderable. Our parquet seat costs 1½ roubles, about ten marks.

6 July.

The Courland count with whom I traveled on the *Daphne* has invited me to take part in a whale hunt that is to take place in the next few days. Count Heinrich Keyserling is director of a large Siberian fishing company specifically devoted to whaling. He is expecting three of his ships from Nagasaki. As soon as these have arrived, the hunt is to begin. That would be a very pleasant diversion during the waiting period! I therefore accept with warm thanks, and only with the reservation that I might not participate if, before the ships arrive, word should come that the waters of the Shilka are beginning to rise. I use the day partly to take a closer look at the city, partly to work. Outside it is terribly dusty. I therefore remain in my “number” for almost the entire afternoon and attend to some work that has fallen into arrears. In the evening I drink two bottles of beer in the Pacific Hotel, one bottle of Russian and one “Löwenbräu”. Total price: two roubles! Here, too, people prefer to take money from the living, because there is nothing more to be gotten from the dead.

7 July.

Vladivostok is a little over 40 years old and is today estimated to have 40,000 inhabitants. The city lies on a promontory that juts into what is known as Peter the Great Bay, also called Victoria Bay, and forks in the middle. The basin formed by this fork is the actual harbour of Vladivostok. The main part of the city lies on the western fork of the promontory and is bounded on the west by Amur Bay and on the east by the harbour basin. A chain of islands may be regarded as an extension of this part of the promontory, jutting even farther into the sea, heavily fortified, and bearing the {p. 119} outer forts of Vladivostok. Amur Bay is connected to the harbour basin by a canal navigable by the largest vessels, which cuts through one of the islands—presumably because the channel over the submarine ridge connecting the promontory with the offshore islands is not deep enough. It exists exclusively for the purposes of the navy; as is well known, Vladivostok remains even today, despite Port Arthur, officially Russia’s principal naval station in the Far East.

The harbour of Vladivostok is defended by strong forts that occupy the surrounding heights and make an approach from either the land or the seaward side extraordinarily difficult. In the south of the city, right in its midst, lies the main fort, which is the most heavily armed and whose guns carry clear over the surrounding mountains. The latest artillery practice against an invisible target located out at sea behind one of the mountains and determined solely by telephone is said to have yielded 80 percent hits.

The city of Vladivostok itself consists largely of wooden buildings that still retain the character of the Russian log house. Yet there are already a good many stately stone buildings that enliven the townscape considerably. Particularly striking are the post office, the Admiralty building, the governor’s residence with a pleasant park before it that overlooks the harbour, then the large building complex of the firm Kunst & Albers, and various other edifices that need not all be listed individually. The harbour scene is quite characteristically enlivened by the beautiful cathedral and by the tall triumphal arch standing by the harbour, dating from the time when the present Tsar, then the Tsarevich, made his journey through Siberia and laid the cornerstone of the Trans-Siberian Railway.

The great main street runs from Amur Bay at first in a straight line, but then, following the indentations of the harbour, skirts along it, leaving room between itself and the water for at most a single row of houses. Along this thoroughfare lie Vladivostok’s principal buildings. The other streets more or less all empty into this main artery, so that finding one’s bearings is relatively easy.

The pleasant impression that the city initially makes from the seaward side largely vanishes, to be sure, once one comes ashore. Only the main street is paved, and even then only for a very short stretch. Malicious tongues claim it was better before it was paved than after. I do not care. I only know that the paved streets, just like the unpaved ones, are either caked with clay muck or {p. 120} impassable with dust, depending on whether the heavens bestow rain or sunshine. There are no stone pavements, only wooden boardwalks. These would, after all, be quite passable. But, like Aslaksen, one must be familiar with local conditions; otherwise, it can easily happen that one strolls along the boardwalk in good faith and suddenly plunges through the timber via a rotted-out hole or, what is said to be even more dangerous, tumbles headlong into a ditch. For the boardwalks have often been used to cover makeshift drainage ditches, and the worthy road surveyor has sometimes thought nothing of simply letting the boardwalk come to an abrupt end whenever, in his foolishness, he deemed its continuation no longer necessary, without covering the gaping ditch beneath. One must be especially cautious in the dark.

Even when sitting in one's carriage, if one does not pay attention, one can suffer severe sprains. The streets, for which apparently nothing at all is done, mostly have deep holes, and since the izvozchik invariably tears across this vile surface like a madman, one is tossed about in the carriage as if upon a cannon galloping across ditches and ploughed fields. Down in front, up in back, into a hole on the left, into a hole on the right—that is how it goes through the streets of Vladivostok! I have always admired the skill with which the izvozchik balances on his high box, which has no backrest whatsoever, whereas I can at least brace my back against the seat and my legs against the carriage wall. To the municipal administration of Vladivostok, however, to my deep regret, I can award no other mark than a five, a flat five, without mercy!

The following little story is characteristic of Russian bureaucratic formalism. Opposite the Pacific Hotel there is a garden-like plot of land enclosed by a wooden palisade. It lies vacant. There was an intention to turn it into a concert garden with a music pavilion, a kind of town park, which would be very fitting {p. 121} in this treeless place. The municipal administration granted permission to the entrepreneur, but they had not first consulted the government engineer who oversees construction matters in Vladivostok and its environs. When the matter came before him, he refused permission because no wooden buildings were allowed to stand in the quarter designated for stone houses. Poor music pavilion!

8 July.

It is getting boring in Vladivostok. I have now strolled or driven up and down the main street about ten times, have been to the theatre twice, once afterwards to a music hall, and one has absolutely no idea how to pass the time. Nothing is more terrible than having to wait in uncertainty. Neither does the Shilka rise, nor do Count Keyserling's ships arrive. I spend most of my time finishing up my correspondence with my East Asian friends.

Much to my regret, my Vladivostok acquaintance had the misfortune of being kicked and severely injured on a riding excursion by a horse galloping beside him. I therefore visit him frequently to help pass the time for both him and myself, and he has the forbearance, despite his pain, to provide me with valuable information about my upcoming journey. Thus time passes, and for me these hours of conversation are among the pleasantest parts of my stay in Vladivostok.

There is also an ethnographic museum here, small, but its contents are interesting and valuable, if only because it contains a large number of original artifacts originating from the indigenous Siberian peoples who are retreating ever further into the interior and are doomed to extinction. Material is available in abundance. But it seems to me that it does not receive proper care. Inside, the museum looks like a disorganised and dusty glass case. The only objects I found not covered with dust {p. 122} were a wheelbarrow and a spade, which are evidently accorded great reverence; for this is the spade with which, ten years ago now, the Tsesarevich turned the first sod for the Siberian railway, and the wheelbarrow with which he carted away the first earth.

It is not my intention here to give a description of this museum, which would only interest a specialist. In Khabarovsk there is said to be an even larger museum. Should anyone intend to devote themselves to a specialized study of the Siberian tribes, they must not fail to draw upon the rich material amassed here.

The museum attendant takes no tips, an exception in Russia well worth recording. Otherwise, they almost all take them, whoever they may be, if one does not go about it too clumsily—often right up to the highest officials.

9 July.

Any German coming to Vladivostok cannot possibly bypass the great trading house of Kunst & Albers. It is an establishment whose importance extends far beyond even that of the largest Chinese import house. The firm was founded shortly after the emergence of Vladivostok by two Hamburg merchants whose names it bears, and today it is a world-class enterprise of the first rank, with branches to be found in almost every major town in Siberia. Its headquarters are in Vladivostok, where the firm possesses a number of splendid stone buildings and large warehouses. It unites virtually every branch of business within itself. The export trade is, of course, of the least importance, for there is next to nothing to export from Vladivostok. But shipping, import on the grandest scale, wholesale and retail trade in palatial department stores conducted by a host of German and Russian employees, all of whom visibly share the pride of belonging to a great and distinguished commercial house—one finds all of this; in short, one gets the impression that one might receive when reading Freytag's *Soll und Haben* [*Debit and Credit*] of the great and distinguished trading house described therein. In Vladivostok alone, the firm has over 150 employees—that is to say, Europeans—all of whom also receive housing, board, and lighting from the firm, in addition to a salary that is quite high by East Asian standards. The best sign of the spirit prevailing within the firm, however, was to be found in the fact that I never heard a single derogatory remark from any of the employees about their treatment in the firm or about its management, something that is otherwise so easily the case. The firm's current senior chief, Mr. Dattan, enjoys equal esteem among Europeans and Russians alike. He is, incidentally, a Russian Commercial Councillor.

If one arrives in Vladivostok as an unknown German, legitimisation by language alone is generally sufficient to receive information and assistance from Kunst & Albers in the most courteous manner. If one comes with recommendations, however, or has personal ties to the firm, one travels through Siberia as if in the chariot of Helios. It {p. 123} is by no means an empty phrase when I frankly confess here that nowhere in the East, or indeed abroad at all, have I found assistance granted so kindly, so generously, and so effectively as on my journey through Siberia from the firm of Kunst & Albers and its branches on the way to Irkutsk.

Apart from the firm of Kunst & Albers, there are several other German houses in Vladivostok, though they by no means possess the universal importance of the former and likely concentrate even more on specialised lines of goods. Thus I might mention the large iron and steel firm Arthur Koppel,

[Illustration: Vladivostok: “The main street, the ‘Svetlanskaya’” (p. 119).]

which does a very substantial business, and the import firm Langelütge, likewise an enterprise that does credit to its fatherland. In general, one can observe that German businessmen stand in high favour with the Russians and certainly outdo their Russian competitors by far—if not in sheer numbers, then in understanding, conduct, and ultimately also in net profit owing to their meticulous bookkeeping. In Russian businesses the craziest things often occur, things one simply would not consider possible: no bookkeeping, fraud on the part of the staff, embezzlement, and much else besides. Nor are the Russian employees in the German firms valued and paid nearly as well as the German ones, {p. 121} precisely because salaries increase with performance and the Russians cannot match the Germans in this respect. A very interesting example of this is provided by the large Vladivostok match factory of Suvorov & Co. Matches are a sought-after commodity in Siberia, and Russian match manufacturing is completely protected against Japanese competition by a high import tariff. At the same time, Russian matches are not cheap. Nevertheless, this large factory had to go into liquidation, reportedly due to slipshod bookkeeping and excessive peculations by individual employees.

As regards the social circumstances of the numerous Germans living in Vladivostok, they associate almost exclusively among themselves. German and Russian notions of comfort and wellbeing are, after all, rather divergent concepts. The employees of Kunst & Albers in particular have their own club, their German choral society, and in addition several private circles. They generally have it quite good; free board and lodging alone cannot be prized too highly. Life in Vladivostok is quite extraordinarily expensive; one pays at least 50 per cent more for all the necessities of life than even in China and Japan, where living is hardly cheap either. The purchasing power of the rouble out here is considerably lower than that of the mark back home. It is therefore bad news for anyone who has marks and needs roubles. On the other hand, every saved rouble is worth two marks fifteen, and in the end it is no harder to save than a mark. For this reason alone, Vladivostok, unlike China or Japan, is still an Eldorado for the “young man.” If he renounces the cult of Aphrodite and Gambrinus, he can save the largest part of his salary, and there are many who arrived here with nothing and went home with quite a handsome sum. If he is capable and works hard, his prospects in the East are still great—greater, at any rate, than at home on 150 marks a month and nothing more. If I were asked, therefore, whether it is advisable for a young man to go to Vladivostok, I would, provided he meets the requirements briefly outlined, answer with a resounding “Yes.”

The pointless waiting is becoming ever more terrible. What is one to do? Merely to pass the time and avoid being reduced to making the rounds of the pubs, this evening I am attending a concert of Russian folk singers being held at the “Naval Club,” the so-called “Morskoe Sobranie.” I love Russian singers in a variety theatre, preferably with dancing and the balalaika, the triangular Russian zither, but listening to an entire concert becomes tedious in the long run. Always the same themes, the half-sentimental, half-wild songs; once, twice—charming! But for an entire evening—as boring as Vladivostok itself! Yet this concert seems to be the main attraction of the evening. The admiral with the two double eagles on his epaulettes is present with his family, as is the governor with his family, along with a great number of military officers and civil officials of colonel and general rank; in short, once again the entire hall is filled with uniforms and ladies’ gowns, once again the same enthusiasm after the songs, once again the same applause and cries of “bis,” {p. 125} just like in the theatre. One has to be Russian to join in with that. Still, I must give the troupe of singers their due: they are the best I have heard so far. A middle-aged lady in old Russian costume serves as conductor; the singers—she is the only lady, soprano and alto being children's voices—appear in boyar costume. The singing has been rehearsed with endless pains and labour into pianissimo and fortissimo and the entire scale in between, yet, to my mind, without really doing justice to the melodiousness of the songs. To be sure, the pianos and pianissimos in particular are of wonderful delicacy, but often a pianissimo follows upon a fortissimo or vice versa so abruptly that I cannot help feeling it is empty effect-seeking. During the intermissions, a warship's band plays.

During these intermissions, the audience mostly goes into the foyer. I stay inside. But when the band treats me to a Hungarian dance by Brahms, at a tempo as if they were playing the Radetzky March, then—I too prefer to blow the smoke of my papiros into the same air as the Governor and the Admiral.

#### 10 July.

He who knows not patience knows what I suffer! Here I have already been sitting in Vladivostok for almost a week, fretting between two hopes that attract me equally. On the one hand, I longingly hope for news that the waters of the Amur are beginning to rise; on the other, I await no less longingly the news that the “Alexander” and Count Keyserling’s whalers have arrived from Nagasaki, to give me the opportunity to take part in a Siberian whale hunt for once. I divide my time between the office of Kunst & Albers, where news of the water level is to be had, and that of Count Keyserling, where shipping news can be obtained; I oscillate back and forth between these two poles, as it were, in swings six hours long. If the news that the water in the Amur is rising arrives first, I will let the whale hunt slide; but if the “Alexander” or one of the whalers arrives earlier, I will let the water level take care of itself and first go along to the whaling station for a few days . . . . Finally! The decision has been made; the {p. 126} “Alexander”, the company’s transport steamer, and one of the whalers arrived this morning, as did the expected schooner; this evening we go aboard the “Alexander”, and at the crack of dawn we put to sea for Gaidamak, the whaling station situated some 50 miles east of Vladivostok on the Siberian coast.

### 2. A Whale Hunt in Siberian Waters.

Aboard the “Daphne”, on which I arrived from Nagasaki, I made, as already mentioned, the acquaintance of Count Heinrich Keyserling, the director-general of a large Siberian fishing company that holds the monopoly on whaling along the Siberian coast. In addition to whaling, it will now also take up freshwater and marine fishing on a large scale, namely salmon fishing in the Amur and herring and sprat fishing along the coast of Sakhalin. The Keyserling family is originally German, that is, Imperial German—for ethnically German it remains to this day, having left its west German homeland over 100 years ago and found a new one in Courland. Besides Count Heinrich Keyserling, who not long ago brought home a charming wife from his Baltic homeland, one who was incidentally also educated in Germany, his brother, Count Maximilian Keyserling, is active as co-director of the company, specifically in charge of the whaling department, while the fishing department is headed by an ethnic Russian director. Count Heinrich, the elder brother, is the founder of systematic Russian whaling on the Norwegian model and of Russian deep-sea fishing in Siberian waters in general. For this reason I commemorate him with particular distinction, quite apart from a sense of gratitude, since he has given me the rare opportunity to attend an interesting hunt.

The fact that Siberia remains to this day practically unexplored and unknown country, despite the travelers who have traversed the vast continent along the well-known Trans-Siberian route, does not quite apply to the sea bordering the Siberian coast to the east—the Sea of Japan, the Strait of Tartary, and the Sea of Okhotsk. The broad back of the ocean naturally allows freer movement for the traffic of nations than does an inland territory; and while the acquisition of Siberia by the Russians all the way to Transbaikalia took place exclusively by land, the Amur region could only be gained through an intimate combination of advances on land and the annexation of the coastal territories from the sea, linked to the illustrious name of Nevelskoy. Even back then, the keels of Japanese vessels furrowed the waves of the northern Siberian seas, but even more so those of American whalers and seal hunters. Their quarry was the valuable bowhead whale living in the waters around Kamchatka, as well as an exceptionally valuable variety of seal occurring on only a few northern islands; the whaler here in {p. 127} these latitudes was the first and best connoisseur of the sea, the rocks, the currents, and the hunting opportunities, which he jealously guarded as a secret, and in which lay his key to success and wealth. Long before the geographical world abandoned the mistaken assumption that Sakhalin was not an island but connected to the mainland by an isthmus, the whalers were aware of the contrary.*) But the Sea of Okhotsk, too, was navigated not only by Russian warships but also by those of France and England, and the bombardment and storming of Petropavlovsk, victoriously repulsed by the Russians

[Illustration: Vladivostok: The largest German trading house, Kunst & Albers (p. 122).]

also caused many a drop of European blood to flow here into the salty waters of the North Asian part of the Pacific Ocean. Now this part of the sea is undisputed Russian possession, and Russia is by no means idle in diligently asserting its rule, namely by protecting the fishing concessions granted to its subjects against predatory American fishermen. Every year, schooners of American fishermen who unlawfully fished or hunted seals in the coastal waters of {p. 128} Siberia are brought in, and the government in Vladivostok has at its disposal quite a number of such vessels that were seized from Americans and are now chartered out to private individuals. One of these formerly American schooners is the very one that, laden with coal, has now been brought in tow by the whaler *Nikolai* from Nagasaki to Vladivostok. Its story is perhaps not entirely uninteresting.

::: notes
*) Even during the Crimean War, an English captain who had spotted Russian ships near Imperatorskaya Bay and was keeping watch at the southern exit of the Tatar Strait allowed himself to be fooled by this error, as the Russian ships made use of the northern passage known to them.
:::

In San Francisco there lived—or still lives, if he has not died—a wealthy shipbuilder who, an Anglo-American, had inherited from his mother country a penchant for sport and from his new home one for high stakes. One day, a well-known racing yacht wins a regatta off San Francisco. As is possible only in countries with a predominantly English population, the racing yacht becomes the talk of the town across half of America, is depicted a thousand times in every possible position, with and without owner, with and without the finery of her sails, the owner himself in sailing, riding, cycling, and possibly even swimming costume in all the sports papers and magazines of America and England—in short, all that fuss develops which we Germans cannot comprehend to such a degree in sporting matters. The trophies of this victory, however, gave the aforementioned shipbuilder no rest, and at last he announced that he would build a yacht that would surpass the much-lauded victor in speed. Everyone is astonished and intrigued in the highest degree, but nobody believes him; a debate arises, and finally the shipbuilder further backs up his assertion by offering to wager 80,000 dollars on the victory of his new yacht. Then the matter begins to get truly interesting; the bet is taken, further wagers are placed, millions are staked on the old yacht and the new, in short, the whole affair becomes more exciting by the day, and the day of the “race” draws nearer and nearer. At last the yacht is completely finished; amid an almost colossal turnout, the race begins. And lo and behold, the new yacht indeed runs like the wind and already has a lead over the former winner. During the sailing, and even beforehand, the shipbuilder had been drinking heavily in his cabin with several friends to the hoped-for victory, and finally, during the second part of the race, it occurs to him to take the helm out of the hands of his proven helmsman, despite the warnings of his friends. Ambition, however, tickles him to cross the finish line as helmsman himself in the face of what seems an undeniable victory. Whether it was the champagne or his lack of skill, in short, he steers so clumsily in the second part of the race that the lead is lost again, and the old yacht ultimately crosses the line ahead of the new, even if only by a few lengths. The loss of his high stake and the mockery over his defeat angered the shipbuilder so greatly that he had the beautiful yacht completely rebuilt, stripped out all the cabin fittings, converted the promising vessel into a cargo ship, altered the rigging, and subsequently sold the whole thing to a seal hunter, who took the costly craft to the Sea of Okhotsk, engaged there with her in {p. 129} illicit seal hunting, and then lost her to a Russian cruiser. This cruiser brought the vessel along with her crew to Vladivostok; the crew was given the opportunity to inspect Siberia a little more closely, not merely from the sea, but also from the inside for two years, and the ship was chartered to Count Keyserling’s fishing company for the ridiculous price of 80 rubles a month. To this day, the yacht still sails splendidly under the command of a Japanese captain, though she is usually towed by one of the whalers, which can make up to eleven miles an hour; and when the wind happens to come directly from astern and the now-schooner spreads her sails, it is not uncommon for the schooner to sail right past her towing steamer. At all events, one may once again apply to this costly ship the saying: “Sic transit gloria mundi!”

Besides, not every American ship captain has such bad luck; the mere large number of ships captured annually proves that the business still brings in money after all, and that the number of vessels that are not caught is probably far greater than that of those seized. For the Russians, too, are like the people of Nuremberg when it comes to hanging. Naturally, there is much romance in this fish poaching, and if one does not let oneself be caught, the profit is rich compensation for the dangers endured. The captains are therefore usually daring fellows, and this petty war between Russian cruisers and American fish marauders could furnish ample material for a thrilling sea novel.

I wish to recount only one such brief episode, which dates from the very latest times and may prove that with the Lloyd passenger steamers, not all the romance of sea travel has yet vanished from the oceans of the world.

One of these captains, together with his no less daring crew, with whom he was accustomed to share the profits, wished to pay a visit to the seal islands in the vicinity of Kamchatka. For a particular species of seal occurs there—and only at a certain time of year*)—whose pelt is of quite extraordinary value. The Russian government protects these animals through special regulations regarding closed seasons, the age of the seals that may be killed, etc. Every spring, on the first steamer of the Volunteer Fleet—which calls there regularly four times in all during the summer on its circuit through the Sea of Okhotsk—the government sends a detachment of soldiers who have nothing else to do but see to the observance of the statutory regulations by the authorized Russian fishery lessees and to keep foreign mariners away from the valuable islands. In addition, there are several cruisers in these waters that continually lie in wait for any unauthorized persons.

::: notes
*) I record the following version, which is to be found among the sailors on the Siberian coast. The seals leave the islands in question (Commander Islands) in winter and migrate southward in whole hosts. Their destination has not yet been determined. It is suspected, however, to be a fabled little island in the middle of the Pacific, said to have been discovered once by seafarers in the past, but knowledge of which has since been lost. It is said to be almost constantly shrouded in fog. At the beginning of the last century, an English whaler was said to have been driven off course there and to have brought back a rich haul of pelts, as well as gold. Whether this sailors' tale has any basis in reality is questionable. In any case, the winter whereabouts of the seals in question have not yet been determined.
:::

{p. 130}

The aforementioned captain, however, dares to land on one of these islands nonetheless and, with his crew, begins a fierce slaughter among the seals basking on the beach, which cannot reach the water so quickly. They are in the thick of their work; a large part of the catch has already been skinned, and the pelts are packed aboard the schooner. Then suddenly, in the distance, a wisp of smoke appears on the horizon—a Russian cruiser! The latter soon notices the American ship as well and now bears down upon it under full steam. The American captain has pushed off from shore with all haste, hoisted every sail, and away they go into the open sea. But the warship cuts him off. The American, whose ship sails excellently, hopes to slip through and steers straight for the warship. The latter at first believes the American recognizes the hopelessness of his situation and is coming to surrender without further ado, so as to escape the threatening guns. But what is this? Having come at full speed to within about fifty feet of the cruiser, the captain shouts a filthy swear word across to the Russian and suddenly veers sharply away, in the direction from which the warship had come. It all happens with such speed that the cruiser's captain hardly realizes what is happening. Before long, however, the cruiser also swings round, and off it goes in pursuit of the schooner at full steam. Now comes the turn of the usual cannon shots. Twice the Russian fires a blank, then a solid shot through the schooner's rigging. He does not want to fire any more. For although he would be able to shoot the wooden vessel to pieces, the share that the captain and officers are entitled to receive from the proceeds of the prized goods is far too valuable to them. As a result, a veritable race develops on the open ocean between these two unequal craft, in which the outcome remains undecided for a long time. Finally nature comes to the Russian's aid: the wind slackens a little, and the Russian is already running parallel to the schooner's bow. Thereupon the schooner attempts a new maneuver: he turns his stem directly toward the flank of the cruiser, as if he meant to ram her to the bottom. Hardly has the cruiser time to sheer off slightly when the schooner's bowsprit collides with the little warship's anchor crane. With that, however, the American has ensnared himself. The bowsprit of his vessel is boarded immediately, soldiers leap over onto the schooner, and meeting no resistance, the Russian captain takes possession of the American ship. The search revealed a nearly full cargo of the valuable seal pelts—a handsome profit for the captain and officers of the cruiser! The schooner was then placed under a Russian guard of six men, moored alongside the cruiser, and they dropped anchor in a not too distant bay. The Russians rejoiced over the good catch. But they had rejoiced too soon. For the next morning, as day began to dawn, the schooner had vanished without a trace!

How was that possible? Watches had been posted all night; on the schooner itself were six soldiers standing guard. Where had they gone? Inquiries were made, in vain; they had vanished along with the schooner. All further searches proved fruitless. They searched for the vessel out on the high seas, though the wind had freshened during the night, to be sure, and pursuit in an uncertain direction was out of the question. Everyone aboard the cruiser was furious; the fine prize {p. 131} [Illustration: Chaidamat Forest Station (p. 134).]

{p. 132}

had vanished. The commander bore the responsibility for the six missing sailors, and both he and his officers had that dull feeling as though they had drunk far too much champagne in the mess yesterday in honor of the good catch, for which the debts now still had to be paid. Fortunately, Russian naval officers are mostly quite well-to-do men.

The mystery was only solved later. Months later, the cruiser, which had in the meantime been relieved, arrived in Nagasaki. And what does one see? The escaped schooner, with its mockingly grinning captain and its sailors, greeting the Russian warship with “three cheers.” Shortly thereafter, the six Russian sailors also reported back aboard their cruiser. They recounted that on that fateful night they had all suddenly and simultaneously been overpowered by the American sailors, and their mouths immediately gagged so that they could not utter another sound.
