To avoid any noise, the American captain had cut the ropes with knives, let the vessel drift slowly with the wind under cover of the suddenly descending fog, and then quickly set all sails. Since he knew the coast like a true, noble, honest seaman, he maneuvered his vessel that very night into a hidden bay known to him, and while the Russian cruiser searched for him on the open sea and finally steamed away empty-handed, he waited there for two days and then steered for Nagasaki. During this time the Russian sailors had it quite good on board; they received as much whiskey as they cared to drink, and claimed to have been better fed by the Americans than at the Russian consulate, where they had been turned over by the captain in Nagasaki. In the port of Nagasaki, the Russians could of course do nothing to the American, but they intended to capture him again after leaving the coastal zone. The Americans, however, were prudent enough to ask an American warship that happened to be lying in port for protection. The warship put out of Nagasaki harbor with the schooner and escorted it far enough that it was able to reach its American home port unmolested.

* * *

The “Alexander,” on which I embarked, is a steamer, and a rather peculiar one at that. For its engine is located not in the middle of the vessel, but in its final third, counting from the bowsprit. The entire forward part of the vessel is cargo hold. Although the “Alexander” is a freight steamer, by no means a passenger steamer, officials of the company almost always travel on it as passengers, especially when it visits regions where no other steamers go. The passengers are then accommodated in the quarters intended for the captain and the mate. If one wishes to get into the cabin, one must first climb up to the bridge on a ladder with iron rungs, and from there go back down a wooden flight of stairs into the cabin situated beneath the bridge. The stairs lead into the saloon, with the captain’s cabin on the right and the mate’s cabin on the left, all three compartments taken together being about six meters long and three meters {p. 133} wide. When one considers that every human being needs a bunk about two meters long and three-quarters of a meter wide to sleep in, one can easily calculate that five persons of not all too substantial girth can reside here quite comfortably after all. I was assigned a sofa, which unfortunately does not reach the requisite width of three-quarters of a meter that I need for lying down, but measures only about 45 centimeters in width. To keep me from falling off, a board was fixed vertically in front of the open side of the sofa. As a result, I would at least have room lying on my side. When the ship then began to roll underway, I would lie with my back against the wall and my chest against the board placed in front, nicely wedged in, but all the more secure. Yet unfortunately, it was only with my chest that I fit into the berth, for somewhat further down, to my sorrow, my diameter on all sides exceeds the norm of 45 centimeters. So what to do? The remaining space on the ship had been given away. First I try the arrangement, and lo and behold, it works if I lie on my left side. If, on the other hand, I want to turn onto my right side, which one does feel the urge to do now and then, I always have to get up first, move the pillow to the other end of the sofa, and only then can I make myself comfortable again. This shifting of the pillow had to take place because I necessarily had to sleep with my back to the wall; otherwise, too much center of gravity would have hovered over the edge of the sofa. But since I am absolutely not accustomed to sleeping on my side, I cannot fall asleep at all. The only thing about me that falls asleep is my right or my left arm, depending on whether I am lying on my right or my left side. Finally, the saving idea occurs to me: not to set the board up vertically, but to lay it horizontally across two folding chairs, and myself on top of it. That works for a while. I lie half on the board, half on the sofa. Once the ship gets underway, I actually sleep quite splendidly, but have an oppressive dream that troubles me for a long time. Namely, I dream that I am in danger of tumbling down a steep cliff. In the end, there is no holding on; I fall in fact, and from the sudden fright I awake, only to find myself lying on the floor of the cabin. The ship is rolling heavily, and because of this the board on the folding chairs has gradually moved away from my bed, finally sailing down along with me. But I am too tired to make any further arrangements, lie down on my left side again, and brace my right arm against the table bolted to the floor so as not to start rolling again. Thus I sleep until morning. To be sure, my left arm has to be specially woken up again.

The next night, the mate gives up his bunk to me. It is considerably wider and just suffices for me. He himself, however, measures a mere 35 centimeters across the chest at the widest point, and eats a little less for supper, so that he has room on the sofa, even with the vertical board in front, lying on his side, of course. But he claims that he always sleeps on his left side. Well, then it works!

{p. 131}

July 11.

We approach the Gaidamak whaling station at about ten o’clock in the morning. The station, which possesses everything necessary for the immediate processing of the whales brought in by the whalers, lies on a wonderful, deep bay. The harbour proper, however, is a very small bay that joins the end of the larger one almost at a right angle, like a wart on a nose. Both the larger and the smaller bay are surrounded by cliffs and hills respectively, so that by their natural situation alone they are sheltered from all storms. The harbour of Gaidamak is one of the finest natural harbours I have ever seen. Indeed, even at the pier, which extends only about six metres out into the sea, the small bay still has such depth that vessels of up to 3,000 tons can berth there. Along these two bays now stand the various buildings of the Gaidamak settlement, which serve exclusively for the harvesting and processing of whales. Naturally, all sorts of tales and talk are exchanged along the way, and it is especially at breakfast that the most important questions of fisheries and whaling are discussed. After all, we have plenty of experts on board who can speak and tell of such matters. First and foremost, there is the director of the whaling department, Count Maximilian Keyserling. He studied agriculture in Halle an der Saale, whereas the memories of my student years gravitate towards Leipzig. Common ground is quickly established, introduced first by the observation—still moving on neutral territory—that Döllnitz Gose tastes no worse in Leipzig than in Halle, though not without an “umbrella”. Then one moves on to more serious questions of science, ultimately to land on fisheries. Furthermore, we have the actual director of fisheries on board, who speaks only Russian, yet is hardly inferior to the Count in courtesy, though naturally this expresses itself only in pantomime. An important personality at this breakfast table—where one begins with zakuska alongside vodka and finishes with a hearty beefsteak—is also the captain of the “Alexander”, a native of Riga, possessing all the unfathomable humour, zest for life, and amiability characteristic of the Baltic German. He formerly sailed his own vessel as his own captain, but sold it and preferred now to bless the “Alexander”, of which he thinks very highly, through the winter in Nagasaki with some new invention of his for the most practical utilization of the cabin’s confined space, and in summer to cruise between Vladivostok, Gaidamak, and Sakhalin. He has now also been in the company’s service for years, has carried many a barrel of train oil to Vladivostok, many a cargo of dried whale meat to Japan, and thus forms, in a manner of speaking, the link between the inhabitants of Gaidamak and the outside world. He is therefore thoroughly versed in the whole business. To this is added myself, who generally remain receptive and perhaps make the impression of a sage, because I maintain constant silence during discussions of fishery matters. For my knowledge of fishing actually rests—aside from the fact that together with my brother I have sometimes pursued trout along the clear streams of the Eifel—solely on being on familiar terms with the secretary-general of the German Fisheries Association, and his name, to top it all off, happens to be Fischer.

{p. 135}

The place where the whales stay changes with the season. In spring and early summer they are mostly in higher latitudes. Towards the beginning to the middle of July, however, they move further south, and in winter they even withdraw as far as the southern tip of Korea. In the harbour of Vladivostok, too, years ago, a whale—admittedly one probably separated from the herd, a so-called “straggler”—was shot. The migration of whales depends on definite rules, which basically all come down to where the animals find the most food. One can well imagine that this enormous mammal also

requires enormous quantities of food for its sustenance; its food consists of fish, and only very small fish at that. For the whale possesses only a very narrow gullet, the opening of which stands in quite disproportionate relation to the animal’s other dimensions. Through this gullet, however, no fish can pass that are thicker than, say, a child’s clenched fist. Since the whale also has no chewing apparatus in its enormous maw, but only a kind of grate of long, fin-like tooth formations, “baleen”, hanging down from the upper jaw, it must swallow the fish whole, and can therefore make use only of fish roughly the size of a herring. The whale occurring here is not the so-called bowhead whale, {p. 136} as is also found off the coasts of Alaska and Kamchatka, but a smaller variety. The so-called “whalebone” of the bowhead whale is roughly twice to three times as long as that of the whale caught here, and far more valuable, since, on account of its delicate texture, it is used to strengthen thread in Japan’s silk industry and fetches a high price, whereas the other whalebone can be used exclusively for the manufacture of corset stays and a few other articles.

Over time and after lengthy study, it has now been possible to determine the places where whales usually stay. The location of these spots can likewise be traced back to natural causes. They are, namely, mostly places where a current breaks and a kind of dead angle is formed in which the water moves very little. One can observe such points on a small scale in rivers. Imagine a stream of water running against a rock jutting out into the river's course and being pushed aside by the rock. The main current will naturally rush past the rock, but a small portion of the water will be deflected from the rock in a direction opposite to the main current, producing a backflow and a slow eddy in the angle formed by the bank and the rock. In such places one will frequently see pieces of wood, foam, and other objects carried along by the river collecting. If one transfers this phenomenon to ocean currents, one will also find here such angles—or rather, in these dimensions, such zones—in which a circular motion, if not a complete standstill, is produced. Most of the fish are also driven to these points, and since the whale follows the fish, this is the whale ground proper. In the vicinity of such zones, which can naturally be square miles in size, the station is then established, from which the whalers set out to hunt and where the brought-in fish are processed.

At first, these hunting grounds were determined empirically, and only later did people realize their connection to current conditions. Men had set out and searched for whales at random. When they found them, they shot them and marked the exact location on the map. In this way, clearly defined areas emerged for particular seasons, shifting with the months. This method was continued for years by entering the individual yearly zones on another map and superimposing each yearly zone in a different color over the preceding one. In this manner, one obtained regions of greatest density and regions of greatest scarcity. The zones of greatest density—or rather, the greatest density of the entire area—then coincided with the zones described above, created by ocean currents. I do not know whether these connections are known, and have therefore recorded them as specifically Siberian observations.

Our arrival in Gaidamak is an event. Flags have been put out to mark the occasion, and everyone is standing on the landing pier to welcome us. Small wonder! The steamers have not been seen in Gaidamak for eight months. Back then, with the coming of winter, they withdrew into Korean waters, and throughout the winter the vessels lay in Nagasaki {p. 137} for general repairs. Communication between Gaidamak and the outside world is not very easy without the sea route. To be sure, one can reach Vladivostok by land by first riding eight versts to the nearest Russian village; roads and paths do not exist, one simply rides across the heath. In the village, one can obtain post-horses, must travel with them for more than a day, I believe, and then reaches a small station on the Ussuri Railway, from which one can take a train to Vladivostok. And yet Gaidamak is still in the “immediate vicinity of Vladivostok.”

Now, however, the season begins anew; everything has been put in order to receive the guests as well as the whales. In the house of the chief engineer’s wife—he being a German—a good dinner awaits us, and in the cooperage, headed by a Berliner, barrels and vats are being built to hold the train oil that is to come.

Gaidamak possesses a complete factory plant. First of all, there is the large boiling house where the oil is boiled, then the cooperage, an establishment of considerable capacity equipped with the best machinery from Flensburg. Then a smithy, a machine repair shop with equipment for manufacturing tin cans and tin carboys for filling with oil; a sawmill buzzes incessantly, large timber yards stand ready there; in short, one can find a bit of everything, even a small office, a large warehouse, and a store of goods for the use of the officials and workers. Except for a few residential houses, all the buildings are constructed of wood—mostly cedarwood, which must grow at least as densely in the neighboring mountains as it once did on Mount Lebanon. The other kind of wood used here is oak, fine heavy oak, such as we are accustomed to in our native forests.

The most important and most interesting building of all is probably the oil-boiling house. Its interior equipment consists mainly of four large, upright boiling caldrons. Each individual one holds the cut-up fat and meat of one whale. No distinction is made here between blubber oil and meat oil. The boiling time is about 24 hours, so that four whales can be processed within a single day. When the whale is brought in, it is first hauled onto the so-called flensing plan in front of the boiling house by means of wire ropes and steam winches. This flensing plan is nothing other than an open-air wooden floor sloping down toward the sea. It is protected against rotting by the oil that has soaked into it over time. On this flensing plan the fish is completely cut up; meat and blubber are stripped from the ribs in long strips, together with the relatively thin skin. For the time being, the bones are stacked beside the flensing plan. As soon as the boiling work is not pressing too hard, they too are boiled out, as they are likewise heavily permeated with oil. Then, owing to the facilities for producing bone meal still lacking for the present, they are shipped to Japan and China, where they are ground up by the farmers and used as fertilizer for the rice fields. The bones are often of enormous girth and almost immense weight. However, they are very porous, and therefore—with the exception of the harder but very brittle jawbones—cannot be used for any bone work.

{p. 138}

The trimmed meat of the whale is then carried into the boiling house, where it is thrown into a cutting machine that slices it into small cubes. These cubes all fall into a pit and from there are lifted into the boiling vats by means of a scooping apparatus that works exactly like a dredger. Once the boiling process is completed, the train oil is pumped into large vats, from which it is then drawn off into barrels and shipped. The residual waste is not yet utilised at present, but simply thrown into the sea.

In chemical factories, all kinds of finer fats, stearin, glycerine, and many another fat, as well as substitutes for foodstuffs, etc., are prepared from the train oil, though these would generally retain little memory of their origin in Siberian waters. Whalebone, likewise exported as a raw product, later enjoys the particular affection of the ladies, who are even accustomed to wearing it over their hearts, provided they have not joined the ranks of dress reform—which is decidedly the more sensible course.

The utilisation of the whale in wintertime is quite different—a method of utilisation that is possible only in East Asia. For then the whale is cut into strips, root and branch, right down to the bones; these are dried in the open air, then simply packed into the ship's hold and shipped to Japan, where they form the daily sustenance of the Japanese, who eat them with their rice. Dried whale meat also finds no small market in China. Over time the meat hardens considerably, but dissolves again upon cooking to an agreeable tenderness.

Incidentally, the prejudice of civilised humanity against whale meat is just like the prejudice against snails, oysters, and horsemeat. It is simply a prejudice. Fresh whale meat in particular can be prepared exactly like veal and tastes quite similar, so that there is no need at all to be horrified upon hearing that there are people capable of eating whale meat. One does not, after all, have to go so far as to drink train oil like the Greenlanders, though here too the climate may well have been father to the thought, and perhaps we too would drink train oil without disgust if we were placed in the living conditions of the Eskimos.

We have an excellent lunch at the home of the German engineer, the director of the Gaidamak station, Mr Sommermeyer. The main dish is roast venison, and the mistress of the house apologises that, in the rush, she was unfortunately able to obtain absolutely nothing else, only roast venison—just think of it, "only" roast venison! As though the roe deer were simply running about the poultry yard! It is almost like that; at least they are said to occur in rich numbers in the neighbouring forest—notabene a genuine primeval forest—as are red deer. The abundance of game in this region is said to border on the fabulous in general. The surrounding country is a sheer park for both roe deer and red deer. To be sure, I personally did not see a single tail on an evening stalk, but I must observe in this connection that just as I set out, I happened to encounter an old Chinese woman; hence my bad luck! It is nevertheless said to be a fact that the forest teems {p. 139} with game. This leads to the following anomaly. As is well known, the Russian government is labouring to colonise these regions. Now the Russian peasant is anything rather than a coloniser. If he has his piece of bread today, his tea, his lump of sugar to nibble on, and his dried fish, he does not think of tomorrow; he lives, in truth, a Christian, contemplative life, taking no thought for the morrow, but acting like the fowls of the air. This applies even to Siberia, to which, after all, the better and more energetic elements still migrate, since the decision to emigrate in itself already presupposes a

[Illustration: Cooperage in Gaidamak (p. 141).]

certain initiative. The settler is assigned a fairly large parcel of land by the government. Upon it he first builds his log cabin from the trees he fells, then he eats up what he has brought with him, and when that is gone, he finally begins, little by little, to turn his land to account. Here the friendly Chinaman comes to his aid. In the eastern parts of Siberia, he is the true husbandman and market gardener in our sense. The Russian peasant knows nothing of vegetable cultivation; his understanding does not extend beyond the few crops of his native agriculture, and he is very slow to learn. The Chinese is the disinherited party here at the hands of the Russian, and yet he defeats his oppressor with peaceful weapons. He leases a piece of land from the Russian's property, grows vegetables on it {p. 140} and takes them to town, where he sells them at a good price. But the lazy Siberian peasant, instead of tilling his land himself, prefers to parcel it out on lease to the Chinese, collects his rent, and folds his hands in his lap or takes to hunting. Here again the Chinaman comes to his aid. For to the latter, the deer's antlers, still covered in velvet, are considered a valuable remedy. Such antlers command very high prices; Chinese merchants pay 50 to 500 (!) roubles for them, depending on the quality. Therefore, unless he is too lazy even for this, the Russian settler takes a gun, goes into the forest, and shoots deer to take their antlers. The meat is then sold cheaply, very much cheaper than roe venison. Once he has shot a few deer, he has enough money to live for a year, lies down on the stove bench, drinks his "chai", and nibbles sugar with it. That is the average peasant in this region. Of course, there are also intelligent Russians with initiative who make something of themselves. Thus one man hit upon the shrewd idea of capturing deer and penning them in a large enclosure. Here the animals live without any care, that is to say, as if in the wild. The peasant now does nothing more than snap off the deer's antlers at the appointed time. He repeats this every year, and thus derives a right handsome income from his stock of deer, which can amount to several thousand roubles a year.

The workers employed in Gaidamak are Russians, Chinese, and Koreans. This composition is typical of the Primorskaya province, that is, the province by the sea, east of the Ussuri. The relatively most valuable among these workers is the Chinese; next comes the Russian, insofar as he is not a skilled worker, which very few are; last comes the Korean. The latter is the frailest and least robust. The number of Koreans living in Southern Siberia is quite significant. They are mostly people who were conscripted in one of the numerous civil wars, crossed the border, found asylum among the Russians who were in need of laborers, and are now unable to return without facing the death penalty. Socially they rank lower than the Chinese, at least if one goes by the work they perform. The Chinese, however, I rate higher as a worker than the Russian because he is considerably more adroit and industrious, and in any case delivers relatively far more work for his lower wages, even if the objective work output of the Russian can be somewhat higher—which, to be sure, is by no means certain either. In any case, the Chinese possesses the great advantage over the Russian that he does not drink, and is therefore a very steady worker. He already perceives it as quite an extraordinary luxury that he is not required to work one day out of seven each week.

Of skilled Russian workers, there are currently only three fishermen from Odessa in Gaidamak, who are on the point of going to Sakhalin to establish herring fisheries there. The only thing in which ultimately every Russian has some experience is woodwork. Yet that is also a necessity in a land where one must cobble everything together for oneself: house, spade, and fur coat. In this, necessity is often the best teacher, though as pupils {p. 144} the Chinese certainly lag nothing behind the Russians. In the cooperage, for instance, only Chinese are employed. European workers could not be more industrious or more painstaking than they are. To be sure, one must keep an eye on them to ensure they do their work properly, for they naturally cannot compete with trained coopers.

In log-cabin building, on the other hand, the Russians are masters. Thus, the fishermen had framed a completely finished, handsome, large wooden house, which is being dismantled, stowed into the belly of the *Alexander*, and shipped to Sakhalin, where it will be re-erected in about two days and will serve as living quarters for the fishermen and the fishery director, along with his bookkeeper and the latter’s wife.

July 12.

Tomorrow morning at four o’clock, the first whale hunt is indeed supposed to take place. Whether it will be successful remains to be seen, since the whaler must first discover the whales’ whereabouts within their cruising range. I am therefore moving my bag and baggage over to the whaler *Nikolai* this very evening, where the captain has placed his bunk at my disposal. Despite its small capacity of only about eighty tons, this little steamer is already one of the largest whale catchers in existence; other whale catchers are mostly smaller, including those in use on the Norwegian coast. Formerly, whaling was even undertaken only in small boats. One tried to get close up to the fish in order to thrust the harpoon into its body by hand. Today, this method of whaling is obsolete and is resorted to at most if the whale refuses to die and thrashes about for too long. Then a boat will usually be lowered, from which the coup de grâce is administered. Nowadays, a cannon mounted forward at the bow of the ship is used to launch the harpoon.

The *Nikolai* is, of course, built in all its parts exclusively for its special purpose. Forward it carries the thick, gray-painted gun, which can easily be swung and turned by the gunner using a handle, as deemed necessary. The gun is already loaded; at the muzzle the tip of the harpoon protrudes, and the thick rope to which the harpoon is attached wraps around a steam winch, which serves the purpose of "giving line" to the struck fish or hauling it in. The rope disappears into the hold of the vessel, where it lies carefully coiled, long enough to give the whale more than 400 meters of slack when, struck by the harpoon, it plunges into the depths. The most interesting instrument of the entire machinery is probably the harpoon itself. It has the respectable weight of over fifty pounds and consists first of a thick iron shaft that fits snugly into the gun, but has an eye corresponding to its length in which an iron ring can slide back and forth. When loading, this harpoon shank is pushed into the gun, while the ring, which slides toward the harpoon's tip, remains outside the gun and holds the attached rope. Upon firing, the ring then slides to the harpoon's rearmost end. The tip of the harpoon consists of nothing other than a pointed cast-iron grenade, filled with powder and {p. 142} fitted with a fuse, which is screwed onto the actual body of the harpoon. On this body are the barbs, which are tied together during firing. However, the cord is stripped off upon penetration into the whale, and as soon as the whale pulls, the barbs spread apart. Through this, the fuse of the grenade is simultaneously pulled taut; the grenade bursts inside the whale and generally kills it quickly, less by the grenade fragments tearing up the whale's innards than by the tremendous pressure exerted by the powder gases inside the animal's body. To be sure, the whale is a powerful fellow; often the harpoon does not strike the right spot, and the grenade therefore does not burst near the vital organs. Thus it happens that the fish thrashes about in the water for a long time. It is then tired out by playing the lines, given as much line as it wants, but at intervals the line is slowly hauled in again, just as when angling one must tire out a large fish before hauling it out, only with the difference that here steam winches do what the hand easily performs there. Once the whale has been tired out, if it does not die of its own accord, it is lanced in the manner described earlier. A well-aimed thrust to the heart almost always kills it on the spot. Once the fish is dead, it is hauled in with ropes and secured alongside one of the sides of the ship, tail forward. In this way it is brought to the station, goes onto the flensing slip, and then into the boiler.

Apart from the equipment for whaling, the *Nikolai* is a small steamer like any other, except that, beyond the coal it needs for itself, it has no accommodations for carrying cargo. The remaining space is fitted out exclusively for the comfort of the ship's crew. And as far as these accommodations are concerned, it cannot be denied that they would do credit to a private yacht in which one could embark on a voyage around the world. The cabin furnishings are almost elegant and bear no comparison whatsoever with those of the *Alexander*. Nor can one begrudge this elegance to the men who, year in and year out, must regard this little vessel as their home. The captain is a Russian German, the actual whale-gunner a Norwegian. He is new on board, as is the captain, who was previously a mate on the *Alexander*.

It is quite questionable whether we will already be able to bring in a "big fellow" tomorrow, for firstly one does not know where the whales are currently staying, secondly one does not know whether the new captain will find them right away, and thirdly one does not know whether the new gunner will shoot the whale well if we do find one. By this time tomorrow evening we will be somewhat better informed regarding these three points.

* * *

On board the *Nikolai*, July 13.

It is 8 o'clock in the morning. The captain wakes me. We have already traveled over twenty miles and are now entering the region where one can find whales if one is lucky. I immediately ask anxiously whether any have already been spotted, but the captain smiles with stoic calm and remarks that things do not move quite so fast after all. Very well, we eat breakfast leisurely; then {p. 143} a camp stool is set up for me on the bridge, a pair of binoculars is pressed into my hand, and we scan the open sea. Here and there a flock of wild ducks takes wing; others let us come quite close and then suddenly dive under, only to resurface once we have passed. At times a tree trunk floating in the water and looking like a whale makes fools of us; once, even the whale-gunner puts on an interested expression. He is indeed entitled to do so, because on every fish he shoots, he has a share of so and

[Illustration: Whalegunner and cannon, ready to fire (p. 111).]

so many percent of the shooting bonus. He looks for a long time through his glass, I through mine, and truly, far off in the distance, something fish-like keeps leaping out of the water—a whole school, probably whales! But not wishing to make a fool of myself, I prefer to keep quiet about my observation, and in the end I am quite right to do so. For as we draw closer, it turns out to be a school of harmless dolphins. Strange—today I see whales teeming everywhere; every stick floating on the water I take at first for a whale. After all, this unsuccessful watching and peering has already been going on for hours. It is gradually beginning to feel wearisome.

{p. 141}

We head straight for Askold, an island with a lighthouse well known in these parts. The island actually consists of nothing more than a conical mountain upon which the lighthouse sits enthroned. Today a veil of clouds hangs about the peak of the mountain, but down below it is bright. This state of affairs is said to occur frequently. Now, there is many a wag among the captains who likes to tease the lighthouse keeper. As is well known, the lighthouse keeper must fire signal shots and sound the foghorn whenever there is fog and he hears a ship’s whistle. If the weather down below is completely clear while the lighthouse keeper sits in the fog, the jesting captain blows his steam whistle, and before long the foghorn begins to toot and the cannon to thunder. Then the captain toots again, and the reply follows thick and fast. The poor, teased lighthouse keeper toils away up there in faithful performance of his duty, while that rascal of a captain laughs up his sleeve. These are sailors’ pranks that happen often, and which are served up anew to every passenger who comes near Askold.

It is already one o’clock, and we still have not seen a single whale spout. The captain therefore suggests that we go below and have lunch—an appealing proposal in its own right. Only the gunner remains on the bridge. So we partake of our *zakuska* in the cabin and then fall upon the excellent cabbage soup. It tastes delicious, and I am just about to ladle myself a second plateful when... suddenly the engine stops. The captain dashes on deck, I after him—the gunner has sighted a whale! So after all! We can still see the water welling up at the spot where the whale has just dived into the deep. So we wait a while, and sure enough, after about a minute, it surfaces again some fifty meters from us. The tall dorsal fin slowly slices through the surface of the sea; at times the whale glides halfway out of the water in long, leaping motions, and once or twice it spouts a tall jet of water vertically into the air—its whistling audible even to us—the water it had taken into its enormous maw along with the fish. The ship’s engine is running again now; the senior sailor stands at the helm and calls his orders down to the engine room through a speaking tube, while the gunner stands ready to fire by his cannon. Our intention is to stalk as close to the fish as possible. But already, with a sudden upward flick of its fluke, it has plunged back into the deep to fill its mouth with fresh sustenance. The engine stops once more, and we wait for it to surface again. Surface it must after a certain time, for it is a mammal, breathes with lungs, and indeed not through gills, but like every other mammal through its pharynx. After a while it does indeed become visible once more. Again an order echoes down into the engine room, the engine runs, and we approach the whale; but before we are within firing range, down it goes into the deep once more.

Firing range is about five to ten fathoms, no more. Until one is within firing range of the whale, shooting makes no sense. Getting close—that is the art; and to get a sure shot, one must approach the whale from the flank. When whales appear in pods, this is generally not so difficult, because {p. 145} one always gets a shot at one whale or another. Here, however, we are dealing with a “loner” that goes its own way and is therefore naturally far more alert than whales in pods. Getting close to such a solitary whale usually requires endless patience, if it is possible at all.

One sees, then, that whale hunting can still prove a great test of patience and demand much composure and alertness even when one already believes one has the whale safely in one’s pocket—provided, that is, that one has pockets so large. In our case, we chase each other about for nearly two hours. Again and again the colossus surfaces from the depths, announced anew each time by a forceful command down to the engine room, but again and again it comes up at a spot that leaves an even greater distance between itself and the whaler than before. To cap it all off, it begins to rain as well, with the result that despite all our efforts this first hunt comes to naught. Once more the characteristic spout is seen; then the whale vanishes back into the deep and is seen no more.

Well, now at least I know from personal observation how it has to be done when one wants to shoot a whale. Next time I can do it too.

[Illustration: Vladivostok: International flea market (p. 155).]

Despite this success, which was only partial after all, we have another interesting hunt today that could almost have been a whale hunt. On the homeward run, we notice a number of very large fish in the water. For the most part, they signal their location by their mighty dorsal fin protruding from the water. But whales they are not. They are not large enough for that, even though they are still thirty or more feet long, and they also lack the characteristic spout. The whale-gunner, however, has never seen these fish in his home waters, and they offer a fairly good target to get some shooting practice with the gun, which he has not yet tested. The day is lost for any further whale hunting anyway, because the captain has orders to bring me back safely to the station in the evening, regardless of whether with or without a whale. Consequently, we steer toward one of these fish, which tolerates this quite indifferently. We come within a few fathoms of it, the gun roars, the line runs out, and the steam winch begins to work. But no counter-pull ensues. Finally the harpoon is hauled out of the water; it is covered with shreds of flesh, and the grenade has exploded. So it was a hit. But the fish has vanished. Perhaps it was too small {p. 116} for the heavy projectile, perhaps its flesh was too soft and could not withstand the explosion of the grenade, so that the barbs of the harpoon could not take a firm hold; at any rate, the how and where remain hidden. The gun is loaded a second time. For a second time one of these sea monsters is steered toward, again with good success. Again the gun roars, again the waves hiss, and again the fish disappears, never to be seen again. Unfortunately, a minor accident occurs on this occasion: the grenade bursts before penetrating the body of the fish. But the harpoon must nevertheless have entered the fish's body, for it brings up shreds of flesh once more. Now, however, the gunner swears high and low that he will not waste another grain of powder on this sort of whale. As we later learn, he had probably shot at so-called swordfish, which belong to the dolphin family, that is, the toothed whales. At nightfall we arrive back in Gaidamak. Thus my first whale hunt ends without result. To be sure, the day had been ill-chosen—it was the 13th of the month!

July 11.
In a year or two, the company will have steered the entire whaling operation into different channels. Already in the autumn of this year (1901), the director of the Gaidamak station, Herr Sommermeyer, is traveling to Hamburg to supervise the construction, or rather conversion, of a large ship intended as a sort of floating whaling station, a factory ship on the high seas. It will be the first ship of its kind in existence in the world, combining all the establishments found at the whaling station, perhaps with the sole exception of the cooperage. The machinery is being supplied by the factory of Friedrich Krupp in Essen. The deck of the ship will be fitted out as a flensing deck. Flensing the fish will naturally be done by machine, and hardly a shred of the whale will go overboard, as everything will find a use and immediate processing. This factory ship is intended—as guaranteed by Krupp—to enable the complete processing of a whale in the incredibly short time of two hours. In this time, then, after the whale has been hauled onto the flensing deck, that is, on deck, it will be cut up; the soft parts will be chopped up, conveyed mechanically to the boilers, boiled there, and the train oil will be filled into barrels immediately after boiling. The boiled-out and, no doubt for good measure, pressed residual parts—at any rate completely de-oiled—will be brought to the drying room, ground, and filled into casks to be used later as fertilizer. The same will be done with the bones. These first pass under massive rollers that crush them until nothing is left but a thick pulp. The oil contained in it will be extracted from this pulp by chemical or mechanical means; finally, the meal also enters the drying apparatus and is poured into casks as bone meal. The casks will be sealed and placed in the hold, ready for shipment at once. And all this will take place within a period of only two hours. The whalers thus will have no need at all to keep returning to the station whenever they have shot a whale, nor do they need to moor it first, but rather tow it through the water straight to the factory ship, which will naturally be stationed in the middle of the main hunting zone, and the latter will then take the whale aboard.

{p. 147}

As far as I know and was able to ascertain, the whales that come into question for the region along the east coast of Siberia belong to the group of rorquals. The Russians themselves asserted that they were the same whales as were caught in Norway. With this would also accord the shortness and thickness of the baleen, which on average does not reach one metre, as well as the fact, observed by myself, that when surfacing the whale blows no more than twice, but usually only once. The Gaidamak whaling station is likely in essentials a copy of the Norwegian whaling stations, as described in exact detail in volume 6 of the *Abhandlungen des deutschen Seefischereivereins* [Transactions of the German Sea Fisheries Association]*). Gaidamak is the best-equipped station; however, as mentioned, it comes into question for whaling only in midsummer and autumn. In winter, when the whales head south, whaling pursues them to the Korean coast, where a whaling station likewise exists, which however is of a more provisional character and is equipped solely for flensing the whales. This station has in a sense the character of a mobile station, as it is customary to relocate it according to the location of the whales. There are no facilities of any kind for extracting train oil there; instead, the whales taken in winter are merely brought ashore, cut up, and the meat and blubber salted down in barrels and shipped to Japan, where they serve as food. The bones of the whale are likewise shipped directly to Japan and marketed there. Traffic between the whalers and the Japanese ports, where Japanese merchants are once again the buyers, is handled by the transport ship *Alexander*. In Japanese ports, Count Keyserling's company maintains no special stations; the vessels call there only now and then to take on cargo or to be docked. The Gaidamak station, on the other hand, possesses the complete facilities of a well-managed Norwegian station, for the present, however, still with the exception of the fertilizer works. The transport of the unground bones, however, is treated only very much as a secondary matter. At the station itself, enormous piles of whalebones lie stacked up, still awaiting subsequent processing and grinding. Presumably no separate guano works will be established at Gaidamak at all, since this will be replaced by the future large factory ship, which in all probability will also process the stocks of bones still stored at Gaidamak as occasion offers. In addition to the apparatus for cutting up the whales and extracting oil, and apart from the cooperage and the living quarters for officials and workers, the Gaidamak station also possesses a kiln for drying the timber used in barrel-making, then a forge that serves mainly for the production of explosive shells and harpoons, and further a small workshop for making large tin vessels that are used in place of barrels. In this workshop all kinds of mechanical work that arise are carried out; this is necessary at a station so completely cut off from communication with the outside world as Gaidamak. Furthermore, there is a sawmill there that is constantly in operation to carry out the rough woodwork. Power is supplied by the steam boiler of the machine shop. The personnel {p. 148} of Gaidamak consists of the chief engineer (a German), a bookkeeper, a warehouse manager, a cooper (likewise a German), two machinists, a mechanic, and about a dozen Russian workers. The salaries of the Europeans range between 300 and about 70 roubles. In addition, about thirty Chinese whose wages range between 15 and 30 roubles, and about ten Koreans who receive 10–20 roubles, are employed and resident at the station as labourers. These salaries are monthly. The train oil obtained at the station is first transported by the company's cargo steamer to Vladivostok, and from there is sent almost without exception to Odessa on vessels of the Russian Volunteer Fleet. For the present, as far as I am aware, the business works exclusively for Russia, and the same is to happen after the inception of fishing operations, specifically herring, sardine, and salmon fishing. The latter in particular will be able to count on a very strong local market, and the surplus is to be shipped to European Russia. Meanwhile, if its enterprises succeed on a large scale, the company intends to expand the market for herring, salmon, and sardines to the rest of Europe as well, specifically to Germany and England. The number of whales taken has hitherto been rather variable; nevertheless, the annual catch at the Gaidamak station is likely to be about 50 to 80 whales. Concerning the catch along the Korean coast, I unfortunately possess no particulars. It must be taken into account here, however, that the company can look back on relatively recent experience, that for the time being a great deal of experimenting is still going on, and above all that closer studies are still being made concerning the haunts and frequency of the whales. In my opinion the operations themselves are not conducted with particular intensity; people have a relatively generous amount of time, and various measures are adopted that subsequently prove superfluous. Yet there is no doubt that whaling in Siberia, once this first period has been overcome, still has a significant future. A chapter entirely of its own is likely to be formed later on by the hunting of the true Greenland whale, which occurs in the Sea of Okhotsk. For the present, this is pursued almost exclusively by Americans, and indeed unlawfully in the manner described. Until it is taken up and systematically exploited by the Russian company, which holds the concession for this as well, much water is likely to flow down the Amur.

::: notes
*) Author Professor Dr. Henking in Hanover.
:::

* * *

As has already been mentioned, the Russian fishing company also intends to establish large-scale fishing operations along the coast of Sakhalin. The fish primarily under consideration for this are herring and sprats. When an unbiased observer first hears talk of the abundance of fish in the waters around Sakhalin, one does not at first believe the seriousness of what is heard, until one has seen photographs and other reliable evidence confirming the truth of the statements. Thus it is indeed a fact that at the time of the herring run on the southeast coast of Sakhalin, when a shoal of herring approaches the shore, millions of these fish are often cast ashore by the waves, remain lying there, and {p. 119} pile up into walls that are often as high as a man, which have to be shoveled back into the sea by the Russian criminals deported to Sakhalin so that they do not fill the air too greatly with putrefaction.

On the Kurils and on northern Ezo, lying opposite Sakhalin, the Japanese likewise engage in herring fishing on a large scale. But this fish is by no means used as food; rather, the catch, numbering in the many millions, is thrown into large boiling cauldrons in which the fish oil is extracted and poured into vats to serve as cooking oil in Japan, China, and Korea. The boiled-out flesh, on the other hand, is exposed to the sun on the beach, dried, loaded onto ships, and finally used as fertilizer for Japanese rice fields. Consider that: herring as fertilizer! One can buy a pood of herring on Sakhalin for one ruble; a pood is roughly forty German pounds. The Russian company expects great success from the herring business and hopes to be able to hold its own with honor on the European market.

Sprats are also said to occur in immense quantities, especially on the northern coast of Sakhalin. In the next few days, the company's director of fisheries will travel to Sakhalin with an {p. 150} expedition of fishermen from Odessa to carry out the preliminary work for taking up fishing on a large scale.

In Vladivostok, I had the opportunity to taste the Siberian herring. As far as I may trust my taste in herring, it is a fine, fat herring, well able to stand comparison with the product we are accustomed to at home.

The salmon fishery on the Amur, which the company likewise intends to take up, also seems very promising to me. The Amur in itself is one of the most fish-abundant rivers in the world, and the Sibiryak who lives along the Amur draws the overwhelming majority of his food from it. Sturgeon is found there in great quantities, yielding a very good caviar that the Russians claim is scarcely inferior in quality to Volga caviar *). At certain times of the year, however, the importance of the sturgeon is pushed entirely into the background by the armies of salmon that ascend the Amur and its tributaries during the spawning season. Three varieties occur there, migrating at different times. First comes a large variety that reaches up to three meters, then comes a small variety measuring only about an ell, and last of all a medium variety. All three are caught and eaten; however, from the first run only a small part of the back is actually used, while the rest is dried and used as dog food or fertilizer. The second variety is said to be finer than the first. It forms, in fact, the daily staple of the Amur Sibiryak. Finally, the third variety, the medium-sized one, is said to be the finest and not inferior in tenderness to our best domestic varieties. I have eaten it in every form of preparation: boiled, fried, smoked, and fresh with oil and salt. In all forms it is equally noble and flavorful. It is this variety, above all, that is being targeted.

To give a vivid picture of the Amur's abundance of salmon during the migration season, I would note that the Amur Sibiryaks—and by this I include the entire river basin of the Amur—do not catch the salmon with much art or elaborate preparations, but simply build brushwood weirs or fences extending a few meters into the river from the shore. The salmon are halted on their migration by these barriers, and the Sibiryak arms himself with a hand net with which he scoops the fish into his boat. They are then killed, gutted, and hung in the smoke, or merely dried **).

That the Sibiryak could ever suffer hunger seems, given this abundance of salmon, simply out of the question, and yet it happens often enough. The reason is to be found in the fact that the Sibiryaks, in their all-too-typical laziness and carelessness, often do not make sufficient use of the first and second salmon runs in the hope of the third, and then in the end come up short if the latter happens not to turn out plentiful enough to cover the entire winter supply. {p. 151} In any case, after all this, one is certainly justified in assuming that rationalized fishing could produce results here, too, that are simply incomprehensible by European standards. As I hear, a factory is soon to be established in Vladivostok that intends to prepare exclusively canned fish. If this factory is properly managed, it could inflict severe damage on European as well as American competition. The East in particular is a region in which disproportionately more canned goods are consumed than, say, back home, because very many items cannot be obtained fresh. If a domestic East Asian canning industry now emerges, it will undoubtedly achieve great success, at least in the fish trade; and what is more, it will also be in a position to open up the European and American markets for itself.

::: notes
*) I myself later bought caviar of wonderful quality on the Amur at the ridiculous price of one ruble per pound.

**) I later saw on the Amur steamers that the sailors lived almost exclusively on bread, tea, a lump of sugar which they put into their mouths to sip the tea through, and this dried salmon, sometimes with a fresh cucumber as dessert. I also saw the ship's cook buying sturgeons weighing about 30 pounds from the fishermen for 15–20 kopecks.
:::

This latter applies primarily to salmon fishing and the herring catch. If the journey through the tropics has hitherto placed an insurmountable obstacle in the way of transporting these fish, this obstacle too will soon be removed—not, to be sure, by the Siberian Railway, since its freight rates would be too high and, after all, in midsummer it also runs through tropical climates (tropical, at least, as far as temperatures are concerned)—but rather by the construction of refrigerated vessels, which will be equipped like the ships with which splendid Australian beef is likewise brought fresh to England through the tropics and the Red Sea. It is hardly an exaggeration to say that this could give rise to strong competition for our domestic fishing industry. Yet it remains questionable whether the dangers inherent in this are not amply outweighed by the fact that in salmon and cheaper herring one obtains an enrichment of our inexpensive and nutritious staple foods. Were the importation to be taken in hand by a benevolent company that does not seek to earn too much in the process, it would seem to me by no means utopian to express the hope that fine Siberian salmon might become a cheap staple food among us, despite the objections of the delicatessen merchants. The precondition for this would, of course, be that deliveries were uninterrupted and regular, and that is precisely the point where even the best Russian company proves mortal. So things will probably not move quite so terribly fast with the new staple food after all.

### 3. Farewell to the Pacific Ocean.

July 15.

The fine days of Gaidamak are now over! Since this morning we have once again been afloat on the open sea. We slept the night on board and then had our tea on the bridge. Toward 9 o'clock in the morning we are already approaching the mountain island of Askold, where the lighthouse keeper is once again sitting under a cap of fog, sounding his foghorn and firing cannon shots, while down below we glide along in the most glorious sunshine. Askold lies off the mouth of the coastal river Suifen, which forms the geographic boundary to the south between the Siberian Maritime Province, the Primorskaya, and Chinese Manchuria, although the political border runs further south, {p. 152} and includes within Siberia the Suifen Valley as well as Possiet Bay, which lies directly to the south on the border of Korea and likewise harbors a good port. In the days of the Russian conquest of these territories, Askold was the stronghold of the Honghuzi, who from here harassed the Russian settlers with raids and incursions and also dug for gold on the island. But when Russia's military power grew stronger on the Pacific coast, this robbers' nest was cleared out, and today a gold mine flourishes here, owned by a Vladivostok merchant named Lindholm and, to my knowledge, the only gold mine in this part of Siberia where quartz gold rather than alluvial gold is extracted. In order to secure operations against predatory attacks and also to supervise and keep in check the workers employed in the mine, a military guard has been stationed there.

Soon, not too far off, we see white masonry gleaming in the morning sun; one more turn around a headland, and through the opening narrow gap, gradually growing wider, we see parts of the panorama of Vladivostok emerge, until we reach the narrow harbor entrance itself and the whole picture now unfolds—an impression marred only by the fact that we know all too well how severely our illusions must suffer as soon as we are jolting over potholes in a dusty troika once again and having to pay four reichsmarks if we want to bathe away the dust. The harbor basin proper has been bestowed by the Russians with the beautiful name "the Golden Horn." The choice of this name is no coincidence; it is meant to evoke Constantinople. It reflects a policy of which Peter the Great was the first successful representative—after whom, after all, the bay on which Vladivostok lies is named—namely, the policy of creating outlets to the sea for continental Russia. The harbor is the gateway to the wide world. That was why Peter created a new Russian capital at the mouth of the Neva; hence Russia's eager endeavor to gain access to the Mediterranean through the Bosporus*); hence the struggle with England for a port on the Persian Gulf; and hence Russia's successful striving for a port on an ice-free sea in the Far East. Before anyone thought of Port Arthur and Dalny, Vladivostok represented the fulfillment of these wishes, and since the winter of 1893–1894 it has also been possible to keep the harbor's fairway open with icebreakers, so that at a pinch Russia could have been content with this outlet. Thus, here in the Far East, Russia received a new Constantinople, as it were, as a provisional installment on its desires regarding European Stamboul. For the stranger, however, the *tertium comparationis* between ancient Constantinople and Vladivostok, the "Ruler of the East," seems at first to lie in the fact that both cities, viewed from the outside, are magnificent, and viewed from within, are dreadful.

We land. I make careful inquiries as to whether rain has fallen westwards in the headwaters of the Amur or the Shilka, roughly 3,000 kilometres from here as the crow flies. Nothing is known of it yet. Well, no matter then; I am determined to travel.

::: notes
*) In modern times, of course, other motives also play a part in the drive towards Constantinople.
:::

{p. 153}

If the rain will not come to meet me, well then, I shall go to meet it, like Muhammad to the mountain. Under no circumstances, however, will I remain stranded in Vladivostok any longer. For if I must sit down and wait, I can certainly do so just as well somewhere else.

So now it is time to take my leave! I cannot exactly claim that parting from Vladivostok is hard for me. But a pinch of sentimentality is in the German’s

[Illustration: Vladivostok: The Railway Station (p. 155).]

blood, after all, and indeed, I have quite a number of people in Vladivostok whom I shall remember with pleasure, and to whom I must still bid a sincere “thank you” and “farewell”. So farewell visits—a warm word here, there at least a fleeting handshake in passing! Even my Łódź Jew, the innkeeper, gets a handshake, even though he charged me on my bill for an extra day’s lodging and at least five vodkas too many, which he nevertheless smiles and allows to be deducted. In Russia, one shakes hands with everyone anyway, unless one wants to get a reputation for being impolite, and one certainly does not want that. If you enter a restaurant where you have twenty acquaintances sitting at twenty different {p. 151} tables, you have to go up to the twenty different tables and give your hand to each of the twenty different acquaintances. There is no need to press or shake the hand. That would be too strenuous—just offer it; nor is it taken amiss if you extend your hand to one person while already looking around for the next. Having packed my things, however, I look around my “number” with a certain melancholy—a room that was, during my stay, an—oh so dear home to me. Now that my things have been cleared out, it looks desolate inside. Four bare whitewashed walls, an iron bedstead with a straw mattress and a bolster without a pillowcase, on the wall a washstand without water with a tin basin beneath it, then a rough wooden table, a few nails driven into the wall on which to hang clothes, a burnt-down candle stub, an empty glass for tea and for rinsing the mouth, in the corner of the room the obligatory black Byzantine Madonna painted on a gold ground and set in a gilded frame, and on the table a long, indeed an extra-long bill for my stay. This last item of furnishing constitutes the only difference in the appearance of my room between the time I moved in and now, as I leave it. Still, the sight moves me, and I am in a pensive mood as I direct my steps down to the street. With hands behind my back, I stroll up to the heights along which the city stretches, climbing higher and higher until I finally reach a hilltop some five hundred feet above the town, where a magnificent panoramic view of it and of the sea captivates me. A boulder shaped like a bench invites one to take a seat, and with the warm afternoon sun at my back I sit upon my stone and “think, leg crossed over leg . . .”

The scene at my feet is full of atmosphere. Without question! To the left, the gently rippling water of the harbour basin; to the right, the expanse of the already somewhat livelier Amur Bay. Between the two lies the Muravyov-Amursky Peninsula, on which the city of Vladivostok stands. Looking out past the spit of land that encloses the Golden Horn, one gazes across to the dusky steel-blue of the open sea, and directly before me the sun reflects its rays off the pale cliff faces of the treeless islands that form the extension of the peninsula. To the left in the harbour lie warships and merchant vessels; among the latter, several fly foreign flags, some German, some Japanese. Down there to the left, a massive hull can be seen moored at the pier of the “Volunteer Fleet”. The other merchant ships, insofar as they are not discharging cargo by means of lighters and thus lying at anchor in the harbour, have warped alongside the long public quay that adjoins the railway buildings. The warships anchor further inside the harbour; one is currently undergoing repairs in the large dry dock, which has been pumped completely dry. The warships have only just returned from Amur Bay, where they have been manoeuvring; one of the mighty ironclads is still smoking inside the canal cut through one of the offshore islands to connect Amur Bay with the harbour basin proper. Down on the shore, however, it teems with people, carts, and small boats. A merchant steamer that arrived recently is swarmed by numerous passenger boats rowed by Shandong Chinese.

{p. 155}

When the passengers come ashore, it is again Chinese who take their luggage from them and load it onto their small two-wheeled carts. They are joined by Korean porters offering their services, but they cannot accomplish as much as the Chinese. Koreans are therefore, on average, employed in the lowest positions—as domestic servants and dishwashers, street sweepers, and farmhands—while the Chinese are at least waiters, coachmen, servants, and boatmen, thus performing work that is socially more highly regarded. And then, it is the rule among the Chinese that in a relatively short time they achieve independence. In any case, the retail trade, insofar as it serves the needs of the colored races, is largely in their hands; even the *izvozchik* occasionally buys something from the Chinese, and remarkably even prefers to do so rather than from his own countryman, who competes with the Chinese as a booth-keeper and petty shopkeeper, as a seller of boots and matches, Japanese seltzer water, and ribbons, cord, buttons, needles, and so forth. So it teems and surges and crawls down there around the harbor, and up to me drifts nothing more than a faint humming and buzzing, from which only now and then the shouts of Chinese coolies heaving a bale of goods, or a broken chord of a harmonica played by a Russian drayman before the serving counter of his regular booth-keeper, steals quietly up. Along the main street, however, droshkies rush by; now and then a horseman is seen; and now, along the railway track from the Amur Bay, a train slowly approaches, momentarily hidden from view in a cutting as it draws near, then crosses the main street at grade and pulls into the station complex situated by the harbor basin. From up here the city itself looks rather bare; between the individual houses, insofar as they have not been crowded into the main street, the Svetlanskaya, making full use of the available space, there is a striking amount of room. Broad areas, mostly wasteland, a smaller part built upon, separate the houses from one another, especially in the higher parts of the city. From up here one also sees all the more clearly how few stone buildings the city actually possesses. Still, this perception does not detract from the overall impression one gains of the whole picture at our feet. On the contrary, the mostly light colors of the houses—on which only the main timbers, the window frames and mullions, and the shutters are painted green—enliven the scene, which suffers at most from a lack of natural greenery. In return, however, the mainland heights and individual islands bear forest at some distance, and the green grassy slopes shimmering darkly in the distance contrast with the bare heights on whose slopes Vladivostok is built and by which it is surrounded. An ingenious Frenchman takes advantage of the abundance of grass on one of the islands near the harbor by having established and tending a herd of cattle on the island, which sustains itself and multiplies there almost free of cost. In this way Vladivostok always has fresh milk and good butter close at hand. This undertaking also has the advantage that cattle plagues frequently rage among the Russian peasants' cattle, which, given the lack of any comprehensive inspection, compels caution in the consumption of milk and dairy products brought to market by the Russian peasants. Through this isolation, the herd is said to have actually remained healthy.

{p. 156}

An important part of the landscape would still be missing were one not to mention the numerous forts, one of which rises right above my head on a hill that overtops mine by a good deal. But directly in front of me, too, I look into one of the forts; I distinctly make out the sentry on the rampart. On the heights around the harbor basin as well, and further out on the islands—everywhere one sees the sharp lines of earthworks and glacis behind which batteries lie concealed.

On the flagship down in the harbor a signal sounds; immediately afterwards the other warships repeat it, and the pennants and flags slowly come down. It is six o'clock in the evening. The merchant steamers haul down their flags as well. The sun is still shining brightly; it is being quite generous. It is, after all, midsummer, and Vladivostok lies on the same latitude as Venice and Nice. It will still be a little while before the sun sinks down back there in the Suifun Valley, behind the mountains over yonder whose contours and hues blur when viewed against the light, to shine for the people far to the west—in Venice and in Nice, in Berlin and in St. Petersburg. Indeed, what time is it over there now, over there in Europe, with our loved ones at home? Six o'clock in the evening in Vladivostok—that is about nine o'clock in the morning by Central European Time! In Petersburg it is an hour later, so the time difference between Petersburg and Vladivostok is still roughly eight hours. A third of the day, then! Only in eight hours will it be six in the evening in Petersburg. Just imagine what it means when, in a single empire that moreover forms a contiguous landmass, it is six o'clock in the evening for eight consecutive hours every day.

Yes, Russia is vast! Had the Trans-Siberian Railway been executed as originally planned, namely along the northern bank of the Amur and the Shilka, the distance from Vladivostok to St. Petersburg would have amounted to 9,877 versts—that is, since one verst equals 1.067 kilometers, 10,538.76 kilometers of direct railway line. The connecting line through Manchuria, which was carried out instead of the Amur project, reduces this figure by several hundred; nevertheless, even then the distance is still over 10,000 kilometers. Indeed, Russia is vast! Astonishingly vast by our standards! People admire this vastness, and the Russian himself admires it most of all. It is above all to this giant body that Russia owes its prestige in the eyes of nations. People marvel at its size and generalize this astonishment to Russia’s standing in other matters as well, since generalizing about things one does not know thoroughly is, after all, simply part of human nature. Yet one ought really to marvel at Russia only as one might marvel at a human being with a particularly long body, without giving thought to his other moral and intellectual merits. As soon as one recovers from this purely external impression, however, and grows accustomed to the fact that this size is ultimately nothing more than a purely external attribute, like shortness in a dwarf, one will presumably proceed by degrees to examine the giant body’s heart, kidneys, and brain. A {p. 157} [Illustration: View of Vladivostok (p. 151).]

{p. 158}

fairly obvious starting point for this examination, however, is usually already provided by the ratio between the extent of the land and population density. In our case, the fact is already quite sobering that Russia in Asia possesses fully a third of the continent, yet on this third lives only a forty-second of Asia’s total population. By way of comparison, China may be cited, which occupies roughly a quarter of the Asian continent while housing half of Asia’s population. Or, to put the ratio differently, on a piece of land where 28 people live in China, only a single person lives in Russian Asia. In this calculation, Asiatic Russia is taken as a whole. The disparity becomes even more glaring, however, if we consider Siberia alone. The land area of Siberia comprises roughly three-quarters of Russia’s total possessions in Asia, yet Siberia’s population in number barely reaches a quarter of the total population of Asiatic Russia. Therefore, on the same patch of earth where one person lives in Siberia, an average of nine people live in the rest of Asiatic Russia. If, however, we correlate Siberia with China in terms of population density, the resulting ratio is 1:252! The population density of Siberia is roughly such that there is one inhabitant for approximately three square kilometers: barely five million people live in Siberian Russia. In his work *Die Erde und ihre Bewohner* [The Earth and Its Inhabitants], the statistician Reclus distributes these five million among no fewer than 59 different races, among whom the Muscovites even retain an absolute majority in numbers. Furthermore, the population of Siberia—which in head count thus remains roughly one and a half million below that of London—is divided by religion into Greek Catholics, Shamanists, Buddhists, Mohammedans, Jews, and unclassifiable heathens. Seen in the cold light of day, the brain of this giant body of Siberia is thus quite tiny and heterogeneous, and it will probably require a very considerable influx of blood from Europe before this bit of brain will be in a position to govern the gigantic limbs properly. Whether the Trans-Siberian Railway, which is intended after all to become the main artery of this invigorating bloodstream, will actually be able to fulfill this task?

Once before, all these lands for which the Tsar’s word is law, and across which the sun travels on its way to Moscow, served a single mighty name. Over there in the Suifen Valley once lay the fabled kingdom of Bohai, whose star sank before the realm of Manchur; this realm of Manchur, however, perished in the gale that swept the hordes of Genghis Khan across the Asian kingdoms and steppes just as it did across Europe, overturning thrones and blowing away borders here as there. To be sure, the differences between the two meeting invasions were great. The yellow tidal wave came swiftly, overwhelming everything, sweeping everything along, but then receded—from Western Europe at least—almost as swiftly as it had come. The counter-current covered in centuries the same path that the first wave had inundated in a few years, and—held its ground. There was, however, no system in either of them, and the movement from west {p. 159} to east in particular, when viewed in its course, appears so unstoppable only because it met with too little—one might almost say with no resistance whatsoever—that could have halted it. In any case, the Russians had an easier time conquering Siberia and advancing to the Pacific than Genghis Khan had in throwing the European realms off balance.

The history of the conquest of Siberia by Russia is rich in features characteristic of Russianness as such. To understand it, it is not enough merely to commit to memory a few dates and the names of a few pioneers to whom, in a purely pragmatic account, the narrative of events is usually tied. These alone do not provide an accurate picture. For what, after all, does it matter what name was borne by this or that Cossack ataman who undertook this or that expedition to determine the course of this or that river, or of what consequence is it whether one knows the place where some insignificant skirmish with natives occurred, or the like? Actual fighting marks essentially only the beginning and the end of Russia's advance into Siberia: in the west, when it was a matter of gaining a firm foothold beyond the Urals, and in the east, when a unified and organized state entity, China, presented an effective “halt” to the Russian advance that had to be overcome. Within these bounds, however, every fur hunter, every Cossack demanding tribute from the natives of a lower cultural level, carried the borders of his fatherland farther out toward the regions of the north and east, where only the waters of the Pacific and the Arctic Ocean finally called a halt to his steps. Siberia was not a land that had to be conquered, but one that needed only to be taken into possession. Proof that the acquisition of Siberia by the Russians proceeded relatively quickly is shown by the very fact that, barely more than fifty years after Yermak's bands had first crossed the Urals (1581), Cossacks had already reached the Sea of Okhotsk and founded on its shores the town of the same name. Thus, as early as the year 1638, Russia possessed a port on the Pacific Ocean. The struggle that unfolded over the centuries that followed, and which, with frequently shifting fortunes, ultimately led to Russia recently reaching the goal of its East Asian expansionist policy, had as its object the push toward more hospitable regions, toward the south—which practically speaking amounted to the struggle for the most direct possible connection with the sea, and indeed with a sea open year-round, an ice-free sea. Yet this task and this struggle entered the consciousness of Russian policy only a little over forty years ago. Until then, the government had been pushed forward more by the impulsive advance of Russian subjects—which it would often enough have preferred to restrain rather than encourage—than it had driven the advance itself. East Asian expansionist policy was consciously pursued politically and economically by the Russian government only after Muravyov was appointed Governor-General of Eastern Siberia, and through him the acquisition of the Amur region was systematically resumed. One can therefore speak of a Russian colonial policy in Siberia only since that time; it was initiated around the year 1860. And there is one more thing to add: for objective reasons, one can essentially speak of a Russian colonial policy only in the {p. 169} Amur region. By its nature, this territory is indeed an economic, political, and geographical colony for Russia, whereas the rest of Siberia, Western as well as Eastern Siberia, lacks the geographical and economic prerequisites to be regarded as anything other than an essential part of Russia. The Ural Mountains are a historical boundary between Europe and Asia, not a climatic or general geographical one; but since it is in any case impossible to place Russia culturally on a level with the European states that stand, on average, at a much higher standard, it ultimately makes no difference if we thereby assign Russia the position that actually belongs to it in geographical and cultural terms, namely that of a European-Asian state. The reasons why the Amur territory is to be distinguished as a colony from the rest of the Siberian possessions in Asia will, it is hoped, become clear from what follows. First, however, I should like to return once more to old Genghis Khan, and with him to the earliest beginnings of Russia's acquisition of Siberia. Before we penetrate into the colossus of Siberia itself, it will nevertheless be necessary to touch briefly upon a few historical questions, since without this much of what we shall see and hear would likely remain incomprehensible.

{p. 161}

[Illustration: Vladivostok: Monument to Nevelskoy, the first Russian commodore in East Asian waters.]

## Chapter III. Russia's Advance from the Ural to the Pacific Ocean.

### 1. The Belt of the World.

Even for contemporaries of the fifteenth century, the world beyond the Ural Mountains—the "Belt"—was "boarded up with planks," as the fairy tale goes. And fairy-tale-like indeed were the tales told of that world. There, people imagined the dwellings of the Laestrygonians and Cyclopes of the fifteenth century, and the reports of the few who had dared to peek through a knothole in that wooden fence into that unknown land of legend served only to confuse notions even further. But the onslaught of the Mongols, who were viewed like the legions of Beelzebub, came so swiftly and passed so swiftly that an enrichment of the European world's knowledge of the land and peoples beyond the mountains could not be expected from it. The first clearer notions of the regions we today designate as Siberia were gained through the extensive expeditions undertaken by fur trappers and traders on behalf of merchants from the city of Novgorod (Newtown). In general, for centuries the fur trade was the driving, and for a long time even the sole, economic motive for the {p. 162} discovery and opening up of Siberia. Novgorod's significance for modern-day Russia is extraordinarily great. In Novgorod, a merchant aristocracy developed that was not only in possession of great wealth, but also knew how to maintain its political independence for a long time amidst the struggles between Poles, Lithuanians, Swedes, Muscovites, and Tatars. Novgorod the Great was in its day the commercial center *kat’ exochn* for the European lands east of the Vistula, and the proud Slavic Augsburg was therefore also capable of extending its political power far to the east, right up to the Urals, at a time when the Grand Duchy of Moscow could not yet contemplate taking up the struggle with powerful Novgorod in addition to its numerous foreign and domestic enemies. The latter founded on the upper Kama the colony of Vyatka, north of what was then still Tatar Kazan, one of the remnants of the dynasty of Genghis Khan. Only in the year 1478, under Ivan III, had Moscow grown strong enough to subjugate Novgorod. With that, its colony of Vyatka likewise passed to Moscow (1479), and thus the state that later became Russia acquired for the first time a common border with the lands beyond the Urals.

Ten years later, an attempt was made to gain a firm foothold in these lands. To be sure, toward the southeast and east the remnants of the realm of Genghis Khan still barred further advance. In the fifteenth century, these remnants were still the kingdoms of Kazan, Astrakhan, Khiva, Bukhara, and the territories of the Nogai and Kirghiz Hordes.
