But in the north of Western Asia there still dwelt Ostyaks, who had been pushed northward by the Mongol swarms and were now hard pressed by the Mongols, who were likewise expanding northward during their transition from nomadism to settled life. For protection against the Tatars—a name that subsequently always replaces the earlier "Mongols"—the Ostyaks now called upon their bordering neighbor, who had only recently demonstrated his power through his conquest of the Vyatka territory. In return, they offered tribute, a form in which suzerainty was customary expressed at that time. However, this Ostyak realm lay quite far north, as the very name "Obdorsk," i.e., "mouth of the Ob," indicates. An expedition that Ivan III dispatched there in the year 1499 brought back in terms of success nothing, apart from a number of furs and a few new fables, that was deemed worthy of record. Nor did this earliest acquisition of the land of Obdorsk gain any other significance for the acquisition of Siberia than that of an inherently rather vague episode.

More important is the fact that in the year 1552 Russia annexed to its land possessions the Khanate of Kazan, which had already been tributary to it since 1487, and that in the year 1556 it also conquered Kazan's Tatar sister kingdom, the Khanate of Astrakhan. With that, it found itself in possession of the entire course of the Volga, furthermore of access to the Caspian Sea and—what is of greater interest to us here—an unbroken common border between Europe and Asia, from the Caspian Sea right up to the Arctic Ocean.

It now appears completely out of the question that with so long a border, and with so low a natural divide as the Ural mountain range with its flat heights and gentle slopes, no traffic should have taken place across this border {p. 163} in the second half of the fifteenth century. Russian historiography extols Yermak's invasion of the land east of the Urals, which took place thirty years later, simultaneously as the discovery of Siberia, ranking it on an equal footing with the deeds of a Cortés, and non-Russian historians follow this presentation almost without exception. However, in judging these deeds, one must always take into account that commercial and economic relations between Russians and Ostyaks are demonstrable more than a century earlier—relations that ultimately led to a political relationship; furthermore, that in all probability mutual trade connections existed between the realms of Kazan and Astrakhan on the one hand and the kindred Tatars east of the Urals on the other, connections that were taken up by the conquerors, the Russians, or at least continued under the Russian flag by the Tatars who remained in the country. Furthermore, the fact is reported that under Ivan IV the ruler of the land of "Yugra"*), by which was understood at that time the entire land beyond the Urals, named Yadigar, placed himself under Russian protection against the Tatars who were pressing him; it is not known whether he was himself a Tatar or an Ostyak, presumably he was the latter.

All of this, however, already presupposes a mutual acquaintance, and the European border dwellers of the Ural region certainly possessed fairly comprehensive knowledge of the lands beyond the Urals by the time of Yermak’s invasion, even if this knowledge was not reflected in the Russian chronicles of that era or in the minds of the rulers in Moscow. It may therefore also be assumed that when the Stroganovs commissioned the Cossack ataman Yermak to conquer the land of Yugra, they were by no means taking a leap into the dark, but were well aware of their plans and of what might come out of it for them. It should be added, moreover, that although the aforementioned Ostyak chieftain Yediger sent tribute to the Tsar, he never received any support from him against the Tatars; rather, in the course of a raid, he lost both life and land to them without Russia troubling itself much about the matter—even though this was the era of Ivan IV, to whom humanistic-sentimental historiography has indeed given the epithet “the Terrible,” but who is nonetheless one of the most formidable figures among rulers. Without Peter the Great, no Europeanized Russia; without Ivan, no Russia! He is the founder of the tsardom, which to this day remains the key to the secret of Russia’s strength and political agility. To him can also be traced the close union of the Muscovite realm with the Greek Catholic Church—two of the sturdy foundation pillars upon which the Russian state edifice rests. Ivan’s mission and significance, however, lay primarily in the domestic realm; Muscovite expansion as far as the Urals was in truth merely an imposed struggle over the eastern border, which he had to undertake in order to gain a free hand to establish peace and order at home. This also explains why Ivan himself considered his political task toward the east of the empire to be completed once he had incorporated the territories of Kazan and Astrakhan into the Muscovite state {p. 161} and used them, as it were, as a buffer against any potential Tatar onslaught from the east that might have been directed against the Muscovite heartland. He was content if virtually unknown peoples east of the Urals delivered a meager consignment of animal skins to him each year as tribute. Yet he hardly gave serious thought to ever offering any return for this tribute in the form of protection for these subject tribes against Tatar encroachments; indeed, he did not even undertake the defense of the eastern frontier of the newly acquired territories of Kazan and Astrakhan and the old colony of Vyatka on the part of the state, but instead left it to private individuals, who in turn recouped their costs by exploiting the authorization to defend the national border toward the east through sending more or less regular minor expeditions into the eastern frontier regions with the assignment of collecting “tribute” from the natives. In practice, however, these expeditions were regular raiding parties, which served as aggressive measures for securing the border, but even more so for enriching the wholesale merchants entrusted with the protection of that border. For them, the protection of the eastern border was thus simultaneously, or primarily, a business. This, too, was and remained one of the fundamental principles of the Russian advance into Siberia until the time when the Russian state as such applied a proper colonial policy in Siberia. Authorized and unauthorized persons alike pushed farther and farther to the east, exacting levies in the form of furs and natural produce from the weak native tribes. In many cases, the natives yielded these levies under the pressure of the moral coercion of a stronger race, but more often they gave way to the brutal force of marauders. All indications suggest that such minor expeditions—which by no means ran counter to the morality of the time—already took place during the first part of the sixteenth century from the settlements along the Volga and Kama, without, however, having acquired any greater significance. In any event, it is certain that authority to do so was granted to certain prominent colonists soon after the annexation of the former Novgorod colony of Vyatka. Here, another characteristic trait of Russian colonization also comes into consideration: namely, the tendency and extraordinary capacity of the Russian race to intermingle with alien races, and above all with inferior ones. In this instance, through the annexation of former Tatar territories, there existed a number of Tatar princes toward whom Russia had certain obligations, while at the same time it was desirable to transform them into useful members of the Russian nation. One such family of Tatar origin, descended from a mirza lineage, was the Stroganov family, already Russified by the time of Ivan IV, whose estates on the Kama—the longest eastern tributary of the Volga, having its source in the Urals—dated back to the days of Ivan III. In the course of time, this family had also attained power and prestige under the new state of affairs. Ivan IV concluded a formal treaty with them. He extended the Stroganovs’ concession on the upper Kama, permitted them to establish colonies, fell timber, recruit laborers, and build saltworks, and exempted them from all taxes for twenty years, imposing on them in return, however, the obligation to defend the eastern border of the realm. They also received the authority to establish blockhouse stations along the frontiers and {p. 165} to maintain an adequate armed force. This was the authorization for the aforementioned raids, which now began to assume political significance—specifically from the moment when chance brought the Stroganovs a very considerable number of reckless mercenaries in these otherwise only sparsely populated regions. Robbers, murderers, thieves, adventurers, rabble, escaped convicts—Germans, Russians, Poles, Lithuanians—and all of them summed up under the euphemistic designation of “Cossacks”: these were the pioneers of the Russian advance into Siberia. Their captain was named Vasily Timofeyevich and was known simply by his nickname, “Yermak.” In German, this means “millstone,” and stems from the fact that Vasily had been a boatman in his youth and had usually been required to prepare food for the Volga sailors, which included the preliminary grinding of the grain. Yermak, the river pirate and Cossack ataman, today possesses a magnificent monument created by Antokolsky; he ranks among the national heroes of Russia and has even been admitted among the saints of the Orthodox Church. From robber captain to national saint—a long step! Yermak was more fortunate than Karl Moor, but in return he “stole a kingdom as well,” so as to purchase with it an indulgence from church and state for his long debt of sin.

::: notes
*) Yugra appears more as a geographical than as a political concept.
:::

The Cossack plays an important role in the history of Russian expansion. It was Cossacks who conquered the Khanate of Sibir; we find them as the first Russian pioneers on the borders of India, the first on the Sea of Okhotsk, the first in the Amur region, the first in Manchuria. Their sotnias fought against the Chinese at Tianjin and marched victorious into Beijing alongside the others.

In foreign eyes, Cossackdom is often regarded as an expression of the despotic form of government, as the bodyguard of tsarism, belonging to it as Jesuitism belongs to the papacy. What is true in this notion is that Russia's Asian successes followed on the heels of the Cossacks; yet the Cossack was always the representative of untrammeled independence, self-reliance, and personal freedom. In essence, he has remained so down to the present day, even though an invigorated state authority has meanwhile imposed a stricter organization upon him—one that, however, still makes ample concessions to his original character today. The homeland of the Cossack is Little Russia, the cradle of the Russian people. In the course of the struggles of the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries, this had fallen to Lithuania, and the systematic suppression of the Orthodox Church as well as of Russianness in general induced numerous adherents of the Muscovite dynasty to migrate to the lower reaches of the Dnieper. Here, the islands formed by the Dnieper rapids*) provided a particularly welcome refuge, and the river-pirate trade became the most obvious source of livelihood. The influx of recruits that the Dnieper Cossacks received over time must have been very substantial. For soon we find swarms of Cossacks in constant struggle with the neighboring Golden Horde, and this perpetual warfare led the Cossacks to adopt the martial qualities of their neighbors as well. Thus, from original {p. 168} river pirates, they became an equestrian people, the feared lords of the steppe, and in their continuous advance they soon reached the mouth of the Don. Their nautical recollections from the Dnieper were not yet forgotten, and in small boats—mostly mere dugouts with side planks—they rowed out into the Black Sea and plundered Turkish vessels just as they did the Russian and Tatar ones coming down the Don. Yet despite this, they did not disown their descent from the Muscovite race, in whom the inclination toward agriculture predominates! They settled in small communities on the territories wrested from the Turks and Tatars, each choosing its ataman from among its own. They tilled their land, and on the side plundered ships and carried out raids into neighboring Tatar territories: even today it still happens that some Cossack village far off on the Amur gathers its male population one fine day and undertakes a raid into neighboring Chinese territory, which is robbed and plundered.

::: notes
*) Cataract = Zaporog, hence the name "Zaporozhians" for the forebears of the Cossacks.
:::

Thus, in essence, the Cossack has remained true to himself through the centuries. And the struggle that developed quite naturally from proximity to Turks and Tatars arose later from proximity to Ostyaks, Kyrgyz, Kalmyks, Samoyeds, Yakuts, Golds, Daurs, Buryats, Kamchadals, Chinese, etc. Far from being willing tools of despotism, they were, on the contrary, the bearers and pioneers of national independence. And the reason why these independent acquisitions of the Cossacks never grew into independent Cossack states lies partly in the lack of organization among the Cossack communities themselves, and partly in the influence of the Orthodox Church, which was intimately linked with the tsardom. The Cossacks represented the impetuosity of a young race that, fully conscious of its strength, seeks an ever wider field for the application and exercise of that strength, and gladly takes advantage of its distance from government authority to give free rein to its wild energies. The government, however, was always clever enough to harvest the acquisitions of these Cossack swarms as a gain for the state, and to take the administration of the newly won tracts of land into its own hands. This was facilitated by the direct geographical contiguity of the newly acquired territories with the motherland. Thus it came about that separatist cravings, such as we encounter, for instance, in numerous English colonies, could not emerge at all, despite the wildness and lawlessness of the conquerors, who were always pioneers only on the frontiers, never attained a consolidated settled existence, and surrendered the conquered and traversed regions to a peasant population that followed hard on their heels, accustomed to sedentary life and authority. Thus the government could unconcernedly let these unruly bands have their way for the time being—though, to be sure, only until the moment arrived when the borders of the empire had been pushed forward to bulwarks that were initially insurmountable: the sea or resilient state structures. Then came the need to train these uncivilized, themselves semi-nomadic Cossack hordes in obedience and settled life, and to provide them with an organization that made them useful members of an orderly commonwealth. This was ultimately achieved, albeit with difficulties and often only after fierce struggles.

Men of the kind described were also the companions of Yermak, whom he had induced to cross with him from the Don over to the Volga, the river that, since the conquest {p. 167} of Astrakhan and Kazan, had developed into the most important trade route across the Russian continent. Yermak’s bands soon became a general scourge here. Navigation ultimately suffered so severely from rampant banditry that the merchants lodged complaints with the tsar. Ivan therefore outlawed the daring Cossack captain along with his Cossacks—among whom various fugitives and rabble whose pasts were not questioned had in the meantime gathered—and dispatched troops to rid the Volga of the troublesome pirates. Under the pressure of these measures, Yermak preferred to shift the scene of his activity further upriver, and thus he gradually arrived at the mouth of the Kama. He also moved up this river and reached his closer homeland, the estates of the Stroganovs. There can scarcely be any doubt that the newcomers continued their trade here as well, and would soon have become a severe burden to the Stroganov estates. The latter therefore killed two birds with one stone by taking Yermak’s bands and Yermak himself, some 800*) men in all, into their service. They thereby freed their own estates from a scourge, but at the same time extended their power beyond the Urals, having obtained authorization to do so a short time earlier through a special rescript from Ivan. To be sure, he had had in mind only the defense of Russian territories, not an offensive. The Stroganovs were thus playing a game of their own; when Ivan heard that they had taken the outlawed and notorious brigands into their service and intended to attack tribes that were allegedly willing to pay tribute, he was greatly incensed and ordered Yermak’s immediate recall. Yet news from the Urals to Moscow and messages back to the Urals took months in those days, and by the time the tsar’s order reached the Stroganovs, it was too late. The ataman was already beyond the Urals. The Stroganovs played va banque and won. Yermak sailed with his men in boats as far up the Chusovaya as possible, then had the boats dragged overland across the gentle heights of the Ural Mountains until he reached the Tura, and with it the river basin of the Ob. This occurred in 1580. In the following two years, he conquered the Ostyak realm, the former Yugra, which had already acknowledged Russian suzerainty in the time of Yadigar. It had in the meantime been conquered by the Tatar khan Kuchum and was ruled from his capital, Isker or Sibir, situated on the Tobol. The city of Sibir, in the vicinity of which Tobolsk later arose, gave the entire land all the way to the Pacific Ocean the name “Siberia.” Yermak sent word of his victories not only to the Stroganovs but also to the tsar, requested amnesty for himself and his comrades, and offered the tsar in return a new kingdom that only awaited his voivodes. The amnesty was granted; as a special token of tsarist favor, the former pirate captain received from Ivan a cloak that had adorned the shoulders of the ruler of all the Russias himself, along with a substantial gift of money. Soon the voivode arrived as well, and Yermak meanwhile conquered with his Cossacks the land extending to the Irtysh. On one of his expeditions he fell {p. 168} into the hands of his old blind enemy Kuchum, and drowned in the Irtysh while trying to save himself by swimming. This occurred in August 1581.

::: notes
*) Krahmer gives the number as 6,000, Yorck von Wartenburg as 7,000; elsewhere one consistently finds 800. The former two figures are too high to be probable. Krahmer even records a further reinforcement of 6,000 men, so that Yermak had more than 11,000 men at his disposal altogether. But that is certainly incorrect.
:::

His death was a great loss for the Russian cause. But the spell had been broken, the stone set rolling. The fame of Yermak’s deeds had attracted a significant influx of people who had nothing to lose, but all the more to hope to gain. In the meantime, the Tatars fell into fighting among themselves, and it was relatively easy for the regular Russian troops, who soon marched in, to deal with them one by one. The Cossacks, meanwhile, ranged ever further; soon they were on the Yenisei, soon on the Lena; nowhere did they encounter any further substantial resistance on the part of the natives. Isker or Sibir was the key to a land of many thousands of square miles in whose forests the sable dwelt, and from which news of other riches, including gold and ivory, soon reached the West. Yet the fur hunter, the “tribute-collecting” Cossack, and the gold prospector were followed by the merchant, and the in part quite dismal social conditions in Russia entailed by serfdom made Siberia appear as the land of freedom, to which peasants and serfs who had fled bondage soon escaped as well. Thus the first agricultural colonies arose in Siberia, established partly in the immediate vicinity of the numerous fortified military stations, the “ostrogs,” or the winter quarters of tax collectors, the “zimovyes,” but partly also somewhere out of all contact in a quiet, fertile spot in the forest. Supplies were soon transported regularly and in varying volume along a road that slowly pushed eastward from the west via Tobolsk, Tomsk, and Yeniseysk, initially reaching the Sea of Okhotsk via Yakutsk, but later branching off toward Irkutsk and pushed further eastward from there until it terminated at the Shilka. Here the waters of this river, and later of the Amur, then took over the role of Siberia’s main commercial artery, which until then the northern highway had had to fulfill. In recent times, this latter route has also been followed, with a few deviations, by the railway.

In the circumstances described lie the causes of Russia’s so successful expansion in Asia. Over the course of centuries, its success was thus not due to systematic government policy, which set in only much later, but to the greed of Cossacks perpetually on the warpath, who found their chief occupation in the exploitation of native peoples; to the advance of the trapper, the prospector, and the merchant; and to the longing for freedom of the peasant who had escaped slavery at home. Parallels with the conquest of North America readily suggest themselves. Here as there, the continuous low-intensity warfare with the native, who is pushed ever deeper into the forests, accompanied by the no less fierce competitive struggle of all against all. It often happened that Cossacks collecting the fur tribute, the “yasak,” from the natives from different ostrogs came into conflict with one another. Frequently enough, this led to murder and manslaughter. Insofar, however, as government policy comes into consideration, intervening case by case on behalf of the Cossacks’ acquisitions, {p. 169} the dictum of the geographer Fr. von Richthofen holds true: “It is Russia’s destiny in Asia that it must everywhere and constantly seek to expand its borders in order to enhance the exploitation and security of its possessions existing at any given time.” Nomadic peoples are indeed an insecure frontier. Count York describes Russia’s restless advance in Central Asia as “the hunt for a frontier.” The image is well chosen and applicable

[Illustration: Khabarovsk: Artillery collection in front of the museum.]

also to Russia’s advance in northern Asia. Here too, “one cannot imagine Russia’s forward progress as having been guided by a systematic idea of conquest. Rather, matters usually stood so that the government in Petersburg would have had to expend far more energy to halt the movement in Asia than to carry it forward. The latter took place, as it were, of its own accord, in accordance with the commercial interests of the Russians and the personal interests of the local Russian power holders, and often enough confronted the Russian government with new, unwelcome, and difficult tasks. And then the fact made itself felt that an orderly state cannot in the long run tolerate predatory nomads on its borders, because by such people the very concept of a border is rendered illusory.”

{p. 170}

If we bear in mind these various motives and factors of Russia’s advance into Siberia, we also have in them the history of this advance, frequently repeating itself in its details, as it unfolded over the first two and a half centuries. The advance to the seacoast in the Far East proceeded relatively quickly; it took scarcely half a century. But the route by which this goal was reached ran quite far north across the Asian continent. For in the southeast Russia reached a frontier whose resistance was formidable enough to halt the advance for two centuries: in the Amur region the Russian intruders collided with the Chinese state, which was then at the height of its power and military capability. To cross this frontier by force, Russian power was not yet strong enough, nor was it yet in the nature of the Russian advance to take up a struggle against extraordinary difficulties. A change in these tactics occurred only when Russia had become a European great power and thereby adopted the tasks of such a power in the colonial sphere. Not until the mid-nineteenth century did Muravyov deliberately take up the struggle for these regions, which two centuries earlier the Russians had already temporarily annexed during their sudden “rush” across Northern Asia, but had been forced to return to their lawful owner in the Treaty of Nerchinsk. For the intervening period, a few regesta will suffice.

### 2. Chronology of Siberian History from the Death of Yermak to the Appearance of Muravyov.

1584. At Yermak's death, the boundaries of the Russian acquisitions in Siberia are formed to the northeast by the confluence of the Ob and Irtysh and the small river Narym, to the southeast by the Ishish, a tributary of the Irtysh, and to the south by the Tobol and the Tura.
1586. Founding of Tyumen and Tobolsk.
1590. As an experiment, 30 peasant families from Solvychegodsk are settled beyond the Urals.
1593. First exile of political criminals to Western Siberia.
1600. Establishment of a postal route between Moscow and Transbaikalia. Tsar Fyodor Alekseyevich settles peasant volunteers from the Perm region in Western Siberia on favorable terms, specifically along the rivers Tura, Tavda, Tobol, Irtysh, and Ob and their tributaries.
1600. Founding of Turinsk.
1604. Founding of Tomsk.
1618. Founding of Kuznetsk and Yeniseysk.
1628. Founding of Krasnoyarsk on the Yenisei.
1630. Cossacks reach the Stanovoy Range in the Far East.
1630. 100 men and 150 women and girls are settled in and around Tobolsk.
1632. Yakutsk is founded.
1635. Olyokminsk on the Lena is founded.
1636–1642. Traveling along the Lena, the Cossack Busa reaches the coast of the Arctic Ocean, sails out upon it, and discovers the mouths of the Olenyok, Yana, and Indigirka, as well as a new tribe on their banks, the Yukaghirs.
1640. The Siberian *yasak* yields, among other things, 6,800 sable pelts.
1641. Vasily Vlasyev subjugates the Buryats south of Yakutsk.
1643. Vasily Poyarkov is the first to reach the Amur, and by way of it the Sea of Okhotsk, traveling from Yakutsk along the Lena and then the rivers Aldan, Uchur, and Gonam, then crossing the Stanovoy Range and sailing down the Bryanta and Zeya. He returns overland from the coast to Yakutsk.

{p. 171}

1644. Mikhail Stadukhin discovers the Kolyma and founds Nizhnekolymsk near its mouth on the Arctic Ocean. Nearby, he discovers rich deposits of mammoth bones and tusks, the extraction of which becomes a new branch of industry.

1647. Ivan Posichakov collects the yasak from the Buryats inhabiting the regions around Lake Baikal, travels south along the Ingoda, and is the first to reach Mongolia and its capital, Urga. Here he induces Khan Yezen to send an embassy to the Tsar, which departs for Moscow the following year.

1647. Ivan Afanasyev, with 51 Cossacks, defeats more than 1,000 Tungus living between the Sea of Okhotsk and the Stanovoy, discovers the Okhota, and founds the ostrog of Okhotsk on its banks, which faces fluctuating fortunes in constant fighting with the Tungus.

1648. Semyon Dezhnyov is the first to sail around the eastern tip of Asia from the mouth of the Kolyma, discovering the strait later rediscovered by Bering and named after him. He finds the coastal river Anadyr, on whose banks he establishes a *zimovye*. Meanwhile, two other explorers, Motora and Stadukhin, have likewise set out overland from Yakutsk, but arrive at the Kolyma later than Dezhnyov. Stadukhin in particular begrudges him the glory of having been first, robs him of the yasak collected along the way, and drives him off. Further south, Dezhnyov scratches out a living for a long time on the proceeds of seal hunting.

1649–1653. The Yakutsk merchant Khabarov equips an expedition to the Zeya and the lower Amur at his own expense; he drives out the resident Daurs, but comes into serious conflict with the Chinese for the first time in Siberian history. His discoveries are received with great interest in Europe; he himself is summoned there to make a report, and the government equips an expedition under Stepanov, which meets with mixed success yet leads to no permanent results. Stepanov himself falls in 1657 in battle with the Chinese, who also completely wipe out his force.

1652. Founding of Irkutsk.

1654. Founding of Nerchinsk. Pashkov, the voivode of Yeniseysk, undertakes an expedition to Lake Baikal and the land beyond the lake. He reaches the Nercha and founds Nerchinsk on its banks.

1665. Nikifor Chernigovsky, who murdered Voivode Obukhov of Ilimsk and is therefore pursued by the authorities, flees with a number of deportees to the Amur Valley and founds the first Russian colony on the upper reaches of the Amur, Albazin, which subsequently becomes the Russians' strongest stronghold against Chinese attacks. The Kangxi Emperor of China protests against this renewed attempt by the Russians to establish themselves in the Amur Valley. Milovanov is sent to Beijing as the first Russian envoy. Meanwhile, Nikifor Chernigovsky and the other fugitives of Albazin are pardoned; the settlement itself is officially recognized by the voivode of Nerchinsk and brought under administration by Ivan Poltekov in 1674.

1675. Nikolai Spafary is sent as an envoy to Beijing on account of repeated protests from China and is received cordially there. In the following year he is sent there again to demand exclusive navigation rights on the Zeya and the lower Amur, albeit without success.

1681. Ignatius Milovanov, who undertakes an exploratory expedition to the lower Amur valley on the orders of the voivode of Nerchinsk, discovers on the left bank of the Amur near the mouth of the Zeya the Chinese town of Aigun, which had meanwhile been built as a bulwark against the Russians.

1683. Grigory Mylnikov is commissioned with a hundred Cossacks to travel down the Amur to bring reinforcements to a Russian settlement on the Amgun, a left tributary of the lower Amur. Near Aigun, he and his Cossacks are captured by a strong Chinese military force and taken to Beijing. (Here the prisoners formed the nucleus of a Russian colony that remains there to this day, though it has since become almost completely Mongolized.) In the following year, two of the captured Russians are sent to Albazin with a letter demanding the immediate evacuation of the place. The Russians resolve to hold the post.

1685. A Chinese force of allegedly 18,000 men attacks Albazin, which is defended for ten days by about 500 Cossacks under Tolbuzin. The Russians are granted free withdrawal to Nerchinsk; the Chinese burn down Albazin and the Russian settlements in its vicinity, withdraw downriver, abandon the town of Aigun built on the left bank of the Amur, and build a fortress of the same name three kilometers {p. 172} downriver, now on the right bank of the Amur, garrisoning it with a strong force; the greater part of the Chinese force returns via the Sungari to inner Manchuria.

1685. Ivan Vlasov, voivode of Nerchinsk, who does not agree to the evacuation of Albazin, sends Tolbuzin back, reinforced by a newly arrived Cossack detachment under a German named Beuthen, with orders to rebuild the ostrog. In the spring of 1686, a strong Chinese army appears before Albazin once again. The Russians, however, hold their ground and weaken the Chinese through sorties. In the course of one such sortie, Tolbuzin falls, and Beuthen continues the defense, which is so stubborn that throughout the winter the Chinese conduct the siege only very perfunctorily and conclude an armistice in May 1687, especially since peace negotiations have also been initiated. At the end of August, they abandon the siege entirely, and militarily the Russians remain masters of the situation. Through two special envoys, they demand that the Chinese enter into negotiations on a definitive settlement of the border. In fact, the cessation of hostilities against Albazin is due to an explicit request expressed in Beijing as early as 1686 by the envoys Nikifor Venyukov and Ivan Favorov. In the same year, the Russian peace negotiator Golovin leaves Moscow accompanied by a regiment of Streltsy. Meanwhile, negotiations over the venue of the conference drag on for quite some time.

1689. Not until the summer of 1689 is an agreement reached regarding the location, and two first-class mandarins leave Beijing toward Nerchinsk, where the negotiations are to take place. Although it has been agreed that the mandarins' escort should not be larger than strictly necessary for their protection, the Chinese entourage, which is almost entirely military, is nevertheless estimated at around 9,000 men. Under the pressure of these measures and as a result of Golovin's lack of diplomatic talent, an extraordinarily unfavorable peace for Russia is concluded at the end of August of that year, in the drafting of which two Jesuits brought along by the Chinese as interpreters—Fathers Gerbillon and Pereira—render them good service. Although Albazin has in fact not fallen, Russia surrenders all its possessions in the Amur basin in this first treaty it concludes with China. The border is initially to be formed by the Gorbitsa River, while its continuation to the sea is determined only in general terms. In the west, the Argun is accepted as the border. Albazin is razed, the Russian settlements on the right bank of the Argun are relocated to the left, and crossing the border is threatened with the death penalty. It is permitted, however, for people in possession of passports who pursue exclusively commercial purposes. With this, Russian expansion to the south and east, toward the Amur valley, is paralyzed for a period of over 180 years.

1697. Kamchatka is taken possession of by Vladimir Atlasov. Now the whole of northern Asia is in Russian hands. (The history of the subjugation of Kamchatka is one of the most unpleasant and protracted, partly because of the increasingly rampant lawlessness of the Cossacks, partly because of the rebelliousness of the Koryak and particularly the Yakut population. The voivode of Yakutsk undertakes every conceivable attempt to restore peace and order. Atlasov himself, who was received in Moscow with high honors, but on the return journey cannot resist the temptation to plunder a wealthy Russian merchant, is imprisoned in Yakutsk as punishment, but later, when everything goes wrong in Kamchatka, is sent back there with extensive powers as the only person who can still restore order. Because of his cruelty, he falls victim to the fury of the Cossacks, a fate that a good number of his successors must share.)

1709. 11,000 Swedish prisoners are settled in Siberia by Peter the Great.

1710. Peter the Great abolishes the voyvoda administration in Siberia and introduces governorate administration. Siberia is divided into the following governorates-general: 1. West Siberia (Tobolsk and Tomsk Governorates), 2. East Siberia (Yeniseysk and Irkutsk Governorates along with the Yakutsk Oblast); then later come 3. the Steppe Governorate-General (Akmolinsk and Semipalatinsk Oblasts), as well as in more recent times 4. the Amur Governorate-General (Transbaikalia, Priamurskaya, and Primorskaya Governorates).

1716. Swedish prisoners of war and deportees build ships on the Sea of Okhotsk, on which Kamchatka is reached by water for the first time.

1723. Minjsa Siniojeff penetrates into the ore-rich Altai region.

1725. Peter the Great equips the Great Northern Expedition to explore the Arctic Ocean, which, under Bering, reaches Kamchatka after three years.

1726. Establishment of the first smelting works in the Altai near Kolyvansk, which passes into the possession of the Crown in 1747 and is still the property of the Tsar today.

{p. 173}

1740–1742. Bering and Chirikov explore the west coast of North America.
1744–1745. Settlement of the Tobolsk–Tara, Ishim–Omsk, and Tikhautsky Ostrog–Tomsk tracts with peasants and teamsters.
1745–1761. Discovery and exploration of the Aleutian Islands.
1761. Discovery of the New Siberian Islands by Shalarov.
1762–1780. Settlement of the Tikhautsky Ostrog–Tara tract.
1763. Construction of the mountain road to Yekaterinburg in the Ural mining district.
1768. Andreyev discovers Wrangel Land.
1768–1769. Empress Catherine II dispatches an expedition under Lieutenant Levashov, which visits the Aleutians, and discovers and takes possession of Alaska.
1799. Founding of the Russian-American Company to exploit the Russian possessions in America and the lands on the Bering Sea.
1807. Russians settle at the mouth of what is today the Columbia River.
1812. Russian trappers found a colony at Bodega near present-day San Francisco. (The Russian possessions in North America were sold to the United States in 1867.)
1847. Count Muravyov takes over the administration as Governor-General of East Siberia.

### 3. How the Amur Basin Finally Became Russian.

The history of the Russification of Siberia reveals the remarkable fact that the Chinese border was an even greater obstacle to Russia’s advance than the Pacific Ocean. While Russian trappers, Cossacks, and merchants had long been ranging across the Bering Strait, annexing Alaska to the Russian domain, and in their forward roving had even taken possession of the more southerly coastal stretches of the Pacific Ocean down as far as California before the stream of European immigrants pouring across North America from east to west ever reached these regions, Russian policy endeavoured, without any notable success, to win back from China by the spoonful the advantages that had been surrendered by the bushel in the Treaty of Nerchinsk. Even though Peter the Great had still managed to wrest from his contemporary Kangxi by diplomatic means the concession that a Russian political agent might take up residence in Beijing—incidentally the first and, for 140 years, the last foreign envoy in the northern capital—the death of this last great ruler of China, which followed soon afterwards, brought in its train a xenophobic backlash that once again rendered illusory the advantage just gained by the Russian side. Highly characteristic of the manner in which the two powers negotiated with one another, moreover, was the ceremony attending the reception of the Russian negotiator, Captain Leon Izmaylov, by the Son of Heaven. Since the Russian envoy refused to perform the demanded kowtow, but without it would have been denied an audience with the Emperor and his mission would have remained hopeless, a compromise was reached: first, a high-ranking Chinese mandarin threw himself down before the Tsar’s letter about to be presented, and then Izmaylov performed his kowtow before the Emperor of China! The political agent left behind by him, de Lange, subsequently lived in his house like a prisoner and was eventually expelled from Beijing. The freedom of trade at the border established in the Treaty of Nerchinsk was likewise subjected to chicanery from the Chinese side.

{p. 171}

To contemporaries, the surrender of the Amur region in the Treaty of Nerchinsk meant nothing more than the abandonment of a possession that was of no substantially greater importance to Russia than any other part of Siberia, and which, strictly speaking, had not even really been acquired yet. That changed when the growing importance of the settlements on the Sea of Okhotsk and the Bering Sea increased the need for a viable route connecting them with the west. For the difficult overland route, impassable altogether during the long winter, could no longer satisfy these needs in any way. Resuming the efforts to regain the Amur, at least as a supply route for the Russian possessions on the Pacific Ocean, thus became an *economic* necessity. The last futile attempt to obtain permission from China by amicable means to use the Amur as a waterway dates from 1806. Not until thirty years later had a sufficient number of diverse inducements accumulated to spur Russia to renewed energy. Apart from the purely economic cause already asserted, it was primarily the driving force of an impulsively inclined Governor-General, endowed with extraordinary gifts, that brought about the transformation. At the same time, the successful actions of France and Britain in China exposed the military weakness of the Middle Kingdom, which had been overestimated for centuries. This weakness was further aggravated by the Taiping Rebellion. Russia, moreover, had reason to fear that the European colonial powers, above all Britain, would demand territorial cessions from China that might deprive Russia of the fruits of centuries of labor; and an attack by an Anglo-French fleet on Russian positions on the Pacific coast during the Crimean War opened the eyes of Russian policy to its position in the Far East and impelled it to adopt a faster pace in its measures to secure its possessions there.

The history of the acquisition of this most valuable part of the Russian possessions in East Asia is closely linked with the name of a single man who inaugurated the modern era of Russian East Asian policy: Count Muravyov-Amursky. He had to fight on two fronts: against China and against the opponents of his policy in St. Petersburg. Foremost among the latter was the Minister of Foreign Affairs at the time, Nesselrode, to whom the Near East seemed a far more desirable objective—a multi-phased struggle that played out over the course of about a dozen years, full of intrigues! Its outcome was a brilliant victory for Muravyov and for Russia!

Even as Muravyov came onto the scene, bringing the possessions on the Sea of Okhotsk and the Bering Sea into closer connection with the west via a viable transport route was already recognized as an economic and political necessity, one that even Nesselrode, hostile as he was to all colonial policy, came to appreciate. This had to be done if only because the coast of Okhotsk, Kamchatka, and Alaska were being rendered unsafe by British whalers and American seal hunters. But the privileges granted to the Russian-American Company deserved that name only if Russia also undertook their defense against foreign encroachments. A decision was therefore made to dispatch {p. 175} warships to the Sea of Okhotsk; they were at the same time to attend to the provisioning of the remote settlements, the rotation of troops, and the conveyance of mail. A beginning was made with the newly built brig "Baikal," which departed under the command of Captain Nevelskoy. The crew of this first representative of Russian naval power in East Asian waters consisted of six officers and thirty men.

Muravyov, who had been chosen personally by Tsar Nicholas I for his responsible post, departed Petersburg for Okhotsk in January 1848 with the promise to attend especially to the Russian possessions on the sea and to visit Kamchatka; Nevelskoy followed in August from Kronstadt. A year later they met again on the coast of the Sea of Okhotsk. Both had accomplished much in the meantime. Muravyov had continued the journey from his residence in Irkutsk and reached the sea at Okhotsk. Meanwhile, on the personal initiative of the Tsar, it had been decided to select a new port on the Sea of Okhotsk, since the port of that name was iced over for most of the year and offshore sandbanks hindered navigation. At the same time, steps were also to be taken to investigate the estuary of the Amur more closely. Nevelskoy was entrusted with both tasks, and Muravyov hastened to be personally present for these important investigations and at the same time to fulfill his promise regarding Kamchatka. He carried out the visit to the latter in the summer of 1849 and was delighted by the situation of the port of Petropavlovsk on the Bering Sea, which forms Avacha Bay here. He designated the port as a naval station and had it fortified with a number of batteries. He then met with Nevelskoy near Ayan on the Sea of Okhotsk. The latter had in the meantime likewise visited Kamchatka and Petropavlovsk, in order then to explore the north and west coasts of Sakhalin Island. He succeeded in making the discovery, highly important for Muravyov's plans, that Sakhalin is not connected to the mainland by an isthmus, but is an island—separated from the mainland, to be sure, only by a narrow strait four nautical miles wide—a fact that had hitherto eluded all explorers, including La Pérouse, Krusenstern, and Broughton. The great significance of this finding lay in the fact that one was no longer dependent solely on the approach through the Sea of Okhotsk, which was iced over for most of the year, when wishing to reach the mouth of the Amur, but had a second route available, namely through the Gulf of Tartary, whose waters remain open for a far greater part of the year because of the strong current running through it. After this discovery, Nevelskoy had also sailed a short distance up the Amur, thereby simultaneously dispelling a second erroneous notion that stemmed from an extremely clumsy expedition by Lieutenant Gavrilov, misleading in its results, which Nesselrode had been especially fond of citing in his campaign against Muravyov's policy. Gavrilov had claimed, namely, to have found the mouth of the Amur impassable for seagoing vessels, because its depth varied only between one and a half and three feet. "Since, moreover, Sakhalin is a peninsula, the Amur has no value for Russia," Nesselrode lectured.

On the basis of his reports and with the support of his friends in St. Petersburg, {p. 176} Muravyov was able to push through that the garrison of Okhotsk be transferred to Petropavlovsk and, furthermore, that in place of Okhotsk a port, Petrovskoye, on Schastye Bay near the mouth of the Amur, be newly occupied. To be sure, the latter shared the bad quality of the other ports on the Sea of Okhotsk in that it remained icebound until June. Consequently, Nevelskoy, who continued his exploratory work after Muravyov's departure and consistently aligned with his intentions, sailed some 25 versts up the Amur and founded on its left bank the river port of Nikolayevsk—the first Russian settlement on the Amur in more than 160 years! He also established that China exercised no sovereign rights whatever over the resident Gilyaks.

Nevelskoy took these steps on his own responsibility; in St. Petersburg, they had very nearly brought about his demotion. He hurried to Irkutsk to report in person to Muravyov, but the latter had himself already departed in haste for St. Petersburg to take up the fight personally against the machinations of his opponents, and Nevelskoy found only orders awaiting him to follow the Governor-General at once. Both arrived in the nick of time to avert disaster. Muravyov ultimately found backing from the Tsar; Nesselrode was removed from the chairmanship of the so-called Gilyak Committee, which was still responsible for these questions at that time, and Muravyov's policy triumphed all along the line. But in order to carry out the bold politician's plans, a strong military force was required, and the financial conditions of that period did not allow Russia to make larger funds available for the creation of a Siberian army. Across the whole of Siberia, only four infantry battalions were dispersed; not even artillery was available. Muravyov knew of a remedy! In the district of Nerchinsk, that is to say on the frontier of the disputed territories, the peasants settled there were bound to hard and poorly paid compulsory labor in the crown mines. Muravyov proposed releasing the peasants from this forced labor and turning them into Cossacks. This measure, which was later carried out with great energy, meant on the one hand the liberation of the poor serfs; on the other hand, it represented the virtually cost-free creation of a dedicated Siberian military force with which larger-scale operations could be initiated. The peasants were only too glad to exchange the lot of a forced laborer for that of a soldier, and Siberia was suddenly richer by twelve battalions of Cossack infantry of 1,000 men each. Nevelskoy had meanwhile been rehabilitated and had returned to the East. He undertook new explorations along the coast of the Tatar Sea, of which the discovery of the so-called De-Kastri Bay was the most important. Its significance lay in the fact that inland, close to it, lies a lake, “Lake Kizi,” which is connected to the lower reaches of the Amur, so that the possibility existed here of creating, as it were, a new southern mouth of the Amur by means of a road to be built toward the sea. Again on his own responsibility, Nevelskoy founded the posts of Aleksandrovsk on De-Kastri Bay and Mariinsk near Lake Kizi as the terminal points of the connecting road from the sea to the Amur. Farther south, Imperator Bay was discovered and likewise occupied, and on Sakhalin the port of Dliusk. It certainly entailed considerable difficulties once again to push through the legalization of these unauthorized acquisitions. But it {p. 177} succeeded. Nevelskoy could look back with pride on his achievements. With a handful of men he had taken possession of the mouth of the Amur, the west coast of Sakhalin, and a section of the lower course of the Amur. From the sea, he reached out his hand to the Russia advancing from the west. In 1850 he received permission to take possession of Petrovskoye, and by 1853 he had already occupied the finest harbors on the Tatar Sea and the estuary region of the Amur. Ludicrously meager were the resources with which he carried out this seizure of territory. In Mariinsk were stationed eight men, in Aleksandrovsk three, in Konstantinovsk eight, in Iliusk six, later eight, and the garrison of Nikolayevsk originally numbered only five sailors.

While Nevelskoy was occupying new ports and Muravyov was drilling his newly formed troop formations, however, storm clouds were gathering in Europe that were to carry Muravyov's far-reaching projects toward their goal with unsuspected speed. The Crimean War was in the making, and Muravyov hastened to Europe to take measures for the protection of the Russian possessions in the Far East, above all Petropavlovsk. He pointed out with all energy that the only natural supply route for the Russian possessions on the Pacific Ocean was the Amur. But while he was then seeking in Marienbad to restore his badly shattered health, the Foreign Ministry played a vile trick on him by sending a letter behind his back to Beijing, in which the Chinese government was invited to the joint erection of boundary markers on the Gorbitsa, a left tributary of the upper Amur, at the point where the course of the frontier had only been determined in general terms in the Treaty of Nerchinsk. Nesselrode thereby deceitfully thwarted the policy of Muravyov, who was anxious to instill in the Chinese the conviction that they had no claims whatsoever to the left bank of the Amur, which in fact they had never occupied.

The situation created for Muravyov by this deceitful maneuver in a matter that should under no circumstances have been broached from the Russian side was not enviable. But the disturbances of 1851, in which Russia, England, France, and Sardinia became embroiled, diverted the attention of St. Petersburg policy from East Asia, and thus Muravyov gained a free hand. The necessity of provisioning and reinforcing both the earlier and the new coastal acquisitions had to serve him—both with his own government and in the eyes of the Chinese—as a pretext to proceed with all energy to the execution of his plans, and ultimately to present both, Russia as well as China, with a fait accompli. But he was diplomat enough to do nothing that could have been construed to his disadvantage. He therefore refrained from any unauthorized action and set forth his view of the Amur question in a detailed memorandum, which was presented with such clarity and good sense that a committee established under the Tsesarevich to examine it gave its approval. Thus in 1854, for the first time, Muravyov obtained authorization from his own government to use the Amur with an armed force and supply vessels as the most direct route to the Russian possessions on the Pacific. At the same time, he was also tasked with negotiating the border demarcation question with China. A diplomatic attaché and an interpreter were assigned to him, and the Chinese government was informed from St. Petersburg that a Russian flotilla would sail down the Amur to convey troops {p. 178} and provisions to the Russian possessions on the Pacific Ocean threatened by war with European powers.

Meanwhile, the first Amur expedition had been equipped with the utmost care; the first Amur steamboat, the *Argun*, had been launched at Sretensk, and the expedition departed from there in grand style on May 14*) 1854.

On May 17, the flotilla, consisting of about 75 vessels, reached the confluence of the Argun and the Shilka, and on the morning of the 18th entered the forbidden river amidst solemn ceremonies. On May 28, it arrived in the vicinity of Aigun. Muravyov sent several of his officers ahead to scout whether the commandant of the fortress had received any instructions from Beijing. That was not the case. Although he lodged a protest against the Russians' breach of treaty, he was so surprised by the sight of the large flotilla that he made no attempt to place obstacles in the way of their onward journey, but on the contrary invited Muravyov to a parade of his troops, who assembled with bow, arrow, and fire-hardened wooden lances. Without further incidents, the fleet continued on its way, its main body reaching Mariinsk on June 14. There Muravyov met with Nevelskoy. He now distributed his men and provisions among the various occupied ports and on August 20 returned to Irkutsk via Ayan.

How Muravyov worked is shown by the activities of the 200-man detachment that had received orders to take up station at Lake Kizi. This detachment was initially tasked with building the projected road from Lake Kizi through the forest to De-Kastri Bay. Since the provisions had spoiled, the detachment was nearly destroyed by hunger, but was saved by a stroke of luck. After the road was completed—that is, cleared—the detachment was sent to Nikolayevsk and from there to Petrovskoye. Out at sea in their old Amur boats, however, the Cossacks were shipwrecked and had to forge a path to Petrovskoye along the impassable coast. From there, the transport ship *Irtysh* brought them to Ayan. Here, together with four cannons, they were put aboard the Russian-American Company's transport ship *Kamchatka* and dispatched to intercept marauding English whalers. Since their search proved fruitless, they remained at sea for only a few days and, upon their return, built two batteries. In mid-September, they were embarked once more and sent to Alaska. All this took place within a span of three months!

Muravyov's measures were soon to receive their brilliant justification. Not in vain had he taken the route along the Amur; not in vain had he sent a considerable portion of the provisions carried along and a detachment of 100 men to Kamchatka to reinforce the garrison of Petropavlovsk. And even if, in the course of time, the Russians were unable to hold the place permanently, they nevertheless won a victory here in the Far East, while in the West fortune was less kind to Russian arms. The repulse of the Anglo-French fleet's attack on Petropavlovsk served, as it were, as the acid test for the correctness of Muravyov's measures.

::: notes
*) The dates in this chapter are according to the Russian calendar.
:::

{p. 179}

The fleet that appeared off Petropavlovsk on 17 August 1854 consisted of seven warships—four British and three French—with a total armament of 236 guns. The fortification of the harbor by several forts had been carried out in accordance with Muravyov’s instructions during his visit. The seven different batteries comprised 35 guns. It seemed almost a matter of course that these would not be able to withstand the fire of 236 cannons in the long run. Nevertheless, the Allies did not succeed in taking the fortress. The bombardment lasted two days; on both days they attempted to land, and on both days they were repulsed with extraordinary losses. In the final, decisive attempt, they lost no less than a third of their landing force. The operations of the Allies were also adversely affected by the fact that the commander of the squadron, the British Admiral Price, shot himself on the morning before the first attack, and command suddenly devolved upon the senior French officer. Had this incident not occurred, the Allies would hardly have abandoned their attempts to destroy the Russian positions so quickly, given their considerable superiority in numbers.

This victory of Muravyov’s young, newly created army was greeted with understandable jubilation in Russia, and above all in Siberia itself, and strengthened his position immensely. Yet it must be regarded as an act of great prudence on Muravyov’s part that he subsequently did not expose himself to the risk of tarnishing the brilliance of this victory a second time with the shadows of an inevitable defeat. It had become known that the following year, as soon as ice conditions permitted, the Allied fleet was to appear once more before Petropavlovsk in reinforced strength. Russian defensive strength would not have been equal to this. Muravyov therefore sent his personal aide-de-camp from Irkutsk in the dead of the Siberian winter by dog sled to Kamchatka to deliver the order to the commandant of Petropavlovsk to evacuate the place, along with its garrison and civilian population. This order was carried out immediately, and in the spring of 1855 the Russian fleet—consisting of the frigate *Aurora*, the corvette *Olivutsa*, and the transport ships *Dvina*, *Irtysh*, and *Baikal*, as well as a boat—sailed south under the command of the commandant of Petropavlovsk, General Zavoiko. Since the Sea of Okhotsk was still frozen so early in the year (April), the fleet, having slipped past two enemy cruisers under cover of fog, entered the Tatar Sea from the south and anchored in Imperatorskaya Bay.

When the combined Anglo-French fleet arrived off Petropavlovsk in May, it found only a few American traders, who asked to be taken aboard in case the fortifications, their only protection against the natives, were destroyed. The Allied fleet then set out on a fruitless search for the Russian ships in the Bering Sea and along the coast of Alaska. On the very same day, however, that the combined squadron under Admiral Bruce arrived off Petropavlovsk, a British squadron under Commodore Elliot also arrived at the Tatar Sea, before which the Russian ships withdrew northward from Imperatorskaya Bay to De Castries Bay. The Russian fleet was now in a highly precarious situation. For the passage {p. 180} to the north discovered by Nevelskoy was still blocked by ice. One had to be prepared, therefore, for a battle whose outcome would undoubtedly have ended in the capture of the Russian ships. Incomprehensibly, however, Elliot did not attack the Russian squadron, but cruised in the south of the Tatar Sea to bar the Russians' exit until reinforcements should arrive. Outside Russia, Nevelskoy’s discovery was not yet known at that time, and Elliot believed the Russian ships were trapped in the "gulf" as in a mousetrap. Yet while he dispatched the brig *Bittern* to Admiral Stirling to ask him for reinforcements, the ice in the passage broke up (on 14 May), and under cover of fog the Russian fleet slipped out of the harbor of De Castries Bay through the northern passage toward the mouth of the Amur. Just in the nick of time! For on 16 May Elliot returned to De Castries Bay and was astonished in the highest degree to find that the Russian ships were no longer there. For him, no other solution to the puzzle remained than that the Russians had escaped southward under cover of the fog. He therefore steered south, and while the Anglo-French fleet searched in vain for the vanished Russian ships in the Bering Sea, Elliot cruised in the Sea of Japan and along the eastern side of Sakhalin in the hope of intercepting the ships after all—at any rate, one of the most amusing jokes world history has ever played.

Yet Muravyov, too, had not lost sight of his goal in the meantime. The Chinese had declared themselves ready to carry out the border settlement on the Gorbitsa proposed in the aforementioned letter from the Foreign Ministry. Muravyov, however, stalled the negotiations and undertook his second great expedition, with which he transported over 8,000 persons of both sexes to the lower reaches of the Amur and had them settled there by his faithful assistant, Prince Volkonsky, who belonged to a family of exiles. On this second transport he also brought significant reinforcements, by which, among other things, the garrison of De-Kastri Bay was increased to 500 Cossacks.

When Elliot had searched in vain for the vanished Russian ships until the onset of autumn, he reappeared at the beginning of October in the Tatar Strait, where, in his opinion, the Russian fleet had to withdraw before winter. In the meantime, De-Kastri Bay had once again been stripped of troops down to 70 men. Nonetheless, under cover of the dense forest, these seventy engaged the English landing force, which fought against an invisible enemy. Meanwhile, reinforcements were also brought up from Mariinsk; however, they no longer intervened, as the English set sail in mid-October. With this, the military operations of that year came to an end, and the following year brought peace.

In September 1855, Chinese negotiators had appeared before Muravyov in Mariinsk, to whom he presented for the first time the demand that the lands occupied by the Russians up to that point should belong to them, and that the Russians be granted unhindered free navigation on the Amur. The Chinese plenipotentiaries declared that they were not authorized for such negotiations, and produced the well-known letter from the Foreign Ministry concerning the border settlement on the Gorbitsa. Muravyov evaded negotiations {p. 181} [Illustration: Khabarovsk: Monument to Count Muravyov-Amursky.]

{p. 182}

on this with the phrase that the Chinese should inform their government of the Russian government's earnest desire to finally arrive at a settlement of its relations with China, and furthermore inform it of his intention to travel down the Amur again the following summer with a supply flotilla.

As a result of the abandonment of Petropavlovsk, whose population was settled along the lower reaches of the Amur, and through Muravyov's two transports down the Amur, a considerable number of settlements had already sprung up. The next transport of troops and settlers was, as mentioned, scheduled for the summer of 1856. In the meantime, however, Muravyov's presence in St. Petersburg had become necessary again, since the Chinese complained about the establishment of Russian settlements not only to the left but also to the right of the Amur (Mariinsk, De-Kastri Bay, etc.), and the Foreign Ministry, in blind incomprehension of Muravyov's plans, was on the verge of demanding from China merely the right of free navigation on the Amur and the construction of storehouses on both banks. On this journey, Muravyov now obtained full powers not only to negotiate the ominous border settlement with China, but to conclude an entirely new treaty, which would no longer settle an open-ended implementation clause of the Treaty of Nerchinsk, but create a completely new foundation.

Muravyov remained in Europe over the summer, while in his absence General Korsakov, in accordance with his instructions, organized and carried out a new—that is, the third—expedition down the Amur. Such expeditions now took place year after year.

In December 1856, the Governor-General departed again for Irkutsk. The government had given expression to the de facto annexation of the territories occupied and partly already settled by Nevelskoy and Muravyov by combining them, along with the entire coastal region up to and including Kamchatka, into a single province, the "Primorskaya." In that winter, work also began on building postal stations along the left bank of the Amur, and a postal service was established using sledges drawn partly by horses and partly by dogs.

In the meantime, events had once again occurred outside Russia that accelerated the realization of Muravyov's plans. It is one of the peculiarities of Russia's East Asian policy that it was able, with little effort, to derive advantages for itself from every major occurrence on the East Asian stage. France and England were endeavoring to conclude commercial treaties with China. For Russia, this was a welcome opportunity to reopen the treaty question with China on its own part as well. Consequently, Putyatin, who had previously concluded a commercial treaty with Japan, was dispatched to Beijing. Muravyov was initially by no means pleased by this, fearing that Putyatin might thwart his plans regarding the Amur question. However, during Putyatin's passage through Irkutsk—he intended to take the route via Mongolia—Muravyov was able to convince himself that, regarding the Amur question, he needed to rein in rather than spur on Putyatin's zeal. Putyatin encountered unforeseen difficulties. First, the Chinese did not permit him to travel through Mongolia, so that he found himself forced to travel down the Amur via Nikolayevsk and from there by sea to Tianjin, where, however, the mandarins again stalled him with useless discussions and barred him {p. 183} from traveling to Beijing, which was permitted only to envoys of foreign rulers who brought tribute. The Tsar's envoy therefore preferred to proceed to Shanghai and attempted to induce the Chinese to negotiate with him there—in vain, of course.

Muravyov was meanwhile untiringly active in settling the entire length of the left bank of the Amur. Opposite Aigun, three kilometres upstream at the confluence of the Zeya with the Amur—the site of old Chinese Aigun from the era of Albazin—Ust-Zeysk, the later Blagoveshchensk, had been founded, and the postal line between Ust-Strelka and Nikolayevsk was completed.

Putyatin's insistence ultimately met with success, which manifested itself, however, in a different direction than he had intended, and was considerably accelerated by the outbreak of the grave Taiping Rebellion, which shook the Chinese state structure, and by China's subsequent war with England and France. Under the pressure of these circumstances, China was anxious not to bring about inconvenient complications in the north as well. Yet without harming its position vis-à-vis England and France, it could not negotiate in Shanghai. Therefore, it preferred to shift the venue of the negotiations deep into the interior of the continent, to Aigun. On 16 May 1858, after only six days of negotiations, Muravyov concluded a treaty here with the Chinese plenipotentiaries, which ceded the entire left bank of the Amur to Russia and declared the coastal territory between the Tatar Sea and the Ussuri, as well as down to the Korean border, to be the joint possession of Russia and China. Navigation on the Amur, Ussuri, and Sungari was to be permitted exclusively to vessels of both nations; by contrast, free commercial activity on these rivers was proclaimed. The precise delimitation of the border on the coast was intentionally reserved for a later occasion. It took place just two years later in the Treaty of Beijing. Putyatin had meanwhile—just eight days after the conclusion of the Convention of Aigun—finally signed a commercial treaty for Russia in Tianjin, almost simultaneously with the English and French negotiators, and in 1859 General Ignatyev was sent to Beijing as envoy. While the latter was now negotiating the definitive border delimitation in Beijing, Muravyov toured the newly acquired coastal region and discovered Peter the Great Bay, where he founded Vladivostok, and Posyet Bay. Then he provided Ignatyev with the necessary information, on the basis of which the latter, in concluding the treaty, insisted that the border be drawn even further south than the coastal river Suifen stipulated in the Convention of Aigun, all the way down to the Korean border. China thereby lost its access in the north to the Sea of Japan, its eastern access to the sea. In this way, eastern Manchuria too, the ancestral homeland of the Chinese ruling dynasty, became a Russian province, and it was only a matter of time before the rest of Manchuria met the same fate.

{p. 181}

[Illustration: Khabarovsk: Captured Chinese flags at the parade.]

## Chapter IV. Through the Primorskaya.

### 1. “There, where once Genghis ruled.”*)

July 16.

If I were superstitious, I would board the next steamer heading south and return to Europe by some other route, anything but right across Siberia. I have not even begun my journey on the Trans-Siberian Railway, and I have already missed a train! It happened like this. There are no porters in Vladivostok. In their stead, Chinese carters handle travelers' luggage to and from the pier or the railway station. Since the only express train departs daily at 9:25 in the morning, a Chinese carter had been ordered for me to be outside the hotel at 8:15. He was indeed there punctually. Loading took ten minutes. The trunks were tied onto the clumsy two-wheeled cart so that they would not go sailing off when the cart rocked across the streets of Vladivostok. The droshky I was using and the cart with my luggage departed from the hotel at the same time. In less than ten minutes I was at the station; the cart, which had to travel more slowly, was supposed to follow within five minutes at the latest. It had no intention of doing so, however. At long, long last, after much waiting and a few suppressed {p. 185} curses, I see it swing around the corner. It is already ten minutes before the train's departure. Now, quick: unload and check the luggage! In passing, I also learn that the honest Chinese had overturned the cart on the way while trying to take a sharp corner in haste, and that as a result he had to untie all the trunks, right the cart, reload it, and lash it down anew—all of which was rather beside the point to me at that moment, because I focused all my attention solely on settling matters with the clerk who was to check my things. I, with my foolish notions of European speed, was firmly convinced that a span of ten minutes would suffice, especially as the other passengers already seemed to have been attended to. But calculating the excess luggage charge evidently caused the clerk great difficulties. Apparently, not a soul in Russia learns mental arithmetic. At any rate, every shopkeeper, every Russian banker, and every counter clerk works out all four basic operations exclusively on the abacus. Indeed, when a Russian wants to know how much two and two make, he first reaches for the abacus, and if right after that you ask him how much two times two is, he clatters away with the beads for a while again and is afterwards extraordinarily surprised when he discovers that two times two is also four, and that you knew it in your head. Just as the prevalence of reading and writing has gradually degraded memory among us, so the abacus has it on its conscience that people in Russia generally cannot perform the simplest addition in their heads. Yet when a Russian is clattering away on his abacus, you must leave him in peace, otherwise he will miscalculate and have to start the whole operation all over again from the beginning. In my non-Russian subject’s intellect, I now thought I could lend the good clerk a helping hand by repeatedly presenting to him—both orally and in writing—the result of the calculation of what the excess luggage charge for my bags as far as Khabarovsk on the Amur would cost, which I had long since worked out in my head. But to no avail! Incredulously he stared at the figures I had written down for him, nor did he seem to feel any fondness for my money, which I had already counted out and placed before him; and since I had unfortunately disturbed him in his clattering, he started anew to calculate the problem of how much six and a half poods—of which one pood is free luggage—would cost if one pood costs such and such an amount. He miscalculated, arrived at too high a sum, I protested, and when he finally had the result right, the train was just pulling out of the station! “If you slip a ruble note under his abacus beforehand, he calculates much faster,” someone explained to me afterwards, to whom I poured out my woes. Well, that is just the sort of thing one needs to know beforehand!

::: notes
*) A line from a poem recited in honor of Muravyov on May 9, 1854, his birthday, in Nerchinsk, on the very day the inhabitants gave him a farewell banquet immediately prior to the departure of the first expedition down the Amur.
:::

Instead of the 9:25 train, I am now traveling on the train that leaves Vladivostok at 3:25 in the afternoon. That is “the” passenger train on this line, whereas the morning train is “the” express train. The latter, to be sure, does not deserve its name because it travels particularly fast; by no means fast for an express train, a mere 21 kilometers per hour! Rather, it is because the passenger train runs even much slower! To cover the 721-verst distance to Khabarovsk, it takes from 3:25 this afternoon until 6:40 in the afternoon the day after tomorrow; that is, on average, {p. 186} 14 versts per hour. For the sake of fairness, however, it must be mentioned that the verst is still somewhat longer than the kilometer—a full 67 meters longer, in fact.

Of course, I will not travel the entire distance to Khabarovsk on the passenger train; instead, I will get off at Nikolsk, where the Manchurian line branches off, and continue tomorrow afternoon on the express train that leaves Vladivostok in the morning.

The large clock on the Vladivostok station building, which looks exactly like the clock at Bahnhof Friedrichstraße in Berlin, shows 3:25. The conductor gives a long, drawn-out blast on his shrill whistle, and the engine answers just as drawn-out. The conductor whistles a second time, now short and sharp, and the engine replies once more, just as short and sharp; then the train slowly sets itself in motion. This time I am sitting inside, leaning back in a corner of a first-class compartment upholstered in red plush. In the Far East, the white man always travels first class if he respects himself and expects the same from others. Everything feels pleasantly familiar and cozy. The soft upholstery, which is not at all as lumpy as the mattress in the “number” of my gostinitsa; the brightly polished window panes; the tidy little children of an officer who sit opposite me with their mama, dressed in a chic Parisian gown of the latest cut, on their way home after shopping in the city—purchases that are now unpacked and inspected once more; and then the agreeable rocking of the railway car! With an indescribable feeling of bliss, I close my eyes to capture the fleeting moment. In a single second, I run through all the various means of travel I have used during my stay in the East: first the railway—ah, the railway!—as far as Genoa, then the fine, large passenger steamer of the Hamburg-America Line, and in between Somali dugouts, Ceylonese dugout canoes, Malay boats, Chinese sampans, steam pinnaces, and steam launches, once even a torpedo boat; from Shanghai onward a large troop transport steamer, then up to Tanggu a former Yangtze steamer, then once more the railway; during the campaign a pony or a mule or a Chinese mandarin cart, or even more frequently shank’s pony; then the railway again, but in what a state! Next, on the journey through Manchuria, came the draisine pulled by Chinese coolies, then the Chinese farm cart, then another stretch of railway, this time third class to be sure; later in Shandong the donkey and the mule litter; from Chefoo to Amoy a German freighter, from there to Hong Kong a Japanese mail steamer; then in South China the sedan chair and the Chinese junk; in Shanghai predominantly the pony carriage and, as in Japan, the rickshaw; in Vladivostok the troika, usually with only two horses, however; and now the railway once again, a soft corner in a first-class compartment—ah, one only learns to appreciate the achievements of civilization after having been deprived of them for so long!

Indeed, I am in very good spirits despite it all, and it does not vex me in the least that this dreadful slow train, having crossed the Svetlanskaya, now creeps along the shore of Amur Bay {p. 187} at the speed of a cyclist taking his first ride on a country road. For a good while the route follows the sea; the scenery could almost be called picturesque, and now and then it truly is, when through the shrubbery covering the hillsides the summer residence of a wealthy Vladivostok merchant peeks out here and there, or when on a curve—of which there are very many—one’s gaze rests on one of the barracks of the Ussuri Railway Regiment, and with the help of a little imagination, construes from it a resemblance to the charming Miramare near Trieste.

[Illustration: Coal mine on the Southern Ussuri Railway (p. 188).]

After five kilometers, the first station, Pervaya Rechka! According to the original plan, the terminus of the Ussuri Railway was to be located here, connected to the landing stage on the Golden Horn only by a spur line. After this was later abandoned, this station serves as an auxiliary station for Vladivostok. It teems with military personnel, as the barracks of the troops stationed near Vladivostok are located here. After a stop of twenty minutes—Russians generally do not manage with anything less—we travel on. The countryside remains consistently pleasant and even becomes idyllic at times, as it does eleven kilometers further on, at the next station, Sedanka, where most of the country villas of the Vladivostok merchants are situated. One large building is particularly striking; it is Suvorov’s match factory, already {p. 188} mentioned earlier, which, despite protective tariffs against Japanese competition, had to go into liquidation on account of Russian slovenliness. At the next station, Khilkovo, we see large dumps of coal, which is mined here and used to fire the railway. The operation was set in motion by the railway administration itself. Vladivostok obtains the bulk of its coal supply from Sakhalin. For that matter, Siberia’s coal deposits have by no means been tackled yet; very little is known as yet about their extent and yield, and, in addition to numerous other obstacles, a lack of workers in particular would impede large-scale extraction, and all the more acutely the deeper the mines lie in the interior of the country. For the present, both the Trans-Siberian Railway, with the exception of the Ussuri Railway, and the entire Siberian steam navigation*) burn wood without exception.
