So long, then, as Siberia’s timber stocks hold out—with which a sheerly incredible reckless overexploitation is practiced—there is no compelling reason to exploit the coal reserves, and if a private entrepreneur wished to begin mining coal even now, he would hardly find it profitable, since the engines are not equipped for coal-firing, and the transition to it would entail a complete reconstruction of the boilers and fireboxes on both the steamers and the railway, which could not be carried out without disproportionately high costs. For the engines are mostly imported from abroad, built specifically for wood-firing, and there is, above all, a lack of marine engineers and shipyards or machine-repair workshops to carry out such work. But these facts suffice to preserve Siberia’s coal wealth for decades to come, and in them lies also the main explanation for why Russia has not yet approached the exploitation of these treasures, even though they have long since been surveyed and known. But to transport the coal from the interior to the coast by rail or by water and to offer it to the East Asian market, the costs would be far too substantial—even if the waterway alone were to be used—to be able to compete with cheap, if not particularly valuable, Japanese coal. In my opinion, that would be possible only for coal in the immediate vicinity of the coast, especially also for the quite good Sakhalin coal. For the present, however, this is not yet mined in such quantities and so cheaply as to make itself felt on the East Asian coal market in any way other than by removing Russian Manchuria from it as a sales market. —

The region through which we are now travelling is more densely wooded. We turn away from the sea; the railway, which until then had maintained a fairly easterly heading, now turns predominantly to the northwest; at a sharp bend, Amur Bay disappears from view, and the line slowly ascends the gently rolling hill country that confines the left bank of the Suifun. The dense spruce forests over which the eye ranges provided the material for the railway ties over which we glide, and the grass-covered slopes, {p. 189} on which massed sawn-off, but even more so burnt-out tree stumps and here and there a solitary surviving giant of a tree tower up, stretch along the railway line, only to be bordered at some distance by dark-green forests that lie there in the deepest hues of ink. To the left the valley opens up, and soon one distinguishes a narrow silver ribbon winding through a depression. We turn into the Suifun Valley and arrive at Razdolnoye on the Suifun, which is navigable from this point on. The officer’s wife with the two most delightful children, who during the journey amused themselves with the French edition of Wilhelm Busch purchased in Vladivostok, alights. Her husband stands by the

::: notes
*) In European Russia, on the other hand, especially in its eastern parts, in the Volga region, where wood would be too expensive for this purpose, the locomotives and steamers burn naphtha.
:::

[Illustration: Market Square in Nikolsk (p. 192).]

first Siberian Rifle Battalion, which is stationed here. The place has not yet 400 inhabitants, but the houses stretch so far along the mountain slope that it looks like a town of 10,000 inhabitants. Here, too, the post road to Possiet Bay joins in. It is already growing dark, but the dusk detracts little from the charm of the scenery. For a long time now no station follows, and we already pass in the moonlight the picturesque Suifun cliffs, by which the railway is pressed close against the riverbank. I am alone in my compartment; the train seems to be used almost exclusively for local traffic. . . . It is ten minutes past nine; we have penetrated the first 100 kilometres into the Asian continent: Nikolsk, the first stage on the way to Prussian- {p. 190} Berlin, is reached. The town lies more than a kilometre from the railway station. And so: “Isvoshchik! . . . Gostinitsa!” “Khorosho!” The horses fly at a gallop across the dusty country road.

My driver duly drops me off in front of what is supposedly the premier hotel in Nikolsk. He receives one rouble as his fare. That is of course far too much. For he curses dreadfully and demands more, until the innkeeper comes out and has the case laid before him. Hearing that the driver received a rouble, he leaps through the front door in a single bound, seizes a stick, and calls out a few flattering remarks in Russian to the isvoshchik, which the latter apparently returns until he catches sight of the brandished club. At that, he brings his whip down across his horses’ backs so that they break straight into a gallop, and—was seen no more.

I am assigned my “number”. It costs one rouble for the night. The barman, for a change, speaks *daitsch* once again.

The geographical location of Nikolsk is extraordinarily favourable. It is also primarily to this that the prosperity and rapid rise are due, which have made the place—a town for the past three years—with its 13,000 inhabitants the most populous and important station on the Ussuri Railway. Here is the gateway that opens onto the rich hinterland of cultivated southern Manchuria. To be sure, in the event of a Chinese insurrection in Manchuria, the town would be the most exposed to danger. The 100 kilometres that separate it from the Chinese border offer no great security. But on the other hand, it is also the most important garrison town in the Primorskaya. Before the outbreak of the Chinese unrest, it garrisoned the 1st East Siberian Rifle Brigade, the 3rd, 4th, and 5th Rifle Battalions, two batteries of field artillery, a mortar battery of the East Siberian Artillery Brigade, the Ussuri Cavalry Brigade, and the 1st Transbaikal Cossack Regiment. Since the Chinese troubles, however, the garrison has been significantly reinforced. Through the construction of the railway, Nikolsk did not lose, like most of the settlements situated directly on the Ussuri, but only gained, and will gain still more once the connecting railway across Manchuria, which terminates here, is finally completed. It is the junction where the transport routes of the easternmost part of the Russian possessions converge: from the north (the Ussuri and Amur regions), from the east (Vladivostok), from the south (Possiet Bay and Korea), and from the west (Manchuria as well as the other tracts of land traversed by the Trans-Siberian Railway). The soil conditions, too, are extremely favourable. Added to this is the fact that Nikolsk is one of the southernmost Russian settlements in Siberia, so that the climatic conditions also accord the place a promising position. In the immediate vicinity of the railway station, which for the time being still lies more than a kilometre from the first houses of the settlement, a separate settlement has already grown up that specifically serves the railway. There we find pleasant officials’ quarters with little gardens in front, alongside machine shops, carriage-building workshops, large warehouses for railway material, a two-class school for the railway officials’ children, a hospital, etc. It will {p. 191} in all probability hardly require a period of more than ten years before the gap between the settlements at the station and the houses of the town is completely filled.

How gladly would I have altered my travel itinerary even now and taken the route from Nikolsk via Harbin straight across Manchuria to Kaidalovo station between Sretensk and Chita, where the so-called Chinese Eastern Railway joins the Transbaikal line of the Trans-Siberian Railway. And so I discuss this question in the greatest detail with the

[Illustration: Vyazemskaya, the largest station on the Northern Ussuri Railway (p. 195).]

*daitsch*-speaking barman, as the only mortal soul with whom I can make myself understood in educated conversation. But the outcome, alas, is quite negative in nature. The barman tells me that on the Nikolsk–Pogranichnaya line (at the Chinese border) trains run regularly, but beyond that all regular transport ceases. In an emergency one could still make headway, but only on work trains, whose stopover times are often unpredictable. As far as Harbin (on the Sungari), where the line branches off to Port Arthur, Dalny, Newchwang, Beijing, etc., one might just manage to get in this manner, and perhaps even as far as Tsitsihar (on the Nonni, a tributary of the Sungari).

{p. 192}

Then, however, one would have to travel onward over the mountains (the Lesser and the Greater Khingan) by post-horses, and it was very questionable whether these had not all been requisitioned for the railway works. In any case, the journey would certainly be extraordinarily arduous, and even if that alone would not deter me from embarking upon it, the consideration that the duration of my trip would likely become entirely unpredictable certainly does. Besides, I am also very keen to see the regions that have been the object of a centuries-long struggle between Russians and Chinese. While conversing with the buffet attendant, I stand at the bar and, in the Russian manner, take my *zakuska* before supper in the form of a caviar roll. I praise the caviar and learn that it is fresh caviar from Amur sturgeon. If I was so fond of caviar, the buffet attendant remarked, then I really ought to travel up the Amur. “There you eat the ikra right with spoons!” So along the Amur we shall go, where caviar is eaten right by the spoonful!

July 17.
Seen by daylight, the town indeed gives the impression of rapid growth. The streets consist of two rows of houses fronted sporadically by wooden boardwalks, with a morass between them. When driving through by carriage, the mud spatters high, and if another carriage passes by, one does well to open an umbrella, as it is after all more convenient to have it cleaned later by the hotel servant than to have to wash one's own face, especially given the general dearness of water. The great Vladivostok trading houses Kunst & Albers, Langelütje, Churin, etc., possess branch establishments here, and the marketplace is full of wooden stalls in which Russians, Jews, and Chinese offer all kinds of bric-a-brac for sale. The houses are for the most part of wood; of stone houses there are at most twenty, among them a beautiful white cathedral towering over everything, which, however, does not suffice for the religious needs of the inhabitants. Besides it, there are three more wooden churches in the town, joined by a fourth in the settlement by the railway station. The troops are quartered in extensive barracks and cantonments in the south of the town. Ancient remnants of walls recall the legendary kingdom of Bohai, whose residence is said to have been situated here. It was followed by the kingdom of the Manchus. This was crushed by Genghis Khan, and upon its ruins arose the new Manchu empire of the Qing, who now sit upon the throne of China and have in the meantime themselves been driven from their ancestral homeland once more by the Russians. That is the fable of the big beast that devours the smaller one, only to be eaten itself by an even bigger one.

Numerous passengers await the arrival of the Vladivostok express toward 2 o'clock in the afternoon. In the morning the train from Manchuria arrived from Pogranichnaya, bringing several high-ranking officers who are traveling to Khabarovsk to attend a great jubilee celebration, taking place next Sunday, of the regiments that took part in the Chinese campaign. The train pulls in punctually; coming from Vladivostok, it is already heavily {p. 193} [Illustration: Khabarovsk on the Amur (p. 201).]

crowded." So one must hurry to secure a good seat. The Russians think so too; in an instant, the doors of the carriages, arranged like our corridor express trains, are crowded, and I succeed in making my way directly behind a general traveling with his wife into a still vacant compartment, where I immediately make myself at home. The officer addresses me; I keep hearing only “madame, madame,” and shake my head regretfully: do not understand! In the meantime, the orderly brings his master's luggage into the compartment, some ten different pieces, including an enormous valise that will not fit into the luggage racks above the seats at all and has to be placed in front of the window—the large bedding sack without which the Russian does not travel out here. We take our seats; besides the three of us, there is also an elderly white-bearded gentleman in the compartment, who is incessantly smoking cigarettes from a holder some twenty centimeters long. From time to time a reproachful glance from the beautiful eyes of the general's wife falls upon me, which, however, I cannot explain and connect only dimly with the “madame, madame” from earlier. Meanwhile, cruder instincts prevail in me for the moment, and so I stroll through the corridor of the train toward the dining car, where I initially seek to soothe my growling stomach with {p. 191} a hearty cabbage soup with beef, a Russian national dish called “shchi,” which I subsequently succeed in doing with the assistance of a “côtelette” à la maréchal. Gradually, one passenger after another arrives—merchants, engineers, officers; I count no fewer than five generals on the train. Among them my neighbor from my compartment, who presently also appears at the dining car table with his wife, accompanied by the white-bearded gentleman with the long cigarette holder.

[Illustration: Khabarovsk: Triumphal Arch (p. 210).]

We exchange greetings, the gentlefolk take their seats near me, and I cannot rid myself of the feeling that they want something from me. Once the general makes a start as if he were about to address me, then another furtive glance from the general's wife catches me, and in between an evidently very difficult question is being deliberated in Russian, the subject of which—with all modesty I cannot help thinking—must be myself. Finally one of the generals turns to me and asks in German whether I am a German. "Yes." And now at last it comes out what is actually the matter. The general's wife had had little sleep the previous night, and so the couple, together with the white-bearded gentleman, had wanted to secure a compartment all to themselves. The old gentleman, however, was to be accommodated in another compartment for the night so that the general's wife could sleep undisturbed. Wretch that I was, my stepping in had now thwarted this fine plan—hence the incomprehensible "madame," and hence the reproachful looks! And in truth, I would not have known what to do with my first-class ticket either, had I not taken that seat. In first class, virtually every seat was taken. Yet even so, of course, Madame shall have her night's rest as far as I am concerned, not another word need be said about it; the gentleman with the white beard has already scouted out that somewhere on the train, in second class, the uppermost of three folding berths stacked one above the other, directly under the ceiling, is still vacant, and it is reserved for me for the night. They are delighted at my kindness; the general, visibly moved, shakes my hand; the general's wife, just as moved, shakes my hand as well—pretense here, pretense there. My general speaks French, my general's wife a little German and French. The other generals all speak French, some of them German. The conversation is conducted alternately, as the case may be, in one language or the other, sometimes even in both at once. The gentleman with the white beard and the long cigarette holder speaks only Russian and talks away at {p. 195} me. Although I do not understand him, I nod my head vigorously. This pleases him so much that he places all his cigarettes at my disposal. Then the four of us return to our compartment. The general invites me to tea, the general's wife to sweets. We chat about all manner of things: about the region, the Chinese campaign, Khabarovsk, where the general is stationed, Warsaw, where he was previously. The general's wife tells of her little daughter, who in Khabarovsk is looking forward to her parents' return, and must be a little beauty if she takes after her mother. Well now—in short order we are the best of friends, as though we had been traveling together for at least half a year. They really are delightful people, the two of them. When we part for the night, they have grown so dear to me that I feel genuine pleasure as I climb up to my bunk on the third tier directly beneath the roof of the second-class compartment, reflecting on how terribly I shall sweat now, simply to afford the gracious lady a pleasant night's rest!

### 2. In the Ussuri Valley.

18 July.

The region through which we are hurrying occupies, climatically, a peculiar intermediate position between the tropics and the cold zone. I mentioned earlier that, on geographical and climatological grounds, the Amur basin must be described as Russia's true colonial territory. While the general conditions for agriculture and other forms of cultivation in the rest of Siberia do not differ essentially from those of European Russia, and in particular the Governor-Generalships of Western and Eastern Siberia—in those zones that can be considered for cultivation at all—are to be regarded as the direct, broad continuation of European Russia, this holds true only to a very limited degree once one has crossed the watershed between the Yenisei and the Amur, and loses its validity entirely as soon as one considers the southeastern territories of the Primorskaya on their own. We have already seen in the political acquisition of these regions that intervention from the east, from the sea, was necessary for this. The first Russian invasion of the Amur region, which took place entirely by land, had failed. It only succeeded when Russian

[Illustration: Khabarovsk: Cathedral (p. 210).] {p. 196} power reached around Asia and thus met the driving force pressing forward from the west. This process has, in addition to its political significance, a profound economic one as well. We can summarize it briefly by saying that the Amur region gravitates toward the east, toward the sea, while the western parts of Siberia lean toward the mother country. Were we to visualize this on a balance beam, its fulcrum would have to be conceived as resting on the crest of the mountain range that stretches under various names from Central Asia all the way to the Bering Sea. The settlement of the Amur region, too, took place to a considerable extent by sea, and would have had to do so even more had an artificial bridging of the natural divide not taken place via the Great Siberian Tract and, in more recent times, by the railway. Except for a vanishingly small amount of transport passing through Sretensk, the Amur region is supplied with its European necessities from the sea, namely via Vladivostok and to a small extent also via Nikolayevsk, and the disproportionately smaller exports produced by the region likewise take the same route, with few exceptions. As regards first of all the trade of the natives, whose principal livelihood is hunting, it is completely dependent on the immigrant Chinese. Wherever Chinese settle across the globe, it is as characteristic of the equally pronounced commercial superiority of this race as it is of the ineptitude of the Russian people in making the goods of their colonial land permanently serviceable to themselves by any means other than predatory exploitation, that the entire fur trade of this region is in Chinese hands. The slant-eyed merchants have kept their Russian competitors away from the natives by paying the latter advances on their annual fur catch, and doing so directly in kind. In this way it has come about that the natives, although politically subjects of the Russians, are economically dependent exclusively on the Chinese, who thus virtually monopolize the fur trade. This phenomenon explains why by far the greatest part of the annual fur harvest of the Amur region, as well as of the parts lying north of it inhabited by Siberian tribes, does not flow to Russia and Europe, but to Manchuria and North China, where fur is the universal winter garment. Since, however, Khabarovsk is now basically the only fur market in Siberia for the nobler furs—sable, ermine, and blue fox having retreated to the northeast from western Siberia, which is more densely settled and has been exploited by trappers for centuries—the Russian himself is in turn forced to meet his own fur needs from the Chinese. Only the trade in finer seal and otter pelts coming from Kamchatka remains predominantly in Russian hands, but it travels by sea. Anyone, therefore, who believes that one can still buy furs well and, above all, cheaply in Siberia is mistaken. In Moscow, furs are considerably cheaper than in Irkutsk and in Khabarovsk! This even goes so far that Siberia no longer covers even its own needs for fine furs. Later on, in Irkutsk, I bought a ready-made genuine Siberian sable fur at great expense. Later it turned out that the sable had never seen Siberia beforehand, but rather—as the stamp on the back of the pelt also proved—had roamed in its lifetime among the boulders of the great Canadian plain {p. 197}. The fur had then been tanned by a furrier on Leipzig's Brühl and sold to Moscow. From there it had made its way to Irkutsk, and from there was brought back as genuine Siberian sable to Russia, Germany, England, and—should chance have it—perhaps even to Canada.

Just as in the fur trade, the Chinese has forged ahead of the Russian in many other matters as well. This, too, is partly due to the fact that the living and working conditions under which the immigrant Russians find themselves here are of a different nature than at home. The Russian peasant is simply, first and foremost, a peasant. Here he has suddenly been transplanted into a kind of Cockaigne, where many a thing grows directly into his hands that previously had cost him the sweat of his brow. I have already mentioned the phenomenon of Russian peasants parceling out the land allotted to them, leasing it to Chinese, playing the landlord themselves, and living off hunting. Others, again, cover their food needs by fishing, while still others live by felling firewood for the steamers plying the rivers. Yet another part is employed in railway construction; in short, the percentage of actual tillers of the soil of Russian nationality remaining after this sifting is unlikely to be all that high.

Thus it is the Chinese who takes the agricultural labour off the Russian's hands. He is a cultivator by tradition; his methods are generally more practical and allow a parcel of land to be utilized more fully with less labour. Their settlements therefore soon attain a certain prosperity. Unfortunately, it cannot be determined numerically how much of the grain ground by the seven steam mills located in the Ussuri region has been produced by Chinese and how much by Russians. This is difficult to ascertain if only because the rent paid by the Chinese is frequently in the form of allowances in kind, so that he does not come into contact with the mills at all. The percentage, however, is likely not {p. 198} small.

Indeed, it is precisely here, where no *dira necessitas* compels the Russian peasant to remain true to the ancestral habit of agriculture even as a colonist, that it is interesting to observe how, transplanted into conditions that are in part more favourable, he deviates from his habits without, however, being able to master the altered conditions. In general, the Russian peasant from the Volga or Don region who is transplanted to Siberia knows only how to grow his rye or his barley. In addition, he understands a little pastoral farming. Now it must, to be sure, be admitted that, according to general observation, this southern tip of Siberia is not particularly well suited to livestock farming. The animals suffer extraordinarily from the bites of a vicious fly and die en masse from the so-called Siberian disease, which in all probability is nothing other than the rinderpest that also frequently occurs in Chinese Manchuria. But especially in agriculture, the Russian immigrant, if he is not altogether too lazy for it, is conservative. He is very difficult to win over to innovations, notably also to the introduction of machinery. Nor does it occur to him to learn from the culturally superior Chinese, although he sees that the latter achieves excellent income from vegetable farming and frequently even from fruit growing and gardening. He leaves the field to him, as long as he can fish and hunt in the summer and in the winter has dried enough fish and enough meat to lie on the warm stove bench and let his brick-tea soup slip over a piece of sugar he has placed on his tongue beforehand. But if, as happens more often than one would think, he has not shot enough red and roe deer over a summer or has missed the salmon run—well, then he simply starves through the rest of the winter, and if that rest is too long, he begs the well-to-do for help. Exactly the same conditions as in European Russia!

To be sure, there are also exceptions. In particular, the best experiences have been had with immigrants from the Baltic provinces. These people also command significantly higher wages than the peasants from the southern Russian provinces. But the average is just as described. I made this observation consistently throughout the rest of my journey, where I heard it shared with concern by sensible Russians. Will Russia be able to carry out its colonising plans with human material of this kind? The Russian peasant is incredibly backward culturally; there is no doubt that in standard of living, skill, industry, and capability—that is, simply culturally—he ranks below the Chinese; the Chinese coolie lives on average in better quarters than the Russian woodcutter and certainly fares no worse. Only in physical strength and brutality in its application is the Russian peasant superior to the Chinese; yet the latter is the more solid character. He also preserves his nationality, whereas the numerous Koreans living on Russian soil have all become Russian subjects, partly, to be sure, because they are political refugees who would face the death penalty upon their return home.

For all these reasons, it is necessary for Russia to pay redoubled attention to the Amur region. Precisely because it offers far more favorable conditions in many respects than the rest of Siberia, it is essential that the Russian peasant transplanted here be taught how to turn them to advantage.

{p. 199}

The southern Primorskaya is rich in land suitable for settlement. To be sure, the climate presents various difficulties, though fewer than the more central parts of Siberia. There are probably few regions on earth that exhibit such considerable temperature differences as the Primorskaya, reaching as much as 100 degrees Celsius: in winter cold down to –40 degrees, in summer heat up to +60 degrees, with an annual mean of about +1 degree. The plant and animal life is constituted accordingly. A cross between the tropics and the Arctic zone, without the gentle transitions of the temperate zone and without the temperature moderation brought by warm ocean currents and winds, as in Europe, where southern France lies at approximately the same latitude we are now passing through. After the harsh winter has held the earth in chains for seven months, the hot and yet far from dry summer strives to make everything good twofold and threefold in five months. At the same time, the climate changes with such regularity that one can determine almost to the day the maximums and minimums, as well as the cessation and return of frost, and the freezing over and reopening of the rivers and harbors. The flora, just like the fauna, produces

[Illustration: Khabarovsk (Museum): Marine Animals Department (p. 211).] {p. 200} products of both the tropics and the far north. Cedars, cork trees, palms, foliage plants, and tigers, antelopes, ibises on the one hand, and oaks, birches, dwarf pines, and sables, polar bears, elk on the other hand, are the extremes.

The indigenous population constitutes only about ten percent of the total population of the Maritime Province, where fewer than 230,000 people live in all, despite its expanse from Posyet Bay to the Bering Sea. In that part of the Primorskaya belonging to the Ussuri basin, there live about 180,000 people. This part of Siberia is relatively among the most densely populated. But to be sure, a large portion of the Russian inhabitants, who make up 72 percent of the population (the remaining 28 percent being divided among Koreans, Chinese, Japanese, and non-Russian Caucasians), lives in the towns and does not enter into consideration as an agricultural settling factor. The settlements naturally followed first the course of the Ussuri and several of its small right tributaries. Among them, one of the most significant is the station of Iman, with the settlement named "Grafskaya" in honor of Muravyov. Here, before the opening of the Ussuri Railway, was the transshipment port for goods using the waterway to Khabarovsk and the Amur ports and the overland route to Vladivostok. When the railway was then built, Grafskaya was the point where the South Ussuri Railway, begun from Vladivostok, met the North Ussuri Railway, tackled from Khabarovsk. The port still possesses a certain importance today and is connected to the railway station of the same name by a branch line one and a half versts long. But to be sure, as a result of the railway construction, the settlements have moved toward the railway line running further east. As long as the settlements are still as small and as mobile as they are now, such migration is easy. Indeed, they are neat little houses that we pass, with well-tended gardens in front. In many cases, their newness is still apparent. The first thing in a new settlement is usually the church, which generally also stands out for its elegance against the low log cabins of the peasants.

Over the years, Russia has done a great deal to populate the Ussuri region. It has drawn immigrants, particularly from southern Russia, more recently also from the Baltic provinces; likewise, it has made favorable offers to the Russians and Galicians living in America, and a portion of these has resettled to East Asia. In spite of this, however, one can discern no real method in Russian colonial policy. In many ways, it looks like the play of a small child who drops his doll when a new one comes within reach, and then tears the hat off the good old doll to put it on the new one. As explained above, this part of Siberia opens toward the sea, across which it can most conveniently and cheaply be provided with supplies. The completion of the railway changes little in this regard, since sea transport, no matter how low the special tariffs may be, will nevertheless always remain more practical and cheaper for many goods, and besides, the existing commercial organization is not designed for a rational retail distribution of goods that might arrive by rail. The commercial centers are all situated on the sea. If the gateway—in this case, access from the sea—is therefore closed by import duties that bring no economic benefit to the colonial territory, which does not yet produce anything for export, {p. 201} and are intended exclusively to benefit the southern Manchurian ports, Dalny above all, the entire hinterland of Vladivostok and Nikolayevsk is suddenly forced to pay a surcharge on its imported goods that is in no way justified by any possibility of making up for it on the other side through increased revenues. Russia has now introduced this import duty since January 1, 1901, and that explicitly to provide advantages to the newly acquired southern Manchurian possessions. That the

[Illustration: Khabarovsk (Museum): Model of a gold washing operation. (The box in the background illustrates cubically the amount of gold extracted in the Primorskaya over the last 10 years, p. 211.)]

That the considerations that led to this are of a purely political nature, and that their consequences signify a completely unjustified preference for political viewpoints over economic necessities, is just as clear as the fact that the favoring of the ports in the south occurs at the expense of the ports in the north. What colonial-political immaturity comes to light in these measures! What schematism! Russia willfully conjures up through them a crisis in regions that are still far too little consolidated internally to endure such notched knives at their most sensitive points. The consequences of this policy are already showing {p. 202} themselves. Agriculture, which most needs nurturing, is severely depressed throughout the Amur region as a result of the increased cost of agricultural implements, etc.; food has become considerably more expensive, particularly flour, especially since the territory still has to import a substantial amount of flour. Added to this is the fact that, since the introduction of the tariff, brisk smuggling has developed across the long, uncontrolled, and indeed entirely uncontrollable Chinese border, which now threatens seriously to damage, if not ruin, legitimate business as well. Only from a repeal of the tariff ukase can salvation be expected for the easternmost Russian colonial territories, whose cultural and economic conditions are already highly unhealthy in themselves. Moreover, this tariff is already the second disappointment for the Amur country. The first consisted in the fact that the originally planned stretch of railway along the northern bank of the Amur, from Sretensk to Khabarovsk, was not carried out, since the line was routed through Manchuria. Instead of the hoped-for connection of this hitherto most flourishing region of eastern Siberia, it was furthermore completely isolated, since the greater part of the transit traffic that previously went along the Amur is now also withdrawn from it by the railway diverted to the south. This policy of Russia is cruel and pursues anything rather than the economically so beneficial principle of state compensation for harsh transitions. The Amur country is additionally burdened with the heavy load of a tariff that serves to promote the southern regions even more and to artificially increase the natural diversion of transit traffic to the south as well. With every kopeck of tariff the Amur country must pay, it therefore enhances the ability of its keenest economic competitor, South Manchuria, to turn the hoped-for benefits of railway construction for the Amur ports into their exact opposite. It is incomprehensible that Russia should willfully tear apart again in this manner the fruits of sixty years of industrious labor. But this incomprehension in the face of real colonial-political problems—where the schematism that is twenty times more at home in Russia than among us fails completely—is encountered only too often out here. There is a lack of solid official personnel; complaints about the unreliability and ignorance of the existing staff are universal. The governors-general and governors are recruited from among the higher officers; fortunately for it, Russia does not yet seem to know any assessorism in the German sense in its colonial policy, otherwise the second fundamental evil would surely join the first out here. In any case, Russia's economic policy in easternmost Siberia reveals dilettantism of the most disastrous kind; the weal and woe of the colonial territory depends on the arbitrariness of whichever one-sided foreign-policy, mostly even purely military considerations happen to govern at the time. Under these circumstances, given the lack of suitable colonizers and the dubious quality of the available human material, a cold skepticism toward the economic enterprises of the Russians in the Far East seems to me, upon mature consideration, entirely appropriate.

For almost the entire morning we traveled through forest. Near the small stations at which we stop, almost all of which are built on the same model, the {p. 203} forest is cleared, and one sees fields and grazing cattle. Frequently the stations consist only of an individual station building and the characteristic water tower for the engines; the settlements around these beginnings are yet to come. There is no doubt: they will come.

Alongside the railway line runs an old road that apparently served during the railway's construction for transporting materials, and perhaps previously also as a tract for overland traffic and communication between the settlements lying away from the river. Today the road is completely overgrown; the numerous corduroy bridges are rotten and for the most part collapsed from decrepitude—proof that the road is no longer used at all. The railway has replaced it completely.

Not until the afternoon of this day do we enter more open country. The forest, which in places was not felled at all but simply burned down to make room for the railway, recedes. We cross a number of tributaries of the Ussuri over iron bridges, and now see extensive fields and settlements dense with houses—a sign {p. 201} that we are approaching the Amur and its capital, Khabarovsk. The roads between the settlements are reasonably well maintained; we see peasants bringing in the new harvest. A cyclist accompanies the train for a while, which is hardly difficult for him at our speed of twenty-five kilometers an hour. At some distance we also catch sight of the waters of the Ussuri glittering in the sunbeams, and before long the first building of Khabarovsk comes into view: the high-perched barracks of the Ussuri Railway Regiment. We round a rise, and suddenly we see the city spread out below us across three hills. Behind it stretches a broad expanse of water. There the waters of the Ussuri unite with those of the Amur. After a few minutes we come to a halt: Khabarovsk Station!

The station lies approximately 80 meters above the level of the Amur and is about a twenty-minute droshky ride from the city. Hasty farewells are exchanged with fellow travelers, and then everyone sees to securing a droshky as quickly as possible. I take my leave of my general's family, after they have extracted a promise from me to visit them once more for tea before my departure. My carriage takes me down a pleasant valley to the city, which lies roughly halfway up between the station and the Amur. I have been advised to take a room at the “Khabarovsk Gostinnitsa,” purportedly the best of the three existing hotels. Affixed to the balcony are also two signs reading “Hôtel de Chabaroffsk” and “Gasthaus Chabarowsk.” So they cater to Germans and Frenchmen as well. My room costs three rubles a day; it is remarkably clean and furnished almost elegantly. Water is also available in sufficient quantity, as are bed linens and towels. Truly a highlight among my Siberian memories!

The firm of Kunst & Albers in Vladivostok kindly gave me letters of introduction to the head of their local branch, a German, Mr. Langschmidt, and has also already telegraphed ahead to reserve a place for me on the next upriver Amur steamer. I therefore deliver my letter this very day, and am received and advised with downright charming kindness. On this occasion I learn that the steamer does not depart until Sunday, July 21. So I have three days to spare for Khabarovsk!

### 3. Khabarovsk.

July 19.

The city, which bears the name of the Yakutsk merchant Khabarov, who came to these regions some 250 years ago as one of the first, was elevated to the status of capital of the Priamur Governor-Generalship because of its central location and because the main communication routes of the region converge there. When Muravyov sailed down the Amur for the first time and reached the mouth of the Ussuri, he exclaimed with delight: “Here a city shall stand for me!” At first, to be sure, he and his successors resided in Nikolayevsk, which lies about 1,000 kilometres downstream on the Amur. The new city received fresh impetus from its elevation to capital in 1880, and today it counts roughly 15,000 inhabitants, of whom about two-thirds are Russian. Yet the place makes by no means as imposing an impression as Vladivostok. It bears a distinctly inland character. The {p. 205} larger firms that maintain their stores there are mostly branch establishments of Vladivostok houses. The population is anything but a peasant population. They are mostly petty craftsmen, traders, officials, soldiers, and petty bourgeois, like in a small town back home, who are simultaneously one another’s customers and suppliers, competing with each other and otherwise practicing economic inbreeding. The wholesale business is in the hands of a few. Khabarovsk owes a not inconsiderable influx to the gold riches of its surrounding region. One must interpret the word “surroundings” here as broadly as possible. A district lying 500 kilometres away is still considered part of the surroundings. Frequently, however, the gold deposits lie even further from Khabarovsk. Two large tracts in the Amur Province are distinguished where gold prospecting is said to be worthwhile. One area lies along the upper reaches of the Amgun, the other along the upper reaches of the Zeya. For the former, Nikolayevsk and Khabarovsk come into consideration as the nearest large towns; for the other area, Blagoveshchensk. Acquiring a concession costs nothing. If a gold prospector finds auriferous earth at any spot, he erects a marker, writes {p. 206} his name and the date upon it, and has the seizure of possession registered at the nearest official office. Then the land around the marker post belongs to him out to a certain distance, provided that third-party rights are not infringed thereby and that the person concerned is Russian. In Khabarovsk lives a family that has become very wealthy through working the goldfields. The story told about them is that the father of the family first acquired an auriferous parcel of land in his own name, then a second adjoining one in the name of his wife, and then six more in the names of his six children. Now he is said to lack only one more piece of land to have fully consolidated his holdings. The seventh child is said to be already on the way. Attached to this manner of taking possession is the further obligation that work on the occupied land must actually be commenced within a certain period of time. However, this regulation is frequently circumvented by having a few workers trek out to the area in question before the deadline expires and dig there for a few days, regardless of whether gold is found or not. But that anyone else might forestall the owner in the meantime is hardly conceivable. For the difficulties under which the Siberian gold industry operates are very substantial. First to be considered are the tremendous distances and the lack of transport routes. Machinery that is to be installed, say, this year had to be transported to the goldfields already in the previous year, and ordered in Europe and shipped to Nikolayevsk, Khabarovsk, or Blagoveshchensk a year before that. The same applies to provisions. Since nothing, not the slightest bit of food, is to be had on site, all provisions must be brought to the work sites in the spring. Halfway along the route, warehouses even frequently have to be built, because it is impossible to manage the transports in a single season. These warehouses are exposed to the depredations of adventurers, wildcat prospectors, or escaped convicts. Furthermore, washing can be carried out for at most five months of the year, because in ice it is simply impossible to wash gold. To these grave obstacles is added the greatest of all: the lack of labourers. True, many hundreds are lured every year by gold fever and hire themselves out to the mine owners. But these are adventurers, often a vile rabble, who outnumber and override the few loyal workers and the officials, cheating and stealing from their master wherever they possibly can. Often enough the officials are in league with them. Attempts have been made to try Chinese labourers, but the government resists this. For anyone who has the governor or someone else of influence as a cousin, a blind eye is turned, sometimes both. For a long time the regulation existed that the gold extracted had to be sold to the Russian State Bank or to the Russo-Chinese Bank, which had specially employed chemists determine the alleged fineness and paid only for that. Lastly, one must not forget that the “washing” of gold is fundamentally always a game of chance, upon which a sound business should really never be built. The solid foundation of gold extraction is formed by the processing of quartz. This, however, is not practiced in Siberia, even though gold quartz is also said to be present in abundant quantities, with the exception of the previously mentioned gold mine of Mr. Lindholm on Askold. To be sure, formal {p. 207} mining and washing plants do exist at individual, particularly productive locations, but by the admission of persons who are themselves involved in such large-scale operations, an exact commercial calculation can scarcely be made in washing operations. At times enormous yields, at times tiny ones; the stability, ton for ton, as in quartz processing, is lacking. If, therefore, the gold riches of Siberia are spoken of so frequently, one must nevertheless bear in mind that Siberia’s gold production is merely gold washing (whether quartz mining exists in the Altai mining region,

[Illustration: Khabarovsk (Museum): Clothes of the Goldi made of fur and fish skin (p. 211).]

is beyond my knowledge). It is, however, certain that certain regions of Siberia are very rich in placer gold, which is extracted partly as gold dust, partly in the form of so-called “erratics”—compact, mostly heavily abraded little nuggets, at times even lumps of native gold. But of the thousands who set out to search for it, even in places lying right next to discovery sites, a very few found—something, and one or two really found enough that they could carry it home. The calculation of probabilities turns out hardly more favorable than in a lottery. In Australia things went, and still go today, in exactly the same way. The situation is quite different as soon as quartz gold comes into question. In {p. 208} general, wherever placer gold is found, this is not far off, and it has already been discovered in many parts of Siberia, for example in the immediate vicinity of the Amur. But the exploitation of quartz requires a thoroughly professional mining operation, to which the above-mentioned difficulties apply on an even greater scale than to the placer gold industry. The Russian out here is not equal to a regularized industrial enterprise, which presupposes a substantial store of comprehensive commercial and technical knowledge—and, for that matter, neither is he in European Russia. The directors of the vast majority of large Russian enterprises are not native-born Russians; at the very least, they hail from the German Baltic provinces or from immigrant parents—a fact, unfortunately, undeniable in the light of the Russian national character, which is otherwise thoroughly congenial and dear to me! Still, the gold industry of Siberia is also still in its infancy. How could it be otherwise, when the development of the entire country is still so immensely backward! But development will soon reach a limit, namely in the lack of transport routes, in the lack of knowledgeable managers of enterprises, of reliable officials, of laborers, and—in the climate! The climate that preserved ivory across millennia thus also ensures that the exploitation of Siberia’s mineral wealth proceeds so slowly that our descendants will hardly be able to complain about us, as Alexander once did of his father, that we have left them nothing more to do. — —

Yesterday evening at the hotel, I made the acquaintance of two young German merchants, one of whom kindly served as my cicerone today. Among other things, we paid a visit to the German brewer, a Mecklenburg man, Mr. Lübbens. Yes indeed, Khabarovsk has a brewery! But Mr. Lübbens, or rather the two Messrs. Lübbens—the brother also relocated here from Mecklenburg at an already very advanced age—brew exclusively bottled beer. Only a mineral water factory run by a Russian competes with them locally in manufactured beverages, producing seltzer water and notably the Russian national drink, “kvass,” drawn as a fruit lemonade under carbonic acid pressure. Genuine kvass, of course, is something quite different. Crumbled and roasted black bread, as black as our army bread, is mixed with sugar and boiled down in a measured quantity of water. The cooled brew is strained and bottled for fermentation. Then the fermentation process is interrupted, the bottles are corked, and after some time it can be drunk. It is thus a kind of non-alcoholic beer and tastes somewhat like it too. The beer that Mr. Lübben brews, however, is good German pale lager. Oh, what a delight, deep in the interior of Siberia, to drink German beer at the source once again! To be sure, it does not taste quite as good as in Germany. That is due to the wretched water conditions. For in Khabarovsk they do not yet have water pipes, but use the water of the Amur, even though all the sewage flows into the Amur as well. And your true, noble, honest Russian—with a haircut that seems to have been produced by turning a flowerpot over his microcephalic head and cutting off all around the {p. 209} hair protruding beneath it, which radiates from the crown in its natural lay—drinks the often brownish-yellow water just as it comes from the river. The better sort of people at least filter it. Yet filtered river water is not well suited for brewing. Since there simply is no Pilsen water in Khabarovsk, the Lübben brothers just have to use Amur water as it is, and the men of Khabarovsk cannot demand beer as good as what we have in Germany. In any case, we must be grateful to the two worthy

[Illustration: Khabarovsk (Museum): Ancestor images of the Ainu (p. 214).]

Germans, of whom the younger Mr. Lübben is a man well experienced in Siberian conditions, that on the Amur we can obtain German young beer at an acceptable price at all.

I have just come from the General’s, where I went for tea. It was delightful! Long live Russian hospitality! On my way there, I felt rather uneasy. For I had discovered that I had no formal attire in my trunks that could have met Russian expectations. In Russia, the social master key is the frock coat. Now, while I had indeed taken my frock coat with me to East Asia “just in case,” I had never used it anywhere. The only item of clothing that one absolutely needs {p. 219} over there—that is, in southern East Asia—is tails. One appears at dinner every day in tails: an English custom, sheer nonsense, but one does it all the same so as not to run the risk of being looked at askance. When I then traveled north, I sent my frock coat home from Hong Kong. Now I need it. Yet I have already adopted many a thing from the Russians that strikes me as good and convenient, among others the word “nichevo!” A magnificent word! Fits every situation in life! Used in exactly the same way as “maskee” in the Chinese ports. Strictly speaking, it simply means “nothing.” And yet with it one says everything. Have you eaten, slept, or drank poorly, then say “nichevo!”; have you failed at something, has your rival driven you from the field, have you received a reprimand, lost at gambling, has your wife run off with another man or the cashier with the cash box, have the directors of the bank on whose board of supervisors you sit gone fraudulently bankrupt, have you once again failed to win the grand prize in the lottery, or has anything else unpleasant happened to you, then do as the Russian does and simply say “nichevo!” All sorrow is gone, and you breathe a sigh of relief as if a great weight had been lifted from your heart. “Nichevo” is the lid you put on the pot into which you have previously tossed everything that troubled you; it is the very sponge that appears in the familiar imperative, “Pass a sponge over it!” In Russian, all of that is “nichevo.” The Russian is entirely tuned to “nichevo.” That is why one finds in him so much divinely blessed lightheartedness. And so—my dear lady, my black coat is already floating through the Suez Canal—nichevo!—isn't that so—nichevo?—yes, nichevo!—ha—ha—ha—ha—everyone is immensely delighted that I already speak Russian so well! How the journey agreed with me, how one slept. There is the little daughter: come, say “bonjour, monsieur!” Indeed just as pretty as her mother! No, not quite yet, perhaps it will come in time! The French governess smiles piquantly. Over there, the black-lacquered panel with the massive golden Chinese characters comes from a large temple in Ninguta. On the railway there was also a very young deportee working who looked so pale, and Madame bought his freedom out of pity. Can such a thing really be done? On that point, the best information can be given to us by the gentleman who is just entering and kissing the lady's hand. He is a senior official of the Governorate of Sakhalin, where the deportees have recently been sent. Of course it cannot be done. Yet the man was soon released and then compulsorily settled. In this, the 1,000 rubles will stand him in good stead. Indeed terribly kind of the general’s wife to give a poor, sick exile 1,000 rubles! New guests arrive: several young staff officers, then one of the generals who had traveled with us just yesterday. Today a Commander’s cross hangs from his collar. And over there is the nice old gentleman with the white beard and the long cigarette holder. Well, then almost all of us are together again. One young officer is still missing, still on duty—bah, he'll turn up soon enough! Nichevo! Ladies and gentlemen, may I ask you to step out onto the veranda!—

So we move out to the veranda. The way leads through the dining room. It is very simply furnished. A large table, a number of chairs, a Japanese folding screen, a wide fireplace, in one corner an image of the Madonna with a burning red votive lamp above it. The {p. 211} drawing room, too, is very simple. A small sofa, two armchairs, an enormous camel-hair carpet—of Tianjin manufacture—, a small table, a wide fireplace, in the corner an image of the Madonna with a burning red votive lamp above it. The veranda is spacious. In the middle stands a richly appointed tea table. In the background, two white-gloved orderlies wait for nods from the hostess. “Prenez place!” Wherever you please, please! First zakuska and a little glass of vodka! But please, another right away! Caviar? Or would you prefer some fresh salmon, with

[Illustration: Khabarovsk (Museum): The Animal Hall (p. 214).]

salt, oil, and onions! Ah... here is a very special delicacy: Mongolian cheese, tastes like genuine Gervais double crème from Normandy. Indeed excellent, exquisite! Sheep’s milk cheese, of course; “madame” brought it back from Manchuria. The orderlies present iced coffee mixed with Fürst Pückler ice cream and whipped cream in champagne glasses on which even the condensation is freezing. The French governess serves wine. “Rouge ou blanc?” The white is sweet, the general explains, tastes like Marsala, grows in the Crimea; the red does too, slightly tart, replaces Bordeaux perfectly! I must try the white, and then the red. I decide on the latter. From the other side, bread and cold cuts are served.

{p. 212}

Then come meringues. In addition, a large bowl of confectionery stands on the table, or rather it circulates without ceasing. Between eating and drinking one nibbles on sweets, the famous Russian confections with all the trimmings! Between eating and drinking, however, one also smokes cigarettes vigorously! The long cigarette holder of the gentleman with the full white beard is back as well. One cannot eat and drink as fast as the courses succeed one another. From the right comes punch again, or something of the sort. "Allow me to prepare that for you?" The General takes my only half-full glass away. I watch him intently as he brews the punch. First he puts a piece of fresh pineapple into the glass, then comes a small spoonful of fruit preserved in sugar, on top of that a good splash of cognac, and finally, topping it all off, champagne. Also genuinely Russian, remarks my kind host. I taste it. Indeed, genuinely Russian; for a German palate accustomed to punch—hm—a matter of taste! Whenever one has taken a drink, the host immediately tops it up again, so that one does not notice how much one is drinking. Nor is one supposed to. When refilling, it makes no difference at all what it is topped up with. Whatever is nearest to hand is taken: champagne, red wine, white wine, and cognac in turn, sometimes one thing from the right and something else simultaneously from the left. The mixture is different with almost every sip. A sip of punch, a piece of confection, a puff of the cigarette, a morsel of white bread with Mongolian cheese, a praline, another puff of the cigarette, a sip of red wine, a spoonful of iced coffee, a puff of the cigarette, a small piece of meringue, a sip of punch, different mixture, another puff of the cigarette—in this manner we amuse ourselves for quite a while. In between, conversation buzzes back and forth; the General’s wife is the most amiable and graceful hostess in the world. Then the gathering is temporarily interrupted; people take a little stroll in the garden, which, to be sure, consists mostly of scrub and lawn. The little daughter is allowed to join. Oh, she can speak German too! "Do speak some German with the gentleman; come, do speak German!" She is shy and will not speak German, even though the governess urges her as well. Instead, two seconds later she clears a wide ditch with a high leap. Meanwhile, the orderlies have placed a large samovar of brightly polished brass on the table. We return to the veranda, and our gracious hostess serves each person tea with her own hands. I am charged with the obligation of drinking tea in the Russian fashion. Do I know how? Yes, certainly! They do not believe it. I am to demonstrate. Everyone watches eagerly to see how I will drink tea. I place a lump of sugar on my tongue and sip the tea over it. Everyone roars with delight and claps their hands: "He already knows how to drink Russian tea!" I always used to think that only we Germans back home behave towards a foreigner as if we had a chimpanzee before us that must be pampered and trained. The Russian, however, is far worse in this respect! For a time it is remarkably pleasant to play the part of the chimpanzee. Meanwhile, however, it has grown late. I was only invited for tea and do not know how long Russian custom permits one to stay. At any rate, it will do no harm if I take my leave now. They insist that I stay longer. I would like to very much, for teatime was truly delightful. But it is better that I make a graceful exit and go while everyone is most sorry to see me leave. Well, then in any case I should like to wait a moment {p. 213} until the coachman has harnessed the horses. Not at all, I shall walk. But no, I must be driven to my hotel. Well then, the gentleman from the Sakhalin Governorate will ride along as well. "Goodbye, many heartfelt thanks, a pleasant journey, do not forget to send us the photographs that we are in!" More handshakes—wait, but do not shake hands across the threshold, that brings bad luck! The elegant troika takes us back to the hotel. Now I am sitting in my room and am still in such a wonderful

Khabarovsk: Open-air church service (p. 215).

mood that I do not want to go to bed at all. Ah, nonsense, a person must have his rest! So off to bed, out with the lamp, eyes shut, sleep!... Ni—che—vo!

20 July.

Khabarovsk possesses a number of sights. The most prominent is the large cathedral built of red brick. Adjoining it at ground level is a low framework with numerous bells that ring harmoniously together when striking the hours. At New Year's, everyone has the right to ring the bells.

Along the fairly steeply sloping bank of the Amur stretches the city park, in the middle of which stands the bronze statue of Muravyov. Adjoining the city garden are most of the official buildings, such as the Governor-General’s residence, a library building, and the very rich {p. 211} Natural History Museum. This is substantially larger and incomparably richer than the one in Vladivostok, a true treasure trove for the naturalist and ethnographer who has made Siberia and its native tribes his special field of study. Today I spent no less than four hours orienting myself within it and taking photographs. I was even permitted to enter the hall reserved especially for Siberian gold finds and antique trophies from the first battles with the Chinese. This hall is generally inaccessible to the public and is guarded from profane eyes like the Ark of the Covenant, although there is no reason at all for this. The hall contains no particular secrets. Already the museum suffers in several departments from overcrowding. Nevertheless, the arrangement makes a considerably better impression than in Vladivostok, although in terms of preservation a good deal occurs that would make a European conservator tear his hair out. For instance, the museum attendant was instructed to arrange a number of pieces that I wished to photograph and that were not standing in the right light. I intended to take pictures of some very rare ancestral figures of the Ainu. One of them would not stand. Thereupon the good museum attendant simply takes a long nail, uses a second ancestral figure as a hammer, and with it nails the first firmly to the floor. My protest meets with no appreciation. The man smiles, evidently thinking I had not wished to trouble him, and makes a gesture as if to say: “Oh, please, I am very glad to do it for you!”

Beside the museum building stands the considerably smaller building of the Nicholas Library, which contains 40,000 volumes, mostly Russian and French literature. German literature is hardly represented at all. In the reading room a number of newspapers are laid out, among them several East Asian English ones. The most recent bear a date from — May! In certain papers one sees thick black brushstrokes. Beneath these black strokes stand subversive, liberal, and other wicked things that the Russian citizen must not read. When I think of how much subversive material I have absorbed out there in the wicked world, which no censor’s loving brush covered up with printer’s ink for me, a shudder comes over me.

21 July.

Today my Amur journey is to begin at last. Yesterday evening I was invited to Mr. and Mrs. Langschmidt’s and learned that a cabin ticket has been procured for me. Unfortunately, during this persistently and extraordinarily low water level, the large and tolerably comfortably appointed passenger steamers of the “Amur Steamship Company for Passenger Traffic and Trade” cannot run. A so-called tugboat, which does not draw too much water, must therefore make the trip. I am told that it is a far from pleasant steamer. But I still prefer to take the next opportunity. Who can tell what else might intervene to detain me against my will. Unfortunately, the eagerly awaited news has still not arrived that in the headwaters of the Shilka and the Argun {p. 215} rain has fallen, so that the water calamity will probably continue for a long time yet. Moreover, it has been incredibly hot over the past two days, hotter than I have ever experienced, even during summer travels in the tropics. In the tropics one feels the heat less because of its high moisture content than the dry warmth here, which is followed at night by a fairly sharp drop in temperature. Throughout the day, the light-colored clay soil reflects the heat rays quite intensely, and at night readily releases the stored heat again. Hence the contrast is all the more palpable. This morning it is 35 degrees Réaumur in the shade—certainly surprising for someone accustomed to associating the concept of Siberia with the idea of great cold! On the water it will hopefully be cooler!

Since early morning the bells have been ringing; military personnel in dress uniform can be seen in large numbers on the street, singly and in columns; military bands and color companies march past my windows to resounding music along the main street, the Muravyov-Amurskaya. Today is the Sunday on which the thanksgiving celebration of the Manchurian troops who took part in the recent fighting with the Chinese is to take place. The main part of the jubilee celebration will be a parade in which all the banners captured from the Chinese, nearly two hundred of them, will be carried along. This will be followed by a large open-air service of thanksgiving. For this purpose, a wooden pavilion draped in the Russian colors has been erected in front of the cathedral. Afterwards, the banners are to be hung in the cathedral. I attended the celebration. The parade proceeded according to regulations and with great participation on the part of the rural population. After the parade, with a display of the tremendous pomp of the Greek Catholic Church, a bishop appeared, accompanied by a retinue of numerous acolytes, choirboys, and priests. The ceremony lasted more than two hours. I had intended to hold out until the end. But although I had pulled my thick pith helmet onto my head, I could not endure standing in the enormous heat. Unlike me, the other participants all held out and endured the two-hour sermon. The peasant folk and the soldiers stood bareheaded for most of the ceremonies—for two hours—at +35 degrees Réaumur in the shade—no, they stood at about 50 degrees in the blazing sun! What thick skulls they must have, since of all the participants in this procedure only a single one was struck by sunstroke—in majorem gloriam ecclesiae! The counterpart to this: on January 6 every year, the waters of the Amur are blessed. January 6 is usually one of the coldest days in this region. A hole is hacked into the thick ice of the Amur until the flowing water is exposed. Immediately after the blessing, the fanatical peasants leap into the holy water, only to stand there moments later with their clothes frozen stiff. Holy reason!

At 5 o'clock in the afternoon my trunks are driven to the landing stage, my hotel bill is paid, the tips are distributed, the postcards prudently brought along from Vladivostok are dropped into the mailbox, and Mr. Langschwadt kindly comes to pick me up from the hotel in {p. 216} his carriage to drive me to the landing stage. Down there lies the ship, a side-wheel paddle steamer of little confidence-inspiring appearance. Mr. Langschwadt attends to the shipping of my luggage, the freight clerk clatters on his calculating machine until he knows how much the excess freight to Blagoveshchensk comes to, and I pay. Then my companion recommends me most warmly to the captain. The two young Germans also arrive to bid me farewell, and finally Mr. Lübben, who had received and guided me so kindly, arrives as well, and we exchange many a handshake and heartfelt farewell. After half an hour, our steamer, the *Sergey Dukhovskoy*, is already plying above the mouth of the Ussuri.

{p. 217}

[Illustration: Clearing the sledge track on the ice of the Amur (p. 217).]

## Chapter V. Over 2,000 Kilometres up the Amur.

### 1. On the Black Dragon River.

Apart from the waterway, there is no connecting road suitable for travel purposes between Khabarovsk and Sretensk, the easternmost station of the Trans-Baikal Railway. Highways have been constructed only in stretches, while the regions in between can be traversed only on horseback and with pack animals. This mode of transport is maintained exclusively during the transitional period between navigation and sleigh traffic on the Amur, and even then only for passengers and urgent mail. For anyone who can bear the cold, the most pleasant trip is the winter journey on the ice by sleigh. Of course, that is a matter of taste. I can well imagine that many a person prefers the steamer journey. To be sure, when I consider that over 2,000 kilometres of travel by water lie ahead of me—strictly speaking, it is 2,066 versts to Sretensk—and when, on the other hand, I look around my tugboat and reflect that this uninviting tub is supposed to be by far the largest and best of the various steamers to which I shall entrust myself, a feeling comes over me as though my heart were being dragged down to the sole of my left boot by a heavy weight. For I have also taken a closer look at the interior of my new lodging and have arrived at the conviction that the most tolerable place to stay on board is wherever one can let the most fresh air blow around one’s nose. It smells horribly of human flesh! Every little spot seems to be taken. It was natural that I should first look around for the little spot where, in the view of the shipping agent who filled in the number on my ticket, I was to lay my weary head. This {p. 218} little spot is not in a cabin, but in a dark room which is already very cramped in itself, is moreover filled by an enormous wooden table, and otherwise represents the ship’s saloon. What must the cabins look like! There was no sign of a bed or anything similar; instead, on the wooden bench that ran around the table along the partly crooked walls, lay mattresses covered with glossy leather and stuffed with something hard. If you stretch out on them, you have roughly the sensation of lying on a wooden plank bed. If only one could at least have settled down on it! But no, that was not possible. For in the spot intended for me someone else was already lying—with long, flowing hair, by the way—a poet?—no, merely a priest! The poor fellow presumably assisted today in the midday heat at the two-hour outdoor church service. He who serves grows weary, so let us let him sleep his fill first! I am about to retreat so as to postpone taking possession of the place due to me until later. Just then the curly-haired man stirs, produces a short snoring sound, then a sound that sounds like the “ch” in “machen”, and with a sudden sideways turn of his curly head, spits under the table. Horrified, I follow his movements: that seems to be where the others spit as well; one man alone could not have been responsible for all that!

It practically spins me right out the door, and I climb the stairs again toward light and air. If you are not careful, you bump the crown of your head going up, and your forehead going down. The stairs lead onto the deck. That is the place where the steerage passengers travel. “Tween-decks”, what a euphemism! On the “Sergei Dukhovskoi” there is only a single deck. This consists of iron and is scorching hot by day, bitterly cold at night. The poor passengers lie directly on the iron; beneath their heads or backs they have placed their belongings. Above them arches the vault of heaven, nothing else. We cabin passengers, for that matter, have it not a whit better! Our abode by day—and I believe, for my part, better by night as well—is likewise the deck. To be sure, we reside one level higher. Over the midsection of the vessel and at the same time over the two side paddlewheels sits a superstructure containing the galley, the ship officers’ cabins, the pantry, the oil room, and a few other similar compartments. It is above these compartments, then, that we find ourselves. Fortunately, our deck is of wood. Yet despite the blazing rays of the sun, we have no awning or anything of the kind above us either. Only the so-called chart house and the captain’s berth, which stand up in the middle of the deck, provide a little shade. My fellow passengers have already recognized the advantage and carried the wooden bench as well as the few existing chairs into the shade. My trusty long chair also stands up on deck, albeit in the burning sun. I see already that here politeness—as on the train toward the general—is out of place, and it is every man for himself. So let us act accordingly! It is, to be sure, an unchristian thought. But the priest down there on my cushion thinks in exactly the same way, and he, to top it off, is a man of God.

As is well known, the sun moves from east to west, and the shadow of an upright body eludes it from west to east. Besides, every river has bends after all. Presently we are heading north-west; a bend will come in a moment, and then we shall head {p. 219} south-west for quite a while. So I place my chair right against the wall of the chart house that is currently facing the sun, and lie down! The bend arrives, the ship swings round, and I—am lying in the finest shade! Now the passengers from the other side arrive too, wanting to sit in the shade again. Indeed, there I sit! First you, now me! But I move over a little and make myself amiable. They thank me, arrange themselves picturesquely around me, and we become acquainted. So I have killed three birds with one

[Illustration: Golds with dog sled on the Amur (p. 217).]

stone: firstly, I am lying in the shade; secondly, I have achieved this without treading on my fellow passengers' toes—on the contrary, they are even grateful to me for the shade I leave them; and thirdly, I have become acquainted with them under such advantageous circumstances. For the coming days I shall carefully elaborate my system.

My travelling company consists exclusively of Russians. I am the only one on board who does not speak Russian. To make up for that, however, the others speak nothing other than Russian either. Only {p. 220} Nikolai Mikhailovich is an exception. He speaks German—not much, it is true, but a little nonetheless. The rest speak German or French no better than I speak Russian. They are going to give me Russian lessons. But Nikolai Mikhailovich speaks German. He will always help me out; I need only tell him if I want anything. Nikolai Mikhailovich, the elegant staff captain of the Guards from St. Petersburg belonging to the Semyonovsky Life Guards Regiment, had been attached by the Tsar to the staff of Vice Admiral Alexeyev in order to conduct studies during the Chinese campaign, and is now returning. We are already becoming friends. The remainder of the cabin passengers likewise consists largely of officers. An elderly colonel with his wife; a captain of the Caucasian Cossacks, Ivan Ivanovich, with his wife; Yelena Grigoryevna; a younger Cossack officer with his wife, who had been stationed in the vicinity of Tiflis and brought here a transport of Cossacks to be settled in the Amur region; a lieutenant colonel; a staff surgeon; and a Red Cross nurse. In addition, there is the priest lying below on my cushion, and a uniformed school inspector who vies with the priest in spitting under the table. Furthermore, there are several other characters who keep more in the background, however, since our company is evidently too refined for them. The deck passengers are a motley assortment. For the most part they are peasants, among them some wonderful physiognomies. Wild red or brown full beards, black hair cut under a flowerpot, and crafty little eyes. Then there are a number of gold prospectors, various petty traders, an itinerant pharmacist, a number of Cossacks from the stanitsas along the Amur, and finally also two deportees bound in chains, evidently hardened criminals. They have been requisitioned for road-building and will disembark at the very next landing stage together with their escort. There is quite a commotion down on the iron deck. Small groups have formed, eagerly discussing. Over there, a man, his wife, and three children sit together eating their supper. It consists of chai (tea), khleb (white bread), keta (dried salmon), of which each consumes about half a pound, sakhar (sugar), and vodka (spirits)—an inferior sort that is diluted with water, firstly to increase the quantity and secondly to make it burn less. That is the menu along and upon the Amur—morning, noon, and night. There is hardly any variety in it, at most game, but that must first be shot. Besides, there is no room on board for the people to boil or roast. We, too, are given fish to eat for dinner, in the form of sturgeon soup. With it comes an enormous piece of rye bread, and afterwards a glass of tea. The meal costs 60 kopecks. That is incredibly expensive! The cook either catches the sturgeon on set lines during the passage or buys the fish from the Cossacks for 15 to 30 kopecks for a sturgeon about a metre in length. A single such sturgeon can fill the bellies of twenty people! Catering on board certainly seems profitable! Apart from expensive tinned sardines, there is not even a decent zakuska. Ivan Ivanovich grumbles along on my behalf.

It is growing dark. The priest is apparently still asleep down on my mattress. I go into the cabin. A dreadful mugginess and a suffocating atmosphere rush to meet me. My mind is made up: I shall sleep on deck under the open sky. I fetch a ministering spirit and have my mattress, in the boldest sense of the word, {p. 221} pulled out from under the priest. The ministering spirit accomplishes this by first alerting the priest with a gentle tap on his thighs. Thereupon the priest turns towards the wall, the servant pulls, the priest rolls further round, the servant pulls the mattress entirely out from under him, and the priest sleeps on upon the wooden bench. Then I have it laid with the inlaid side facing up onto my long cane chair, and my bed is ready. As it threatens to turn cool at night, I pull a second suit of flannel pyjamas over my first, over that a Japanese kimono, then wrap myself in a flannel blanket and keep my plaid ready to cover myself. Finally, in order to be secured against any incivilities from Jupiter Pluvius, I put on my wide waterproof rubber coat and pull the hood over my head. Now let come what may—I shall sleep.

But despite my good intentions, sleep proves impossible until midnight. For around 10 o'clock in the evening, after it has grown dark, we tie up at the postal station of Lugovoy, which consists of nine houses. Here wood is taken on board. The massive logs are laid across two wooden carrying poles and borne aboard by pairs of strong men. They then lower one of the carrying poles so that the entire load, which often weighs over a hundredweight and a half, drops straight down into the iron hold. The crashing makes such an incredible din that half-asleep one imagines one hears the trumpets of the Last Judgment sounding. At last, around midnight, the sailors finish loading the wood. I am just falling asleep. But I wake up once more, {p. 222} because a raindrop falls on my nose. I therefore pull the hood completely over my face and also turn up the rubber collar so that the water running off the hood has to stream down it. The rain runs off me, and I can sleep.

July 22.
We have been under way again since daybreak. The sun wakes me. I feel the need to wash. The washroom is located below, next to the cabin. For all the cabin passengers there is only a single washroom. It is of course occupied, and three other fellow travellers are already waiting outside the door. Turns are taken in order. Very kind of these ladies and gentlemen that each requires only about two minutes for their ablutions! Because I value thoroughness more than speed, I take longer. But I make haste and manage in five minutes.
