Outside, five men are already waiting again; evidently unable to imagine what I could be doing in the washroom for so long, and therefore anxious that something might have happened to me, they are pounding on the locked door with their fists. As I step out, I protest this behavior very energetically. I can afford to do so all the more boldly because they do not understand me anyway. The priest and the school inspector, who are next in line, go straight in together and are both done after just two minutes. —

The river widens noticeably. At times it is more than ten kilometers wide and shallow. Navigation is therefore difficult. But since no accurate river chart of the Amur exists, because the river continually changes its channel owing to silt deposits, soundings must be taken constantly. From morning till night, therefore, a sailor stands forward at the bow, casting into the water a long pole attached to a line looped around his hand, and calling out the water depth in feet in a loud, monotonous voice. Our steamer draws 4½ feet of water. Often the depth approaches this figure alarmingly; then the vessel slows to half-speed until the shallow spot is successfully cleared. The sailor’s monotonous calling becomes a steady music to which one grows so accustomed that one only takes notice when he stops calling because he can no longer find bottom. The sun is well disposed toward us. My system proves its worth. There is no longer any room for the priest and the school inspector in the shade of the wheelhouse. They have therefore stretched a plaid blanket across the railing of the paddle box, sleep in the shade of the blanket, and now spit directly into the Amur. On deck there is also a little black bear belonging to the captain. He is very comical and his name is Mitya. The young Cossack officer is also named Mitya, and since his wife is very fond of him, she transfers this affection to the little bear Mitya as well. She feeds him sugar and scratches his fur so that he rolls onto his back and sprawls out with all four paws in the air. Because it is very hot, Mitya the man fetches a bucket of water, and Mitya the bear splashes around in it. Thus Mitya the bear and Mitya the man become friends too. Only now is the young woman completely happy.

The happiness will not last long, however; for this afternoon we are already supposed to exchange our steamer for a second one, which is to meet us {p. 223} from Blagoveshchensk and then turn back, because our steamer cannot cross the bar. The expected steamer is also said to have better accommodations for passengers than ours. The captain has the written order for the captain of the oncoming steamer in his pocket.

At noon today there was fish again, this time boiled. Nikolai Mikhailovich treated us to a tin of sprats from his trunk. In this way we had a zakuska.

[Illustration: Above the paddle box of the "Sergey Dukhovskoy" (p. 222).]

The drinks on board have run out, the bottled beer as well as the lemonade, the seltzer water, and the kvass. If we are thirsty now, nothing is left for us but tea or Amur water. A ridiculous state of affairs!

Around 2 o'clock in the afternoon we put in at the Cossack village of Mikhailovo-Semyonovsk to discharge passengers and mail and to take on wood. On the bank, the village women are waiting for the steamer with all kinds of wares they wish to sell: butter, milk, bread, kvass, cucumbers, blueberries, fresh caviar, white cheese, potatoes, etc. They find a roaring {p. 221} sale everywhere, and our ladies, assisted by their husbands, also take part in the shopping. Mitya the man leaps ashore even before the gangplank has properly touched the bank and dashes toward a bowl of blueberries he had already secured from above. Nor is Mitya the bear forgotten. He receives a large piece of fresh bread, which he evidently finds delicious. Half an hour later he is whimpering because he has got a bellyache. People proceed very cautiously with their purchases. Before buying, everything is tasted, especially milk and butter—the latter with a knife, whereas the milk bottle is simply put to the mouth, an operation performed on one and the same bottle from various quarters until someone decides to spend 20 kopecks for the remainder. While the clatter of wood-loading proceeds, one strolls through the long, sprawling village of some 700 souls, over which a handsome church towers.

At the second blast of the steam whistle, everyone is back on board. We cast off and have already been steering upriver for an hour when the expected steamer comes into sight. Everyone hurries to pack up their belongings. Our steamer stops, drops anchor, and gives the other vessel the signal to halt. The latter is towing a large barge behind it, on which the third-class passengers are accommodated; it swings around so that the current does not drive the towed barge onto it, and moors alongside the *Sergei Dukhovskoi*. Our captain conveys the management’s order to the other captain, and the latter announces on his ship that the passengers and cargo are to be transferred.

What followed really defies all description. No sooner has the announcement been made than a storm of protest erupts among the passengers of the *Sergei Witte*, and while down below they are already beginning to take over the third-class passengers, a fierce dispute develops up above, at first between the captain of the other steamer and his passengers. Soon, however, this dispute spreads over to our steamer as well, and now it is passengers against passengers. A most vehement war of words begins: we resent the passengers of the other steamer for not wanting to cross over to our dreadful old crate, and they resent us for expecting such a thing of them. Grand popular harangues are delivered, people are already moving closer across the paddle boxes, and the air is filled with the shrill voices of quarreling people. The captains are powerless. The captain of the *Sergei Witte* is naturally subjected to the worst of it. In the end, he covers his ears, runs to the speaking tube, and shouts down to the engine room, “Steam to the anchor winch!” Five minutes later, the *Sergei Witte* steams off downriver towards Mikhailovo-Semyonovsk. There he goes, and that is the end of that! But now our captain is furious as well. He, too, will now travel back to Mikhailovo-Semyonovsk, report the incident by telegraph to the management from there, and request instructions for himself and the other captain. And so we, too, turn around and travel back to the stanitsa from which we departed at noon today. Then we moor alongside the *Sergei Witte*, paddle box to paddle box; the passengers, among whom good humor gains the upper hand, mingle and spend the evening together in cheerful chatter and mutual teasing. The buffet on the *Sergei Witte* is likewise sold out. We cannot {p. 225} therefore offer one another any drinks. For that matter, the news we receive is hardly comforting. The passengers had taken no less than four weeks for the stretch from Sretensk, and that on the downstream voyage! Up to Blagoveshchensk they had used four different steamers, and from there two more. Over shallow stretches they had been drifted along on a barge. Numerous steamers along the way are stranded together with their passengers, waiting for high water. A small band of itinerant musicians, mostly Germans who were among the deck passengers, brought them some diversion. Grim prospects for us! By midnight, no reply to the captain’s dispatch has arrived.

July 23.
We remain moored until noon before the telegraphic reply arrives from Khabarovsk. Either the management was unable to decide this awkward case immediately, or the telegraph was not functioning properly. That happens frequently; private telegrams, too, must often wait for days when the wire is heavily congested with government dispatches, which is the rule. The dispatch repeats the order: the transfer is to take place. But the passengers of the *Witte* stand firm and say they can only be removed by force from their steamer, which is decidedly more comfortably appointed than ours, though that does not take much. There is nothing left for the captain to do, therefore, but to submit. He accordingly telegrams his management that he has engine trouble and must therefore proceed to Khabarovsk. And what about us? Oh, word has already been wired to Blagoveshchensk to send out a new steamer to meet us and take us aboard when we can go no further. Towards one o’clock in the afternoon we finally get underway again, once more over the same stretch we already know. After thirty-five versts, we reach the confluence area of the Sungari.

The Amur is nearly fifteen kilometers wide here. One can clearly distinguish where the greenish water of the Amur and the yellow water of the Chinese river, flowing with a steeper gradient, unite. At the mouth lies a thriving Korean colony. The Sungari, alongside the Ussuri, is the second great Manchurian river open to Russian navigation. It forms a waterway that reaches deep into the heart of Manchuria, especially when one includes its tributary, the Nonni. On the Sungari and its tributaries lie some of the principal stations of the Chinese Eastern Railway: Ninguta, Harbin, and Tsitsihar. A large portion of the railway construction material was transported along it to these ports, which are also the terminal points of the railway construction divisions. Its course, which carries more water than the Amur and is also regarded by the Chinese as the main stream, is over two thousand versts long. Its mouth area is dotted with islands formed by the alluvial deposits of loess silt carried down by the Sungari.

At 4 o'clock in the afternoon, anchor is dropped again near a woodcutter's hut. The steamer is packed from top to bottom with wood; wood is stacked on deck as well. After all, we have plenty of time. The steamer that is supposed to meet us will take a couple of days to reach the bar that we cannot cross. It is not until 6 o'clock that we continue on {p. 226} our way. The wheels have barely made some twenty turns when we gently run aground. The wheels now only churn up a mighty spray; nothing helps, our steamer has run onto a sandbank. How long will we be stuck here now? Also until the high water is kind enough to make its way to us? Great excitement on board! Boats are lowered; sailors row around the steamer and probe the perimeter of the vessel with their sounding poles. Fortunately, the ship is not grounded along its entire bottom, but only at the bow. Sailors and deck passengers therefore join forces in hauling the wood supplies stacked forward to the stern. In addition, massive spars are lowered into the water, a chain is laid across them, and the steam winch uses them to heave the ship up at the bow. At the same time, the wheels run in reverse. After several futile attempts, during which the passengers too must withdraw aft to lighten the ship forward, we happily break free again. We travel for a few more hours between the flat banks, and then drop anchor somewhere. It is raining outside, and the cold, wet weather prompts us to pour a little more cognac than usual into our tea for dinner. In this way, it turned into grog.

July 24.

We have been under way since daybreak, but since 8 o'clock in the morning we have been at anchor again, namely before the rather large Cossack stanitsa of Yekaterino-Nikolsk. The settlement, like most larger settlements in this part of the Amur region, dates from 1858. It is the headquarters of the staff of the Amur Cossack Division. Here a number of our Cossacks leave the ship. The place is considered one of the most flourishing Cossack settlements on the Amur. The inhabitants, to be sure, live under the same conditions and engage in the same occupations as the other Cossacks on the Amur: fishing, hunting, navigation, logging, providing post horses in winter, etc. Little farming is done, no more than is necessary for their own needs. It could hardly be otherwise, since transport conditions almost preclude working for export. The Amur fisheries belong to the Cossacks, one of the many privileges granted to them. In other respects, however, the land on which they live has only been given to them in fief; it is not their property. Land tenure conditions will likely yet prove a source of considerable vexation for Russia in its colonial territory.

This morning we reached the southernmost sweep of the Amur; the river now runs due north for a long distance.

From here to the bar that we cannot cross because our steamer draws too much water, it is only a few hours. This morning around 9 o'clock the captain wired Blagoveshchensk once again. If our experiences of yesterday and the day before are anything to go by, we will not get away from here before tomorrow morning. Soon we will know the place by heart; we have already visited every single shop and bought up whatever zakuska we could get hold of. We hear that three large {p. 227} steamers are said to be stuck upstream. On deck we walk around the chart house with the sun and wait anxiously for the telegram. Noon comes, afternoon, evening, midnight. We are still stuck fast. As yet, no telegram has arrived.

July 23.
Evening and morning became a new day; this day had another morning and another evening, and we are still stuck fast. The village youth of Yekaterino-

[Illustration: “Sergei Dukhovskoi” contra “Sergei Witte” (p. 221).]

Nikolsk no longer has any interest in us at all. Today we once again walked around the chart house with the sun. Despite its 1,200 inhabitants, the stanitsa does not possess anything like a village tavern, or we would certainly have stopped in there. And so it grows dark again, but the agent in Blagoveshchensk makes no sign of life. Splendid conditions indeed on this state-subsidized Amur mail-steamer line! And to top it off, virtually the entire time we have been on this tub, we have received no other meat dish than—fish! The prospect that these conditions will continue for days to come makes me nervous. My Russian fellow passengers, seemingly, do not get nervous. They pass the time in their own way; already lively sympathies and antipathies have developed {p. 228} among the passengers, people play cards and—wait. One simply waits! For me, this waiting with no end in sight is, I believe, a greater torment than—if I were to have a ring put through my nose!

26 July.
We are on the move again—only five kilometers, to be sure, but at least we are moving. The agent has replied from Blagoveshchensk that the requested steamer has departed. The mood on board is therefore considerably brighter. With the glimmer of hope in our hearts that the ordeal can now last at most three more days, we now view the whole affair more as a country outing. And indeed, after traveling five kilometers, we drop anchor again at the mouth of a small mountain brook that has clear, cool water. We make our way into the neighboring bushes and cut a large quantity of foliage, with which we build ourselves shady little spots on deck. Mitya the man builds Mitya the bear a veritable hut of leaves. We eat—nothing but fish, drink—nothing but tea, take walks, take river, shade, and sun baths—the gentlemen 100 meters downriver, the ladies 100 meters upriver. Then, resigned to our fate, we lie down to sleep.

27 July.
The program calls for a repetition of yesterday’s, once again with the very same fish soup for lunch! Yesterday morning the captain crossed the fateful bar in a boat with a few sailors and determined that the water depth exceeds five feet, so that he could easily pass right over it with his steamer. He returns enthusiastic about this discovery and declares himself able to reach Blagoveshchensk with his steamer. But the agent in Yekaterino-Nikolsk forbids him to do so, because this would be such an unheard-of novelty that, in his view, it is beyond doubt that the captain is mistaken. If this is not bureaucratic rigidity, what is?

This morning a second passenger steamer arrived, carrying mainly workers and peasants. It, too, is not permitted to cross the bar and ties up in our immediate neighborhood. Now real life begins on the bank. Hearths are built from large stones lying about, and soon something is simmering in the potato pot. Otherwise, over there they also indulge in all the amusements we can afford ourselves. But while on our bathing beach men and women bathe separately, the people over there are less inhibited and establish a family bath.

We are just taking a stroll ashore when we are summoned to our ship by the steam whistle. Naturally our eyes turn upstream. We see a small cloud of smoke and soon clearly make out the outline of a steamer. Deliverance is approaching, but slowly. The steamer halts on the far side of the bar, about nine kilometers away from us.

{p. 228}

Our captain has already gone over with a number of sailors. The newly arrived steamer has a barge in tow. The passengers and cargo are loaded onto this, and then the barge must drift across the shoal with the flowing water. After a few hours, through the binoculars that hardly leave our eyes, we indeed see the barge slowly detach itself from the large ship's hull. It takes a full three hours to reach us. That would correspond to a water speed of three kilometers per hour. The barge comes alongside the steamer, and now begins the exchange of passengers and cargo, which proceeds without any system amid shoving and jostling, leaving behind many a bruise. Among the passengers of the *Sibir*, which is now to take us aboard, are four Americans, {p. 230} who are making a journey around the world, photographing everything that crosses their path, and still have quite considerable supplies of all kinds of good canned goods, which they no longer need so badly, as they are at the end of their nightmare journey. For money and kind words they part with some of it to me, and I now have sufficient *zakuska* for myself and Nikolai Mikhailovich until Blagoveshchensk. By 9 o'clock in the evening, all the passengers of the *Sergei Dukhovskoi* are on board the barge. We set off. But that has its difficulties. For our sake, after all, the Amur does not suddenly run upstream for three hours so that we could just drift along. Consequently, the barge must be towed from the bank. Just as Yermak once did in his youth along the banks of the Kama, the sailors and the male deck passengers now tow our heavy barge from the shore. Progress is very slow; reportedly we will not reach the *Sibir* before daybreak. On the deck of the barge, a colorful throng and confusion still prevail. Cabin passengers and deck passengers are now equal, just as in church. Everyone tries to find a spot as best they can. The iron deck is covered several inches deep in sand. This forms the bed for most people through the night. One wraps oneself in blankets and places some bundle or other under one's head. Thus they sleep: master and servant, lady and peasant woman. Only the Red Cross sister is an exception. Shortly before we crossed over to the barge, she did not feel well. I therefore yield my chair to her for the night. Once she is lying on it, she immediately feels better.

#### July 28.

As the Sunday morning breaks across the land, I awake. It has turned very chilly; the iron deck and the layer of earth have chilled me through and through. Nikolai Mikhailovich’s orderly is just waking up as well; his master is still asleep, like most of the other companions in misfortune. After all, we have a field kitchen with us! So we are going to make tea. The guardsman chops some wood found somewhere or other, and on a stove standing by the bowsprit we boil water for tea. Then the newly purchased provisions, about which Nikolai Mikhailovich knows nothing yet, are unpacked; I have my eye especially on a tin of Brunswick liver sausage. When the tea is ready, Nikolai Mikhailovich is woken up. How his eyes widen when he sees our preparations! An unexpected feast at an early hour! Yesterday evening, in the excitement, no one had had anything to eat, of course. How good it tastes to us now! The orderly also gets a generous brandy.

Overnight we have already come considerably closer to our new steamer. Even so, we have covered at most three-quarters of the distance. About a quarter of a kilometer ahead of us on the bank, we see a long chain of people—our hauling gang! It is 10 o’clock in the morning by the time we lie alongside the *Sibir*. We have thus taken thirteen hours for a distance of nine kilometers. Consequently, we traveled a kilometer in a little under an hour and a half, or to be exact: we covered 11½ meters per minute. A week ago today we departed from Khabarovsk. We {p. 321} [Illustration: On the towed barge across the bar (p. 230).]

{p. 232}

have covered 366 versts since then. As a result, from Khabarovsk to the point where we reached the *Sibir*, we traveled at a speed of 2¼ kilometers per hour. These are speeds that, in the realm of travel by modern means of transport, can undoubtedly pass as records for slowness.

The *Sibir* at least outwardly deserves the name of passenger steamer. It has a proper sun deck, compartments for different classes, a large saloon, though admittedly only a single washroom and a single toilet for all the cabin passengers. That both are soon in an almost unbearable state is due less to the steamer than to the passengers.

My ticket is for cabin number 5. Going in search of the number in question with the pleasant sensation of finally being able to sleep in a cabin, I find it already occupied once again. But I am fed up now with perpetual consideration. Consequently, I fetch the captain. In the meantime, however, four animatedly arguing parties, whose tickets all bear number 5, have already gathered in the cabin, which contains only two berths. I have the fortune of being the fifth with number 5. Meanwhile, I chime in shouting and scolding vigorously in German, heaping the bitterest reproaches upon the captain. But my opponents are—ladies, who from the mere fact that they are ladies construct the right to claim every comfort entirely for themselves at the expense of their male fellow travelers. There is simply no more ruthless creature on earth than the traveling Russian woman. One must fight tooth and nail against her if one does not wish to be flayed, but after all, no one really holds it against you. In recent days the drama with my deck chair started up again, just as on the *Daphne*. At first I remained polite. But I could not get by with that in the long run, because my politeness was interpreted as good nature. So if I felt like lying down on my chair and someone was already lying in it, I asked them, whether male or female, to get up, because I needed the chair myself now; only to the latter did I occasionally permit sitting on it as well, on a corner at my feet. Which they were quite glad to do.

On the cabin question, the captain finally settles the matter by evicting third-class passengers who have already settled themselves comfortably in the second-class saloon, and accommodating us surplus men there together. In itself, nothing can be said against this. Only one thing is disagreeable to me. It turns out, namely, that my sofa berth lies right between that of the Orthodox priest and that of the school inspector. That does not suit me at all. I can almost say “fortunately” someone was left over who had not gotten a place either: namely, the colonel. It therefore works out very well that I can place my spot in the cabin at his disposal. Now he has the pleasure, and I sleep once again on my deck chair outside under the sun deck. On this long journey, my chair has become one of my few true friends.

{p. 233}

Around 10:30 we are already in full steam. The *Sibir* is a sternwheeler, a model typical for the upper reaches of the Amur. We are now traveling through the Lesser Khingan, whose hills hem in the riverbed. China to the left, Russia to the right. But not a single Chinese junk is to be seen, only Russian flags, on the banks both on this side and the other. No doubt about it, Russia here as well as there! Late in the evening we drop anchor near the picturesquely situated Radde, named after the famous explorer of Siberia of the same name.

* * *

July 29. The river, which until now exhibited quite a considerable width, is substantially narrowed by the mountains of the Lesser Khingan range closing in tightly from both sides, in places to as little as a single kilometer. Its current is considerably stronger, and the steamer runs quite close to the bank, where the water flows more slowly. The stops to take on wood are curtailed as far as possible; only just as much wood is loaded as is strictly necessary. In recent years, with the increasing deforestation of the banks, wood has risen greatly in price; on average a fathom costs six rubles. The river now runs in numerous tight bends, imparting to the scenery a peculiar charm that had hitherto been missed, especially since the water level was so low that for the most part one could not even see over the flat margins of the banks. Our steamer makes good speed. Today we covered 140 kilometers and are anchoring by the village of Innokentyevskaya.

* * *

July 30. The Lesser Khingan now lies behind us as well. Toward noon we reach the settlement of Poyarkovo. It is a fairly large village, named in honor of the first pioneer in the Amur Valley, Poyarkov. A handsome church done in white and blue towers over the settlement. We are now only 160 versts away from Blagoveshchensk; the city can already be reached from here by carriage, a "tarantass." The weather is splendid; on board, "all well." Nikolai Mikhailovich is playing a Russian card game called "Wind" with Elena Grigoryevna, and I am playing Sixty-Six with the Cossack officer's little wife. We are playing for sweets. We each bought ourselves a large bag of "karamelli," and every lost game costs one sweet. When I lose, I pay honestly; when she loses, she does not want to pay. If one threatens to call her husband, Mitya, to help, she pays immediately. Cheating is permitted. But whoever gets caught at it pays two "karamelli."

{p. 231}

[Illustration: The ruins of Aigun, seen from the Amur.]

### 2. A City in Ruins on the Amur.

July 31.

Around 9 o'clock in the morning we come near Aigun, once the northernmost Chinese bulwark erected against the advance of Russian influence, and most recently famous for the Convention of Aigun, in which the left bank of the Amur was ceded to Russia. Only a year ago it was a flourishing city, estimated at 15,000 inhabitants. A strong Chinese garrison was stationed there. Today the city is a heap of ruins. It was just around the turn of July that the fighting took place in which Aigun was destroyed, and with it China's power definitively broken on the right bank of the Amur as well. Two kilometers from ruined Aigun, a handsome stone building, a Russian barracks, now rises on the right bank of the Amur. To those people who still insist today that Manchuria is still a Chinese province and that Russia entertains the intention of eventually returning its rule over the province to China, this fact may serve as proof to the contrary. The turmoil in Zhili had absorbed the world's interest to such an extent that the important fighting in northern Manchuria completely escaped it. Until last year, Russia was not yet the de facto master of the right bank of the Amur. Only the Chinese turmoil gave it a welcome pretext to complete the work of Muravyov, namely to take full possession of the right bank of the Amur as well, thus cutting China off completely from the "Black Dragon River." It has likewise escaped the outside world that it cost very considerable struggles to drive the Chinese from the banks of the Amur. The cemeteries near Aigun and Blagoveshchensk are an eloquent testimony to these battles. But the work has been thoroughly accomplished. In Aigun not a single roof beam remains; indeed, one can say almost literally: "not one stone is left standing upon another." In the Convention of Aigun, navigation on the Amur is guaranteed to both nations, the Chinese as well as the Russians. During my journey along the Amur I have not seen a single Chinese junk! Russia has hushed up these battles, or at least failed to let their full significance be seen from its reports. Should I not, then, be surprised to find, instead of a Chinese city, only a vast field of debris and a burned-out ruin? A single year had been enough to allow the site on which a flourishing city had stood until then to become overgrown with man-high grass and weeds, so that from the water one would not even have been able to tell that Aigun once lay here, had one not had one's attention {p. 235} drawn to it, and had not a stone tower and the protruding stone walls of a yamen with their fire-blackened remnants of beams caught the eye.

One must grant Russia this: it does a clean job. In two years it builds an entire city on Dalian Bay at government expense, yet one year of Russian civilization is also enough to remove from the banks of the Amur the last tiny vestige of Chinese civilization and to let the ruins of a city that was flourishing only just now become overgrown with weeds and grass. In the very same year, in the extreme north—Aigun, the last and northernmost outpost of Chinese power, razed to the ground; in the extreme south—Dalny, the new Russian San Francisco. The intervening country was taken into de facto possession only in this past year. Until then, the Russian in Manchuria was merely a privileged concessionaire; today he is the master and acts as such—*ut exemplum docet*.

As I am interested in viewing the ruins of Aigun close up, I ask the captain to put in to shore. He does me the favor, and I go ashore together with the staff surgeon. The sight that meets my eyes is one of the uttermost devastation. Exactly the same precision in destruction as previously along the Taku–Tientsin line, where the Cossacks had likewise dealt so thoroughly with the Chinese *fanzi* that not one stone remained upon another. We forge a path through the rank weeds of a former vegetable garden. The rapid growth of vegetation has sufficed to overgrow even the former streets with grass and thistles. Here and there a foundation and a scorched, red-burned remnant of a wall jut upward; heaps of shards and charred timbers lie along the street alignments. The main street ran parallel to the Amur. We wander through the ruins. Burrs and grass seeds cling to our clothing. As souvenirs we take along a few shell splinters and some broken potsherds. It is a sorrowful scene of destruction that etches itself into our minds.

From Aigun, a carriage road leads south, soon reaching the valley of the Nonni, the longest tributary of the Sungari. Here it already encounters more densely populated areas, in the center of which lies the town of Mergen. The road then follows the course of the Nonni via Tsitsihar toward southern Manchuria. It is no coincidence that the advance of Chinese-Manchurian culture took place northward in precisely this direction. For, following the river valley of the Nonni to the north and continuing further in the valley of the Zeya, a peninsula, as it were, of good arable soil juts into the surrounding sea of steppes. Indeed, Russian colonization advancing from the north, from Yakutsk, also first reached the Amur valley within the reach of this cultivated zone.

We have hardly lingered ashore twenty minutes when the ship's whistle sounds, urging our return. And so we hurry back to the bank. Meanwhile the whistle has urged us on even more insistently, and as we arrive below, we see the boat that brought us across just pushing off from the steamer again to fetch us. As it draws nearer, we catch sight {p. 236} inside of Russian officers unknown to us. They come closer and land. We greet them briefly with the usual handshake and prepare to board our craft to be ferried across. The officers seem inclined to strike up a conversation. One captain in particular speaks at me insistently. I do not understand him. The staff surgeon translates for me in his flawed German, and from it I gather that the captain is particularly interested in my camera. I show him the camera, turning it to one side and then to the other. He takes hold of it, and to my horror I notice that he handles it very carelessly and is about to let light get to the films. I therefore take the camera back from him with gentle force. Meanwhile, however, the staff surgeon translates for me that the officer wishes to possess my photographic plates; and while I consider myself exceedingly polite for engaging so thoroughly with the stranger officer, it only now dawns on me that he wants nothing other than to confiscate the photographs I took in Aigun. It really is a bit much that one should not be allowed to photograph in a territory that has as yet by no legal or constitutional act been ceded to Russia. Yet indignation is of no use here; only cunning and slyness will do. For whoever has the might has the right. First, I therefore try appealing to the facts of international law, declaring that, firstly, I am a German; secondly, we are on Chinese soil; and thirdly, Russia has no right to curtail the liberties of citizens of a foreign state on foreign soil. But the captain merely declares to me, short and sweet: “*Nichevo!*” What that means, I know full well, and for my part I think “*Nichevo*” as well. Since this attempt has misfired, it now comes down purely to that shrewdness more or less common to all nations, if I am at least to prevent the officer from simply taking my roll of film out of the camera, exposing it to the sun's rays, and thus ruining my fine pictures of the Amur voyage along with those of Aigun. I therefore become very polite again and have it conveyed to him that I give him my assurance that I have taken no more than three shots in Aigun. Would he not be so kind as not to ruin the rest of the films for me, but rather—if he must—take the roll with him and cut off the three Aigun pictures in the dark. In the end, he is no monster after all; he claims that he is a photographer himself and thus understands the matter, and says he will cut off only the three Aigun pictures. So I wind up my roll of film, remove it from the camera, and hand it over to him most ceremoniously with a bow. He promises to have the roll of film delivered to Kunst & Albers in Blagoveshchensk as early as tomorrow. Then we take our leave of one another and shake hands as if nothing had happened. Back on board, I am greeted with shouts and teased thoroughly by my Russian fellow travelers. To anticipate matters, I should add right here that in this instance too the old proverb proved true: “he who laughs last, laughs best”—and the latter I did. When the roll of film is returned to me the next day, I hurry immediately to a Japanese photographer to develop it and see what has actually become of my pictures. I am not a little surprised when developing reveals that all the pictures are still {p. 237} on the film—even my pictures of Aigun! It had been too much trouble for the good captain to develop the entire roll and then cut off the right negatives. So he had simply snipped off the last three exposures, and these had not been exposed at all.

However insignificant this incident may be in itself, it nevertheless proves that Russia regards itself as master of the right bank of the Amur, and the ban on photographing the ruins of Aigun indicates that Russia is by no means pleased to have the world initiated more closely into the details of its actions in Manchuria. During

[Illustration: In the ruins of Aigun (p. 235).]

our onward journey, about a kilometer upstream on the right bank of the Amur, we find large stone buildings. They are Russian barracks. The fairly densely populated Chinese bank of the Amur between Aigun and Blagoveshchensk presents the same sight as the town of Aigun. Only rubble and ruins, as well as gardens and fields overgrown with weeds, bear witness that industrious countryfolk once lived here who supplied Blagoveshchensk with fruit and vegetables. For a year now, vegetables in Blagoveshchensk are said to have become prohibitively expensive. The poor captain who stopped at Aigun at my request {p. 238} and thereby enabled me to gain a deeper insight into the secrets of Russian colonial policy later received, incidentally, quite a proper reprimand.

Towards two o’clock in the afternoon we reach the confluence area of the Zeya, and from the flat banks the domes and gilded crosses of the churches of Blagoveshchensk already gleam towards us from afar. There, where the Zeya and Amur flow together, lies the city whose name in German means “the Promise.” Originally, Blagoveshchensk was founded as a Cossack post under the name Ust-Zeysk. But when Muravyov had concluded the Treaty of Aigun, he reported this “glad promise” from Ust-Zeysk to the Tsar, and its present name dates from that time. The city has also kept its promises in other respects, in that it has become the center of a thriving gold industry that developed along the banks of the Zeya and on the upper reaches of the Bureya, yielding on average better results than the gold production along the lower reaches of the Amur.

Blagoveshchensk has two hotels, among which we divide ourselves. I move into the Hotel Rossiya, where I have to pay three rubles for the “number,” and in addition, as in Vladivostok, ten kopecks each for towel, pillow, sheet, soap, etc. The finger-deep dust covering all the objects in the room costs nothing. Dusting, however, costs a tip of 20 kopecks. As I hear, it was none too clever of me to choose this particular hotel. The other one, which was founded by a French émigré family and is called “Mon génie,” is considered the better of the two. In any case, the “côtelette à la maréchal” to which Nikolai Mikhailovich invited me this evening is exquisite. Today I also deliver my letter of recommendation to Kunst & Albers, who are undoubtedly the premier firm in town and own the finest building in all of Blagoveshchensk. The manager, Mr. Kloss, a Russian-German, receives me with his customary amiability. Tomorrow morning he will pick me up at my hotel, and then we shall take a drive through the city.

### 3. Blagoveshchensk.

1 August.

Blagoveshchensk is laid out rather spaciously on the flat stretch of riverbank bounded by the Amur and the Zeya. Stone houses are rare, and the streets and sidewalks still display the primitive condition typical of Siberian towns. The town has slightly fewer than 40,000 inhabitants, most of whom make their living from trade, shipping, and the gold industry. It is the only town in the Priamurskaya, the seat of the governor. His residence stands in an elongated park that extends along the bank of the Amur. Many cyclists are to be seen on the streets.

Mr. Kloss calls for me around 11 o'clock with his carriage. We first drive to the landing stage. The agent of the mail steamer line cannot yet give any definite information about continuing our journey in the direction of Sretensk. For the moment, the mail steamers are largely stranded on the upper reaches of the Amur, but the agent hopes that a steamer which has managed to cross the shallows will arrive tomorrow and continue on {p. 239} westward right away. The prospects of moving on are nevertheless rather dismal. Mr. Kloss believes that when we inquire again tomorrow, the agent will simply hope that the steamer will arrive the day after tomorrow. To be on the safe side, I have a berth reserved in any case.

We drive a short distance through the town and then up toward the hills that rise to the west. Up there on one of the heights lies the military cemetery, and today is the anniversary of the relief of Blagoveshchensk. A year ago, Russian troops crossed the Amur, drove the Chinese border army from its positions near the Manchurian village of Sakhalin directly opposite Blagoveshchensk, and then took all the Chinese settlements on the right bank of the Amur down as far as Aigun, which was likewise overtaken by its fate a few days later. The anniversary is being observed today and tomorrow with every pomp. We hope to arrive in time for at least part of the ceremony. Our troika carries us up the hill at a gallop. We pass a place enclosed by palisades nearly five meters high, which serves as a prison. There, the 21st Rifle Regiment, which had been in action at the time, is already marching toward us. The ceremony at the cemetery is therefore over. Nevertheless, we drive on. When we reach the cemetery, we alight from the carriage. We are met by the governor, the Cossack General Gribsky, accompanied by his suite of officers. Mr. Kloss greets him, and I am introduced. The general addresses me in almost dialect-free German, and we converse for a while with him and the police master, who holds a rather important position in Siberian towns. Then the general takes his leave with his suite, and we visit the small wooden chapel in {p. 210} the cemetery, view the monument unveiled today, as well as the graves of the fallen decorated with fresh flowers. The view opening before us from up here is picturesque. The broad silver ribbons of the Amur and the Zeya, glittering in the sun, wind across a gently undulating plain covered with dense green. One might imagine oneself somewhere in Europe, but the awareness that no villages lie beyond the hills, and that across the way the dense forests only rarely echo with the footsteps of a trapper or a nomadic Buryat, causes a feeling of isolation to steal over us despite the flourishing town at our feet, marring the unadulterated enjoyment. Indeed, there is probably no town of Blagoveshchensk's size so confined to the barest minimum of communication with the outside world. One must travel 1,000 kilometers upriver or downriver before reaching the next town, the last station connected by railway to the great global transport network. The difficulties with which communication between these two cultural poles must contend can readily be imagined in light of the obstacles that attended our journey to Blagoveshchensk and our departure from it. This isolated position of Blagoveshchensk naturally creates quite unique economic conditions. Clearly, everything that has already been said above regarding the economic conditions of the Amur region as such manifests itself in an almost typical manner in the vicinity of Blagoveshchensk. Above all, this holds true for the mining industry. Fairly rich coal seams have been discovered nearby. Naturally, deforestation around so large a town yields its unwelcome consequences far more acutely than near the small Cossack settlements. Furthermore, the gold industry on the Zeya and the Bureya is far more advanced than on the lower Amur. This is evident from the fact that factories have been established in Blagoveshchensk to manufacture implements and even machinery for gold mining. Moreover, the primitive method of gold washing is no longer used exclusively; here and there good results have already been achieved with the amalgamation process. Although the gold industry near Blagoveshchensk still suffers enormously for the present from the fact that Russia's actions against the Chinese living along the Amur have driven these laborers—who, given the general shortage of manpower, were exceptionally valuable—almost completely away from the gold operations, it may nevertheless be assumed that the resulting slump is only temporary, and that under peaceful conditions it will very soon be followed by a recovery. Should the gold industry, over the course of decades, gradually overcome the rigors of the climate and transition to the introduction of steam-powered machinery, the need to extract the existing coal reserves will presumably arise first at this very point in the Amur region. Yet here, too, development is likely to proceed at a slow pace.

The isolated location of the city, however, also led politically to a catastrophe that stands fairly unique in world history in its kind and scope. It will be remembered that at the beginning of the Chinese campaign, the news passed through the press of all countries, with the exception of Russia, that near Blagoveshchensk the Russians had driven no fewer than 5,000 Chinese into the Amur. At the time, this news aroused a storm {p. 241} [Illustration: Landing place near Blagoveshchensk (p. 238).]

{p. 212}

of indignation among all humanely minded people.

It was that same General Gripsky to whom I have just been introduced who gave the order for that deed. Upon his head the entire wrath of the world was discharged. I took pains to see clearly in this undoubtedly terrible affair, and discussed it both in Khabarovsk and in Blagoveshchensk with a wide variety of figures and eyewitnesses. The facts that could be established are briefly as follows. When the news arrived of the outbreak of the Boxer disturbances in Zhili, which also threatened the young Russian possessions in southern Manchuria, Governor-General Grodekov in Khabarovsk issued the order that all available troops stationed in northern Manchuria and along the Chinese border were to be moved immediately to southern Manchuria and to the Trans-Manchurian railway line. The entire garrison of Blagoveshchensk was also imprudently withdrawn, leaving in the city only about 150 men of so-called police troops, who, moreover, were not even armed with modern rifles. This measure was undoubtedly the cause from which what followed arose essentially as a mere consequence. Opposite Blagoveshchensk {p. 213} China assembled a fairly considerable military force. The entire right bank of the Amur from the village of Sakhalin down to Aigun was occupied by troops. The Chinese fired upon the Russian steamers maintaining traffic to Stretensk and Khabarovsk, thus cutting off Blagoveshchensk’s supplies along the only possible route. The Chinese troops, however, did not remain in an observational posture, but began already from the right bank of the Amur to shell the city in earnest. Now, among the inhabitants of Blagoveshchensk there were approximately 3,000 Chinese, active mostly as merchants, servants, and

::: notes
Market square in Blagoveshchensk (the large building is the branch of Kunst & Albers. p. 238).
:::

[Illustration: Blagoveshchensk: Garrison church (p. 238).]

laborers. It was impossible to prevent intercourse between the Chinese on this side and the other, and it is therefore self-evident that the Chinese on the right bank of the Amur used their compatriots in the city as accomplices. The latter also began already to set fires, so that the town, built almost entirely of wood, fell into grave danger. It is equally clear that with 150 police soldiers it was impossible to suppress a potential uprising of the Chinese in Blagoveshchensk itself, much less, therefore, to repel a possible attack across the river. It was therefore a necessity for the governor to put an end, in any case first of all, to the Chinese machinations within the town itself.

{p. 241}

[Illustration: Blagoveshchensk: Bank of the Amur with triumphal arch (p. 238).]

{p. 245}

It is hard to say how he could have done this otherwise in the best manner—one that took into account both the security of the Russians and the humane sentiments of contemporaries—than by imprisoning all the Chinese who were in the city. On the other hand, it would also have been impossible to intern and support the roughly 5,000 Chinese inside or outside the city, because guarding them would have deprived the city of still more defenders. Consequently, the only remaining possibility was to dispatch the Chinese to the other side of the river. But that side, as noted, was occupied by Chinese troops. Had the Russians now placed boats at the disposal of the 5,000 Chinese to cross over to the other bank of the river, nothing would have provided a guarantee that the Chinese military,

[Illustration: A floating department store on the upper Amur (p. 261).]

which had very few craft at its disposal, would not have seized the opportunity to reach the other bank. Consequently, the Chinese were led to a spot situated upriver where a ford was supposedly located, and were told to see that they got across to the other side. The soldiers drove those who hesitated into the river with fixed bayonets. Of the 5,000 people, according to unanimous estimates, only about 40 reached the other bank alive; the rest drifted down the river as corpses toward the Sea of Okhotsk. So much for the account from Russian sources.

Whether this measure can now be justified on human and political grounds is difficult to say. On the Russian side, the lives and property of 35,000 subjects—indeed the entire position on the Amur, won with such great effort—were at stake. A drastic measure also seems to have been unavoidably necessary under the conditions of the time, and despite {p. 246} the monstrous cruelty involved in sacrificing the lives of 5,000 people, no other way out seems to have remained. Indeed, the resolve to do so, if dictated not by fear but by cool deliberation, could lay claim to tragic greatness. But even if one does not acknowledge the necessity of such a measure, in judging it one must nevertheless take the view that such sorrowful events are merely unfamiliar phases of the war of all against all, unfamiliar because

A so-called “burning mountain” on the Amur (p. 262).

so much misery comes to light all at once. Many people, however, react to this much as they do to a large sum of money, at which they take fright when they see it all at once, whereas distributed over ten installments or so, it would make no such majestic impression on them at all. Yet it seems that in this case, one can grant General Gribsky the attribute of tragic heroism only with reservations. For in the subsequent investigation that the Governor-General instituted against him, because the latter did not wish to assume responsibility for the fateful order, the General appears not to have accepted the consequences of his actions, but rather shifted the blame onto a misunderstanding on the part of the police, the police shifted it onto the priests, and the latter ultimately back onto the Governor again, so that the whole act seems after all to have been dictated more by cowardly fear than by sublime, self-assured greatness.

{p. 217}

This morning a small private steamer arrived here from Sretensk, which brought, among others, a young German merchant who is to enter the firm of Langelütge in Vladivostok. In addition, at noon today during dinner at Mr. Kloss’s in the firm of Kunst & Albers, I made the acquaintance of Mr. Albers Jr., the son of one of the heads of the firm. *Tres faciunt collegium*, and so in the evening we find ourselves together again in the dining room of the Grand Hotel “Mon géni”. Several other Germans join us. Soon we also discover a room in which a “Blüthner” stands, and over a bowl of punch we celebrate a thoroughly pleasant German evening. The young merchant, who left home only five weeks ago, is a jolly fellow; he has brought along Berlin couplets still unknown here, and so “Die lauschige Nacht” and “Auf dem Baume, da hängt ’ne Pflaume” ring out for the first time in innermost Siberia, in the capital of the Priamurskaya.

{p. 258}

August 2.

It is, of course, exactly as Mr. Kloss predicted: the expected mail steamer has not arrived, the agent does not know at all where it currently is, but he “hopes” that it will arrive tomorrow. Nikolai Mikhailovich has already visited all the shipowners of Blagoveshchensk and ascertained that a private passenger steamer is scheduled to depart tomorrow. If the mail steamer does not arrive, then we may perhaps travel on a private steamer. Throughout the entire day, pompous processions alternate with military parades. At noon a grand parade takes place on the market square, and an illumination in the evening. Today, as already mentioned, is the one-year anniversary of the liberation of the city from the Chinese peril.

August 3.

Having traveled toward the water with the greatest difficulty to get this far, we might really expect some accommodation on the part of the water now. But no such luck; on the contrary, it is falling day by day. The dispatches from the upper course of the Amur report ever lower water levels. Naturally, the mail steamer has not arrived today either. {p. 219} Nikolai Mikhailovich is in possession of a passport from the Tsar’s own hand, which places him on an equal footing with an imperial courier. On the strength of this passport, he energetically demands of the mail line’s agent that he be transported as expeditiously as possible. In view of this, the agent admits that the mail steamer has run aground en route and suffered an accident that must first be makeshiftly repaired, and that it is therefore very questionable when the steamer will arrive. That is the last straw; our entire traveling party declares a boycott against the wretched mail steamer line with its steamers that make no headway and its eternal fish menus. Since the advertised private steamer is also slow in coming, Nikolai Mikhailovich and Ivan Ivanovich set out together on a search. Their combined efforts succeed in ferreting out a very small steamer that draws only two feet of water, three and a half with cargo, and is said never to have run aground even at the lowest water level. With this steamer, we can travel all the way to Sretensk without changing ships. On the condition that the steamer sails tomorrow, they immediately book all the available cabin berths. We shall therefore be able to travel on tomorrow afternoon. They immediately tell me {p. 250} triumphantly that the steamer belongs to a German who will also be traveling along to Sretensk himself. Indeed, the owner bears an entirely German name. He is named Neumann, but is a Russian subject and, of course, speaks not German, but *daitsch*.

### 4. On the Upper Amur.

Our steamer, the *Kharbin*, is lying right in front of the Hotel Rossiya. Not very many are left of the original traveling party. We divide the available cabin berths among ourselves. Ivan Ivanovich, being the wealthiest, wants to have a cabin all to himself

[Illustration: Settlement on the Shilka (p. 275).]

and his wife, and pays for three first-class tickets each. Mitya and his wife find the fare somewhat expensive. They therefore want to travel second class. In addition, there is the sister and an officer's wife traveling alone, who wants to take her two boys to school in Tomsk. They likewise travel second class because on a private steamer they do not enjoy the fare discount they receive on the mail steamer and on the railway. Consequently, Mitya must part from his little wife, which grieves them both greatly. The sister, the officer's wife with her youngest boy, and Mitya's wife move into “the” second-class cabin. Nikolai Mikhailovich and I occupy the first-class saloon. Mitya, wanting to economize, now even travels as a deck passenger and, through his wife's intercession, gets my long chair to sleep on at night. But since two sofa berths in the first-class saloon are still {p. 251} free, the officer's wife traveling alone decides to take a first-class ticket for her eldest son as well, who as a child pays only half fare, and for the fourth place we gain a captain of the Ussuri Cossacks whose destination is Albazin. So far, everything is quite nicely arranged. We go on board after lunch; the crew is still busily loading. The steamer's cargo consists for the most part of dried salmon, which, tied together in bundles without any further packaging, is stacked directly in the ship's

[Illustration: Rocky bluff with navigation mark on the Shilka (p. 277).]

hold and tickles the nose with an agreeable smell of grease. Little by little, the deck passengers also arrive. I must confess that when we see them advancing in droves, we feel somewhat anxious. How is the little ship to carry so many people, and above all, where are the poor people to find room to sleep, and what if it begins to rain? But these considerations seem matters of indifference to the child of Israel, for the rubles are none the worse if the passengers who pay them are packed onto the deck just like the salmon down in the ship's belly. To be sure, the steamer's certificate hangs in the saloon, stating precisely the number of passengers that must not {p. 252} be exceeded. But for a ruble, the inspecting gendarme overlooks many a thing, and for the rest, the Siberian proverb proves true: “Heaven is high, and the tsar is far away.”

The steamer is scheduled to depart at five o'clock; naturally it does not depart, for Herr Neumann is still having salmon packed into the hold, and this or that deck passenger still turns up. Soon even the little patch of deck intended for the cabin passengers is covered with people, and the only space where one still possesses some freedom of movement is our cabin, although freedom of movement here too is limited to sticking one's legs under the table or stretching out on one's sofa berth. But our saloon has one good feature: it is located right at the bow and has windows on three sides. So we shall have a good view.

Two weeks ago today we departed from Khabarovsk, and we can bear witness that we have not lost a single minute in pressing onward—at least not voluntarily!

{p. 258}

August 5.

The *Kharbin* is supposed to depart at ten o'clock in the morning. I therefore make haste to purchase a goodly portion of *zakuska* at Kunst & Albers; I also arm myself, according to local custom, with a travel pillow, since of course on the *Kharbin*, too, any kind of bedding is out of the question. Nikolai Mikhailovich has undertaken to procure the beverages, Crimean wine and vodka. We will regularly take our meals together and share the expenses afterward.

[Illustration: Mail steamer on the Shilka (p. 277).]

Before ten o'clock I am back on board, but Herr Neumann is still not ready. Yet he swears by Mount Sinai that the steamer will leave at twelve o'clock.

At twelve o'clock the steamer naturally does not leave. Herr Neumann now declares he will hang himself from the mast if the steamer does not leave at two o'clock. Even my Russian fellow travelers lose patience and press the ship's owner hard.

At two o'clock the steamer still does not depart, nor do we see Herr Neumann hang himself from the mast, much as we wait in vain. With a resigned smile, we merely hear the assurance {p. 254} that the steamer will now definitely leave at four o'clock. I try a psychologically more effective form of pressure. Namely, I have not yet paid for my luggage freight, and I inform Herr Neumann that I will not pay it to him either if the steamer does not depart at four o'clock as agreed.

The steamer does not leave at four o'clock, and I declare to Mr. Neumann that under no circumstances will he receive my luggage freight now. He is very sad about this and assures me that he will make up the lost time by sailing right through the night tonight. In the end, it is all the same to me as long as he reaches Sretensk after eleven days, as promised. If he keeps his promise, he will receive his luggage freight, otherwise not another kopeck. At six o'clock in the evening the steamer is actually cast off, the engine begins to work, but despite this we still do not set out, for Mr. Neumann has loaded too many {p. 255} [Illustration: On the banks of the Shilka (p. 279).]

{p. 256}

salmon and people. The steamer is already stuck on the bottom before it has even departed. A wire cable is therefore fastened to a large steamer lying alongside the "Kharbin," and the steam winch slowly drags the steamer off the shallow bank and into the fairway. At half past six the "Kharbin" is finally freed, and just as the "kommandir" (captain) is about to give the order for departure, a man hurriedly arrives on the shore, waves and shouts to the steamer, jumps into a skiff, attempts, standing upright in the skiff, to row up to

[Illustration: Sretensk Station, the easternmost station of the Transbaikal Railway (p. 281).]

the vessel, loses his balance, capsizes along with the skiff, and finally wades through the water up to the ship, soaking wet. Amid general hilarity he then collects three rubles from a passenger, and now the "Kharbin" can set off. And so it does, and sails right through the night, just as Mr. Neumann had promised.

August 6.

By eight o'clock in the morning we are already 150 versts from Blagoveshchensk. The entire distance to Sretensk is 1,160.5 versts. During the night, the occupants of the salon were awakened by an absolutely dreadful banging. The boiler room is located on the same {p. 257} deck as our salon, and the wood being replenished is tossed into a hatch situated right next to the back wall of our bedchamber. Once the boiler room is full, another layer of wood is stacked directly against the outer wall of our cabin. But the sailors go about this with incredible recklessness, hurling the heavy logs directly against the wall. Nikolai Mikhailovich has already lodged a very forceful complaint. This afternoon, while Nikolai is taking his midday nap, wood is being loaded again, and with the same persistent malice the sailors once more fling the wood against the wall against which the officer is sleeping.

[Illustration: Chita Station (p. 284).]

Now he is already threatening the sailors that he will drum on their thick skulls with the logs if they do not proceed more cautiously.

It has been raining through the day, for the first time in a long while. Now the water will surely rise soon as well, although that is pretty much all the same to us now. Today we have already passed over shallows where the sounding lead indicated little more than 3 1/2 feet of water depth; after all, the Amur cannot get much shallower than that.

August 7.
Outside, alternating sunshine and rain—sometimes even both at the same time! Our little salon is truly a bright spot on this river voyage so far.

{p. 258}

[Illustration: Transbaikal peasant village (p. 281).]

{p. 259}

[Illustration: Tea caravan (p. 285).]

{p. 260}

Within our four walls no one disturbs us, and so for the first time I have the opportunity again to unpack my typewriter and set about working on my neglected manuscripts. During breaks I go upstairs on deck, squeeze through the deck passengers, and visit Mitya, who feels very comfortable in my chair and, out of gratitude, offers me sweets until his little wife insists that these must first be earned. So the cards are brought out, and the caramels are played for in

[Illustration: On Lake Baikal, eastern shore (p. 285).]

"Sixty-Six." Nikolai Mikhailovich and Elena Grigoryevna also play "Vint," on account of the lack of space in such a manner that she sits in the cabin, he on a camp stool outside the cabin window, and they pass the cards to each other through the cabin window. The funnel of our steamer has no spark arrester; glowing fragments of wood therefore spray out of it in a high column, creating a magnificent display of fireworks in the dark. To be sure, the deck passengers suffer from the sparks: they burn holes in their clothes, and repeatedly one sees a hand hurriedly reach for some part of the body where the sensation suddenly arises: "It's burning!" Along the way we repeatedly encounter {p. 261} large timber rafts carrying emigrants. These people have reached Sretensk by rail with their farming implements and livestock from a more westerly part of Siberia, built themselves the raft, and are now travelling downstream in the cheapest way in the world. In the evening they moor and mostly sleep somewhere in the sand of the shore. This evening we are sitting in our cabin by a stearin candle. The prospective cadet is studying geometry; Nikolai Mikhailovich is helping him. The Ussuri Cossack captain is likewise a good mathematician, who to two fellow passengers

[Illustration: The train ferry “Baikal” taking on a freight train. Beside it, the train ferry “Angara” (p. 286).]

students of the non-commissioned officers' school, even giving them daily mathematics lessons. Thus on this occasion we review the whole of geometry, beginning with the Pythagorean theorem, and finally even calculate conic sections, until our candle stump on the table and we on our sofa cushions collapse.

8 August.
Today we even encountered a floating department store. The goods had reached Sretensk by rail. There the merchant constructed an ordinary raft, built a booth upon it, and stacked his wares inside. Now he travels from Cossack village to Cossack village, and once he has sold out his goods, he will sell his raft into the bargain in Nikolayevsk and travel back upriver on the next steamer.

{p. 262}

Among the deck passengers is a man who is still young, but shockingly hollow-cheeked. He suffers from galloping consumption, but still hopes to reach his home town of Reval. In the cold, wet weather, the poor fellow shivers so pitifully that it is heartbreaking to see. Although the passengers assist him as best they can, he is already so weak that he will scarcely reach home.

[Illustration: Landing stage for the train ferry “Baikal” near Mysovaya on Lake Baikal (p. 288).]

This afternoon we observed a very interesting phenomenon. The river, which is bordered almost along its entire length by low hills, suddenly makes a sharp turn and flows in a nearly closed curve along steeply sloping, brightly glowing white hillsides, on which small puffs of smoke can be observed. These are the so-called Burning Mountains, past which we are traveling, and which are found in several places in this region. The Russians explain the phenomenon by the assumption that a seam of coal is gradually burning away. This view is correct to the extent that a coal seam is indeed present, running along the wall at about three-quarters of its height. However, {p. 263} the fire is sustained mainly by naphtha gases escaping from the coal seam, which evidently ignite spontaneously. For in winter, neither smoke nor, at night, the formation of flames is said to appear. Now it is very interesting to observe how fire and water have worked together to cause the river positively to eat its way into the mountain. Even now one can clearly see that the river valley originally ran fairly straight. The loop, which later expanded into an almost closed circle, formed only gradually. The question is which force is the primary one and promotes the erosive activity most strongly, that of fire or that of water. Probably four different factors combine to produce the gnawing effect: the flowing water of the river, precipitation, fire, and the expansive force of freezing ice. The process may perhaps be conceived as follows: the fire begins first; so long as a sufficient quantity of oxygen can reach the coal seam, the fire digs into the mountain. The rain carries sand downward from the overlying layers of soil; part of this sand remains lying on the edge of the cavity, and the cavity partially fills with water. When frost arrives, the water freezes, and the surface of the steep wall above and below the cavity likewise freezes; and when a thaw sets in, the soil has been loosened by the expansive force of the ice, so that rain- or meltwater coming from above has an easy task washing the loosened layer into the river. Because the soil situated above the naphtha-bearing coal seam now also tumbles down, and the residues left lying in the combustion cavity, together with the underlying layer of sand, are washed into the river, the coal seam is exposed once more; and when the escaping gases ignite again under the influence of the spring sun or as a result of electrical discharge, the same process can begin anew. In any case, however—whether this theory be correct or not—it could be established that the erosive power of the river is not the primary force. This is evident from the fact alone that the river does not simply undermine the soil so that it would have to break off {p. 261} downward under the force of gravity. Rather, the bank sloping against the Burning Mountain runs parabolically, that is, in the same way as the slope of a sandhill formed by constantly letting dry sand run, say, from a funnel onto the top of the hill, so that the sand can follow the law of gravity downward evenly along the slopes. The river thus has enough to do simply washing away the earth carried by the other forces

[Illustration: On the western shore of Lake Baikal (p. 288).]

downward, rather than exerting any advance erosive activity. Its strength is just barely sufficient to keep pace with the combustion process.

#### 9 August.

My work is progressing quite briskly, so briskly that I have already run out of writing paper. When we put in at a Cossack settlement in the afternoon to take on wood, I go into the local village store intending to buy some paper. Writing paper has just run out. I therefore buy grey wrapping paper, carefully cut it to size for my typewriter, and continue writing on that. In a pinch, even the devil eats flies!

{p. 265}

10 August.

We are lying off Albazin, the historic Albazin of Tolbuzin and Beuton; at least, the present-day Albazin has been rebuilt on the very site where, in 1561, the first outpost of Russian power in the Amur Valley was founded by Chernigovsky. We are thus on historic ground. The village lies on the bank, which rises quite high. One can still discern the remains of the old fortifications that were once so heroically defended against the Chinese. Reverently, much as we at home might visit the battlefield of Leipzig, my Russian fellow travellers inspect these sites so rich in memories. We linger by Tolbuzin’s grave and by the monument erected to him and his Cossacks, as well as to Beuton and his streltsy, by a grateful Russia. The importance of present-day Albazin does not rise above that of the other Cossack villages along the Amur.

11 August.

It is Sunday once again. This is noteworthy if only because the arduous journey on the Amur has now lasted a full three weeks. The day brings little variety. It is very warm. I feel sluggish and yawn, which is notoriously contagious. That is probably why I see nothing but yawning faces today. From this I gather that the day seems terribly boring to other people besides me.

{p. 266}

12 August.

It is raining as hard as the heavens can pour. It rained all through the night, and water is dripping through the cabin ceiling onto the table, onto our legs, onto our papers. Although this introduces a little variety into the general monotony, it is not exactly pleasant. We therefore complain to Herr Neumann, and he sends us a sailor to putty up the cracks. This is duly done, but not properly; the rain softens the

[Illustration: Post road on Lake Baikal in winter (p. 288).]

putty again, and the water drips merrily through the ceiling just as before. Now we vigorously demand a remedy and let Herr Neumann hear a few flattering remarks about his ship, which he takes amiss. Furthermore, the sailors begin throwing wood against the cabin wall again, and our renewed complaint about it bears no fruit, because Herr Neumann is cross with us. But he is mistaken if he thinks he can defy us. Thank God there is a complaint book on board! Every captain and every shipowner has an immense respect for it. For after every voyage the complaint book must be sub- {p. 267} [Illustration: Angara landscape (p. 290).]

{p. 268}

[Illustration: Irkutsk railway station (p. 290).]

mitted to the authorities, and a justified complaint can become quite unpleasant for the captain and shipowner. And so, Herr Neumann, we request the complaint book. His face falls, but he has to hand the book over. We will not write a complaint into it, although that would have been only fair and proper, but we will, in a sense, keep the complaint book in our custody for the time being as a hostage for decent treatment. The effect is instantaneous. Herr Neumann suddenly hits upon the clever idea that the rain leaking through the cabin roof can very easily be prevented simply by laying the waterproof tarpaulin across the cabin roof, and secondly, that the banging about with the wooden logs can also very easily be stopped by personally giving a sailor, who happens once again to be hurling wood against the cabin wall, a blow with the rope's end.

{p. 269}

In this way we suddenly have everything we could possibly wish for. The complaint book, however, is locked away for the time being and will not be surrendered until we are in Sretensk. Ever since we asked for the complaint book, Herr Neumann and the “commandir” have given us a wide berth, as far as the space on the “Kharbin” permits, and any wishes we express are promptly fulfilled. Indeed, a complaint book is an extraordinarily sensible institution!

13 August.
Our steamer does make rather slow progress; it runs at only about 15 kilometres an hour. The current of the river on the upper reaches is considerably stronger than before, amounting to nearly five kilometres, so that we advance at a speed of little more than ten kilometres. Towards midday we are overtaken by a small tugboat belonging to the postal line, which is towing a barge behind it, similar to the one on which we were towed across the bar, only with the difference that the barge has cabin accommodations where the passengers {p. 270} are housed. When we put in at Pokrovskaya at noon, the tugboat is just casting off again.

Pokrovskaya is the last station on the Amur. Four versts upstream, the name “Amur” comes to an end. There, the Argun and the Shilka flow together and form the Amur. The Shilka is the headstream richer in water, which also determines the direction of the Amur in the confluence area, although the course of the Argun is significantly longer. That the Shilka is also regarded by the indigenous

[Illustration: Irkutsk, seen from the town side (p. 292).]

peoples as the main stream is evident from the fact that, as already mentioned, the Tungusic name “Shilkar” is also applied by them to the entire course of the Amur.

From now on we shall travel exclusively through Russian territory. If hitherto the Amur was officially the border between Manchuria and the old Russian possessions, the Argun now assumes this role. We have thus reached the end of our observations, which were intended primarily for the newest Russian territories, namely Manchuria. Within the scope of our task, the remainder of the journey therefore possesses only secondary interest. It is advisable, therefore, upon departing from these regions, to cast another glance backward and append a few observations of a colonial-economic nature.

{p. 271}

[Illustration: Pendulum ferry between the town and railway station of Sretensk (p. 282).]

### 5. The Amur Region.

As has already been mentioned in passing earlier, one must regard the Amur basin as Russia’s true colonial territory. Even though those parts of Transbaikalia lying east of the Yablonovy Mountains belong geographically and climatologically to the Amur lands, economically they nevertheless occupy an exceptional position in that they have already been in Russian possession for several hundred years and have developed under the same phenomena and conditions as the parts west of the mountains. The Amur region, by contrast, looks back upon a relatively short period of settlement. But if one wishes to assess the economic value of a country, what matters is not so much the extent of the territories under consideration as how many people live in these areas, and what kind of people they are. A country’s mineral resources are dead assets until human hands unlock them.

Russia is first and foremost an agricultural country. This circumstance also stamps its imprint upon the manner in which Russia populates its colonial territories. The settlement of Siberia, and above all Western Siberia, unmistakably bears this hallmark. The opening of industrial enterprises has lagged disproportionately behind agricultural activity, and has mostly occurred only when the exploitation of mineral treasures was so obvious and appeared so tempting that substantial profits were readily to be expected. Despite the diverse mineral wealth of Siberia, really only the exploitation of the goldfields has so far been taken in hand. In support of the Russian’s aversion to industrial activity is also the fact that the relatively very few factories that produce the necessary working materials and tools for this flourishing industry arose only very late on the spot, {p. 272} that is, in the industrial centres. The greater part of the needs of the Siberian mining industry is still supplied today from European Russia, and even more so from abroad.

In the manner of the settlement of Siberia, therefore, various differences come to light that assign a special status to the Amur region. One can distinguish here three different types of procedure. When, after the abolition of serfdom in Russia, the Russian government proceeded to take the settlement of Siberia into its own hands, having until then confined itself to restraining the unauthorized flow of immigration

[Illustration: Cathedral Square in Irkutsk (p. 295).]

or at least directing it into certain channels, the same method was applied that had also become law in European Russia. The emigrants were settled in communes or had to join already existing communes. The latter were jointly and severally liable for raising the poll tax of their community members. The commune as such was assigned a tract of land fit for settlement, with 15 desyatinas reckoned for each member of the commune. The commune then distributed the land among its members; it remained communal land, and in the event of an increase in numbers within the commune, the respective holders had to submit to consolidations and redistributions of the communal property carried out periodically from time to time. It is obvious that this type of settlement entails great disadvantages, if only because improvements which the respective holder might have wished to make to his {p. 273} land were bound to appear disadvantageous, since the holder in question could never know whether his land would be awarded to him again in a repartition. The same system of agrarian communism which had already made a rational development of agriculture impossible in European Russia manifested itself in the same way in Siberia as well. Here it encountered often stubborn resistance on the part of the long-established peasant population, who had wished to flee from precisely this system in European Russia. But peasant defiance expresses itself nowhere more strongly and with greater justification than in the face of a plot of soil won from the inhospitable primeval forest by one's own sweat

[Illustration: Cathedral in Irkutsk (p. 295).] {p. 271} was. In the nature of things, this method of settlement was still easiest to carry out in the more densely populated regions of Western Siberia. The homogeneity of these territories with the mother country, which also shows only a few insignificant differences of degree in soil conditions and climate, contributes to the fact that the provinces of Western and Eastern Siberia form, as it were, merely the extension of European Russia thrust into the Asian continent. The communal settlements with poll tax and repartition encountered difficulties the further east they advanced. In the less settled Eastern Siberia and Transbaikalia, they met with the so-called *zaimka* system.
