This consists in the settler occupying land in the vicinity of his homestead on a fairly large scale and bringing it under cultivation in rotation, observing fairly long periods of fallow. The effect of the communal settlement that followed later was frequently that *zaimka* owners whose land was incorporated into the newly settled communes abandoned their *zaimkas* and established new settlements somewhere at a measured distance from the commune. In the Amur region, on the other hand, and in part of Transbaikalia, the *volnaya* system is predominant. It consists essentially in each peasant holding firm possession only of the land he has under cultivation, while the grass belongs to whoever mows it. As soon as the population becomes denser, this manifests itself in a shortening of the fallow periods and in a regulation of the use of meadows. While west of the Yablonovy Range, in the cultivable parts of Siberia, identical agricultural conditions and the same communal organization and distribution of property prevail as in European Russia, in the Amur Basin a more liberal conception of ownership predominates. Legally, too, this fact finds expression in that the peasant in the Amur region can acquire land as private property, whereas elsewhere it is absorbed into communal ownership. Added to this is the fact that the different climatic conditions of the Amur region—above all the humidity prevailing in summer and the lack of land that, as in the steppe regions, can be put to the plow almost without preparation—entail a departure from the old familiar working methods of the homeland. In the Amur country, only about five months of the year come into consideration for agriculture. These five months, however, display conditions of an almost subtropical character. Thus, while the severe, snow-poor winter makes the sowing of winter grain an impossibility, of the customary field crops only spring grain remains for Russian agriculture. The cultivation of crops that would adapt to the subtropical climate of the summer months is unknown to the Russian peasant. Furthermore, these crops, just like the moist soil that is harder to work, demand an intensive farming method, almost market gardening, which the Russian, however, does not adopt despite the Chinese example. And so it is that the Amur lands, at any rate today, cannot yet be regarded as a Russian agricultural colony.*) To this a second factor is added. The settlement of the old parts of Siberia was carried out by peasants, the settlement of the Amur region by Cossacks.

::: notes
*) The settlers from the Baltic provinces, who have repeatedly been brought to Vladivostok in recent years, adapt much more easily to the altered conditions, and it seems as if the agricultural future of the Amur lands rests upon them, where the peasant from Little Russia and from the Don and the Caucasus does not really prosper.
:::

{p. 275}

Even though the Cossack is likewise a farmer, he still divides his agricultural activities with those of a soldier, boatman, hunter, and post-driver. A thorough division of labor, however, has so far remained largely impossible for political reasons; in the Ussuri region, it is only just emerging. It is also political reasons that induce Russia to keep away the Chinese—who would be equal to the agricultural conditions of the Amur provinces—to exclude them from land ownership, and to tolerate them only reluctantly even as industrial laborers. Furthermore, the Cossack population has always been accustomed to enjoying certain privileges, and allowance has accordingly been made for this circumstance by granting them the river fisheries, as well as the entire left bank of the Amur from Pokrovskaya all the way to Khabarovsk, to a depth of up to 25 versts inland from the course of the river, as landed property. This allocation, however, was an independent act of Governor-General Baron Korff that lacks government sanction to this day. Should the Amur territory truly experience an extraordinary boom as a colonial land in the future, this measure would likely take bitter revenge, for through it new settlers are cut off from the vital artery of these parts, in that any settlement along the banks would always first have to be purchased from the currently legitimate owners, the Cossacks. But should Russia find itself compelled to strip the Cossacks of this property again, that could hardly take place without serious conflict. It was therefore very imprudent of the Russian government to leave these conditions unregulated up to the present, and it would be still more imprudent were the current state of affairs to be maintained for a long time. On the other hand, the authorities have gone to another extreme by barring new settlers from acquiring private ownership of land over a considerable stretch to the right and left of the Ussuri Railway. This measure is intended to prevent land speculation in the vicinity of the railway line, and no doubt many of our land reformers will warm to it. It is, however, an often-observed fact that in regions whose value is first to be developed through the enterprise of new settlers, free {p. 276} competition often accomplishes more within the space of a year than the wisest of government measures within ten. Practice often works differently from theory. If one therefore considers the prospects of the Amur region as a colonial territory in itself, a whole series of favorable conditions stand out, which, if rationally cultivated, justify great hopes. Yet the colonial politician finds only few measures of Russian policy that do justice to these conditions. On the contrary, it seems as though a one-sided policy—one that looks less to the interests of the colonial land itself than to the external political power position of Russia in the Far East—has allied itself with the shortcomings of the human material available for colonization, in order to create here in the Far East the very same most deplorable economic conditions that we lament in European Russia. Here, proof seems once again to be furnished that lands taken into possession by Russia are withdrawn from global economic progress of the kind pursued by distinct trading nations in their colonizing activity; and political daydreams, which already adorn Russian possessions in Siberia with fantastical visions of the future, cannot obscure from anyone who probes to the bottom of things the clear foresight that the same Russia which in Europe cannot prevent peasants from starving in one province while another does not know how to dispose of its surplus harvest is likewise incapable of guiding its colonial land to the calm economic development and prosperity it deserves by its nature. Those with intimate knowledge of Siberian settlement conditions already claim that the peak of the settlement movement has passed, and the thousands of immigrants who return home disappointed and impoverished suggest that there is some truth to this assumption. If it is indeed the case that Siberia cannot support as many people in its narrow zone suitable for cultivation as Russian optimists seem to assume in view of the country's vast size, then the economic prospects for these regions, even under a different administration, are not so brilliant that we need consequently fear an overwhelming influence on {p. 277} existing world market conditions from the completion of the Trans-Siberian Railway. The economic benefits of this enterprise—which is heavily calculated on future growth and has swallowed capital whose servicing would require an extraordinary elevation of the Siberian and Russian level of civilization—then appear in by no means rosy light. What does it profit a modern state to throw away millions upon millions for the sake of its position as a world power, without being able to shape the economic conditions of the country itself so that, out of their own resources, they are capable of replenishing the expended sums within the foreseeable future? A state that engages heavily in foreign policy without its domestic circumstances compelling it to do so is on a slippery slope—and he who is to feed upon his own fat will grow lean, even if he be a giant!

### 6. On the Shilka to Sretensk.

11–17 August.

The journey on the Shilka is much more picturesque than on the Amur. The rather swift-flowing water has carved out a deep bed. Numerous bends wind around the rock formations pressing against the bank, and the stands of timber here seem to be better preserved than along the course of the Amur. Navigation markers—lanterns—stand along the banks in such a way that from one lantern the next one upstream and downstream is always visible. The boatman takes his bearings from these markers. They are found along the entire course of the Amur and its navigable tributaries, and the lamplighter in his small boat is often the only human soul for fifty kilometers or more. The settlements we pass make much more the impression of agricultural colonies, although the type of village is exactly the same as along the Amur proper. Whenever the steamer replenishes its firewood, we gladly leave our boat for an hour and stretch our legs a little in the forest or scramble up the steep riverbanks. On one such occasion, we discover a Buryat family that has pitched its summer camp in a small clearing. They live in a tent made of birch bark. A number of their utensils—buckets, cups, and so on—are made of the same material. Their domestic animals are dogs and reindeer, whose cows are also milked. Nowadays it is not easy, on a journey through Siberia, to still encounter unspoiled natives in their primitive state. I was told of a young French scholar who had traveled into the tundra to seek out natives and study them in the wilderness. He traveled for months and finally tracked down a single Tungus family, about whom he then wrote an entire book.

We all long for the steamer trip to come to an end very soon. By noon on Saturday, 17 August, we have already reached Ust-Kara, the principal center of the Transbaikal gold industry. The place is only 65 kilometers from Sretensk. For the last time, we buy milk and fruit from a peasant woman; now we will not put ashore again until Sretensk. En route we encounter a tugboat coming down from Sretensk, pulling behind it a barge with deportees. The tow- {p. 278} [Illustration: German Evangelical Church in Irkutsk (p. 293).]

The barge on which the deportees are housed is in any case a considerably better and more comfortable means of conveyance than the rafts on which the voluntary emigrants travel down the river. Today, despite the great shortage of industrial laborers, the deportees are employed only in the mines belonging to the Crown. Siberia as a land of deportation proper already belongs to a bygone era. Colonial conditions meant that the deportation system was having a detrimental effect on free immigration, and thus the island of Sakhalin was chosen as the place of deportation, where the deportees are employed in road construction and coal mining. Lately there has been much talk that the Tsar intends to abolish deportation entirely. Given the shortage of labor, this measure would have a rather drastic impact on Siberian mining and, on a smaller scale, might well appear just as hasty and produce just as unpleasant conditions as the emancipation of the serfs once did. It is deeply regrettable that feuilletonists determined at all costs to write sensationally have depicted the Siberian deportation system in such lurid colors that, despite their otherwise quite commendable works, little benefit has been generated for an objective assessment of the system. Books such as that by Kennan are only suited to confusing still further the already very vague notions of Siberian conditions, and Tolstoy's novels about Siberian deportation likewise often produce impressions that do violence to the actual facts. I share the view of a large {p. 279} number of objective-minded Siberian men who by no means consider the lot of the deportees so deplorable in comparison to criminals interned in prisons. On Sakhalin, for instance, the officials assigned to guard the deportees are often completely powerless. Yet because each is dependent on the other, almost patriarchal relations have developed, which an outsider can scarcely comprehend at all. Socially, too, a criminal who has served his sentence faces far fewer obstacles to his further advancement in Siberia than under narrow-minded European conditions. In Vladivostok and the towns along the Amur, there are a good many highly intelligent and industrious people who were deported to the Far East on account of past missteps, and in those parts it is generally regarded as bad manners to ask people one has just met how they happened to come to Siberia. Profound social distinctions between new immigrants and former deportees or their descendants do not inherently exist, and if it has recently been claimed that voluntary immigrants in Siberia are protesting against continued deportation, this is attributable to the agitation of political zealots who, for the sake of {p. 280} their theories, set aside economic and state interests. Deportation has acquired a very considerable colonial-economic importance for Siberia in a positive sense, and retains it to this day. Yet if one wishes to adopt an exclusively humanitarian standpoint, it is, in my view, far more humane to readmit a wrongdoer who genuinely intends to become a useful member of human society into the community of others almost without prejudice, and to afford him the opportunity to do so, than for such a person—as happens with us in Europe—to be permanently struck from the ranks of respectable people, or of those who might ever become so again, on account of such a dark stain in his past. Deportation is one of those questions that is rarely treated *sine ira et studio*, and it is therefore all the more fitting to push back against the literature that believes it has found in Siberian deportation ready-made material for sentimental novels. On the other hand, it hardly needs special emphasis that the application of deportation to so-called political criminals is an outgrowth of despotism, in the condemnation of which the educated of all civilized nations are united.

Darkness has fallen, but we continue traveling for several hours after sunset. I am packing up the last of my things in the cabin and have just stepped out onto the deck when a sound catches my ear that gives me a pleasant jolt. It is the shrill whistle of a locomotive. Shortly thereafter, our steamer rounds the last hill obstructing our view, and to the right and left on both banks we see a glittering sea of lights approaching. It is Sretensk: on the left, the town; on the right, the railway station. The brief whistling and clatter of a shunting train drifts delightfully over to us. Heaven be praised! The connection to civilization has been regained. It took us four weeks to travel from Khabarovsk to Sretensk, without having stopped for even a single minute longer than was strictly necessary. Soon the *Kharbin* also ties up at the bank, and the first thing we do upon going ashore is to head to the hotel to consult the timetable. Tomorrow morning at eight o'clock our train departs, which will take us first as far as Lake Baikal. At the hotel we find the first newspapers of recent date, among them a non-Russian periodical—and of all things, the *Journal amusant* [Amusing Journal], complete, of course, with various strokes of the censor's brush. With the censor, then, Franco-Russian friendship seems to come to a halt.

{p. 281}

[Illustration: Posolsky Monastery on an island in Lake Baikal (p. 288).]

## Chapter VI. Through Transbaikalia.

### 1. To Lake Baikal.

August 18.

It is indeed a pleasant feeling to know that the ribbon of rails before us extends, with only a single interruption, all the way to our home soil and beyond. Now only train travel lies ahead of us, apart from the short stretch across Lake Baikal, which we shall cross on a train ferry. Only 11 days of train travel left. A long journey, to be sure! One would almost shrink from it had one not just passed through the school of suffering on the water. To be sure, it does not make a particularly good initial impression that the railway line has been impassable for the past three days. The rain that set in over the last few days—unfortunately too late for us—has partially washed away the railway embankment along the line, and Russians take plenty of time for repairs. Yesterday the first train ran again, but it was unable to cope with all the passengers who had accumulated in Sretensk in the meantime. We must therefore expect a very substantial crush today. Even so, the concern that prompts my Russian fellow travelers to get up as early as 1 o'clock in the morning and head to the station with their luggage at 5 o'clock initially strikes me as somewhat exaggerated. But as it turns out, this precaution is entirely warranted. Originally, I had considered taking the so-called luxury train, which since last week has been running once a week between Sretensk and Mysovaya, the terminus of the Trans-Baikal Railway on the eastern shore of Lake Baikal. From Irkutsk, the luxury train then departs for Moscow twice a week. However, {p. 282} I would have had to wait three days for that train; so I prefer to take the slower mail train and spend the three-day layover on Irkutsk.

Nikolai Mikhailovich's orderly is at the *Kharbin* promptly at 5 o'clock in the morning with two carts. After I finally return the complaint book to Herr Neumann and pay for my excess baggage, even though we took 13 days instead of the promised eleven, we walk alongside our baggage carts to the ferry. It is a so-called reaction ferry. Attached to a pile anchored in the river some distance upstream is a long wire cable from which the ferry hangs. Making use of the river current and the rudder, it swings automatically back and forth between the two banks. After a long wait, we are finally ferried across and pile our luggage in the baggage room. At 7 o'clock sharp the ticket window opens, and Nikolai Mikhailovich manages to be the first to buy tickets. The crush is indeed quite extraordinary, and while Nikolai Mikhailovich checks the baggage, I reserve a first-class compartment with two berths for the two of us. Checking the baggage of two hundred passengers takes an incredibly long time, even though the baggage clerk never tires of clattering on his calculating machine to determine the baggage charges. After having already been busy for three and a half hours, we gather again around 7:30 with our former traveling companions at the tea table in the waiting room and then set out to take our seats. To our immense astonishment, however, we find our compartment locked, and a subordinate railway official hands us our coats, which I had already hung up inside. At the same time, we learn that the compartment has been requisitioned for a Russian colonel and for the stationmaster. Naturally, we protest very vigorously. The stationmaster is fetched, and Nikolai Mikhailovich parleys with him at length to no avail. Meanwhile, however, all the other seats on the train have been taken as well, and they would leave us behind without a second thought if we did not stand up for our rights with utmost resolve. But although Nikolai Mikhailovich is by no means at a loss for words and throws his full weight as a staff captain of the Semyonovsky Guards Regiment into the scale, he achieves no result. Surely we would be polite enough, the stationmaster remarks, to give precedence to a colonel. When the negotiations stall, Nikolai Mikhailovich resorts to a ruse of war. A few days ago, you see, a senior German official in the diplomatic service, who was traveling with special recommendations from the Governor-General and had been conveyed on the government steamer from Blagoveshchensk to Sretensk, passed through, and the "German minister" had frequently figured in our conversations. For anyone with such recommendations at his disposal, everything looks quite different than it does for the unrecommended traveler; in that case, the Russians do everything possible to make a good impression. Although it would have been easy for me to obtain similar recommendations as well, I had deliberately declined to do so, precisely because I wanted to experience Siberia as it presents itself to an unrecommended traveler. Suddenly I notice the stationmaster making one bow after another before me, and my astonishment grows as he opens the door of the compartment, {p. 283} which he had hitherto desperately defended, and invites both of us with a polite wave of the hand to take possession of our seats. The puzzle is solved very soon. Nikolai Mikhailovich, it turns out, had asked him quite out of the blue when the "German minister" had passed through. The German minister? Oh, he left only yesterday. "Ah, the gentleman here will be terribly sorry to hear that." — "Why, how so?" asks the official in dismay, "is the gentleman with the German minister, then?" — "Well, of course," says Nikolai

Mikhailovich, “he has to do everything in his power to catch up with him, because he has an important message to deliver to him. He asked me to travel with him because he doesn’t understand Russian!”—I can assure you that I am innocent of this white lie. But the rumor that I was a person of high rank traveling after the “German minister” and absolutely having to catch up with him had evidently spread with the speed of the wind. Everyone treats me with exceptional respect. Even the colonel who wanted to evict us has himself introduced to me. He is too much of a military man to dare inquire into my state secrets in any detail. All that matters now is that I accept the respect shown to me from all sides with the requisite dignity.

{p. 284}

Another carriage is attached, in which the colonel and the stationmaster now take their seats. In terms of punctuality we are already such hardened sinners that we no longer think anything of it when the train departs at 10:00 a.m. instead of 8:00 a.m. In any case, we are departing, and that is the main thing. At first the line runs along the Shilka. Over there lies our good old “Harbin,” that confounded tub; the “komandir” waves from the bridge, and Mr. Neumann also waves farewell to us with the recaptured complaints book. Our journey proceeds slowly. Not until the afternoon do we arrive in Nerchinsk, the scene of that shameful peace by which Russia in its day was forced to return the Amur region to China. The railway bypasses the town at a distance of three kilometers, although there was no reason at all why it should not have been laid closer. But the engineers who built the railway demanded that the town pay them a “subsidy” of four million rubles, in which case the line would be built right up to Nerchinsk. The merchants of Nerchinsk refused to pay the four million rubles, and so the railway was simply not brought close to the town. Other towns were wiser, and the railway engineers are rich men today. Heaven is equally high everywhere, and even calculated from Nerchinsk, the Tsar is still far away. Nerchinsk station boasts a well-stocked buffet where we get excellent Russian food. A true relief after the miserable ship’s fare of recent weeks! Besides, it has its good side that the train runs so slowly: all the better shall we sleep. And so, Nikolai Mikhailovich and I lie down on our sleeping cushions, dream away the torment of the last few days—which was great—from four o’clock in the afternoon until two o’clock the following afternoon, and in this way save the cost of supper and morning tea.

August 19–20.

The region of Transbaikalia through which our railway line passes is well cultivated. The contrast with the Amur regions is readily apparent. Large peasant villages alternate with isolated homesteads. Herds of cattle numbering several hundred head graze on the lawns that are a lush green after the rain. Forest, farmland, and meadow succeed one another. During the night we passed the station of Kaidalovo, where the Trans-Manchurian Railway, still under construction, branches off, running from here to the Manchurian border and then via Hailar and Qiqihar to Harbin; there it forks into a southern line to Mukden, Newchwang, and Port Arthur and Dalny respectively, and an eastern one to Ninguta, Pogranichnaya, Nikolsk, and Vladivostok. In the afternoon of our second day of travel we pass Chita, the capital of Transbaikalia, and cross the Yablonovy Mountains near the station of Yablonovaya in a tunnel roughly 300 meters long, which my fellow travelers look upon as a wonder of the world, and of which they have been speaking for days as the great forthcoming event. On the Vladivostok–Petersburg line, this is the only tunnel. After leaving the valley of the Shilka, we ascended the valley of the Ingoda, which together with the Onon forms the Shilka, up to the watershed, the Yablonovy Mountains,

{p. 285}

and now find ourselves already in the river basin of the Yenisei. In the valley of the Khilok, the railway leads down the gentle heights of the mountains and, near the charmingly situated Verkhneudinsk, reaches the valley of the Selenga, which gathers its waters in Mongolia to pour them into Lake Baikal. The landscape near Verkhneudinsk is enchantingly beautiful; one has a wide view to the south, into the Selenga Valley, the very valley into which the caravan route from Peking via Kalgan through Mongolia and via Kyakhta runs. In winter especially, endless camel caravans move along this road, bringing Chinese tea overland from Hankou on the Yangtze in Central China to the principal tea mart, Kyakhta. Here the Russian wholesalers are established; the tea is repackaged and goes from here to Europe as so-called caravan tea. The so-called Russian tea that we drink in Europe is thus, after all, nothing other than Chinese tea.

### 2. Lake Baikal.

21 August.

The high plateau along which the Trans-Baikal Railway runs reaches an elevation of up to 2,000 feet in the Khamar-Daban. The foothills of this mountain range crowd close to Lake Baikal and, together with the so-called Baikal Mountains, enclose the largest freshwater lake of the Old World. After the Great Lakes of North America and Victoria Nyanza in Africa, Lake Baikal is the largest freshwater lake anywhere. It is over 600 kilometres long, its width varies between 30 and 90 kilometres, and the surface area it covers amounts to 34,179 square kilometres. Numerous legends of the surrounding Buryats and Mongols have been spun around it. Its name "bai-kul" means in German "the rich lake", which refers to its abundance of fish. The Mongols call it the "holy lake", the "dalai-nor". In their legends, they place the home of their greatest hero, Genghis Khan, on one of the islands washed by its waves.

As early as three o'clock at night, the splashing roar of the surf, drowning out even the rolling of the wheels, wakes me from my sleep, which in anticipation had been merely a half-slumber. It is approaching four o'clock in the morning; we have left the Selenga Valley and have already been travelling for a considerable distance along the {p. 286} shore of the lake, which gives the impression of a real sea. Before long, we have arrived at the terminus of the Trans-Baikal Railway in Mysovaya. From what was once a wretched Cossack village, the construction of the railway has caused a large settlement to develop, which is only waiting to be elevated to the status of a town. Our train halts, the passengers gradually wake up, and a certain excitement soon becomes noticeable, if only because the customs frontier lies here. It is rather curious that the traveller, who already had to cross a customs frontier in Vladivostok, must undergo another inspection of his luggage here. But the customs frontier in Mysovaya—or, more correctly put, in Irkutsk—is maintained, partly because the old trade route from China through Mongolia joins here, and partly because goods imported overland from Manchuria into Russia without having passed the customs frontier in Vladivostok are also to be subjected to duty. Customs clearance proceeds without any particular difficulties. Because everything went so smoothly, I press a rouble into the porter's hand as a tip. He is not satisfied with it and demands three roubles. The impudent fellow holds out his open hand to me with the rouble coin. Since one rouble is too little for him, I take the coin back out of his hand, put it into my purse, and now he gets no tip at all. He is so taken aback by this somewhat unusual treatment that at first he stands there open-mouthed, and then slinks off completely crestfallen after I thunder a forceful "Pashol!" (march!) at him.

Once customs clearance is completed, we travel a short distance further to the landing place on the lakeshore. The train is pushed by the engine and halts beside the mighty wooden breakwater, which serves at the same time as a quay.

Lake Baikal is the only section where the railway is still interrupted. Originally, the intention had been to avoid the costly construction of a railway around the steep southern shore of the lake and to maintain the connection between the eastern and western shores by train ferry. Therefore, with great difficulty, an icebreaker built by Armstrong was transported in separate parts to Lake Baikal and assembled over on the other side at Listvyannichnaya. The icebreaker was fitted out in such a way that it can accommodate entire railway trains in its massive hull. Train ferry service was to be maintained by this steamer in summer and winter alike. Another smaller icebreaker, the *Angara*, was also built to assist it. Yet considerable difficulties soon stood in the way of the original plans. In winter, it proved extraordinarily difficult to keep a passage clear through the ice of the lake, which is up to eight feet thick; and in summer, the lake is violently churned up by the air currents that occur with the regularity and force of trade winds as a result of the continental location of Central Asia, so that loading the steamer, which rises quite high out of the water, with heavy railway trains at times appears quite hazardous—quite apart from the fact that the *Baikal* alone would not suffice for the traffic, and the entire operation would grind to a halt if it were to suffer any accidents. The icebreaker *Angara*, however, is not equipped to take on entire railway trains. Goods therefore had to be transshipped. Thus, the Committee of the Siberian Railway {p. 287} had to decide after all on the construction of the Circum-Baikal Railway. I read in a number of books that the construction of this railway is said to involve completely abnormal technical difficulties, and the Russian engineers told me the same. To me, however, it seems as though these accounts are greatly exaggerated and base the assessment of the difficulties solely on the views of the Russian engineers. But given the flat topography of Russia, Russian railway engineers are accustomed to building railways mostly only in level terrain, and therefore frequently perceive difficulties that the railway construction of more mountainous countries overcomes with ease. They have a veritable dread of deep cuttings and tunnel construction. Even to avoid small cuttings, they prefer to lay out sweeping curves. The best illustration of this fear of tunnels is provided by the railway across the Urals, which ascends and descends the entire ridge in sweeping turns and serpentines without deeper cuttings or any tunnel construction at all. It is this fear of tunnel building that gives rise to the rumour that the construction of the Circum-Baikal Railway is attended by such enormous difficulties. To a German engineer, these difficulties would hardly seem worth mentioning. From Irkutsk, the railway can initially follow the valley of the Irkut for quite a distance,

[Illustration: Bridge over the Yenisei (p. 298).] {p. 288} and then, apart from a few cuttings, a tunnel of about 600 feet needs to be driven only once. These, however, are difficulties that cause the Russian engineers such headaches that for years now they have been telling the world what enormous technical difficulties they have to overcome on the Circum-Baikal railway, and what capable men they fundamentally are to accomplish such a feat. Yet even on the plains, their work holds up remarkably poorly to expert criticism. The railway on which we have just arrived is also quite carelessly built. Already now, barely a year after its opening, the embankment has subsided in places to such an extent that, despite the slow speed, the carriages continuously sway to and fro. Operation is therefore conducted only with great caution, and at every turn some sort of sloppiness comes to light that severely compromises operational safety on this section.

Once the morning mists have dispersed, the clear green expanse of water lies before us; a chain of hills and mountains rims the lake. In hazy outlines one can see the other shore lying opposite; in this region the lake is at its narrowest. The landing stage is protected against the swell by a massive wooden breakwater built out into the water, against which the spray of the surf dashes up nearly ten meters high. Moored alongside it is the steamer, the icebreaker *Angara*, which will take us to the other shore. The much larger four-funneled icebreaker *Baikal* is still awaited from the other side. It is no longer used at all to carry passengers, but solely for transporting freight trains. The railway tracks inside the steamer are linked at the landing stage with the tracks on land. A bascule bridge, which can be raised and lowered by an elevator depending on the water level of the lake, forms the movable link between shore and hold, over which the freight trains are shunted into the train ferry. Since our luggage has been checked through to Irkutsk, the railway administration takes care of the loading, and the passengers need only proceed to the elegant salon of the steamer, which is built on the model of an ocean-going vessel, in order, after four hours, to disembark on the western side at Baikal station, opposite Listvyannichnaya, at a similarly equipped landing stage. A brisk wind blows against us from the southwest. The waves come dead ahead; they are short, high waves that set the icebreaker, which as such has no keel forward but is flat-bottomed, into violent pitching motions. As a result, more than ninety percent of the passengers are seasick. Such an epidemic outbreak of seasickness I have never yet witnessed. Particularly among the deck passengers, who generally possess less willpower, one can see veritable corpses, who only begin to revive somewhat as the waves grow lower, the closer we approach the western shore of the lake. We pass an island from which the white masonry and tall tower of the Posolsky Monastery “of the Holy Transfiguration” shine picturesquely across. At Baikal station begins the so-called Central Siberian Railway, which brings us sixty kilometers to Irkutsk in four hours. When we land, our train is ready and waiting, and a veritable rush on it begins. Yet preparations have already been made for this. All carriages are locked, and only when all {p. 289} [Illustration: Station in the Kirghiz Steppe (p. 299).]

{p. 290}

passengers have gathered does the stationmaster give the signal for the carriages to be opened. Now it is a case of: “First come, first served.” Not until four o’clock in the afternoon—customs clearance, the embarkation at Mysovaya, the disembarkation at Baikal, and the transfer of the luggage had delayed us that long—do we continue on our way. The railway runs along the left bank of the Angara. This is the voluminous outlet of Lake Baikal, connecting it to the river system of the Yenisei. In this way, the waters of the Selenga, the Tunguska (the so-called Upper Angara), and the numerous small coastal rivers emptying into the lake reach the Arctic Ocean. The Angara has an almost torrential gradient; it is considered the clearest and most uniformly cold river on earth. From various side valleys, its transparent, faintly green-tinted water, through which one can discern every little pebble on the fairly deep bottom, receives fresh inflow. Toward eight o’clock in the evening—it has already grown dark—numerous lights, indeed a veritable sea of lights, appear to our right on the other bank of the river. Irkutsk station is situated on the left bank of the Angara, but directly opposite, on the other side, lies the city with its more than 50,000 inhabitants, the capital of the Governorate of Eastern Siberia and, after Tomsk, the largest city in Siberia. A troika carries Nikolai Mikhailovich and me across the long wooden pontoon bridge over the Angara to the city with its numerous white houses, its golden and blue domes, the former residence of Muravyov.

In spirit, we are now as good as back in Europe. To be sure, we still need eight days of travel by express train to reach Moscow, and the distance that lies behind us and still ahead of us only becomes clear to us when we determine on the map that, although we have already traveled eastward for nearly five weeks, we are still only at approximately the same degree of longitude as Singapore.

{p. 291}

[Illustration: View of the western slope of the Ural Mountains (p. 308).]

## Chapter VII. In the “Train de Luxe”.

### 1. Irkutsk, the Capital of Eastern Siberia.

We put up at the Hotel “Decko,” which is said to be the best. It will be the last time that I have to endure the miseries of Siberian inns. Yet it seems as though their drawbacks are to be brought home to me once more at the very end in a particularly vivid manner. The hotel is heavily booked, so that Nikolai Mikhailovich and I have to share a room with two beds. Earlier, we had dinner in a restaurant while a troupe of folk singers performed national dances and sang Russian songs. We retired early. Nikolai is already snoring lustily, while I, on the other hand, am fighting a battle with my mattress. Several coil springs are broken; in one {p. 292} place one sinks half a foot deep, in another the unbroken springs dig into one's back with their full elasticity. Despite sincere attempts to fall asleep, I do not succeed. Everyone will know from personal experience that the beast in man awakes when one wants to sleep and has a miserable bed, even though one pays good money for it. In my annoyance, I ring the electric bell to summon some servant. For out in the corridor I have noticed another mattress, and he is to exchange it for mine. But no one appears, and so I resolve to clear out my bed myself, lift my mattress out, carry it into the corridor, and drag the other mattress that is standing there into the room. Alone, I cannot get it into the bedstead, so I leave it lying on the floor and pile the bedding and myself on top of it. This mattress is substantially better than the previous one, but soon I feel a very unpleasant itching, which evidently comes from certain little critters that I now begin to hunt down. There are too many of them, however, and just when I think I am finished with them, new ones appear. Consequently, I put on stockings and gloves, tie my night trousers and night jacket shut at the ankles, and cover my face firmly with a handkerchief. In this way my unwelcome company cannot get at me, and I manage to spend the remainder of the night without further disturbance.

August 22.

Irkutsk is a city in which much wealth resides. It is claimed to have the relatively highest consumption of champagne in the world. The city is largely built of wood. Along the main streets, however, one finds numerous distinguished stone buildings. In the shops that line the main street, one can satisfy all European needs, though everything at a very high price. In particular, I admire the display windows of an extremely elegant clothing store, in which the most modern Parisian fashions are displayed. On the streets one likewise sees much elegance; numerous smart carriages with finely dressed people drive along them, and it causes no embarrassment when the mud splashes high from under the horses' hooves and the wheels. The city possesses a very fine museum belonging to the Irkutsk Section of the Imperial Russian Geographical Society. Furthermore, there is a monumental theater, donated by wealthy citizens. In general, given their mostly quite deficient municipal administration, Siberian towns rely heavily on private assistance. The orphanages and schools of Irkutsk owe their establishment and maintenance largely to private charitable efforts. A large foundling hospital has also been endowed, but it no longer suffices for the need. The life of the upper classes out here, even more than in European Russia, is rather loose, and in the admission of children the principle “La recherche de la paternité est interdit” is considered extended to “maternité” as well. In the city park along the Angara, concerts are held occasionally, at which high society is present, or during which they ride in procession along the street running alongside. Irkutsk is the seat of the highest authorities of Eastern Siberia, which through the railway is directly connected to European {p. 293} [Illustration: The most common type of bridge, e.g., bridge over the Ob, the Belaya, etc. (p. 298).]

{p. 291}

[Illustration: Chapel in a third-class waiting room (p. 299).]

{p. 295}

Russia. In the administration, too, the railway has brought about change, insofar as Siberia, which had hitherto been governed fairly independently, has been brought into closer connection with the St. Petersburg administrative organism, and the administration of justice has been reorganized along European lines. The city is rich in monumental buildings; in it we find a wonderful Cathedral of “Our Lady of Kazan,” which is built entirely of stone and is a masterpiece of Russian architecture. The city also possesses a Roman Catholic and a German Evangelical church. The German congregation of Irkutsk is very numerous. There, one gets by almost entirely with German, while elsewhere French serves very well; English, on the other hand, is spoken almost nowhere. Ever since the railway has existed, the merchants of Irkutsk can afford more frequent stays in Europe, and we find many of them in the {p. 296} German and Bohemian spas. The city is undoubtedly the most elegant and modern of all the cities we have passed through so far—taking Siberian conditions as the basis, of course. Objectively considered, Irkutsk makes no different impression than a mid-sized German town in which a prosperous merchant class sets the tone.

### 2. Through Primeval Forest and Steppe.

One of our greatest worries is whether we can still secure a ticket for the luxury train that leaves Irkutsk tomorrow evening. Nikolai Mikhailovich, who undertook to arrange this, has already been on his way since nine o'clock and brings word that good seats in first class are no longer available. We therefore decide to travel second class, the appointments of which differ in no respect from first class, except that in first class the cushions are upholstered in red plush, while in second class they are in a fashionable shade. We immediately buy tickets through to St. Petersburg; since the railway has a zone tariff, we save over ten rubles by doing so, compared to if we had taken our tickets only as far as Moscow and tried to purchase a new ticket from there to St. Petersburg. The ticket costs 60 rubles: 42 rubles for the fare, 15 rubles for the luxury-train surcharge, and three rubles for the bed-linen fee; the linen is supposed to be changed every three days.

We feel as if we were already as good as home in Irkutsk. Our luxury train leaves Irkutsk at midnight; every single seat is taken. Several different companies provide the luxury trains, among them the International Sleeping Car Company. Our train is a Russian one, and the attendants speak only Russian. The carriages are a good deal wider than our corridor express cars, but otherwise fitted out in exactly the same way. The interior of the compartments is designed specifically for long journeys; the cushions are long and broad. Electric light, generated on the train itself, shines down from the ceiling, and the white linen covers—the attendant having already made up the berths—give us a cozy, welcoming feeling. The train departs on time, and we are just stretching out on our snowy cushions when suddenly there is a jolt that practically flings us against the wall! The Carpenter brake has gripped, and the train comes to a dead stop. An intermezzo already, right at the start of our journey! A few meters ahead of the locomotive, a huge pile of railway ties lies across the embankment. The clear intention was nothing other than to derail the train. I am reminded of the sensational novels depicting hold-ups on the Pacific Railroad. But the Siberian highwaymen—whose bands consist largely of escaped convicts, frequently joined by local peasants—are somewhat less ferocious than the Indians; their sights are set only on the baggage car. They wait with their carts in the adjacent forest, and amid the general confusion caused by a derailment, they plunder the travelers' luggage. In our case, thanks to the vigilance of the train conductor, the matter ends harmlessly enough; the passengers band together to telegraph the Minister of {p. 297} Railways so that the engine driver may receive official recognition. The next day, we hear that a similar attack was made on the postal train approaching from Moscow, unfortunately with greater success. The locomotive and two other cars were smashed and thrown down the embankment, and most of the passengers' luggage was plundered. There are thieves on our train as well. A Red Cross sister has had a trunk stolen from the train. Despite all inquiries, it remains missing. Naturally, the Russian press keeps silent about such incidents, as the censorship does not allow them through, but they are simply part of the reality of the Trans-Siberian Railway. One must not allow oneself to be too swayed by the skillful publicity the Russian Ministry of Transport puts out abroad for the Trans-Siberian Railway. On this railway, things are far from being as they are reported and as they should be, even if one sets one's expectations quite low. During the first few days, we still travel through well-cultivated regions. Zima, Tulun, and Nizhneudinsk pass by us. To afford us the opportunity for close observations of the land and its people, the train naturally travels too fast, although our luxury train is by no means fast in itself. It, too, averages only twenty-five kilometers an hour. The stops at the stations are quite long; once we waited up to three hours, albeit to let the postal train, which had been attacked en route, pass by at a siding.

On August 25, we reach Kansk. We are now already in Yeniseysk Governorate and travel almost continuously through the primeval forest, the "taiga." This does not, at least along the railway line, consist of great, mighty trees; most trunks reach only the thickness of telegraph poles. The forest is heavily thinned; in many places we see stretches of meadow on which herds of cattle, sheep, and horses graze. Farther away from the tracks, however, the forest is said to be denser still and to boast large, mighty trunks. On the train I find a rather different company of travelers than {p. 298} before: a Belgian scholar on a study tour, traveling in the company of a compatriot, a mining engineer who has set up a gold mine in Mongolia for a Belgian syndicate; further, a Russian naval officer who took part in the siege of Peking; an old English colonel who formerly led campaigns in northern India, accompanied by his wife; and a Czech professor from Prague, a thoroughly delightful traveling companion who is up for any mischief to which we spur him on in high spirits. In addition, there are quite a number of passengers who get on and off at intermediate stations. Soon we are a large company in which everyone knows everyone else without having been formally introduced, each helping the other to kill time in a respectable fashion. The gathering place is the elegantly appointed parlor and dining car, which also contains a library, though it holds only Russian works. Incidentally, I must not forget an observation characteristic of us Germans. There happens to be a German officer traveling on the train.

It would, after all, have been the most natural thing for the two of us, as the only Germans, to have become acquainted with each other first. But no, each skirts around the other; strangers to one another, because no opportunity has yet presented itself to be mutually—introduced! Only later, at the end of the journey, are we introduced to each other through third parties. In the evenings, things are generally very lively in the lounge; there is a piano in it, a lady plays the violin, the professor sings Czech songs, and we hold a musical soirée. On the night of August 25, we reach the Yenisei, which we cross on a mighty bridge of iron construction at Krasnoyarsk station, which gleams in electric light. The mighty river reaches deep into Chinese Turkestan, and along its course lie numerous settlements extending almost to the Arctic Ocean. The next day we pass the stations of Achinsk and Mariinsk; in the afternoon we reach Taiga station, which lies in the middle of the densest primeval forest and takes its name from it. Here a branch line connects, leading up to Tomsk, the capital of Western Siberia. Tomsk is the most populous city in Siberia, the seat of a university and various technical schools. It is the most important commercial center of the western parts of Siberia. While the railway has so far essentially followed the old Siberian cart tract, which crossed the Urals near Yekaterinburg and then led via Tyumen, Tobolsk, and Tara to Tomsk and continued from there, from now on the line keeps further south, crosses the Ob—one of Siberia's great rivers alongside the Lena, Yenisei, and Irtysh—on a massive bridge near Krivoshchekovo, and runs via Kainsk to Omsk, where it crosses the Irtysh. The taiga character of the landscape now comes to an end, and the vast Kirghiz steppe comes into its own. The journey {p. 299} through the steppe is very exhausting; the stations are far apart, often consisting of only a few small houses. Otherwise, however, the gaze sweeps across the flat landscape without finding any points upon which it can linger. Countless herds graze within pens, and in vain does one look out for a native Kirghiz. It is truly characteristic of these regions that one can travel for days through the steppe without catching sight of a single native. At intermediate stations we repeatedly pass oncoming trains. The passenger trains frequently carry a dozen carriages of emigrants, who enjoy extraordinarily cheap fares and various other forms of care during their journey. At every station they are provided with hot water free of charge; there is usually also an opportunity for cooking, and local peasants offer provisions for sale. Even the religious needs of the emigrants are catered for, as prie-dieus—one might almost say chapels—have frequently been set up in some corner of the third-class waiting rooms, in which divine service is held. In

[Illustration: Farewell to the Urals (p. 309).] {p. 301} some trains there is even a carriage included that is fully fitted out as a chapel—an itinerant church, certainly also one of the latest achievements of transportation technology. The stations all look more or less alike: wooden buildings, wooden platforms, and never lacking the characteristic water tower and the wood yard that supplies the engines with fuel. From Omsk onward, the stations already make a distinctly European impression. From now on we also find itinerant booksellers at the stations, and even German books and newspapers, which are eagerly bought and read. To be sure, they are not newspapers from the German Empire, but German papers from St. Petersburg and Moscow. Of literature from the German Empire, however, one does not get a high opinion if one is to judge by the stocks found at the station booksellers. It is almost exclusively the travel reading published by Otto Janke that serves as the representative of German literature. I pick out German translations of Russian authors and eagerly study the novels of Tolstoy, Dostoevsky, and Turgenev.

There are also stalls at the wooden stations where one can buy souvenirs, mostly manufactured in the Urals. At one station stands a stall with magnificent carvings of mammoth ivory, which had been preserved in the earth for several thousand years. Now and then, peasant women offer for sale very artfully modeled figures made of kneaded, rock-hard dried, and painted bread dough.

We pass Petropavlovsk, cross the Tobol near Kurgan, and in the night of August 28–29 reach Chelyabinsk station. Here the Trans-Siberian Railway ends. Here begins the Zlatoust–Samara Railway, which connects the Trans-Siberian Railway across the Ural Mountains with the European rail network. On the journey between Kurgan and Chelyabinsk, we passed an obelisk that marks the political border between Asia and Europe and is at the same time intended as a monument to Yermak. Chelyabinsk already lies in Europe; the political boundary thus extends beyond the natural boundary, the Ural Mountains, and incorporates into Europe administratively a tract of land that geographically still belongs to Asia. —

The Trans-Siberian Railway now lies behind us. Without doubt a monumental engineering feat that belongs among the wonders of the modern world! It is a tremendous civilizing task that Russia has accomplished with the construction of this link to the Far East, and this glory remains undiminished, even if the construction in its particulars gives manifold cause for sharp criticism. The building of the railway was both a political and an economic necessity for Russia, and there can also be no doubt that the railway has influenced the development of the vast territory through which we have traveled over a distance of more than 8,000 kilometers in the most advantageous manner. It is, of course, another question whether one should join in the hymn of praise that the press of all countries—and naturally not least the Russian—is accustomed to singing to the railway's construction. For us Western Europeans, the significance of the railway lies primarily in the fact that through it a relatively {p. 301} [Illustration: On the great Volga bridge (p. 309).]

{p. 302}

rapid connection with the Far East has been created. If, however, it is constantly claimed on the Russian side that travel from Western Europe to East Asia would be possible in nine days upon completion of the Manchurian railway line, this assertion belongs, for the next ten years at any rate, to the realm of pious wishes. In the foreseeable future, it is hardly conceivable that the journey between Berlin and Dalny will be possible in less time than at least 14 days. The Trans-Siberian Railway is single-track, switches are not very numerous, and the traffic that the railway already has to handle—which is of almost exclusively Russian origin—is so considerable that the line can hardly bear any further increase through the participation of foreign trade. Even now there is a shortage of rolling stock. In addition, the operation of the railway leaves an extraordinary amount to be desired, as does its construction. I mentioned that our luxury train travels at a speed of only 25 kilometers per hour. That is a speed roughly corresponding to that of our slow branch lines. But the Trans-Siberian trains can hardly {p. 308} travel any faster. The roadbed was laid rather carelessly in the haste of its construction, and by the Russians' own admission the quality of the rail material leaves much to be desired; above all, the rails are far too light for express train traffic. They are the same rails used among us, for example, for the construction of a light railway. Whether the use of these thin rails is to be attributed to embezzlement, which is after all no rarity in Russia, or to a miscalculation may be left an open question. At all events, it is a fact that these immense blunders have already become very clearly apparent, and that Russia is already forced to tear up the entire track again from Chelyabinsk onward and replace it with heavier rails, while the lighter ones are to be used for laying shunting tracks and the like. Added to this, furthermore, as quite a

[Illustration: Landscape in the Volga lowlands (p. 309).]

substantial defect, which unfavourably affects both operational safety and regular transport on the line, is the fact that all the sleepers are made of wood that has not been impregnated and therefore quickly falls victim to the changeable weather. Moreover, the sleepers lie comparatively far apart, so that when travelling one often has the sensation that the train is gliding across an elastic swamp. For the time being, therefore, one can hardly assume any substantial possibility of traffic increase on the Trans-Siberian Railway. The repairs that will foreseeably be needed very soon will do their part to depress the feasibility and speed of transport to a level that falls far short of the claims put about by the Russian side. It is not the intention here to study the economic changes that the construction of the Trans-Siberian Railway has already brought about for Siberia itself and for world commerce. But one thing is clear: on financial grounds alone, Russia cannot possibly manage in the long run with the cheap passenger and {p. 301} freight rates. If the profitability of the railway is to be ensured, either its capacity must be increased to a level unattainable under present conditions, or an increase in tariffs must be undertaken. In no case, however, would the railway fulfill the purpose so often invoked: it would not be in a position to divert significantly the transit goods traffic with the Far East, which hitherto has chosen exclusively the sea route, because freight traffic on Russian railways proceeds at an incredibly slow pace. Based on Russian commercial practice, through-freight from Germany to East Asia would foreseeably take about three times longer than by sea. Added to this is the fact that the sea route is in the long run cheaper under all circumstances. For us, therefore, the only advantages that remain are in passenger and postal traffic. There is no question that these advantages exist, although a final verdict will only be possible once the railway is completed along its entire length. For people who wish to travel in haste from Europe to the Far East, 14 days is without doubt a considerable shortening of travel time, and the same applies to postal dispatches. But the journey remains, even when the line is completed, quite exhausting, and the low cost of the trip is offset by very considerable hardships that do not arise on a sea voyage. Above all, one will also have to reckon in future with severe overcrowding on the trains, so that, first of all, travel comfort will suffer greatly, and furthermore, the prospects of securing smooth connections at all times are none too favourable.

For Siberia itself, the railway undoubtedly has immense—local—significance, for freight traffic as well. Already it has brought it about that the Siberian territories are much more closely connected to Russia than was previously the case. The Irbit Fair, which in the past almost single-handedly connected the trade between Russia and Siberia, has completely lost its significance. Siberian merchants now deal directly with Moscow and Petersburg, and often with Western Europe as well, which was previously quite out of the question, as business at that time took its course through the Irbit Fair. The railway has thus already eliminated an expensive intermediary link for Siberia's trade with Russia and abroad. But the local significance that the Trans-Siberian Railway possesses will also, in all likelihood, remain the only one that comes into consideration for freight traffic.

Insofar as the significance of the Trans-Siberian Railway for communication specifically between Germany and our East Asian possessions and spheres of interest comes into consideration, the following points can be asserted. The shortening of postal and passenger traffic benefits our colony of Tsingtao by reducing the travel time by about 14 days. Admittedly, we would only have direct advantages to expect from the Trans-Siberian Railway for our East Asian possessions if the planned connecting line through the Selenga Valley via Kyakhta and Kalgan to Peking were to be constructed, which Russia indeed apparently intends to build.*) To be sure, Russia would then be cutting into its own flesh and again rendering illusory the advantages it wished to create for its new possessions in the Far East through the Trans- {p. 305} [Illustration: General view of Moscow (p. 309).]

::: notes
*) As is well known, an obstacle has recently arisen to Russian plans in British opposition.
:::

{p. 306}

Manchurian Railway, although Russia might well be considered capable of this commercial-political folly too, after having cut off the Amur territories from world transit traffic for political reasons through the construction of the Manchurian Railway. The line from Irkutsk through Mongolia to Peking, which would then continue to Tsinanfu, where the railway connects to Tsingtao, would reach the sea over a substantially shorter distance,

[Illustration: Moscow: Kremlin and Moscow Bridge (p. 311).]

than the Manchurian Railway does at Dalny or Vladivostok. The possibility would thus exist that, through this connecting line, Tsingtao might unexpectedly be put in the place of Dalny. But if the Trans-Siberian Railway were to reach the Pacific Ocean by the shortest route at Tsingtao, this locality would likely—lying as it does more centrally within the commercially relevant part of East Asia than Dalny, and being easier to reach than the latter both from South China and from the Philippines and Japan—be able to derive a direct benefit from the Trans-Siberian Railway. For this reason, we Germans have a considerable interest in the construction of the railway across Mongolia, and that is, in my opinion, {p. 307} a viewpoint from which the Trans-Siberian Railway as such and the study of Russian transport policy is also a matter of great importance for us Germans. To be sure, one must not forget in this regard that the construction of the Mongolian line would mean an influence over Peking for Russia that would likely appear more valuable than the future of Manchuria and of easternmost Asia. The advantages that we

[Illustration: Moscow: The Kremlin from the Southwest (p. 311).]

would thus gain on the one hand for Shantung through this railway construction would in all probability be cancelled out again by Russia pressing even more ominously close to us in China. And given the character of Russian colonial policy, it is by no means out of the question that Russia itself may in the future abandon Port Arthur and Dalny just as lightheartedly as it once abandoned Petropavlovsk and more recently abandoned or disadvantaged the Amur territory and even Vladivostok in favour of a more southerly, more alluring possession. Even these direct advantages, which Germany might hope for from the Trans-Siberian Railway for its East Asian possessions, are thus of such a nature that they could easily turn into their opposite.

{p. 308}

### 3. Ural-Volga-Moscow-Petersburg-Berlin.

August 29 to September 1.

An interesting journey still lies ahead of us. Since early morning we have entered the territory of the Ural Mountains. In mighty curves the railway winds up the gentle heights, which are covered with numerous woodlands shading from the lightest to the darkest green and lush meadow slopes. Interesting rock formations alternate

[Illustration: Interior of the Kremlin (p. 311).]

with gorges and gentle valleys through which little brooks flow. The landscape gives an impression somewhat like that of the Thuringian Forest. The Urals are rich in minerals. Gold, precious stones, and above all platinum and iron are extracted here. We reach the crest of the mountain range before noon. At the end, the railway line winds up to the heights in what one might almost call corkscrew turns. At one point one can see the track twice more below on the mountainside. The descent on the other side is somewhat steeper, and at a pace that is positively breakneck for Russian conditions we speed down to Zlatoust, {p. 309} one of the centers of the Ural iron industry, where bayonets and sidearms are manufactured for the Russian army. The town itself lies picturesquely by a mountain lake, whose outflow already belongs to the Volga basin. In Zlatoust decorative cast-iron objects are also manufactured. Russian models depicting troikas, Cossacks on horseback, sled teams, and the like alternate with Western European forms. Behind Zlatoust, the European slope of the Urals merges into the plain almost more gently than on the Asian side. The train now travels considerably faster than before; after all, broader rails are laid here. The locomotives are very powerfully built and are fired no longer with wood, but with naphtha. Towards evening we have taken our leave of the mountain range bordering Asia and Europe; only in shadowy outlines do we still see the low ridges in the distance, and soon we reach the tributary of the Volga, the Belaya, which we cross on an imposing bridge near Ufa. Now we enter the Samara plain. We travel through the cultivated steppe; Russian villages and Tatar ones, recognizable by the domes of their mosques, pass by us.

At 8 o'clock on the morning of August 30 we have reached the city of Samara and with it the broad course of the Volga. The city rises picturesquely on the bank of this mighty river. It is the first major city of European Russia that we reach. Yet we continue to travel along the left bank of the Volga for quite some distance, and only after about two hours do we cross it on an enormous bridge, the longest we pass on the line, indeed one of the longest bridges in the world! As we travel across the river, we see beneath us steamers and barges plying this main waterway of European Russia. From the Volga also comes the magnificent Astrakhan caviar. Yet one hears general complaints that the abundance of sturgeon in the Volga has declined quite alarmingly. The die-off of sturgeon is attributed primarily to the discharge from the naphtha-firing used by the Volga steamers. Back on the Amur, I had purchased a large portion of caviar, for which I paid about one ruble a pound. This caviar was actually intended to be taken home; but, as things go, I ate it on the way with Nikolai Mikhailovich. Now throughout the whole journey I had consoled myself with the prospect of caviar on the Volga. Yet when I ask the price at a stall at the station in Samara, they demand 2½ rubles a pound from me. I still have my prices from the Amur in mind, and consequently the price strikes me as far too high, even though that is not the case at all. I therefore refrain from buying any. In Moscow a pound already costs four, and in Petersburg as much as six rubles.

The journey beyond the Volga continues through steppe country. Here lie the great latifundia in which the wealth of the old Russian families consists. The train speeds past towns and villages from which white church towers gleam. Our pious Russian fellow travelers never fail to cross themselves when passing a church tower. The evening of August 31 brings us to Moscow. Nikolai Mikhailovich travels on to St. Petersburg this very day, and the rest of our traveling party also scatters in all directions. The morning of September 1 shows me Moscow in all its {p. 310} [Illustration: The famous basilica in Moscow (p. 311).]

{p. 311}

splendor. I can imagine no finer conclusion to my Siberian-Russian impressions than standing on the terrace of the Kremlin, the national sanctuary of the Muscovite empire, and letting my gaze sweep from there over the countless blue and gold domes and church towers of the city, over the colorful houses through which the blue water of the Moskva winds its way. Half Oriental, half European, the ancient capital of the Russian Empire lies at my feet, and the impression is so overwhelming and imprints itself so firmly on my memory that I could almost draw the picture from my head. Yet the impression is pushed aside by a longing that now culminates in only one wish—to finally be home again. That very evening, on September 1, I travel onward to St. Petersburg, where I arrive the following morning. Sorting out my passport still causes some difficulties. I had neglected to have my departure from Vladivostok officially certified. But a three-ruble tip, which the hotel commissionaire shares with the police officer, works wonders, and so by noon on September 3 I am already able to travel onward by express train from St. Petersburg to Eydtkuhnen. After a thirty-two-hour journey, I am back in the capital of the German Empire.

I bring home the impression that, upon closer inspection, much of the aura of the unknown with which we in Germany are accustomed to cloaking our eastern neighbour does indeed dissolve, but that we will nonetheless experience much from Russia in the future, both politically and economically, the anticipation of which already compels us today to take up a position toward our eastern neighbour that, wherever our interests collide across the globe, can be nothing other than the opposite of “laisser faire, laisser aller”.

{p. 312}

# List of Some Works on Siberia.

(G. = German; R. = Russian; E. = English; F. = French.)

1. Andriyevich. History of Siberia and Siberia in the 19th Century. R.

2. [Anonymous]. History of the River Amur. St. Petersburg, 1859. R.

3. Proceedings of the Eastern Subcommission for the Construction of the Ussuri and Amur Railway. R.

4. Proceedings of the West Siberian Branch of the Imperial Russian Geographical Society. Tomsk, 1888 et seq. R.

5. Atkinson, The Book on the Amur. E.

6. Baflevsky, Nesterovsky, Troyan, Avsimov, and others. Mining Industry and Metallurgy at the Nizhny Novgorod Exhibition. A Guide through the Urals. Yekaterinburg, 1899. R.

7. Baryukov. Count Muravyov-Amursky. R.

8. Reports of the Governors of Yeniseysk, Irkutsk, and Yakutsk to the Tsar. R.

{p. [332]}

Published by Georg Wigand in Leipzig

### Deutschland in China [Germany in China]

By

Rudolf Zabel

Large 8vo. XVI and 433 pp. Price paperbound Mk. 7.50, bound Mk. 9.—

The book, which falls into two main parts, represents a gratifying achievement. First, the policy of the powers toward China, Russia's tendency toward territorial acquisition, and, in contrast to this, the commercial practice of the other powers, are discussed from a strictly historical-juridical standpoint. Then, however, the author, who took part in the campaign as a German war correspondent, reports his own experiences and the results of his exploratory travels in Manchuria, Shandong, and South China, with other observations also being taken into account and addressed. Thus the actual history of the campaign is given further color through vivid presentation and firsthand observation, in particular through the departure, the first actions, and the occupation of the province of Zhili in its full extent and development. All kinds of East Asian questions, answers, and further problems form the instructive supplement. The book is practical, clear, and well-organized, and will thoroughly dispel many erroneous accounts that have circulated since then. *Münchener Allgemeine Zeitung* [Munich General Newspaper], November 11, 1902.

{p. [333]}

Published by Georg Wigand in Leipzig

### Dreiunddreißig Jahre in Ost-Asien [Thirty-Three Years in East Asia]

Memoirs of a German Diplomat

By M. von Brandt
Actual Privy Councillor, Imperial Envoy (ret.)

3 volumes. With a frontispiece in each.
Large 8vo. Price stitched Mk. 19.50, bound Mk. 24.—

* * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * *

. . . . One may rightly lend an ear to the words of a man who has lived for decades in the Far East and must therefore know the land and its people intimately: much of what he says must help to clarify views. Now that Herr von Brandt has decided to publish the results and experiences of his long official career, he can be assured of the attention of a large readership. Hamburger Nachrichten [Hamburg News].
